=== theory-01 | Alphabets, strings and languages
--- teach
In Discrete Mathematics you worked with sets, functions and the diagonal argument. In the algorithms courses you wrote programs that answer questions: is there a path, is this list sorted, does this item exist. This course asks a bigger question: **which problems can a machine solve at all, and with how much memory and time?** To answer it, a "problem" has to be as precise as a set. This lesson builds that language. Everything in the rest of the course is written in it.

### A command link that says yes or no

Picture the command link to a satellite. The ground sends short strings of symbols, and the flight computer checks each one before acting on it. Some strings are well-formed commands, and the rest are rejected. Forget, for now, *how* it checks. The whole behaviour of the checker is captured by one thing: the set of strings it says yes to. That set is called its **language**. This lesson defines the words in that sentence precisely.

### Alphabets and strings

An **alphabet** is a finite, non-empty set of symbols. It is usually written Σ (the Greek capital letter sigma). Examples:

- Σ = {0, 1}, the binary alphabet;
- Σ = {a, b};
- Σ = {A, C, G, T}, the four bases of DNA;
- the 128 [[ASCII characters|finite-alphabet]].

A **string** over Σ (also called a **word**) is a finite sequence of symbols from Σ, such as `0110` or `abba`. Its **length**, written |w| and read "the length of w", is how many symbols it has: |abba| = 4.

There is exactly one string of length 0, the **empty string**. It is written **[[ε|epsilon-name]]** (the Greek letter epsilon), because an empty space on the page is invisible. In Python it is `""`.

In code, a Python `str` is a string, and this course writes an alphabet as a Python string of distinct symbols, such as `"01"` or `"abc"`. The order of the symbols in that Python string gives the alphabet an order, which matters later.

### Joining strings

The **concatenation** of x and y, written xy, is x followed by y: if x = `ab` and y = `ba`, then xy = `abba` and yx = `baab`. Three facts follow straight from the definition:

- |xy| = |x| + |y|.
- It is **associative**: (xy)z = x(yz), so brackets are never needed.
- ε is its **identity**: εw = wε = w, the way 0 is for addition.

It is not **commutative**: xy and yx usually differ, as `abba` and `baab` show.

A few more words you will use all course:

- **Powers:** w⁰ = ε, and wᵏ⁺¹ = wᵏw. So (ab)³ = `ababab`.
- **Reversal:** wᴿ is w written backwards: (`abb`)ᴿ = `bba`.
- **Prefix:** x is a prefix of w when w = xy for some string y. So ε, `a`, `ab` and `abb` are the prefixes of `abb`. Every string is a prefix of itself, and ε is a prefix of every string.
- **Suffix:** y is a suffix of w when w = xy for some x.
- **Substring:** v is a substring of w when w = xvy for some x and y.

### Why (xy)ᴿ = yᴿxᴿ

Reversing a join reverses both parts *and* swaps their order, the way taking off socks and shoes happens in the opposite order to putting them on. Here is the proof, by induction on |y|, the length of y:

- **Base case, |y| = 0:** then y = ε, so (xε)ᴿ = xᴿ = εxᴿ = εᴿxᴿ.
- **Step:** assume the claim for every y of length n, and take y of length n + 1. Write y = za, where z has length n and a is one symbol. Reversing puts the last symbol first, so (xza)ᴿ = a(xz)ᴿ. By the assumption, (xz)ᴿ = zᴿxᴿ. So (xy)ᴿ = a zᴿ xᴿ. And a zᴿ is exactly (za)ᴿ = yᴿ. So (xy)ᴿ = yᴿxᴿ.

Almost every fact about strings is proved this way: induction on the length, peeling off one symbol.

### All the strings of one length

Σᵏ is the set of all strings over Σ of length exactly k. There are |Σ|ᵏ of them, by the product rule from counting: k positions, and |Σ| choices at each. So {0, 1}³ has 2³ = 8 strings. Note that Σ⁰ = {ε}, which has one string, not zero.

Python's `itertools.product` builds them. With `repeat=k` it gives every k-tuple of symbols, in the order of the symbols you pass:

```python
from itertools import product

pairs = ["".join(t) for t in product("xyz", repeat=2)]
print(len(pairs))    # 9, which is 3 * 3
print(pairs[:4])     # ['xx', 'xy', 'xz', 'yx']
```

### Σ*, the set of every string

**Σ\*** (read "sigma star") is the set of *all* strings over Σ, of every length: Σ⁰ ∪ Σ¹ ∪ Σ² ∪ … It is infinite, but every string in it is finite. **Σ⁺** is the same without ε.

How many strings have length at most n? Add up the lengths: 1 + k + k² + … + kⁿ, where k = |Σ|. That is a geometric sum, which you met in the loop analysis lesson. For k ≥ 2 it equals (kⁿ⁺¹ − 1)/(k − 1). For k = 1 it is n + 1, since there is one string of each length. So listing every binary string of length at most 30 means about 2.1 billion strings. **Enumerating strings costs time exponential in the length**, and this fact comes back all course.

### Putting Σ* in order

Can you list Σ* so that every string shows up at some finite position? Dictionary order fails. Over {a, b}, dictionary order starts ε, a, aa, aaa, aaaa, … and never reaches `b`.

**Shortlex order** fixes this: shorter strings come first, and strings of the same length are in dictionary order (using the alphabet's order). Over {a, b}:

ε, a, b, aa, ab, ba, bb, aaa, aab, …

Each length has finitely many strings, so every string appears after finitely many others. That gives a one-to-one matching between Σ* and the natural numbers 0, 1, 2, …. In the words of the countability lesson: **Σ\* is countably infinite**.

### Languages

A **language** over Σ is any set of strings over Σ: any subset L ⊆ Σ*. Some languages over {0, 1}:

- ∅, the empty language, with no strings at all;
- {ε}, the language holding only the empty string. It is **not** the same as ∅: it has one member;
- Σ* itself;
- {w : w has an even number of 1s}, which is infinite;
- {w : w is the binary form of a prime}, such as `10`, `11`, `101`.

Languages are how this course says "problem". A yes-or-no question about finite objects becomes a question about strings once you [[encode|encoding]] the objects as strings. "Is this graph connected?" becomes "is this string, which spells out a graph's edge list, in the language of strings that spell out connected graphs?" A problem is **a language, and solving it means deciding membership**: given w, answer whether w ∈ L.

### Operations on languages

Languages are sets, so union (L₁ ∪ L₂), intersection (L₁ ∩ L₂) and difference work as usual. The **complement** of L is Σ* − L, the strings over Σ that are not in L. Three more operations come from concatenation:

- **Concatenation:** L₁L₂ = {xy : x ∈ L₁ and y ∈ L₂}. Every way of gluing a string of L₁ in front of a string of L₂.
- **Powers:** L⁰ = {ε}, and Lᵏ⁺¹ = LᵏL. So Lᵏ holds every join of k strings from L, one after another.
- **Kleene star:** L* = L⁰ ∪ L¹ ∪ L² ∪ …, every join of **zero or more** strings from L. It is named after [[Stephen Kleene|kleene]]. L⁺ = LL* is one or more.

A worked concatenation, with A = {a, ab} and B = {b, ε}:

| x from A | y from B | xy |
|---|---|---|
| a | b | ab |
| a | ε | a |
| ab | b | abb |
| ab | ε | ab |

Four pairs, but AB = {a, ab, abb} has only 3 strings, because `ab` comes out twice. In general |L₁L₂| ≤ |L₁|·|L₂|, and it can be smaller.

The corner cases are worth learning by heart:

- L∅ = ∅L = ∅. There is no string in ∅ to glue on, so no pairs exist.
- L{ε} = {ε}L = L.
- ε ∈ L* for every L, because L⁰ = {ε} is part of the union.
- ∅* = {ε} and {ε}* = {ε}. Both are finite.
- L* is infinite exactly when L contains some non-empty string.

### A proof about languages: L*L* = L*

To show two sets are equal, show each is a subset of the other, as in Discrete Mathematics.

- **L\* ⊆ L\*L\*:** take w ∈ L*. Then w = wε, with w ∈ L* and ε ∈ L*. So w ∈ L*L*.
- **L\*L\* ⊆ L\*:** take w ∈ L*L*. Then w = xy with x ∈ L* and y ∈ L*. So x is a join of some i strings from L, and y a join of some j strings from L. Then xy is a join of i + j strings from L, so xy ∈ Lⁱ⁺ʲ ⊆ L*.

Both inclusions hold, so the sets are equal. The same style proves (L*)* = L*.

### Computing with infinite languages

A program cannot list L*: it is usually infinite. But it can list the part of L* with length at most n. The idea is a **fixed point**: start with the set {ε}. Repeatedly take a string you have, glue a string of L on the end, and keep the result if its length is at most n and it is new. Stop when a whole round adds nothing.

Why does this stop? Everything it adds is a string of length at most n, and there are only finitely many of those. Why is the answer right? Every string it adds is a join of strings from L, so it is in L*. And every string of L* with length at most n is a join x₁x₂…xₘ of strings from L. Skipping the empty ones leaves a join of non-empty strings, and each prefix x₁…xᵢ is at most as long as the whole. So the process builds x₁, then x₁x₂, and so on, one round each, and reaches the whole string.

Here is a trace with L = {ab, b} and n = 3:

| round | new strings |
|---|---|
| start | ε |
| 1 | ab, b |
| 2 | abb, bab, bb |
| 3 | bbb |
| 4 | (none, so stop) |

Round 2 skips `abab`, which has length 4. Round 3 adds only `bbb`: every other join, such as `abbb` or `bbab`, is too long.

### More languages than programs

Here is the first big result of the course, and it needs only the diagonal argument you already know.

A program is a finite string over the keyboard's alphabet, so there are only countably many programs: list them in shortlex order. But there are **uncountably many** languages. List Σ* in shortlex order as w₀, w₁, w₂, … Now suppose someone hands you a list of languages L₀, L₁, L₂, …, and claims it has them all. Build the **diagonal language**:

D = {wᵢ : wᵢ ∉ Lᵢ}.

D differs from L₀ on w₀, from L₁ on w₁, and from each Lᵢ on wᵢ: exactly one of D and Lᵢ contains wᵢ. So D is on nobody's list. No list of languages is complete, while the programs can be listed. So **some languages are decided by no program at all**. Lesson 13 turns this counting argument into a specific one: the halting problem.

### Where it is used

- **Protocols and file formats** are specified as languages: the set of valid packets, the set of valid JSON documents.
- **Compilers** first split source code into tokens, each token kind a language (identifiers, numbers). You will build that from lesson 4 on.
- **Biology** treats DNA as strings over {A, C, G, T} and searches them with the same tools.
- **Complexity theory** writes every problem, from shortest paths to scheduling, as a language, so "how hard is it" has one precise meaning.

**Watch out:** three mix-ups cause most mistakes in this lesson.

- ∅, {ε} and ε are three different things: a set with no strings, a set with one string, and a string. ∅* = {ε}, while L∅ = ∅.
- Concatenation of languages is not the cartesian product. Pairs that give the same string collapse into one string, so count the strings, not the pairs.
- When computing a star, an empty string in L adds nothing new. A loop that waits for the set to stop growing is safe; a loop that counts on every step making something longer never ends.

::: context finite-alphabet Why the alphabet must be finite
A machine reads one symbol at a time and must react to each possible symbol, so a finite machine needs a finite list of possible symbols. Real alphabets are finite: ASCII has 128 characters, bytes have 256 values, and Unicode, though huge, is finite too. Anything richer, like a number of any size, is written as a string of symbols from a small alphabet, the way 2025 is written with the digits 2, 0 and 5.
:::

::: context epsilon-name Other names for the empty string
Books disagree on the symbol. This course and Sipser's textbook use ε. Some older books use λ (lambda) or Λ, and some logic texts write 1 or e, because the empty string is the identity for joining, the way 1 is for multiplying. In Python it is `""`, and `len("") == 0`. Whatever the symbol, it means one string with no symbols in it, never "nothing at all".
:::

::: context encoding Turning objects into strings
Any finite object can be written as a string. A number becomes its binary digits. A graph with vertices 0 to 3 and edges (0,1) and (2,3) can become the string `4;0-1;2-3`. A program is already a string. The exact encoding rarely matters, as long as a program can translate between reasonable encodings quickly. What does matter is that the input size is the length of the string, which is why a number n counts as size about log₂ n (its digit count), not n.
:::

::: context kleene Who Kleene was
Stephen Cole Kleene (pronounced "clay-nee") was an American logician. In 1951 he wrote a report for the RAND Corporation on nerve nets and automata, published in 1956. In it he introduced what are now called regular expressions and the star operation, and proved that they describe exactly the languages finite machines recognise. You will prove that theorem yourself in lessons 3 and 4.
:::
--- task
Write four functions. Strings are Python `str`, and sets of strings are Python `set`s. An alphabet `sigma` is a Python string of distinct symbols, and its order is the order the symbols appear in it.

- `strings_up_to(sigma, n)`: return a **list** of every string over `sigma` with length at most `n` (where `n ≥ 0`), in shortlex order: shorter strings first, and strings of the same length in dictionary order using the order of `sigma`. For example, `strings_up_to("10", 2)` is `["", "1", "0", "11", "10", "01", "00"]`.
- `concat(A, B)`: return the set AB, every string `x + y` with `x` in `A` and `y` in `B`.
- `power(A, k)`: return the set Aᵏ, where A⁰ is `{""}` (for any `A`, even the empty set) and k ≥ 0.
- `star_up_to(A, n)`: return the set of strings in A* whose length is at most `n`. It must always finish, even when `""` is in `A` and when `A` is empty.

Do not change `A` or `B`.
--- starter
def strings_up_to(sigma, n):
    return []


def concat(A, B):
    return set()


def power(A, k):
    return set()


def star_up_to(A, n):
    return set()
--- solution
from itertools import product


def strings_up_to(sigma, n):
    out = []
    for length in range(n + 1):
        for letters in product(sigma, repeat=length):
            out.append("".join(letters))
    return out


def concat(A, B):
    return {x + y for x in A for y in B}


def power(A, k):
    result = {""}
    for _ in range(k):
        result = concat(result, A)
    return result


def star_up_to(A, n):
    pieces = [a for a in A if 0 < len(a) <= n]
    found = {""}
    frontier = [""]
    while frontier:
        new = []
        for w in frontier:
            for a in pieces:
                longer = w + a
                if len(longer) <= n and longer not in found:
                    found.add(longer)
                    new.append(longer)
        frontier = new
    return found
--- hint
For `strings_up_to`, go through the lengths 0, 1, …, n in turn. For one length, `itertools.product(sigma, repeat=length)` gives the tuples in dictionary order of `sigma`; join each tuple into a string.
--- hint
`power` starts from `{""}` (that is A⁰) and concatenates with `A` once per step, k times. `star_up_to` also starts from `{""}`: keep a list of strings found in the last round, and glue every non-empty string of `A` onto each of them.
--- hint
In `star_up_to`, keep a new string only if its length is at most `n` and it is not already in your set. Stop when a round finds nothing new. Skipping `""` in `A` is safe, because gluing it on never makes anything new.
--- check case | Shortlex order over "ab" up to length 2
strings_up_to("ab", 2)
=> ["", "a", "b", "aa", "ab", "ba", "bb"]
--- check case | The alphabet's own order is used
strings_up_to("ba", 2)
=> ["", "b", "a", "bb", "ba", "ab", "aa"]
--- check case | Length 0 gives only the empty string, one symbol gives one string per length
(strings_up_to("01", 0), strings_up_to("x", 3))
=> ([""], ["", "x", "xx", "xxx"])
--- check test | 3 symbols up to length 5: 1 + 3 + 9 + 27 + 81 + 243 = 364 strings, all different, lengths never decreasing
(lambda L: len(L) == 364 and len(set(L)) == 364 and all(len(L[i]) <= len(L[i + 1]) for i in range(363)) and L[4] == "00")(strings_up_to("012", 5))
--- check case | concat collapses pairs that give the same string
concat({"a", "ab"}, {"b", ""})
=> {"a", "ab", "abb"}
--- check case | concat with the empty set is empty, with {""} is unchanged
(concat({"x", "y"}, set()), concat(set(), {"x"}), concat({"x", "y"}, {""}))
=> (set(), set(), {"x", "y"})
--- check case | concat is not commutative
(concat({"0"}, {"1"}), concat({"1"}, {"0"}))
=> ({"01"}, {"10"})
--- check case | power(A, 0) is {""}, even for the empty set
(power({"a", "b"}, 0), power(set(), 0), power(set(), 3))
=> ({""}, {""}, set())
--- check case | power({"a", "ab"}, 2)
power({"a", "ab"}, 2)
=> {"aa", "aab", "aba", "abab"}
--- check test | power of a 3-string set: 546 different strings in A^6
len(power({"0", "1", "01"}, 6)) == 546
--- check case | The star of the empty set, and of {""}, is {""}
(star_up_to(set(), 5), star_up_to({""}, 5))
=> ({""}, {""})
--- check case | "" in A does not stop the loop from ending
star_up_to({"", "ab"}, 5)
=> {"", "ab", "abab"}
--- check case | star of {"aa", "aaa"} up to length 7: every length except 1
star_up_to({"aa", "aaa"}, 7)
=> {"", "aa", "aaa", "aaaa", "aaaaa", "aaaaaa", "aaaaaaa"}
--- check case | A string of A longer than n never appears, and n = 0 gives {""}
(star_up_to({"abc", "z"}, 2), star_up_to({"a"}, 0))
=> ({"", "z", "zz"}, {""})
--- check test | star_up_to agrees with the definition, on seeded random sets
(lambda R, re, P: all((lambda A, n: star_up_to(A, n) == {"".join(t) for k in range(n + 1) for t in P("ab", repeat=k) if re.fullmatch("(?:" + "|".join(sorted(A)) + ")*", "".join(t))})({"".join(R.choice("ab") for _ in range(R.randint(0, 3))) for _ in range(R.randint(0, 3))}, R.randint(0, 7)) for _ in range(60)))(__import__("random").Random(11), __import__("re"), __import__("itertools").product)
--- check test | star of {"0", "1"} up to length 16 has 2^17 - 1 = 131,071 strings
len(star_up_to({"0", "1"}, 16)) == 131071
--- check test | Does not change A
(lambda A: (star_up_to(A, 4), power(A, 2), concat(A, A), A)[3] == {"a", ""})({"a", ""})

+++ practice | Every prefix, suffix and substring
--- task
Write `pieces(w)` that returns a tuple of three sets: `(prefixes, suffixes, substrings)` of the string `w`. Include `""` and `w` itself in all three, since the empty string and `w` are prefixes, suffixes and substrings of `w`.

For example, `pieces("aba")` is `({"", "a", "ab", "aba"}, {"", "a", "ba", "aba"}, {"", "a", "b", "ab", "ba", "aba"})`. For `w = ""` all three sets are `{""}`.
--- starter
def pieces(w):
    return set(), set(), set()
--- solution
def pieces(w):
    n = len(w)
    prefixes = {w[:i] for i in range(n + 1)}
    suffixes = {w[i:] for i in range(n + 1)}
    substrings = {w[i:j] for i in range(n + 1) for j in range(i, n + 1)}
    return prefixes, suffixes, substrings
--- hint
A prefix is `w[:i]` and a suffix is `w[i:]`, for `i` from 0 up to and including `len(w)`.
--- hint
A substring is `w[i:j]` with `0 <= i <= j <= len(w)`. When `i == j` the slice is `""`, which is how the empty string gets in.
--- check case | The example from the task
pieces("aba")
=> ({"", "a", "ab", "aba"}, {"", "a", "ba", "aba"}, {"", "a", "b", "ab", "ba", "aba"})
--- check case | The empty string
pieces("")
=> ({""}, {""}, {""})
--- check case | One symbol
pieces("z")
=> ({"", "z"}, {"", "z"}, {"", "z"})
--- check test | Repeats collapse: "aaaa" has 5 different substrings, "banana" has 16
(len(pieces("aaaa")[2]), len(pieces("banana")[2])) == (5, 16)
--- check test | A string of length n has exactly n + 1 prefixes and n + 1 suffixes
all(len(pieces(w)[0]) == len(w) + 1 == len(pieces(w)[1]) for w in ["", "ab", "abcab", "0110100"])

+++ practice | A string's position in shortlex order
--- task
List every string over an alphabet `sigma` in shortlex order, starting at position 0: over `"ab"` that is `""` (0), `"a"` (1), `"b"` (2), `"aa"` (3), `"ab"` (4), and so on.

Write two functions:

- `shortlex_rank(w, sigma)`: the position of the string `w`.
- `shortlex_unrank(i, sigma)`: the string at position `i` (an integer, `i ≥ 0`).

They must be quick for long strings: a check ranks a string of length 60, whose position is about 2⁶¹, so listing strings one by one is far too slow. Work it out with arithmetic, one symbol at a time. The alphabet may have just one symbol: over `"a"`, the string `"aaa"` is at position 3.
--- starter
def shortlex_rank(w, sigma):
    return 0


def shortlex_unrank(i, sigma):
    return ""
--- solution
def shortlex_rank(w, sigma):
    k = len(sigma)
    rank = 0
    for c in w:
        rank = rank * k + sigma.index(c) + 1
    return rank


def shortlex_unrank(i, sigma):
    k = len(sigma)
    letters = []
    while i > 0:
        i -= 1
        letters.append(sigma[i % k])
        i //= k
    return "".join(reversed(letters))
--- hint
Give the symbols the digits 1, 2, …, k (where k is the alphabet size), not 0 to k − 1. Then read `w` as a number in base k with those digits: for each symbol, multiply the total so far by k and add the symbol's digit. Try it on `"aa"` over `"ab"`: 1·2 + 1 = 3.
--- hint
This works because it counts exactly the strings before `w`: all the shorter ones, then the same-length ones that come earlier. To go back, peel off the last symbol: subtract 1, the remainder mod k picks the symbol, and integer division by k gives the position of the rest.
--- check case | Positions over "ab"
[shortlex_rank(w, "ab") for w in ["", "a", "b", "aa", "ab", "bb", "aaa"]]
=> [0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 7]
--- check case | Unrank over "ab"
[shortlex_unrank(i, "ab") for i in range(8)]
=> ["", "a", "b", "aa", "ab", "ba", "bb", "aaa"]
--- check case | One-symbol alphabet, and the alphabet's own order
(shortlex_rank("aaa", "a"), shortlex_unrank(3, "a"), shortlex_rank("ba", "ba"), shortlex_rank("", "xyz"))
=> (3, "aaa", 4, 0)
--- check test | Rank and unrank match the real shortlex list over "abc" for every string up to length 4
(lambda P: all(shortlex_rank(w, "abc") == i and shortlex_unrank(i, "abc") == w for i, w in enumerate("".join(t) for n in range(5) for t in P("abc", repeat=n))))(__import__("itertools").product)
--- check test | A string of length 60, and back again
shortlex_rank("10" * 30, "01") == 1921535841011411625 and shortlex_unrank(1921535841011411625, "01") == "10" * 30
--- check case | Position 10^18 over "01"
shortlex_unrank(10**18, "01")
=> "10111100000101101101011001110100111011001000000000000000001"

+++ practice | Is this string in A*?
--- task
Write `in_star(w, A)` that returns `True` when the string `w` can be cut into pieces that all belong to the finite set `A` (so `w` is in A*), and `False` otherwise. The empty string is always in A*, even when `A` is empty.

It must be fast: a check uses strings of 3,000 symbols. Trying every way of cutting takes exponential time, and deep recursion hits Python's recursion limit, so use **dynamic programming** over prefixes: `ok[i]` is `True` when the first `i` symbols of `w` are in A*. Then `ok[0]` is `True`, and `ok[i]` is `True` when some non-empty piece `a` in `A` ends at position `i` and `ok[i - len(a)]` is `True`.

For example, `in_star("0110", {"01", "10"})` is `True` (`01` then `10`), and `in_star("a", {"aa", "aaa"})` is `False`.
--- starter
def in_star(w, A):
    return w in A
--- solution
def in_star(w, A):
    pieces = [a for a in A if a]
    ok = [False] * (len(w) + 1)
    ok[0] = True
    for end in range(1, len(w) + 1):
        for a in pieces:
            start = end - len(a)
            if start >= 0 and ok[start] and w[start:end] == a:
                ok[end] = True
                break
    return ok[len(w)]
--- hint
Make a list `ok` of `len(w) + 1` values, all `False` except `ok[0]`. Fill it from left to right.
--- hint
For each end position, try every non-empty piece `a`: it fits when `w[end - len(a):end] == a` and the part before it, `ok[end - len(a)]`, is already `True`. Skip `""`: it never helps and would make you look at the same position again.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(in_star("0110", {"01", "10"}), in_star("a", {"aa", "aaa"}))
=> (True, False)
--- check case | The empty string is always in A*, and nothing else is in the star of the empty set
(in_star("", set()), in_star("", {"x"}), in_star("a", set()))
=> (True, True, False)
--- check case | An empty piece in A changes nothing
(in_star("aba", {"ab", ""}), in_star("abab", {"ab", ""}))
=> (False, True)
--- check case | Pieces that overlap in more than one way
(in_star("abcab", {"ab", "abc", "cab"}), in_star("abca", {"ab", "abc", "cab"}))
=> (True, False)
--- check test | 3,000 symbols: every length from 2 on is a sum of 2s and 3s, and a stray b is never in
in_star("a" * 3000, {"aa", "aaa"}) and not in_star("a" * 3000 + "b", {"a", "aa", "aaa"})
?? Try every cut position once, left to right, and remember the answer for each prefix.

+++ practice | What is left after a prefix
--- task
The **left quotient** of a language L by a string u is the set of strings v with uv ∈ L: what can follow u to finish a string of L. For a set of strings U, the quotient is the union of the quotients by each u in U.

Write two functions, for finite sets of strings:

- `quotient(u, L)`: the set `{v : u + v is in L}`.
- `quotient_set(U, L)`: the union of `quotient(u, L)` over every `u` in `U`.

For example, `quotient("ab", {"abc", "ab", "a", "abab"})` is `{"c", "", "ab"}`. Watch the edge cases: `quotient("", L)` is `L` itself, a `u` that no string of `L` starts with gives the empty set, and so does an empty `U`.
--- starter
def quotient(u, L):
    return {w for w in L if w.startswith(u)}


def quotient_set(U, L):
    return set()
--- solution
def quotient(u, L):
    return {w[len(u):] for w in L if w.startswith(u)}


def quotient_set(U, L):
    out = set()
    for u in U:
        out |= quotient(u, L)
    return out
--- hint
A string `w` of `L` counts only if it starts with `u`. What is left of it is the slice after the first `len(u)` symbols.
--- hint
When `w == u`, the slice is `""`, so the empty string belongs to the quotient: that is right, since u followed by ε is in L.
--- check case | The example from the task
quotient("ab", {"abc", "ab", "a", "abab"})
=> {"c", "", "ab"}
--- check case | Quotient by the empty string is L itself
quotient("", {"x", "", "yz"})
=> {"x", "", "yz"}
--- check case | Nothing starts with u
(quotient("zz", {"z", "az"}), quotient("a", set()))
=> (set(), set())
--- check case | quotient_set joins the pieces
quotient_set({"", "a"}, {"ab", "b"})
=> {"ab", "b"}
--- check case | An empty U gives the empty set
quotient_set(set(), {"a", "b"})
=> set()
--- check test | Does not change L
(lambda L: (quotient("a", L), quotient_set({"a", ""}, L), L)[2] == {"ab", "a"})({"ab", "a"})

+++ practice | Debug: the first strings in the wrong order
--- task
`first_strings(sigma, count)` should return a list of the first `count` strings over the alphabet `sigma` in **shortlex order**: shorter strings first, and strings of the same length in dictionary order by the order of the symbols in `sigma`. For example, `first_strings("ab", 7)` should be `["", "a", "b", "aa", "ab", "ba", "bb"]`, and `first_strings("ab", 0)` is `[]`.

It builds the strings one length at a time, but it returns `["", "a", "aa", "ab", "b", "ba", "bb"]` for that example. Find the bug and fix it. The fix is one line.
--- starter
def first_strings(sigma, count):
    out = [""]
    frontier = [""]
    while len(out) < count:
        frontier = [w + c for w in frontier for c in sigma]
        out.extend(frontier)
    return sorted(out)[:count]
--- solution
def first_strings(sigma, count):
    out = [""]
    frontier = [""]
    while len(out) < count:
        frontier = [w + c for w in frontier for c in sigma]
        out.extend(frontier)
    return out[:count]
--- hint
What order does `sorted` put strings in? Is `"aa"` before `"b"` in shortlex order?
--- hint
The list is already built in shortlex order: each length comes after the one before, and within a length the comprehension follows `sigma`. Sorting breaks it, and it also ignores the order of `sigma`.
--- check case | The example from the task
first_strings("ab", 7)
=> ["", "a", "b", "aa", "ab", "ba", "bb"]
--- check case | The alphabet's order, not Python's
first_strings("10", 7)
=> ["", "1", "0", "11", "10", "01", "00"]
--- check case | Count 0 and count 1
(first_strings("ab", 0), first_strings("ab", 1))
=> ([], [""])
--- check case | One symbol
first_strings("x", 4)
=> ["", "x", "xx", "xxx"]

+++ practice | Can the ground always decode the downlink?
--- task
A spacecraft sends its readings as a stream of **codewords** from a finite set C, with no separators. The ground can decode every stream only if the code is **uniquely decodable**: no string can be cut into codewords in two different ways. For example, C = {0, 01, 10} is not: `010` is both `0`·`10` and `01`·`0`.

The **Sardinas–Patterson algorithm** decides this. For two sets X and Y, let dangling(X, Y) be the set of strings v with xv in Y for some x in X (the left quotient from the last problem). Then:

1. S₁ = dangling(C, C) with `""` removed.
2. Sₙ₊₁ = dangling(C, Sₙ) ∪ dangling(Sₙ, C).
3. If some Sₙ contains `""`, the code is **not** uniquely decodable. If Sₙ is empty, or equals a set seen before, it **is**.

It always stops, because every Sₙ holds only suffixes of codewords, and there are finitely many of those.

Write `is_uniquely_decodable(code)` for a non-empty set of strings `code`, returning `True` or `False`. A code that contains `""` is never uniquely decodable (the empty codeword can be inserted anywhere), so return `False` for it.
--- starter
def is_uniquely_decodable(code):
    return True
--- solution
def dangling(X, Y):
    return {y[len(x):] for x in X for y in Y if y.startswith(x)}


def is_uniquely_decodable(code):
    if "" in code:
        return False
    current = dangling(code, code) - {""}
    seen = set()
    while current:
        if "" in current:
            return False
        key = frozenset(current)
        if key in seen:
            return True
        seen.add(key)
        current = dangling(code, current) | dangling(current, code)
    return True
--- hint
Write `dangling(X, Y)` first: for every `x` in `X` and `y` in `Y` where `y` starts with `x`, keep `y[len(x):]`.
--- hint
Loop while the current set is not empty. Return `False` as soon as it holds `""`. Keep a set of the sets you have seen (as `frozenset`s, since a normal set cannot go inside a set); seeing one again means the sequence repeats forever without `""`, so return `True`.
--- check case | {0, 01, 10} is not uniquely decodable: 010 splits two ways
is_uniquely_decodable({"0", "01", "10"})
=> False
--- check case | A prefix code always is
is_uniquely_decodable({"0", "10", "110", "111"})
=> True
--- check case | {0, 01, 11} is, although 0 is a prefix of 01
is_uniquely_decodable({"0", "01", "11"})
=> True
--- check case | The classic hard example is not
is_uniquely_decodable({"1", "011", "01110", "1110", "10011"})
=> False
--- check case | One codeword, a codeword and its double, and the empty codeword
(is_uniquely_decodable({"a"}), is_uniquely_decodable({"a", "aa"}), is_uniquely_decodable({"", "a"}))
=> (True, False, False)
--- check case | More codes
[is_uniquely_decodable(c) for c in [{"0", "01", "011", "0111"}, {"01", "10", "0110"}, {"a", "b", "ab"}, {"ab", "abb", "bab", "b"}]]
=> [True, False, False, False]

+++ question | Three different empties
--- ask
Let L = {ab}. Which of these is true?
--- choice
L∅ = L, because gluing on nothing changes nothing.
--- choice correct
L∅ = ∅ and L{ε} = L.
--- choice
L{ε} = ∅, because ε has no symbols.
--- choice
∅* = ∅, because there is nothing to repeat.
--- why
L∅ is the set of xy with x in L and y in ∅. There is no y to choose, so there are no pairs, and L∅ = ∅. With {ε} there is one choice, y = ε, and xε = x, so L{ε} = L. And ∅* = {ε}, because the star always includes the join of zero strings.

+++ question | Why the diagonal language is on no list
--- ask
List Σ* in shortlex order as w₀, w₁, w₂, … and let L₀, L₁, L₂, … be any list of languages. Let D = {wᵢ : wᵢ ∉ Lᵢ}. Why is D different from every Lᵢ?
--- choice
Because D is infinite and each Lᵢ is finite.
--- choice correct
Because for each i, the string wᵢ is in exactly one of D and Lᵢ.
--- choice
Because D contains every string that no Lᵢ contains.
--- choice
Because D is the complement of the union of all the Lᵢ.
--- why
By the definition of D, wᵢ ∈ D exactly when wᵢ ∉ Lᵢ. So D and Lᵢ disagree on the string wᵢ, and two sets that disagree on one string are different. Nothing is assumed about the sizes of the Lᵢ, and D is not built from their union: it looks at Lᵢ only at the one string wᵢ.

+++ question | Counting the strings of a concatenation
--- ask
A has 3 strings and B has 4 strings. What can you say for certain about |AB|, the number of strings in AB?
--- choice
|AB| = 12, by the product rule.
--- choice correct
1 ≤ |AB| ≤ 12, and it can be less than 12 when two pairs give the same string.
--- choice
|AB| = 7, because concatenation adds the sets together.
--- why
There are 3 · 4 = 12 pairs (x, y), so at most 12 strings. But different pairs can give the same string: with A = {a, ab} and B = {b, ε}, the pairs (a, b) and (ab, ε) both give ab. Since both sets are non-empty there is at least one string, and A = {ε, a, aa}, B = {ε, a, aa, aaa} gives only 6.
=== theory-02 | Deterministic finite automata
--- teach
Last lesson a problem became a language: a set of strings, and solving the problem means deciding whether a given string is in the set. This lesson meets the simplest machine that decides languages. It reads the string one symbol at a time, from left to right, and remembers only which of a fixed, finite number of **states** it is in. It is called a **deterministic finite automaton**, or **DFA**. Its memory never grows, however long the input is, and that single limit decides everything it can and cannot do.

### A machine with a few moods

Picture the parity check on a radio link. The receiver counts the 1 bits in each frame, but it never needs the actual count. It only needs to know whether the count so far is even or odd. So it keeps one of two moods, "even" and "odd", and flips mood at every 1. At the end of the frame, the mood is the answer.

That is a DFA with two states. Here it is as a **transition table**: the row is the state you are in, the column is the symbol you read, and the entry is the state you move to.

| state | read 0 | read 1 |
|---|---|---|
| → even (accepting) | even | odd |
| odd | odd | even |

The arrow marks the **start state**. "Accepting" marks the states in which the machine says yes if the input ends there.

### The precise definition

A DFA is a 5-tuple M = (Q, Σ, δ, q₀, F), where:

1. Q is a finite set of **states**;
2. Σ is the alphabet;
3. δ : Q × Σ → Q is the **transition function** (δ is the Greek letter delta). For every state q and every symbol a, δ(q, a) is exactly **one** state: that is what "deterministic" means;
4. q₀ ∈ Q is the **start state**;
5. F ⊆ Q is the set of **accepting states** (also called final states).

In code, this course writes a DFA as a Python dictionary with the same five parts. The transition function is a dictionary from `(state, symbol)` pairs to states:

```python
parity = {
    "states": {"even", "odd"},
    "alphabet": "01",
    "delta": {
        ("even", "0"): "even", ("even", "1"): "odd",
        ("odd", "0"): "odd", ("odd", "1"): "even",
    },
    "start": "even",
    "accept": {"even"},
}
print(parity["delta"][("odd", "1")])    # even
print(len(parity["delta"]))            # 4 = |Q| * |Σ| = 2 * 2
```

Because δ is a function on all of Q × Σ, the dictionary has exactly |Q|·|Σ| entries. A DFA with an entry missing is **partial**, and the definition does not allow it.

### Running a DFA

To run M on w = a₁a₂…aₙ, start in r₀ = q₀ and apply δ once per symbol: rᵢ = δ(rᵢ₋₁, aᵢ). The list r₀, r₁, …, rₙ is the **run**, and it has n + 1 states: one before any symbol, then one after each. M **accepts** w when the last state rₙ is in F.

The run of `parity` on `1101`:

| read | (start) | 1 | 1 | 0 | 1 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| state | even | odd | even | even | odd |

The last state is `odd`, which is not accepting, so `1101` is rejected: it has three 1s. On the empty string the run is only the start state, so M accepts ε exactly when q₀ ∈ F.

The same thing as a formula: the **extended transition function** δ\* says where a whole string takes you.

- δ\*(q, ε) = q;
- δ\*(q, wa) = δ(δ\*(q, w), a), for a string w and a symbol a.

M accepts w when δ\*(q₀, w) ∈ F. The **language of M** is L(M) = {w ∈ Σ\* : M accepts w}, and M **recognises** that language. A language is **regular** when some DFA recognises it. Lessons 3 to 6 are about exactly which languages those are.

### What running costs

Each symbol costs one dictionary lookup, so running a DFA on a string of length n takes **Θ(n) time**. The extra memory is **O(1)**: one variable for the current state, whatever n is. The machine never looks back at the input. That makes DFAs ideal for [[streams|streaming]]: telemetry, network packets, a log file too big for memory.

### Designing a DFA: what must you remember?

Designing a DFA means answering one question: **what must I remember about the part I have read, to decide the rest?** Each state stands for one answer. Two prefixes that need the same future treatment can share a state.

Take the strings over {a, b} that end in `ab`. After any prefix, what matters is how much of `ab` you have just seen:

- `none`: the prefix does not end in `a` or `ab`;
- `a`: it ends in `a`;
- `ab`: it ends in `ab`. This is the accepting state.

Then fill in each move by asking what the new ending is. From `a`, reading `a` still ends in `a`; reading `b` ends in `ab`. From `ab`, reading `a` ends in `a`, and reading `b` ends in `bb`, which is `none`.

| state | read a | read b |
|---|---|---|
| → none | a | none |
| a | a | ab |
| ab (accepting) | a | none |

### Remembering a number modulo k

A DFA can also track arithmetic, as long as only finitely many values matter. Reading the decimal digits of a number from the left, which numbers are divisible by 3? You cannot remember the whole number, since it could be huge. But you can remember its **remainder** mod 3.

If the digits so far spell the number v and the next digit is d, the new number is 10v + d. From the modular arithmetic lesson, (10v + d) mod 3 depends only on v mod 3 and d. So the states are the remainders 0, 1 and 2, the start is 0 (the empty prefix counts as the number 0), the move from r on digit d goes to (10r + d) mod 3, and 0 is the only accepting state. After reading `47`: from 0, digit 4 gives (0 + 4) mod 3 = 1, then digit 7 gives (10 + 7) mod 3 = 2. Indeed 47 = 15·3 + 2.

### Why a design is correct

A DFA is a small program, so it needs a correctness proof, just like an algorithm. The tool is a **state invariant**: say, for each state, exactly which prefixes lead there, and prove it by induction on the length of the prefix.

For the remainder machine the invariant is: **after reading the digits of w, the machine is in state val(w) mod 3**, where val(w) is the number w spells and val(ε) = 0.

- **Base case:** after ε the machine is in the start state 0, and val(ε) mod 3 = 0.
- **Step:** suppose the invariant holds after w, so the state is r = val(w) mod 3. Read digit d. The machine moves to (10r + d) mod 3. Since r and val(w) leave the same remainder, this equals (10·val(w) + d) mod 3 = val(wd) mod 3. So the invariant holds after wd.

Now the machine accepts w exactly when it ends in state 0, which by the invariant is exactly when val(w) mod 3 = 0. That is the language we wanted. Notice that the proof has two parts: the invariant, then the choice of accepting states.

### Dead states

Some prefixes can never be rescued. In "strings over {a, b} that start with `a`", once the first symbol is `b`, nothing later can help. A DFA sends such prefixes to a **dead state** (also called a trap): not accepting, and every symbol leads back to it. Diagrams often leave the dead state out to save ink, with the rule "a missing arrow goes to the dead state". Your code must put it back, because δ has to be total.

### Complement: swap yes and no

If M recognises L, then swapping accepting and non-accepting states (F becomes Q − F) gives a DFA for the complement Σ\* − L. The runs do not change at all, only the answer at the end. So **the complement of a regular language is regular**. This only works because every string has exactly one run that reaches the end. In a partial DFA, a string that falls off a missing arrow has no last state, and swapping F does not make it accepted.

### Two machines at once: the product construction

To recognise L₁ ∩ L₂, run a DFA for each at the same time, and accept when both accept. One machine can do this if its state is a **pair** (p, q): p is where M₁ would be, q where M₂ would be. Given M₁ = (Q₁, Σ, δ₁, p₀, F₁) and M₂ = (Q₂, Σ, δ₂, q₀, F₂), the **product** has:

- states Q₁ × Q₂, the pairs;
- the move δ((p, q), a) = (δ₁(p, a), δ₂(q, a)): both machines take their own step;
- start state (p₀, q₀);
- accepting pairs chosen by what you want: both accept for ∩, at least one for ∪, the first but not the second for the difference L₁ − L₂.

**Why it is correct.** The invariant is δ\*((p₀, q₀), w) = (δ₁\*(p₀, w), δ₂\*(q₀, w)): the pair tracks both runs. It holds for w = ε, and one step of the product is one step of each machine, so it holds for wa whenever it holds for w. Then the product ends in an accepting pair exactly when the chosen combination of M₁ and M₂ accept.

So **regular languages are closed under union, intersection and difference**. The product has |Q₁|·|Q₂| states, but often many pairs can never be reached from the start. Building only the pairs a [[breadth-first search|product-bfs]] reaches keeps it small.

### Questions a program can answer about a DFA

- **Is L(M) empty?** Only if no accepting state can be reached from the start. A BFS over the states answers this in O(|Q|·|Σ|) time.
- **Do M₁ and M₂ recognise the same language?** Exactly when (L₁ − L₂) ∪ (L₂ − L₁) is empty. Build the product with "exactly one accepts" as its accepting pairs, and test it for emptiness.

These are algorithms, and they always finish. Later in the course you will see that the same questions about programs cannot be answered by any algorithm.

### Where it is used

- **[[Lexers|lexer-dfa]]** in every compiler run a DFA over the source code to find numbers, names and keywords.
- **Regular-expression engines** such as RE2 and grep's fast path compile patterns into DFAs, so they match in time linear in the text.
- **Protocols** are specified as state machines: the TCP connection has eleven states, from LISTEN to TIME-WAIT.
- **Spacecraft mode logic**: a mode manager that allows ARM only after SAFE-CHECK, and FIRE only while ARMED, is a DFA over the command stream, and it can be checked exhaustively before flight.
- **Hardware**: every digital controller is a finite-state machine built from flip-flops, which you will meet in Computer Organization.

**Watch out:** the classic DFA bugs.

- **A partial transition function.** A missing `(state, symbol)` entry crashes the simulator with a `KeyError`, or, worse, breaks the complement. Add the dead state.
- **Forgetting ε.** The empty string is accepted exactly when the start state is accepting. Decide it on purpose.
- **Deciding too early.** Passing through an accepting state in the middle means nothing. Only the state after the **last** symbol counts.
- **Symbols outside the alphabet.** A string with a symbol the machine does not know is not in its language, so reject it rather than crash.

::: context streaming Why constant memory matters
A DFA reads each symbol once and forgets it. That is exactly how data arrives from a network card, a sensor or a radio: a stream you cannot rewind and may not have room to store. A packet filter in a router sees millions of packets a second and can afford only a few bytes of state per connection. Algorithms that work in one pass with small memory are called streaming algorithms, and the DFA is the simplest of them.
:::

::: context product-bfs Building only the reachable pairs
Start a queue with the pair (p₀, q₀). Take a pair out, compute its move on every symbol, and add each resulting pair to the queue the first time you see it. When the queue is empty, you have exactly the pairs that some input string can reach. The others can never affect the answer, so leaving them out changes nothing. This is the breadth-first search from DSA I, run on a graph you build as you go.
:::

::: context lexer-dfa How a lexer uses a DFA
A lexer reads source code such as `x1 = 42` and cuts it into tokens: the name `x1`, the symbol `=`, the number `42`. Each kind of token is a regular language. Tools like lex and flex join all of them into one big DFA, run it from the current position as far as it can go, and take the longest match. You will build a lexer by hand in the compilers course, and lesson 4 shows how the DFA is made from patterns.
:::
--- task
A DFA is a dictionary like the one in the explanation: `"states"` (a set), `"alphabet"` (a string of symbols), `"delta"` (a dictionary from `(state, symbol)` pairs to states), `"start"` and `"accept"` (a set). Write three functions.

- `run_states(dfa, w)`: return the run of the DFA on the string `w`, as a list of the `len(w) + 1` states it passes through, starting with the start state. Every symbol of `w` is in the alphabet here.
- `accepts(dfa, w)`: return `True` when the DFA accepts `w`, and `False` otherwise. If `w` contains a symbol that is not in the alphabet, return `False`.
- `divisible_dfa(k)`: for a whole number `k ≥ 1`, return a DFA over the alphabet `"01"` that accepts exactly the binary strings whose value is divisible by `k`. Leading zeros are allowed, and the empty string counts as the number 0. Use the states `0, 1, …, k - 1`, where state `r` means "the value so far leaves remainder `r` when divided by `k`". The start state is `0`, and the transition function must be total.

For example, `divisible_dfa(3)` accepts `"110"` (6) and `""`, and rejects `"111"` (7).
--- starter
def run_states(dfa, w):
    return [dfa["start"]]


def accepts(dfa, w):
    return False


def divisible_dfa(k):
    return {"states": set(), "alphabet": "01", "delta": {}, "start": 0, "accept": set()}
--- solution
def run_states(dfa, w):
    q = dfa["start"]
    states = [q]
    for c in w:
        q = dfa["delta"][(q, c)]
        states.append(q)
    return states


def accepts(dfa, w):
    if any(c not in dfa["alphabet"] for c in w):
        return False
    return run_states(dfa, w)[-1] in dfa["accept"]


def divisible_dfa(k):
    delta = {}
    for r in range(k):
        for bit in "01":
            delta[(r, bit)] = (2 * r + int(bit)) % k
    return {"states": set(range(k)), "alphabet": "01", "delta": delta, "start": 0, "accept": {0}}
--- hint
`run_states` keeps a current state, starting at `dfa["start"]`, and for each symbol looks up `dfa["delta"][(state, symbol)]`, appending every state it reaches. `accepts` checks the symbols first, then asks whether the **last** state of the run is in `dfa["accept"]`.
--- hint
For `divisible_dfa`, the explanation did the same thing in base 10. Reading one more bit `b` after a prefix with value v gives the value 2v + b. So from remainder `r`, the bit `b` leads to remainder `(2 * r + b) % k`.
--- hint
Fill `delta` with two nested loops, over `r in range(k)` and over the bits `"0"` and `"1"` (convert with `int`). The only accepting state is remainder `0`.
--- check case | The run of a two-state parity DFA
run_states({"states": {"e", "o"}, "alphabet": "01", "delta": {("e", "0"): "e", ("e", "1"): "o", ("o", "0"): "o", ("o", "1"): "e"}, "start": "e", "accept": {"e"}}, "1101")
=> ["e", "o", "e", "e", "o"]
--- check case | The run on the empty string is only the start state
run_states({"states": {"s"}, "alphabet": "x", "delta": {("s", "x"): "s"}, "start": "s", "accept": set()}, "")
=> ["s"]
--- check case | accepts on the parity DFA, including the empty string
(lambda d: [accepts(d, w) for w in ["", "1", "11", "0110", "10101"]])({"states": {"e", "o"}, "alphabet": "01", "delta": {("e", "0"): "e", ("e", "1"): "o", ("o", "0"): "o", ("o", "1"): "e"}, "start": "e", "accept": {"e"}})
=> [True, False, True, True, False]
--- check case | Passing through an accepting state in the middle is not enough

+++ practice | No two faults in a row
--- task
A sensor reports one symbol per second: `n` for nominal and `f` for a fault. The link is healthy as long as there are never **two faults in a row**.

Write `no_double_fault()` that returns a DFA (a dictionary with `"states"`, `"alphabet"`, `"delta"`, `"start"` and `"accept"`, as in the lesson) over the alphabet `"fn"` that accepts exactly the strings with no `ff` in them. The empty string is accepted. The transition function must be total, and it can be done with 3 states: use at most 3.
--- starter
def no_double_fault():
    return {"states": {"ok"}, "alphabet": "fn", "delta": {("ok", "f"): "ok", ("ok", "n"): "ok"}, "start": "ok", "accept": {"ok"}}
--- solution
def no_double_fault():
    delta = {
        ("ok", "n"): "ok", ("ok", "f"): "one",
        ("one", "n"): "ok", ("one", "f"): "dead",
        ("dead", "n"): "dead", ("dead", "f"): "dead",
    }
    return {"states": {"ok", "one", "dead"}, "alphabet": "fn", "delta": delta, "start": "ok", "accept": {"ok", "one"}}
--- hint
What do you need to remember? Whether the last symbol was a fault, and whether two faults in a row have already happened. That is three situations.
--- hint
Once `ff` has appeared, nothing can fix it: that situation is a dead state that loops to itself on both symbols. The other two states are both accepting.
--- check test | The transition function is total, over the alphabet "fn", with at most 3 states
(lambda d: set(d["alphabet"]) == {"f", "n"} and len(d["states"]) <= 3 and set(d["delta"]) == {(q, c) for q in d["states"] for c in "fn"} and set(d["delta"].values()) <= d["states"] and d["start"] in d["states"])(no_double_fault())
--- check test | The empty string and single symbols are accepted
(lambda d, R: R(d, "") and R(d, "f") and R(d, "n"))(no_double_fault(), lambda d, w: __import__("functools").reduce(lambda q, c: d["delta"][(q, c)], w, d["start"]) in d["accept"])
--- check test | "ff" anywhere is rejected, even at the very end
(lambda d, R: not R(d, "ff") and not R(d, "nnnff") and not R(d, "ffnnn") and R(d, "fnfnf"))(no_double_fault(), lambda d, w: __import__("functools").reduce(lambda q, c: d["delta"][(q, c)], w, d["start"]) in d["accept"])
--- check test | Every string up to length 11 is judged right
(lambda d, P, red: all((red(lambda q, c: d["delta"][(q, c)], w, d["start"]) in d["accept"]) == ("ff" not in w) for n in range(12) for w in ("".join(t) for t in P("fn", repeat=n))))(no_double_fault(), __import__("itertools").product, __import__("functools").reduce)

+++ practice | Complement a DFA with missing arrows
--- task
Diagrams often leave out the arrows to a dead state. Write two functions for such **partial** DFAs (dictionaries as in the lesson, where some `(state, symbol)` pairs may be missing from `"delta"`):

- `complete(dfa)`: return a new, total DFA with the same language. Send every missing `(state, symbol)` pair to a new state named `"dead"`, which is not accepting and loops to itself on every symbol. Add `"dead"` only if some pair was missing. (You may assume no state is already called `"dead"`.)
- `complement(dfa)`: return a total DFA that accepts exactly the strings over the alphabet that `dfa` rejects.

Do not change the DFA you are given. For example, the partial DFA with states `{0, 1, 2}`, alphabet `"ab"`, moves `0 -a-> 1`, `1 -b-> 2`, `2 -a-> 1`, start `0` and accepting `{2}` recognises `ab`, `abab`, `ababab`, …. Its complement must accept `b`, `aa`, `""` and every other string not of that form.
--- starter
def complete(dfa):
    return dfa


def complement(dfa):
    return {**dfa, "accept": dfa["states"] - dfa["accept"]}
--- solution
def complete(dfa):
    delta = dict(dfa["delta"])
    states = set(dfa["states"])
    for q in dfa["states"]:
        for c in dfa["alphabet"]:
            if (q, c) not in delta:
                delta[(q, c)] = "dead"
                states.add("dead")
    if "dead" in states:
        for c in dfa["alphabet"]:
            delta[("dead", c)] = "dead"
    return {"states": states, "alphabet": dfa["alphabet"], "delta": delta, "start": dfa["start"], "accept": set(dfa["accept"])}


def complement(dfa):
    full = complete(dfa)
    full["accept"] = full["states"] - full["accept"]
    return full
--- hint
In `complete`, copy `delta` and `states` first, then walk over every state and every symbol. Where a pair is missing, point it at `"dead"`. If you used `"dead"`, give it its own loops.
--- hint
The starter's `complement` swaps the accepting states without completing first. A string that falls off a missing arrow was rejected, and it is still rejected, because it never reaches any state. Complete first, then swap, so the dead state becomes accepting.
--- check test | The complement of the partial "(ab)+" DFA, on every string up to length 8
(lambda c, P, red, re: all((red(lambda q, s: c["delta"][(q, s)], w, c["start"]) in c["accept"]) == (re.fullmatch("(ab)+", w) is None) for n in range(9) for w in ("".join(t) for t in P("ab", repeat=n))))(complement({"states": {0, 1, 2}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, "a"): 1, (1, "b"): 2, (2, "a"): 1}, "start": 0, "accept": {2}}), __import__("itertools").product, __import__("functools").reduce, __import__("re"))
?? A string that falls off a missing arrow must be accepted by the complement: complete the DFA first.
--- check test | complete adds "dead" with its own loops, and the result is total
(lambda c: c["states"] == {0, 1, 2, "dead"} and len(c["delta"]) == 8 and c["delta"][("dead", "a")] == "dead" and c["delta"][(0, "b")] == "dead" and c["accept"] == {2})(complete({"states": {0, 1, 2}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, "a"): 1, (1, "b"): 2, (2, "a"): 1}, "start": 0, "accept": {2}}))
--- check test | A DFA that is already total gets no dead state
(lambda d: complete(d)["states"] == {"e", "o"} and complement(d)["accept"] == {"o"})({"states": {"e", "o"}, "alphabet": "01", "delta": {("e", "0"): "e", ("e", "1"): "o", ("o", "0"): "o", ("o", "1"): "e"}, "start": "e", "accept": {"e"}})
--- check test | A DFA with no moves at all: its complement accepts everything except what it accepted
(lambda c, red: red(lambda q, s: c["delta"][(q, s)], "", c["start"]) not in c["accept"] and all(red(lambda q, s: c["delta"][(q, s)], w, c["start"]) in c["accept"] for w in ["x", "xy", "yyx"]))(complement({"states": {"s"}, "alphabet": "xy", "delta": {}, "start": "s", "accept": {"s"}}), __import__("functools").reduce)
--- check test | Does not change the DFA it is given
(lambda d: (complement(d), complete(d), d)[2] == {"states": {0, 1}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, "a"): 1}, "start": 0, "accept": {1}})({"states": {0, 1}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, "a"): 1}, "start": 0, "accept": {1}})

+++ practice | The product construction
--- task
Write `product(d1, d2, op)` that builds the product of two total DFAs over the same alphabet, as in the lesson. `op` is one of three strings:

- `"and"`: accept when both accept (the intersection);
- `"or"`: accept when at least one accepts (the union);
- `"diff"`: accept when `d1` accepts and `d2` does not (the difference).

The states of the result are pairs `(p, q)`, the start is `(d1["start"], d2["start"])`, and the move on symbol `c` is `(d1's move from p on c, d2's move from q on c)`. Include **only the pairs that can be reached from the start**: build them with a search that starts from the start pair. `"delta"` must have an entry for every reached pair and every symbol, and the alphabet is `d1["alphabet"]`.

For example, the product of a DFA for "binary value divisible by 2" (2 states) and one for "divisible by 4" (4 states) can only reach 4 of the 8 pairs.
--- starter
def product(d1, d2, op):
    start = (d1["start"], d2["start"])
    return {"states": {start}, "alphabet": d1["alphabet"], "delta": {}, "start": start, "accept": set()}
--- solution
def product(d1, d2, op):
    sigma = d1["alphabet"]
    start = (d1["start"], d2["start"])
    states = {start}
    delta = {}
    todo = [start]
    while todo:
        p, q = todo.pop()
        for c in sigma:
            nxt = (d1["delta"][(p, c)], d2["delta"][(q, c)])
            delta[((p, q), c)] = nxt
            if nxt not in states:
                states.add(nxt)
                todo.append(nxt)
    want = {
        "and": lambda a, b: a and b,
        "or": lambda a, b: a or b,
        "diff": lambda a, b: a and not b,
    }[op]
    accept = {(p, q) for (p, q) in states if want(p in d1["accept"], q in d2["accept"])}
    return {"states": states, "alphabet": sigma, "delta": delta, "start": start, "accept": accept}
--- hint
Keep a set of pairs found and a list of pairs still to expand, both starting with the start pair. Expanding `(p, q)` means computing its move on every symbol, recording it in `delta`, and adding the new pair to both collections if it was not found before.
--- hint
When no pairs are left to expand, choose the accepting pairs: test `p in d1["accept"]` and `q in d2["accept"]`, and combine the two answers with `and`, `or`, or "first and not second".
--- check test | "and", "or" and "diff" of divisibility by 3 and by 4, on every binary string up to length 9
(lambda M, P, red: all(all((red(lambda s, c: pr["delta"][(s, c)], w, pr["start"]) in pr["accept"]) == f(int(w or "0", 2)) for n in range(10) for w in ("".join(t) for t in P("01", repeat=n))) for op, f in [("and", lambda v: v % 12 == 0), ("or", lambda v: v % 3 == 0 or v % 4 == 0), ("diff", lambda v: v % 3 == 0 and v % 4 != 0)] for pr in [product(M(3), M(4), op)]))(lambda k: {"states": set(range(k)), "alphabet": "01", "delta": {(r, b): (2 * r + int(b)) % k for r in range(k) for b in "01"}, "start": 0, "accept": {0}}, __import__("itertools").product, __import__("functools").reduce)
--- check test | Only reachable pairs: divisible by 2 times divisible by 4 has 4 pairs, not 8
(lambda M: len(product(M(2), M(4), "and")["states"]) == 4 and len(product(M(3), M(4), "or")["states"]) == 12)(lambda k: {"states": set(range(k)), "alphabet": "01", "delta": {(r, b): (2 * r + int(b)) % k for r in range(k) for b in "01"}, "start": 0, "accept": {0}})
--- check test | The start is the pair of starts, and delta is total on the reached pairs
(lambda M: (lambda pr: pr["start"] == (0, 0) and set(pr["delta"]) == {(s, c) for s in pr["states"] for c in "01"} and set(pr["delta"].values()) <= pr["states"])(product(M(5), M(3), "diff")))(lambda k: {"states": set(range(k)), "alphabet": "01", "delta": {(r, b): (2 * r + int(b)) % k for r in range(k) for b in "01"}, "start": 0, "accept": {0}})
--- check test | Intersecting "even number of a's" with "ends in b", over "ab"
(lambda pr, red: [red(lambda s, c: pr["delta"][(s, c)], w, pr["start"]) in pr["accept"] for w in ["", "b", "ab", "aab", "aabb", "aa"]] == [False, True, False, True, True, False])(product({"states": {0, 1}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, "a"): 1, (1, "a"): 0, (0, "b"): 0, (1, "b"): 1}, "start": 0, "accept": {0}}, {"states": {"x", "y"}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {("x", "a"): "x", ("y", "a"): "x", ("x", "b"): "y", ("y", "b"): "y"}, "start": "x", "accept": {"y"}}, "and"), __import__("functools").reduce)

+++ practice | The first string a DFA accepts
--- task
Write `shortest_accepted(dfa)` that returns the **first string in shortlex order** that a total DFA accepts: the shortest one, and among the shortest, the first in dictionary order, using the order of the symbols in `dfa["alphabet"]`. If the DFA accepts nothing, return `None`.

Use breadth-first search from the start state, trying the symbols in alphabet order and remembering, for each state, the string that first reached it. The states come out of the queue in shortlex order of those strings, so the first accepting state to come out gives the answer.

Edge cases: if the start state is accepting, the answer is `""`. An accepting state that cannot be reached does not count.
--- starter
def shortest_accepted(dfa):
    return ""
--- solution
def shortest_accepted(dfa):
    start = dfa["start"]
    reached_by = {start: ""}
    queue = [start]
    i = 0
    while i < len(queue):
        q = queue[i]
        i += 1
        if q in dfa["accept"]:
            return reached_by[q]
        for c in dfa["alphabet"]:
            r = dfa["delta"][(q, c)]
            if r not in reached_by:
                reached_by[r] = reached_by[q] + c
                queue.append(r)
    return None
--- hint
Keep a dictionary `reached_by` from state to the string that first reached it, starting with `{start: ""}`, and a queue that starts with the start state.
--- hint
Take states from the front of the queue. If one is accepting, return its string. Otherwise, for each symbol in alphabet order, find the next state, and if it is new, record its string (the current one plus the symbol) and put it on the queue. If the queue runs out, return `None`.
--- check case | Divisible by 7, accepting only remainder 5: the first string is "101"
shortest_accepted({"states": set(range(7)), "alphabet": "01", "delta": {(r, b): (2 * r + int(b)) % 7 for r in range(7) for b in "01"}, "start": 0, "accept": {5}})
=> "101"
--- check case | A start state that accepts gives ""
shortest_accepted({"states": set(range(3)), "alphabet": "01", "delta": {(r, b): (2 * r + int(b)) % 3 for r in range(3) for b in "01"}, "start": 0, "accept": {0}})
=> ""
--- check case | Ties of the same length follow the alphabet's order
(shortest_accepted({"states": set(range(7)), "alphabet": "01", "delta": {(r, b): (2 * r + int(b)) % 7 for r in range(7) for b in "01"}, "start": 0, "accept": {2, 3}}), shortest_accepted({"states": set(range(7)), "alphabet": "10", "delta": {(r, b): (2 * r + int(b)) % 7 for r in range(7) for b in "01"}, "start": 0, "accept": {2, 3}}))
=> ("10", "11")
--- check case | No accepting state, or only an unreachable one: None
(shortest_accepted({"states": {0, 1}, "alphabet": "a", "delta": {(0, "a"): 0, (1, "a"): 1}, "start": 0, "accept": {1}}), shortest_accepted({"states": {0}, "alphabet": "a", "delta": {(0, "a"): 0}, "start": 0, "accept": set()}))
=> (None, None)
--- check case | A longer path through a chain of states
shortest_accepted({"states": set(range(6)), "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {**{(i, "a"): min(i + 1, 5) for i in range(6)}, **{(i, "b"): 0 for i in range(6)}}, "start": 0, "accept": {5}})
=> "aaaaa"

+++ practice | Debug: the checker that decides too early
--- task
A ground station checks a batch of command strings with a DFA. `check_batch(dfa, commands)` should return a list with one `True` or `False` per command: whether the DFA accepts it. Every symbol is in the alphabet.

It gets some commands wrong. With a DFA for "ends in `ab`", it says `True` for `"aba"`, which ends in `a`, and it says `False` for `""` even when the start state is accepting. Find the bug in `accepts_one` and fix it.
--- starter
def accepts_one(dfa, w):
    q = dfa["start"]
    for c in w:
        q = dfa["delta"][(q, c)]
        if q in dfa["accept"]:
            return True
    return False


def check_batch(dfa, commands):
    return [accepts_one(dfa, w) for w in commands]
--- solution
def accepts_one(dfa, w):
    q = dfa["start"]
    for c in w:
        q = dfa["delta"][(q, c)]
    return q in dfa["accept"]


def check_batch(dfa, commands):
    return [accepts_one(dfa, w) for w in commands]
--- hint
When does a DFA decide? Passing through an accepting state in the middle of the string does not mean the whole string is accepted.
--- hint
Remove the test inside the loop, and after the loop return whether the final state is accepting. That also fixes the empty string, where the loop never runs.

=> [True, False, True, False, False]
