+++ practice | How many valid commands of length n?
--- task
A command link uses fixed-length frames of `n` symbols, and a DFA says which frames are valid. Before choosing `n`, the team wants to know **how many** valid frames there are.

Write `count_accepted(dfa, n)` that returns the number of strings of length exactly `n` that the total DFA accepts. It must be fast: a check uses `n = 2000`, where there are 2²⁰⁰⁰ strings to consider, so listing them is impossible.

Use dynamic programming over the length: keep, for each state, how many strings of the current length lead to it. Start with length 0: one string (ε), which leads to the start state. One more symbol sends the count of each state along each of its arrows. At the end, add up the counts of the accepting states. Python's integers never overflow, so return the exact number.

For example, for "no two faults in a row" over `"fn"` the counts for n = 0, 1, 2, 3, 4 are 1, 2, 3, 5, 8.
--- starter
def count_accepted(dfa, n):
    return 0
--- solution
def count_accepted(dfa, n):
    counts = {q: 0 for q in dfa["states"]}
    counts[dfa["start"]] = 1
    for _ in range(n):
        nxt = {q: 0 for q in dfa["states"]}
        for q, ways in counts.items():
            if ways:
                for c in dfa["alphabet"]:
                    nxt[dfa["delta"][(q, c)]] += ways
        counts = nxt
    return sum(counts[q] for q in dfa["accept"])
--- hint
`counts[q]` is the number of strings of the current length whose run ends in `q`. At length 0 only the start state has a count, and it is 1.
--- hint
To go from length i to i + 1, start a fresh dictionary of zeros. For each state `q` and each symbol `c`, add `counts[q]` to the entry of `delta[(q, c)]`. Each string of length i + 1 is counted exactly once, by its last symbol. This takes O(n·|Q|·|Σ|) time.
--- check case | "No two faults in a row": the counts are Fibonacci numbers
(lambda d: [count_accepted(d, n) for n in range(8)])({"states": {"ok", "one", "dead"}, "alphabet": "fn", "delta": {("ok", "n"): "ok", ("ok", "f"): "one", ("one", "n"): "ok", ("one", "f"): "dead", ("dead", "n"): "dead", ("dead", "f"): "dead"}, "start": "ok", "accept": {"ok", "one"}})
=> [1, 2, 3, 5, 8, 13, 21, 34]
--- check case | Length 0: 1 if the start state accepts, else 0
(count_accepted({"states": {0, 1}, "alphabet": "a", "delta": {(0, "a"): 1, (1, "a"): 1}, "start": 0, "accept": {0}}, 0), count_accepted({"states": {0, 1}, "alphabet": "a", "delta": {(0, "a"): 1, (1, "a"): 1}, "start": 0, "accept": {1}}, 0))
=> (1, 0)
--- check case | Binary strings divisible by 3, lengths 0 to 5
(lambda d: [count_accepted(d, n) for n in range(6)])({"states": set(range(3)), "alphabet": "01", "delta": {(r, b): (2 * r + int(b)) % 3 for r in range(3) for b in "01"}, "start": 0, "accept": {0}})
=> [1, 1, 2, 3, 6, 11]
--- check test | Length 2000, divisible by 3: exactly (2^2000 + 2) / 3
count_accepted({"states": set(range(3)), "alphabet": "01", "delta": {(r, b): (2 * r + int(b)) % 3 for r in range(3) for b in "01"}, "start": 0, "accept": {0}}, 2000) == (2**2000 + 2) // 3
?? Keep one count per state and push the counts along the arrows, one length at a time.
--- check test | An accepting state that is never reached counts 0
count_accepted({"states": {0, 1, 2}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, "a"): 0, (0, "b"): 0, (1, "a"): 1, (1, "b"): 1, (2, "a"): 2, (2, "b"): 2}, "start": 0, "accept": {1, 2}}, 5) == 0

+++ question | Why the complement needs a total DFA
--- ask
A partial DFA M recognises L. You build M′ by swapping accepting and non-accepting states of M, without adding a dead state. Why can L(M′) be wrong as the complement of L?
--- choice
Because the start state might be accepting, so M′ rejects ε.
--- choice correct
Because a string that falls off a missing arrow has no final state, so it is rejected by both M and M′, although the complement should accept it.
--- choice
Because swapping the states changes the runs, so M′ reaches different states from M.
--- choice
It is never wrong: swapping always gives the complement.
--- why
Swapping F leaves every run unchanged; only the verdict at the end flips. That flip needs every string to have a final state. With a missing arrow, some strings never finish their run, and they stay rejected on both sides. Completing the DFA first sends them to a dead state, which becomes accepting after the swap.

+++ question | The step in the remainder proof
--- ask
The invariant for the "divisible by k" DFA over binary strings is: after reading w, the state is val(w) mod k. In the induction step, which fact lets the machine use only the remainder r instead of the whole value val(w)?
--- choice
val(w) is always less than k.
--- choice correct
(2·val(w) + b) mod k = (2·r + b) mod k whenever r = val(w) mod k, because remainders can be used in place of numbers in sums and products.
--- choice
Leading zeros do not change the value.
--- choice
The DFA has exactly k states.
--- why
val(wb) = 2·val(w) + b. Modular arithmetic says that if two numbers leave the same remainder, so do 2 times each of them plus b. So the machine's move from r gives the right remainder for wb. The number of states is a consequence of the design, not the reason it works, and val(w) is usually much larger than k.

+++ question | The size of a product
--- ask
M₁ has 5 states and M₂ has 7 states, over the same alphabet. You build the product DFA for L(M₁) ∪ L(M₂), keeping only the reachable pairs. What is certain about its number of states?
--- choice
It is exactly 35.
--- choice
It is exactly 12, one state for each state of M₁ or M₂.
--- choice correct
It is at most 35, and it can be fewer when some pairs cannot be reached.
--- choice
It is at least 35, because union needs extra states for "at least one accepts".
--- why
The pairs (p, q) number 5 · 7 = 35, and every product state is one of them. Union and intersection use the same states and moves; they differ only in which pairs accept. Some pairs may never be reached from (p₀, q₀), as with "divisible by 2" and "divisible by 4", where only 4 of 8 pairs occur.
=== theory-03 | Nondeterministic finite automata and the subset construction
--- teach
Last lesson's DFA has exactly one move for every state and symbol. This lesson removes that rule. A **nondeterministic finite automaton**, or **NFA**, may have several moves on the same symbol, or none, and it may even move without reading anything. It accepts if **some** way of making its choices ends in an accepting state. That sounds far more powerful. The main result of the lesson is that it is not: every NFA can be turned into a DFA for the same language, by a construction you will implement. What NFAs buy is size and ease of design, and lesson 4 builds on exactly that.

### Guessing, then checking

Take the binary strings whose **third symbol from the end** is a 1. A DFA reading from the left cannot know which symbol will turn out to be third from the end, so it must remember the last three symbols it has read: 2³ = 8 possibilities.

Now imagine a reader who may guess. It skips along, and at some 1 it says "I bet this is the third from the end". Then it checks: exactly two more symbols, and the string must end. If the guess was wrong, that attempt simply fails. The string is accepted if **some** guess works. That reader needs only 4 states: "still skipping", then "guessed, 0, 1 or 2 symbols after it". That is an NFA.

A good picture is a cave with forking tunnels. At every fork you send a copy of yourself down each branch. A copy that reaches a dead end disappears. You win if any copy is standing in a treasure room when the input runs out.

### The precise definition

An NFA is a 5-tuple N = (Q, Σ, δ, q₀, F) like a DFA, except for the transition function:

δ : Q × Σ_ε → P(Q).

Here Σ_ε means Σ ∪ {ε}, and P(Q) is the **power set** of Q, the set of all subsets. So δ(q, a) is a **set** of states, which may be empty, and δ(q, ε) is the set of states N can jump to from q **without reading a symbol**. Those jumps are **ε-moves**.

N **accepts** w when w can be written as y₁y₂…yₘ, where each yᵢ is a symbol or ε, and there are states r₀, r₁, …, rₘ with:

1. r₀ = q₀;
2. rᵢ₊₁ ∈ δ(rᵢ, yᵢ₊₁) for each i, so each step follows an allowed move;
3. rₘ ∈ F.

In words: some path from the start to an accepting state spells out w, if you ignore the ε labels. If no such path exists, N rejects w. There is no "wrong move" error: a branch with no move just dies.

In code, this course writes an NFA as a dictionary like a DFA, except that `"delta"` maps a pair to a **set** of states, the symbol `""` stands for ε, and a missing pair means the empty set:

```python
guess = {
    "states": {"p", "q", "r"},
    "alphabet": "xy",
    "delta": {
        ("p", "x"): {"p", "q"},    # two choices on x
        ("q", "y"): {"r"},
        ("p", ""): {"r"},          # a jump without reading
    },
    "start": "p",
    "accept": {"r"},
}
print(guess["delta"].get(("q", "x"), set()))    # set(): no move, that branch dies
```

Every DFA is an NFA whose sets all have exactly one state and which has no ε-moves.

### The ε-closure

Before reading anything, and after every symbol, an NFA may take any number of ε-moves. The **ε-closure** of a set of states S, written E(S), is every state reachable from a state of S by following **zero or more** ε-moves. It always contains S itself.

Computing it is a graph search, like DFS in DSA I, on the ε-arrows only. Keep a set of found states and a stack; pop a state, push every ε-successor not yet found. Each state and each ε-arrow is handled at most once, so it costs O(|Q| + number of ε-arrows). The "found" set matters: ε-moves can form a **cycle**, and a search without it would go round forever.

An example with ε-arrows 1 → 2, 2 → 3, 3 → 1 and 3 → 4:

- E({1}) = {1, 2, 3, 4}: follow 1 → 2 → 3, then both 3 → 1 (already found) and 3 → 4.
- E({4}) = {4}: no ε-arrows leave 4, and zero moves is allowed.
- E(∅) = ∅.

### Running every branch at once

You could explore the branches one by one, backing up at dead ends, but that can take exponential time: a string of length n can have 2ⁿ paths. The fix is to follow **all** branches together, as one set of "where a copy might be":

1. Start with S₀ = E({q₀}).
2. For each symbol a: collect every state reachable from a state in S by one a-move, then take the ε-closure. That is the new S.
3. At the end, accept when S contains an accepting state.

A trace, for an NFA with states 0, 1, 2 over {a, b}: 0 loops on a and b, 0 goes to 1 on a, and 1 goes to 2 on a; the only accepting state is 2. It accepts strings that end in `aa`. On input `baa`:

| read | (start) | b | a | a |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| set of states | {0} | {0} | {0, 1} | {0, 1, 2} |

The last set contains 2, so `baa` is accepted.

**Why it is correct.** The invariant is: after reading the first i symbols, S is exactly the set of states that some path from q₀ can reach while spelling those i symbols. At the start that is E({q₀}), since only ε-moves spell nothing. If it holds after i symbols, then a path for i + 1 symbols is a path for i symbols, one move on the next symbol, and then any ε-moves, which is exactly how the step builds the next set. So at the end, S meets F exactly when some accepting path exists, which is the definition of acceptance.

**What it costs.** Each step touches every state in S and each of its arrows once, so a step costs O(|Q| + |δ|), where |δ| is the number of arrows. A string of length n costs **O(n·(|Q| + |δ|))**, and the memory is one set of at most |Q| states. No backtracking, and never exponential.

### The subset construction

The simulation already does the trick. The set S is the only thing it remembers, and there are at most 2^|Q| possible sets. That is a finite number, so the sets themselves can be the states of a DFA. This is the **subset construction**, published by Michael Rabin and Dana Scott in 1959 (work that won them the [[Turing Award|rabin-scott]]). From N = (Q, Σ, δ, q₀, F), build D:

- the states of D are sets of states of N;
- the start state is E({q₀});
- the move from a set S on symbol a is E(the union of δ(q, a) over all q in S);
- a set is accepting when it contains at least one state of F.

Build only the sets that can be reached, with a search from the start set, like the product construction. The **empty set** can turn up: it is where all branches have died. Keep it as an ordinary state. It is D's dead state, and it keeps D's transition function total.

In Python a set cannot be a dictionary key or a member of another set, because it can change. A **[[frozenset|frozenset]]** can, so use frozensets for D's states:

```python
seen = {frozenset({1, 2}): "first"}
print(frozenset({2, 1}) in seen)    # True: the order of the members does not matter
print(frozenset() == frozenset(set()))    # True: one empty frozenset
```

**Why D recognises the same language.** D's moves are exactly the steps of the simulation above, starting from the same set. So after reading w, D is in the set the simulation computes, and it accepts exactly when that set meets F: exactly when N accepts w. This proves the theorem: **a language is recognised by some NFA if and only if it is recognised by some DFA**. Every DFA is an NFA, which gives the other direction. So "regular" can mean either.

### The price: up to 2ⁿ states

The DFA can have up to 2^|Q| states: an [[exponential number|exp-blowup]] of the NFA's size. Sometimes it really needs them. The NFA for "the k-th symbol from the end is 1" has k + 1 states. **Every** DFA for it has at least 2ᵏ states. Here is the proof, a style of argument you will use again in lessons 5 and 6.

Take two different strings x and y of length k. They differ at some position i, counting from the left; say x has a 1 there and y a 0. Append z = 0ⁱ⁻¹ (i − 1 zeros) to both. In xz, the k-th symbol from the end is the i-th symbol of x, which is 1; in yz it is 0. So xz is in the language and yz is not. If a DFA were in the same state after reading x and after reading y, it would then read z identically and give the same answer on both. So after reading any two different strings of length k, a correct DFA is in two different states. There are 2ᵏ strings of length k, so it has at least 2ᵏ states.

In practice most NFAs reach only a small share of their subsets. Tools such as grep and RE2 go further and build D **lazily**: they create a DFA state only when the input first leads there, and keep it in a cache.

### Where it is used

- **Regex engines.** Thompson-style engines (RE2, Go's `regexp`, Rust's `regex`, grep) run the set simulation or a lazy DFA, so matching is linear in the text. Backtracking engines (Python's `re`, PCRE, Java) follow one branch at a time, which can take exponential time on some patterns.
- **Lexer generators** such as flex build one NFA per token kind, join them with ε-moves from a new start state, and determinise the result.
- **Network intrusion detection** (Snort, Intel's Hyperscan) scans traffic for thousands of patterns at once with automata built this way.
- **Model checking** of flight software and chips explores nondeterministic automata that describe every possible behaviour.

**Watch out:** the subset construction's classic bugs.

- **Forgetting ε-closures.** Take the closure of the start state, **and** the closure after every move. Missing the second is the most common bug of all.
- **ε-cycles.** A closure search without a "found" set loops forever on ε-moves that go round in a circle.
- **Dropping the empty set.** It is a real DFA state, the dead state. Without it the DFA is partial.
- **Thinking nondeterminism is randomness.** An NFA does not flip coins, and it is not parallel hardware. It is a definition: accept if **some** path accepts. The simulation is an ordinary deterministic program.

::: context rabin-scott Rabin and Scott's paper
In "Finite Automata and Their Decision Problems" (1959), Michael Rabin and Dana Scott defined nondeterministic machines, proved the subset construction, and asked which questions about automata can be decided. In 1976 they shared the Turing Award, computing's highest prize, for this paper and specifically for introducing nondeterminism. The idea grew into one of the central questions of computer science: does nondeterminism help polynomial-time machines, the P versus NP question of lesson 15?
:::

::: context frozenset Why a set cannot be a key
A dictionary finds a key by its hash, a number computed from the key's contents. If the contents could change after the key went in, its hash would change and the dictionary could never find it again. So Python only lets unchangeable (immutable) values be keys: numbers, strings, tuples of those, and frozensets. A frozenset is a set that can never be changed after it is made. `frozenset({1, 2}) | {3}` makes a new frozenset rather than changing the old one.
:::

::: context exp-blowup How big 2ⁿ gets
An NFA with 20 states can in principle determinise to 2²⁰, about a million DFA states, and one with 40 states to about a trillion. Regex engines guard against this. RE2 builds its DFA lazily and caps its memory; if the cache fills up, it throws the cache away and starts again, and if that keeps happening it falls back to the slower set simulation. Either way the time stays linear in the text, which a backtracking engine cannot promise.
:::
--- task
An NFA is a dictionary like the one in the explanation: `"delta"` maps `(state, symbol)` to a **set** of states, the symbol `""` stands for an ε-move, and a missing pair means no moves. Write three functions.

- `eps_closure(nfa, states)`: return the ε-closure of the set `states`, as a `frozenset`. It must finish even when ε-moves form a cycle.
- `nfa_accepts(nfa, w)`: return `True` when the NFA accepts `w`, by following all branches at once as in the lesson. A symbol that is not in `nfa["alphabet"]` makes the answer `False`.
- `subset_construction(nfa)`: return an equivalent **DFA** (a dictionary as in lesson 2). Its states are `frozenset`s of NFA states, and it has **only the states reachable** from its start state, E({q₀}). Include the empty frozenset as a state when some move leads to it. The transition function must be total over `nfa["alphabet"]`, and a state is accepting when it contains an accepting NFA state.
--- starter
def eps_closure(nfa, states):
    return frozenset(states)


def nfa_accepts(nfa, w):
    return False


def subset_construction(nfa):
    return {"states": set(), "alphabet": nfa["alphabet"], "delta": {}, "start": frozenset(), "accept": set()}
--- solution
def eps_closure(nfa, states):
    found = set(states)
    stack = list(states)
    while stack:
        q = stack.pop()
        for r in nfa["delta"].get((q, ""), set()):
            if r not in found:
                found.add(r)
                stack.append(r)
    return frozenset(found)


def step(nfa, states, c):
    moved = set()
    for q in states:
        moved |= nfa["delta"].get((q, c), set())
    return eps_closure(nfa, moved)


def nfa_accepts(nfa, w):
    current = eps_closure(nfa, {nfa["start"]})
    for c in w:
        if c not in nfa["alphabet"]:
            return False
        current = step(nfa, current, c)
    return bool(current & nfa["accept"])


def subset_construction(nfa):
    start = eps_closure(nfa, {nfa["start"]})
    states = {start}
    delta = {}
    queue = [start]
    while queue:
        S = queue.pop()
        for c in nfa["alphabet"]:
            T = step(nfa, S, c)
            delta[(S, c)] = T
            if T not in states:
                states.add(T)
                queue.append(T)
    accept = {S for S in states if S & nfa["accept"]}
    return {"states": states, "alphabet": nfa["alphabet"], "delta": delta, "start": start, "accept": accept}
--- hint
`eps_closure` is a depth-first search on ε-arrows: a `found` set and a stack, both starting with `states`. Pop a state, and push each state in `nfa["delta"].get((q, ""), set())` that is not yet found. Return `frozenset(found)`.
--- hint
Write a helper `step(nfa, S, c)`: the union of the `c`-moves of every state in `S`, then its ε-closure. `nfa_accepts` starts from the closure of `{nfa["start"]}` and applies `step` once per symbol. `subset_construction` uses the same `step` to compute every DFA move.
--- hint
In `subset_construction`, keep a set of DFA states found and a list still to expand, both starting with the start closure. For each state and each symbol, record the move in `delta` and add the target if it is new, even when it is the empty frozenset. When nothing is left to expand, the accepting states are the sets `S` with `S & nfa["accept"]` non-empty.

=> frozenset({0, 1, 2})

=> (frozenset({1}), frozenset())

+++ practice | Has the last symbol been seen before?
--- task
Write `last_seen_before_nfa()` that returns an NFA (a dictionary as in the lesson) over the alphabet `"abc"` that accepts exactly the strings whose **last symbol also appears earlier** in the string. For example, `"abca"` and `"cc"` are accepted, while `""`, `"a"` and `"abc"` are not.

Use nondeterminism: at some symbol, guess "this symbol will also be the last one", remember which symbol it was, and check the guess at the end. Use **no ε-moves** and at most 5 states.
--- starter
def last_seen_before_nfa():
    return {"states": {"s"}, "alphabet": "abc", "delta": {}, "start": "s", "accept": set()}
--- solution
def last_seen_before_nfa():
    delta = {}
    for c in "abc":
        delta[("start", c)] = {"start", c}
        for d in "abc":
            delta[(c, d)] = {c, "end"} if d == c else {c}
    return {"states": {"start", "a", "b", "c", "end"}, "alphabet": "abc", "delta": delta, "start": "start", "accept": {"end"}}
--- hint
Use a start state that loops on every symbol (still waiting), one state per symbol ("I guessed this symbol"), and one accepting state `"end"` with no moves out.
--- hint
From the start, reading `c` can stay at the start or go to the state for `c`. The state for `c` loops on every symbol, and reading `c` again may also go to `"end"`. Since `"end"` has no moves, a guess only succeeds if that `c` was the last symbol.
--- check test | No ε-moves and at most 5 states, over "abc"
(lambda N: len(N["states"]) <= 5 and set(N["alphabet"]) == set("abc") and all(c in "abc" for (_, c) in N["delta"]) and N["start"] in N["states"])(last_seen_before_nfa())
--- check test | The examples from the task
(lambda N, red: [bool(red(lambda S, c: {r for q in S for r in N["delta"].get((q, c), ())}, w, {N["start"]}) & N["accept"]) for w in ["abca", "cc", "", "a", "abc"]] == [True, True, False, False, False])(last_seen_before_nfa(), __import__("functools").reduce)
--- check test | Every string up to length 7 is judged right
(lambda N, P, red: all(bool(red(lambda S, c: {r for q in S for r in N["delta"].get((q, c), ())}, w, {N["start"]}) & N["accept"]) == (len(w) >= 1 and w[-1] in w[:-1]) for n in range(8) for w in ("".join(t) for t in P("abc", repeat=n))))(last_seen_before_nfa(), __import__("itertools").product, __import__("functools").reduce)

+++ practice | Reverse a DFA
--- task
If L is regular, so is its **reversal**, Lᴿ = {wᴿ : w ∈ L}. The proof is a construction: turn every arrow of a DFA for L around, start at the old accepting states, and accept at the old start state. Turning arrows around can give a state several arrows on one symbol, and there may be several old accepting states, so the result is an NFA.

Write `reverse_nfa(dfa)` that takes a total DFA (a dictionary as in lesson 2) and returns an NFA (as in this lesson) for the reversed language:

- its states are the DFA's states plus one new state named `"new_start"` (you may assume the DFA has no state with that name), which is the start state;
- `"new_start"` has ε-moves to every accepting state of the DFA;
- for every DFA arrow from `q` to `r` on symbol `c`, the NFA has an arrow from `r` to `q` on `c`;
- the only accepting state is the DFA's start state.

For example, for the DFA of "binary value leaves remainder 1 when divided by 5", the NFA accepts `"011"`, because `"110"` is 6, which leaves remainder 1.
--- starter
def reverse_nfa(dfa):
    delta = {(q, c): {r} for (q, c), r in dfa["delta"].items()}
    return {"states": set(dfa["states"]), "alphabet": dfa["alphabet"], "delta": delta, "start": dfa["start"], "accept": set(dfa["accept"])}
--- solution
def reverse_nfa(dfa):
    delta = {}
    for (q, c), r in dfa["delta"].items():
        delta.setdefault((r, c), set()).add(q)
    delta[("new_start", "")] = set(dfa["accept"])
    states = set(dfa["states"]) | {"new_start"}
    return {"states": states, "alphabet": dfa["alphabet"], "delta": delta, "start": "new_start", "accept": {dfa["start"]}}
--- hint
Several DFA arrows can point into the same state on the same symbol, so the reversed moves must collect a set: `delta.setdefault((r, c), set()).add(q)`.
--- hint
Finish with the ε-moves from `"new_start"` to every old accepting state, and make the old start state the only accepting one.
--- check test | Remainder 1 mod 5, read backwards, on every binary string up to length 9

+++ practice | The first string an NFA accepts
--- task
Write `shortest_accepted_nfa(nfa)` that returns the first string in **shortlex order** (shortest first, then by the order of the symbols in `nfa["alphabet"]`) that the NFA accepts, or `None` if it accepts nothing. The NFA may have ε-moves.

Combine two ideas: the subset construction and the breadth-first search from lesson 2. Search over **sets** of NFA states, starting from the ε-closure of the start state, trying the symbols in alphabet order, and remembering the string that first reached each set. The first set that contains an accepting state gives the answer. You can skip the empty set, since nothing is accepted after it. Build the sets as you go; do not build the whole DFA first.

For example, for an NFA of "the 4th symbol from the end is 1" the answer is `"1000"`, and for one that accepts `""` the answer is `""`.
--- starter
def shortest_accepted_nfa(nfa):
    return None
--- solution
def eps_closure(nfa, states):
    found = set(states)
    stack = list(states)
    while stack:
        q = stack.pop()
        for r in nfa["delta"].get((q, ""), set()):
            if r not in found:
                found.add(r)
                stack.append(r)
    return frozenset(found)


def shortest_accepted_nfa(nfa):
    start = eps_closure(nfa, {nfa["start"]})
    reached_by = {start: ""}
    queue = [start]
    i = 0
    while i < len(queue):
        S = queue[i]
        i += 1
        if S & nfa["accept"]:
            return reached_by[S]
        for c in nfa["alphabet"]:
            moved = set()
            for q in S:
                moved |= nfa["delta"].get((q, c), set())
            T = eps_closure(nfa, moved)
            if T and T not in reached_by:
                reached_by[T] = reached_by[S] + c
                queue.append(T)
    return None
--- hint
You need the ε-closure (write it as in the lesson) and a queue of frozensets, starting with the closure of `{nfa["start"]}`. A dictionary from each set to the string that first reached it both records the answer and stops you visiting a set twice.
--- hint
Take sets from the front of the queue. If one contains an accepting state, return its string. Otherwise, for each symbol in order, compute the next set (all moves on the symbol, then the closure), and queue it if it is non-empty and new.
--- check case | "Ends in abb"
shortest_accepted_nfa({"states": set(range(4)), "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, "a"): {0, 1}, (0, "b"): {0}, (1, "b"): {2}, (2, "b"): {3}}, "start": 0, "accept": {3}})
=> "abb"
--- check case | ε-moves reach an accepting state before any symbol
shortest_accepted_nfa({"states": {0, 1}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, "a"): {0}, (0, ""): {1}, (1, "b"): {1}}, "start": 0, "accept": {1}})
=> ""
--- check case | "The 4th symbol from the end is 1"
shortest_accepted_nfa({"states": set(range(5)), "alphabet": "01", "delta": {(0, "0"): {0}, (0, "1"): {0, 1}, **{(i, s): {i + 1} for i in range(1, 4) for s in "01"}}, "start": 0, "accept": {4}})
=> "1000"
--- check case | An accepting state that no path reaches: None
shortest_accepted_nfa({"states": {0, 1, 2}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, "a"): {0}, (2, ""): {1}}, "start": 0, "accept": {1}})
=> None
--- check case | The alphabet's order breaks ties
(shortest_accepted_nfa({"states": {0, 1}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, "a"): {1}, (0, "b"): {1}}, "start": 0, "accept": {1}}), shortest_accepted_nfa({"states": {0, 1}, "alphabet": "ba", "delta": {(0, "a"): {1}, (0, "b"): {1}}, "start": 0, "accept": {1}}))
=> ("a", "b")
--- check case | Accepting only after an ε-move that follows a symbol
shortest_accepted_nfa({"states": {0, 1, 2}, "alphabet": "xy", "delta": {(0, "y"): {1}, (1, ""): {2}}, "start": 0, "accept": {2}})
=> "y"

+++ practice | Remove the ε-moves
--- task
Every NFA can be changed into one **without ε-moves** that has the same states and the same language. For each state q and symbol c, the new moves are: take the ε-closure of {q}, follow every c-move from those states, then take the ε-closure of the result. A state becomes accepting if its ε-closure contains an old accepting state. The start state stays the same.

Write `remove_epsilons(nfa)` that returns the new NFA. Leave out pairs whose set of moves would be empty, so `"delta"` has no entry with an empty set and no entry with the symbol `""`.

Watch the edge cases: a start state that reaches an accepting state only through ε-moves must become accepting (so the empty string stays accepted), and ε-moves may form a cycle.
--- starter
def remove_epsilons(nfa):
    delta = {key: set(v) for key, v in nfa["delta"].items() if key[1] != ""}
    return {"states": set(nfa["states"]), "alphabet": nfa["alphabet"], "delta": delta, "start": nfa["start"], "accept": set(nfa["accept"])}
--- solution
def eps_closure(nfa, states):
    found = set(states)
    stack = list(states)
    while stack:
        q = stack.pop()
        for r in nfa["delta"].get((q, ""), set()):
            if r not in found:
                found.add(r)
                stack.append(r)
    return found


def remove_epsilons(nfa):
    delta = {}
    for q in nfa["states"]:
        before = eps_closure(nfa, {q})
        for c in nfa["alphabet"]:
            moved = set()
            for p in before:
                moved |= nfa["delta"].get((p, c), set())
            after = eps_closure(nfa, moved)
            if after:
                delta[(q, c)] = after
    accept = {q for q in nfa["states"] if eps_closure(nfa, {q}) & nfa["accept"]}
    return {"states": set(nfa["states"]), "alphabet": nfa["alphabet"], "delta": delta, "start": nfa["start"], "accept": accept}
--- hint
You need the ε-closure of a set of states; write it with a stack and a "found" set, so that cycles are safe.
--- hint
For each state `q` and each symbol `c`: close `{q}`, collect every `c`-move from the closed set, and close the result. Store it only if it is non-empty. Then a state is accepting when the closure of `{q}` meets the old accepting states.
--- check test | No ε-moves and no empty sets are left
(lambda Z: all(c != "" and v for (_, c), v in Z["delta"].items()))(remove_epsilons({"states": {0, 1, 2, 3}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, ""): {1}, (1, ""): {2}, (2, ""): {0}, (2, "a"): {3}, (3, ""): {0}}, "start": 0, "accept": {3}}))
--- check test | a*b*: the start state becomes accepting, so "" is still accepted
(lambda Z: 0 in Z["accept"] and Z["start"] == 0)(remove_epsilons({"states": {0, 1}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, "a"): {0}, (0, ""): {1}, (1, "b"): {1}}, "start": 0, "accept": {1}}))
--- check test | Same language on every string up to length 8, for three NFAs with ε-moves
(lambda P, red, re: all(all(bool(red(lambda S, c: {r for q in S for r in Z["delta"].get((q, c), ())}, w, {Z["start"]}) & Z["accept"]) == bool(re.fullmatch(rx, w)) for n in range(9) for w in ("".join(t) for t in P("ab", repeat=n))) for N, rx in [({"states": {0, 1}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, "a"): {0}, (0, ""): {1}, (1, "b"): {1}}, "start": 0, "accept": {1}}, "a*b*"), ({"states": {0, 1, 2, 3}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, ""): {1}, (1, ""): {2}, (2, ""): {0}, (2, "a"): {3}, (3, ""): {0}}, "start": 0, "accept": {3}}, "a+"), ({"states": {"s", "x0", "x1", "y0", "y1"}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {("s", ""): {"x0", "y0"}, ("x0", "a"): {"x1"}, ("x1", "a"): {"x0"}, ("x0", "b"): {"x0"}, ("x1", "b"): {"x1"}, ("y0", "a"): {"y0"}, ("y0", "b"): {"y0", "y1"}}, "start": "s", "accept": {"x0", "y1"}}, "(b*ab*a)*b*|(a|b)*b")] for Z in [remove_epsilons(N)]))(__import__("itertools").product, __import__("functools").reduce, __import__("re"))
--- check test | The same states, and an NFA without ε-moves keeps its moves
(lambda Z: Z["states"] == {0, 1} and Z["delta"] == {(0, "a"): {0, 1}, (1, "b"): {0}} and Z["accept"] == {1})(remove_epsilons({"states": {0, 1}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, "a"): {0, 1}, (1, "b"): {0}}, "start": 0, "accept": {1}}))

+++ practice | Debug: the DFA that misses a jump
--- task
`to_dfa(nfa)` should be the subset construction: it returns a total DFA whose states are frozensets of NFA states, reachable from the ε-closure of the start state, with the same language as the NFA.

It works on NFAs whose ε-moves all leave the start state, but it goes wrong when an ε-move comes **after** a symbol. For the NFA with moves `0 -y-> 1` and `1 -ε-> 2`, where only 2 accepts, the DFA should accept `"y"`, but it rejects it. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
def closure(nfa, states):
    found = set(states)
    stack = list(states)
    while stack:
        q = stack.pop()
        for r in nfa["delta"].get((q, ""), set()):
            if r not in found:
                found.add(r)
                stack.append(r)
    return frozenset(found)


def move(nfa, S, c):
    out = set()
    for q in S:
        out |= nfa["delta"].get((q, c), set())
    return frozenset(out)


def to_dfa(nfa):
    start = closure(nfa, {nfa["start"]})
    states = {start}
    delta = {}
    todo = [start]
    while todo:
        S = todo.pop()
        for c in nfa["alphabet"]:
            T = move(nfa, S, c)
            delta[(S, c)] = T
            if T not in states:
                states.add(T)
                todo.append(T)
    accept = {S for S in states if S & nfa["accept"]}
    return {"states": states, "alphabet": nfa["alphabet"], "delta": delta, "start": start, "accept": accept}
--- solution
def closure(nfa, states):
    found = set(states)
    stack = list(states)
    while stack:
        q = stack.pop()
        for r in nfa["delta"].get((q, ""), set()):
            if r not in found:
                found.add(r)
                stack.append(r)
    return frozenset(found)


def move(nfa, S, c):
    out = set()
    for q in S:
        out |= nfa["delta"].get((q, c), set())
    return frozenset(out)


def to_dfa(nfa):
    start = closure(nfa, {nfa["start"]})
    states = {start}
    delta = {}
    todo = [start]
    while todo:
        S = todo.pop()
        for c in nfa["alphabet"]:
            T = closure(nfa, move(nfa, S, c))
            delta[(S, c)] = T
            if T not in states:
                states.add(T)
                todo.append(T)
    accept = {S for S in states if S & nfa["accept"]}
    return {"states": states, "alphabet": nfa["alphabet"], "delta": delta, "start": start, "accept": accept}
--- hint
The ε-closure is taken once, for the start state. When else can the NFA take ε-moves?
--- hint
After every move on a symbol, the NFA may take ε-moves before the next symbol. Take the closure of the result of `move`.
--- check test | The example from the task: "y" is accepted
(lambda D, red: red(lambda q, c: D["delta"][(q, c)], "y", D["start"]) in D["accept"])(to_dfa({"states": {0, 1, 2}, "alphabet": "xy", "delta": {(0, "y"): {1}, (1, ""): {2}}, "start": 0, "accept": {2}}), __import__("functools").reduce)
--- check test | One or more a's through an ε-cycle, on every string up to length 7
(lambda D, P, red: all((red(lambda q, c: D["delta"][(q, c)], w, D["start"]) in D["accept"]) == (len(w) > 0 and set(w) == {"a"}) for n in range(8) for w in ("".join(t) for t in P("ab", repeat=n))))(to_dfa({"states": {0, 1, 2, 3}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, ""): {1}, (1, ""): {2}, (2, ""): {0}, (2, "a"): {3}, (3, ""): {0}}, "start": 0, "accept": {3}}), __import__("itertools").product, __import__("functools").reduce)
--- check test | a*b* still works, with three reachable sets
(lambda D, red: len(D["states"]) == 3 and [red(lambda q, c: D["delta"][(q, c)], w, D["start"]) in D["accept"] for w in ["", "ab", "ba"]] == [True, True, False])(to_dfa({"states": {0, 1}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, "a"): {0}, (0, ""): {1}, (1, "b"): {1}}, "start": 0, "accept": {1}}), __import__("functools").reduce)

+++ practice | Scanning a log for alarm words
--- task
A ground station scans a long telemetry log for any of several **alarm words**. Write `alarm_positions(words, text)` that returns a sorted list of every position `i` (counting from 0) where some alarm word **ends** at `text[i]`. Words may overlap, and a position appears once even if several words end there. For example, `alarm_positions(["TEMP", "EMP"], "XTEMPTEMP")` is `[4, 8]`.

Do it with an automaton, in one pass over the text:

1. Build an NFA with a state `"root"` that loops on every symbol (every character in the text or the words). For each word, add a chain of states from `"root"` that spells the word, and make the end of each chain accepting.
2. Scan the text with the set of current NFA states, starting from `{"root"}`. After each character, if the set contains an accepting state, record the position.
3. Build the DFA **lazily**: keep a dictionary from `(frozenset of states, character)` to the next frozenset, and compute a move only the first time it is needed.

A check scans 200,000 characters for 60 words, so each character must cost about one dictionary lookup once the cache is warm. `words` holds non-empty strings; the text may be empty.
--- starter
def alarm_positions(words, text):
    return []
--- solution
def alarm_positions(words, text):
    symbols = set(text) | set("".join(words))
    delta = {("root", c): {"root"} for c in symbols}
    accept = set()
    for w in words:
        for i, c in enumerate(w):
            prev = "root" if i == 0 else (w, i)
            delta.setdefault((prev, c), set()).add((w, i + 1))
        accept.add((w, len(w)))
    cache = {}
    current = frozenset({"root"})
    hits = []
    for pos, c in enumerate(text):
        key = (current, c)
        if key not in cache:
            moved = set()
            for q in current:
                moved |= delta.get((q, c), set())
            cache[key] = frozenset(moved)
        current = cache[key]
        if current & accept:
            hits.append(pos)
    return hits
--- hint
Name the chain states `(word, i)`, meaning "the first `i` symbols of `word` have just been read". The move on `word[i]` goes from `(word, i)` to `(word, i + 1)`, with `"root"` in place of `(word, 0)`. The accepting states are `(word, len(word))`.
--- hint
There are no ε-moves, so a step is only the union of the moves of the current states. Before computing it, look the pair `(current, c)` up in your cache; store the result there the first time. Because `"root"` loops on everything, it is in every set, so a word can start at any position.
--- check case | The example from the task
alarm_positions(["TEMP", "EMP"], "XTEMPTEMP")
=> [4, 8]
--- check case | Overlapping matches of one word
alarm_positions(["aa"], "aaaa")
=> [1, 2, 3]
--- check case | Empty text, and a word that never occurs
(alarm_positions(["ab"], ""), alarm_positions(["zz"], "zazaz"))
=> ([], [])
--- check case | A word inside another word
alarm_positions(["FAULT", "AUL", "T"], "NOFAULTS")
=> [5, 6]
--- check test | Agrees with checking every position, on seeded random logs
(lambda R: all(alarm_positions(ws, t) == [i for i in range(len(t)) if any(t[: i + 1].endswith(w) for w in ws)] for ws, t in (([ "".join(R.choice("abc") for _ in range(R.randint(1, 4))) for _ in range(R.randint(1, 4))], "".join(R.choice("abcd") for _ in range(R.randint(0, 40)))) for _ in range(200))))(__import__("random").Random(8))
--- check test | 200,000 characters and 60 words
(lambda R: (lambda t, ws: (lambda h: len(h) == len(set(h)) and h == sorted(h) and all(any(t[: i + 1].endswith(w) for w in ws) for i in h[:300]) and len(h) == sum(1 for i in range(len(t)) if any(t.startswith(w, i + 1 - len(w)) and i + 1 >= len(w) for w in ws)))(alarm_positions(ws, t)))("".join(R.choice("ACGT") for _ in range(200000)), ["".join(R.choice("ACGT") for _ in range(R.randint(5, 9))) for _ in range(60)]))(__import__("random").Random(8))
?? Cache each (set, character) move the first time you compute it, so later characters cost one lookup.

+++ question | Why 2ᵏ states are needed
--- ask
In the proof that every DFA for "the k-th symbol from the end is 1" has at least 2ᵏ states, two different strings x and y of length k are taken. What is shown about them?
--- choice
That x and y are both in the language.
--- choice correct
That some string z makes exactly one of xz and yz belong to the language, so a correct DFA cannot be in the same state after x and after y.
--- choice
That the NFA reaches different sets of states after x and after y.
--- choice
That x and y have different lengths, so they need different states.
--- why
If a DFA were in the same state after x and after y, it would treat xz and yz identically for every z. The proof picks the position i where x and y differ and appends i − 1 symbols, so that position becomes the k-th from the end: one string is in and the other is out. So all 2ᵏ strings of length k need different states. The argument is about any DFA, not about the NFA.

+++ question | Following branches one at a time
--- ask
A backtracking matcher explores an NFA's branches one at a time, backing up at dead ends. The set simulation follows all branches together. On a string of length n, what are their worst-case running times, for a fixed NFA?
--- choice
Both are O(n), since the NFA is fixed.
--- choice correct
Backtracking can take time exponential in n; the set simulation takes O(n) steps, each costing O(|Q| + |δ|).
--- choice
The set simulation is exponential, because it keeps up to 2^|Q| sets.
--- choice
Backtracking is O(n·|Q|), and the set simulation is O(n·2^|Q|).
--- why
The number of paths through an NFA can double with every symbol, and backtracking may try them all. The set simulation keeps only the one current set, at most |Q| states, and updates it once per symbol. The 2^|Q| is how many different sets could exist, which matters for building the whole DFA, not for simulating one string.
=== theory-04 | Regular expressions and Thompson's construction
--- teach
The last two lessons described languages with machines: states and arrows. This lesson describes them with a formula instead. A **regular expression** is a short piece of text, such as `(a|b)*abb`, built from symbols with three operations: union, concatenation and star, the same operations as lesson 1. You have used them in search boxes and in `grep`. This lesson makes them precise, turns any regular expression into an NFA with **Thompson's construction**, and goes back the other way with **state elimination**. Together with the subset construction, that proves **Kleene's theorem**: regular expressions, NFAs and DFAs describe exactly the same languages.

### A formula for a set of strings

Picture a search box in a log viewer. You type `ERR(OR)*` and it finds `ERR`, `ERROR`, `ERROROR`, and so on. That short text names an infinite set of strings. A regular expression is exactly such a name, built by a few fixed rules.

### The definition

Regular expressions over an alphabet Σ are defined by six rules. Three build the smallest expressions, and three combine smaller ones. Next to each is the language it names, written L(R):

| expression | its language |
|---|---|
| ∅ | the empty language ∅ |
| ε | {ε}, just the empty string |
| a (a symbol of Σ) | {a} |
| R \| S | L(R) ∪ L(S), the union |
| RS | L(R)L(S), the concatenation |
| R\* | L(R)\*, the Kleene star |

Nothing else is a regular expression. A definition like this, where big things are built from smaller things of the same kind, is **inductive**, and it lets you prove things about every regular expression by **structural induction**: prove the claim for the three smallest cases, then show it survives each of the three ways of combining.

### Precedence

Without brackets, star binds tightest, then concatenation, then union, the way powers bind tighter than times, and times tighter than plus:

- `ab*` means a(b\*): an `a` followed by any number of `b`s. It is not (ab)\*.
- `ab|c` means (ab)|c, not a(b|c).
- `a|bc*` means a | (b(c\*)).

Some expressions and their languages, over {0, 1}:

- `0*10*`: exactly one 1.
- `(0|1)*1(0|1)(0|1)`: the third symbol from the end is 1, from lesson 3.
- `(00|11)*`: strings made of doubled symbols, such as `0011` and `111100`.

Practical regex languages add shorthand that means nothing new: R+ is RR\*, R? is R | ε, `[abc]` is a | b | c, and `.` is any single symbol.

### Expressions as trees

A program works with a regular expression as a tree, called its **syntax tree**. This course writes the tree as nested tuples, one kind of tuple per rule:

```python
empty = ("empty",)             # ∅
eps = ("eps",)                 # ε
x = ("sym", "x")               # the symbol x
either = ("alt", x, eps)       # x | ε
twice = ("cat", x, x)          # xx
many = ("star", twice)         # (xx)*
print(many)    # ('star', ('cat', ('sym', 'x'), ('sym', 'x')))
```

The tree settles every question about precedence: `ab*` is `("cat", ("sym", "a"), ("star", ("sym", "b")))`. A function on regular expressions looks at `node[0]` and handles the six cases, [[calling itself on the parts|structural-recursion]]. The **size** of an expression is its number of nodes.

### From an expression to an NFA

Ken Thompson, who later co-created Unix, published this construction in 1968 to add [[searching by pattern|thompson-qed]] to a text editor. It builds an NFA for an expression by building one for each part and wiring them together with ε-moves.

Every piece it builds is a **fragment**: an NFA with exactly one start state and exactly one accepting state, where no arrow enters the start state and no arrow leaves the accepting state. There is one recipe per rule. Write s and f for a new start and a new accepting state.

- **∅:** two states s and f, and no arrow. Nothing is accepted.
- **ε:** s —ε→ f.
- **a symbol a:** s —a→ f.
- **RS:** build fragments for R and S, then add one ε-arrow from R's accepting state to S's start state. The start is R's start and the accepting state is S's. No new states.
- **R | S:** build both, add new s and f, with ε-arrows from s to both starts, and from both accepting states to f.
- **R\*:** build R, add new s and f, and four ε-arrows: s to R's start (go in), s to f (zero times), R's accepting state to R's start (go round again), and R's accepting state to f (stop).

The whole NFA is the fragment of the whole expression. Its states are numbered 0, 1, 2, … as they are created.

### Why the construction is correct

By structural induction, prove this claim for every expression R: **the fragment for R accepts exactly L(R), has one start state with no arrows in, and one accepting state with no arrows out.**

- **The three small cases:** check each by looking at it. The ∅ fragment has no path from s to f, the ε fragment has one path spelling ε, and the symbol fragment one path spelling a.
- **RS:** a path from R's start to S's accepting state must cross the new ε-arrow exactly once: it is the only arrow from R's part to S's part, and no arrow leads back. Before it, the path goes from R's start to R's accepting state, so it spells some x ∈ L(R) (by the claim for R); after it, it spells some y ∈ L(S). These are exactly the strings of L(R)L(S).
- **R | S:** a path from s first chooses one ε-arrow into R or into S, and then cannot cross over: the only way out of either part is to f. So it spells a string of L(R) or of L(S).
- **R\*:** a path from s to f either takes the skip arrow (spelling ε) or goes through R some number k ≥ 1 of times, returning along the "round again" arrow between visits. That spells a join of k strings of L(R). So the fragment accepts L(R)⁰ ∪ L(R)¹ ∪ … = L(R)\*.

The **"no arrows into the start, none out of the accept"** property is what makes the gluing safe. Each proof step above used it: a path can enter a fragment only at its start and leave only from its accepting state, so it reads a whole string of the fragment's language in between. If a fragment's start state had arrows coming in, or its accepting state had arrows going out, a path could slip out of a fragment and back in part-way through, spelling strings the expression does not describe. That is why the star adds a new s and f instead of reusing R's states. A question at the end of the lesson shows a concrete case.

### What it costs

Each node of the tree adds at most two states and at most four arrows. So an expression of size m becomes an NFA with **at most 2m states and O(m) arrows**, and every state has at most two arrows leaving it. Building it takes O(m) time. Running it with the set simulation of lesson 3 takes **O(m·n)** time on a string of length n.

A **backtracking** matcher, which is what Python's `re`, Perl, PCRE and Java use, tries one way of matching at a time and backs up when it fails. For most patterns that is fast. For some, it tries exponentially many ways. With `(a*)*b`, the string `aaa…a` (with no `b`) can be split among the inner and outer stars in about 2ⁿ ways, and a backtracking engine may try each before giving up. Patterns like that have taken down real websites; this attack is called [[ReDoS|redos]]. A Thompson NFA has no such worst case.

### The pipeline

Put the lessons together:

1. regular expression → NFA, by Thompson's construction (O(m) states);
2. NFA → DFA, by the subset construction (up to 2^(2m) states, usually far fewer);
3. DFA → the smallest DFA, by minimisation, which is lesson 5.

A lexer generator such as flex runs this pipeline once at build time, and then every token costs one table lookup per character. A search tool such as grep or RE2 often builds the DFA lazily instead, during the search.

### From an automaton back to an expression

Kleene's theorem also needs the other direction: every DFA has a regular expression. The method is **state elimination**, and it uses a **generalised NFA**, whose arrows are labelled by whole regular expressions instead of single symbols.

1. Start from the DFA. Add a new start state S with an ε-arrow to the old start, and a new final state F with ε-arrows from every old accepting state. Where several arrows join the same two states, merge them into one arrow labelled with their union, such as `0|1`.
2. Pick any old state q and remove it. For every pair of other states p and r with arrows p → q and q → r (p and r may be the same state, which makes a loop), add the detour through q to the label of p → r:

   new label(p, r) = label(p, r) | label(p, q) (label(q, q))\* label(q, r).

   If there was no p → r arrow, the "label(p, r) |" part is left out, and if q has no loop, the star part is ε.
3. Repeat until only S and F are left. The label of S → F is a regular expression for the DFA's language. If there is no S → F arrow at all, the language is ∅.

**Why it is correct.** One removal keeps the set of strings spelled by paths from S to F the same. A path that passed through q entered from some p, looped at q some number of times, and left to some r: exactly the strings of label(p, q)(label(q, q))\*label(q, r), which the new label now includes. And every string of the new label is spelled by such a path in the old automaton. So the language never changes, and at the end it is the one label left.

A small example. A DFA over {x, y} for "ends in y" has states A (start) and B (accepting): A —x→ A, A —y→ B, B —x→ A, B —y→ B. Add S —ε→ A and B —ε→ F, then remove B first. The paths through B are A → B → A and A → B → F, and B's loop is y, so A's loop becomes `x|yy*x` and a new arrow A → F gets `yy*`. Removing A, whose loop is now `x|yy*x`, leaves S → F labelled `(x|yy*x)*yy*`. Every string it names ends in y, as it should.

The expressions can grow quickly, since each removal may copy labels into many places. In the worst case the result is exponentially long, but it always exists.

### Kleene's theorem

Now the circle is closed:

- regular expression → NFA (Thompson);
- NFA → DFA (subset construction);
- DFA → regular expression (state elimination).

So **a language is regular exactly when some regular expression describes it**, and three very different descriptions (a formula, a nondeterministic machine and a deterministic machine) have exactly the same power.

### Where it is used

- **Search tools:** grep, ripgrep and every editor's find box.
- **Lexers:** the tokens of almost every programming language are specified by regular expressions.
- **Validation:** checking that a field looks like a date, a callsign or a packet header. A flight software team might require every command mnemonic to match `[A-Z]+_[A-Z0-9]+` before it is uplinked.
- **Log and telemetry analysis:** pulling fields out of millions of lines.
- **Security:** network intrusion detection matches traffic against thousands of expressions at once.

**Watch out:** the classic regular expression mistakes.

- **Precedence.** `ab*` is a(b\*), and `ab|cd` is (ab)|(cd). When in doubt, add brackets, or look at the tree.
- **∅ is not ε.** ∅ names no strings; ε names one. So ∅\* = {ε}, and R∅ = ∅ for every R.
- **The star must allow zero.** A star fragment without the skip arrow accepts only one or more repetitions, which is R+, not R\*.
- **Features that are not regular.** Backreferences such as `(a*)b\1` in Python's `re` look for a repeat of what was matched earlier. They can describe languages no DFA can, which lesson 6 shows, and matching them is NP-hard in general.

::: context structural-recursion Recursion that follows the tree
Every function in this lesson has the same shape: look at which of the six kinds of node you have, handle the three leaf kinds directly, and for the three combining kinds call yourself on the parts and combine the answers. This is structural recursion. It always ends, because every call is on a smaller tree, and it is correct for the same reason a structural induction proof is. You will write interpreters and type checkers this way in the compilers course.
:::

::: context thompson-qed Ken Thompson's editor
In 1968 Ken Thompson published "Regular Expression Search Algorithm" in the Communications of the ACM. It described compiling a pattern into machine code that ran the NFA directly, for the QED text editor. The same idea went into the Unix editor ed, and from there into grep, whose name comes from the ed command g/re/p: globally search for a regular expression and print. Thompson went on to co-create Unix and C, and later the Go language.
:::

::: context redos When a regex takes a website down
In July 2019 Cloudflare, which carries a large share of the web's traffic, had a global outage of about half an hour. A new firewall rule contained a regular expression with nested repetition, and on some inputs the backtracking engine used nearly all the CPU of machines around the world. In 2016 Stack Overflow went down for 34 minutes because a line with about 20,000 spaces made a trimming regex backtrack quadratically. Both engines were backtracking ones. Thompson-style engines such as RE2 were designed to rule this out.
:::

--- task
A regular expression is a syntax tree of nested tuples, as in the explanation: `("empty",)`, `("eps",)`, `("sym", c)`, `("alt", R, S)`, `("cat", R, S)` and `("star", R)`. An NFA is a dictionary as in lesson 3. Write two functions.

- `thompson(ast)`: return the NFA that Thompson's construction builds for the expression, following the six recipes in the lesson exactly (the concatenation adds no new states, the others add two). Number the states `0, 1, 2, …` in the order you create them, so `"states"` is `set(range(number of states))`. `"accept"` holds the single accepting state. `"alphabet"` is the distinct symbols of the expression, sorted, joined into one string (`""` when there are none).
- `matches(ast, w)`: return `True` when the string `w` is in the language of the expression. Build the NFA with `thompson` and run it with the set simulation from lesson 3 (you may copy your ε-closure). A symbol that does not appear in the expression makes the answer `False`.

It must be fast: a check matches `(a|aa)*b` against a string of 3,000 `a`s. For example, `matches(("star", ("cat", ("sym", "a"), ("sym", "b"))), "abab")` is `True`.
--- starter
def thompson(ast):
    return {"states": {0}, "alphabet": "", "delta": {}, "start": 0, "accept": {0}}


def matches(ast, w):
    return False
--- solution
def symbols_of(ast):
    if ast[0] == "sym":
        return {ast[1]}
    out = set()
    for part in ast[1:]:
        out |= symbols_of(part)
    return out


def thompson(ast):
    delta = {}
    made = [0]

    def new_state():
        made[0] += 1
        return made[0] - 1

    def arrow(p, c, q):
        delta.setdefault((p, c), set()).add(q)

    def build(node):
        kind = node[0]
        if kind in ("empty", "eps", "sym"):
            s, f = new_state(), new_state()
            if kind == "eps":
                arrow(s, "", f)
            elif kind == "sym":
                arrow(s, node[1], f)
            return s, f
        if kind == "cat":
            s1, f1 = build(node[1])
            s2, f2 = build(node[2])
            arrow(f1, "", s2)
            return s1, f2
        if kind == "alt":
            s1, f1 = build(node[1])
            s2, f2 = build(node[2])
            s, f = new_state(), new_state()
            arrow(s, "", s1)
            arrow(s, "", s2)
            arrow(f1, "", f)
            arrow(f2, "", f)
            return s, f
        s1, f1 = build(node[1])
        s, f = new_state(), new_state()
        arrow(s, "", s1)
        arrow(s, "", f)
        arrow(f1, "", s1)
        arrow(f1, "", f)
        return s, f

    start, accept = build(ast)
    alphabet = "".join(sorted(symbols_of(ast)))
    return {"states": set(range(made[0])), "alphabet": alphabet, "delta": delta, "start": start, "accept": {accept}}


def eps_closure(nfa, states):
    found = set(states)
    stack = list(states)
    while stack:
        q = stack.pop()
        for r in nfa["delta"].get((q, ""), set()):
            if r not in found:
                found.add(r)
                stack.append(r)
    return frozenset(found)


def matches(ast, w):
    nfa = thompson(ast)
    current = eps_closure(nfa, {nfa["start"]})
    for c in w:
        if c not in nfa["alphabet"]:
            return False
        moved = set()
        for q in current:
            moved |= nfa["delta"].get((q, c), set())
        current = eps_closure(nfa, moved)
    return bool(current & nfa["accept"])
--- hint

--- hint
The three leaf kinds make two new states each, joined by an ε-arrow, a symbol arrow, or nothing. For `"cat"`, build both parts and add one ε-arrow from the first part's accepting state to the second part's start. For `"alt"` and `"star"`, build the part or parts first, then make the two new states and add the ε-arrows from the recipe.
--- hint
The star needs four ε-arrows: new start to the part's start, new start to new accept, the part's accept back to the part's start, and the part's accept to the new accept. `matches` is the lesson 3 simulation on `thompson(ast)`, with a check that every symbol is in the alphabet.

+++ practice | Write the expressions
--- task
Write five regular expressions over the alphabet {a, b}, as Python strings, using only the symbols `a` and `b`, the union `|`, the star `*` and brackets `(` `)`. Concatenation is writing things next to each other. (These strings also work as patterns for Python's `re.fullmatch`, which is how they are checked.)

- `HAS_ABA`: strings that contain `aba` somewhere.
- `EVEN_LENGTH`: strings of even length, including the empty string.
- `NO_BB`: strings with no two `b`s in a row.
- `A_COUNT_3`: strings whose number of `a`s is divisible by 3 (so `""`, `bbb` and `abaab` are in).
- `SAME_ENDS`: non-empty strings that start and end with the same symbol (so `a` and `abba` are in, `ab` is not).
--- starter
HAS_ABA = "aba"
EVEN_LENGTH = "(aa)*"
NO_BB = "a*"
A_COUNT_3 = "(aaa)*"
SAME_ENDS = "a|b"
--- solution
HAS_ABA = "(a|b)*aba(a|b)*"
EVEN_LENGTH = "((a|b)(a|b))*"
NO_BB = "(a|ba)*(b|)"
A_COUNT_3 = "b*(ab*ab*ab*)*"
SAME_ENDS = "a|b|a(a|b)*a|b(a|b)*b"
--- hint
`(a|b)*` stands for "anything". An even-length string is a sequence of blocks of two symbols. For `NO_BB`, think of every `b` except possibly the last one as being followed by an `a`: blocks of `a` or `ba`, then an optional final `b` (an empty branch, as in `(b|)`, means ε).
--- hint
For `A_COUNT_3`, allow `b`s anywhere: some `b`s first, then blocks that each hold exactly three `a`s with `b`s between them. For `SAME_ENDS`, list the cases: a single symbol, or `a`…`a`, or `b`…`b`.

+++ practice | The strings of an expression, up to a length
--- task
Write `lang_up_to(ast, n)` that returns the set of strings in the language of the expression `ast` (a syntax tree as in the lesson) whose length is at most `n`. Work straight from the definition, with one case per kind of node, calling yourself on the parts:

- `("empty",)` gives the empty set, `("eps",)` gives `{""}`, and `("sym", c)` gives `{c}` (if `n ≥ 1`);
- `("alt", R, S)` is the union of the two parts' sets;
- `("cat", R, S)` joins every string of the first part's set to every string of the second's, keeping only the results of length at most `n`;
- `("star", R)` is the star of the part's set, up to length `n`, as in lesson 1.

For example, `lang_up_to(("star", ("cat", ("sym", "a"), ("sym", "b"))), 4)` is `{"", "ab", "abab"}`.
--- starter
def lang_up_to(ast, n):
    return set()
--- solution
def star_up_to(A, n):
    pieces = [a for a in A if a]
    found = {""}
    frontier = [""]
    while frontier:
        new = []
        for w in frontier:
            for a in pieces:
                longer = w + a
                if len(longer) <= n and longer not in found:
                    found.add(longer)
                    new.append(longer)
        frontier = new
    return found


def lang_up_to(ast, n):
    kind = ast[0]
    if kind == "empty":
        return set()
    if kind == "eps":
        return {""}
    if kind == "sym":
        return {ast[1]} if n >= 1 else set()
    if kind == "alt":
        return lang_up_to(ast[1], n) | lang_up_to(ast[2], n)
    if kind == "cat":
        left = lang_up_to(ast[1], n)
        right = lang_up_to(ast[2], n)
        return {x + y for x in left for y in right if len(x) + len(y) <= n}
    return star_up_to(lang_up_to(ast[1], n), n)
--- hint
Each case returns a set, and the combining cases first call `lang_up_to` on their parts with the same `n`. The parts never need longer strings than `n`, because joining only makes strings longer.
--- hint
For the star, reuse the fixed-point idea of lesson 1: start from `{""}` and keep gluing non-empty strings of the part's set onto what you have, as long as the result has length at most `n` and is new.
--- check case | The example from the task
lang_up_to(("star", ("cat", ("sym", "a"), ("sym", "b"))), 4)
=> {"", "ab", "abab"}
--- check case | ∅ joined to anything is ∅, and ∅* is {""}
(lang_up_to(("cat", ("empty",), ("sym", "a")), 3), lang_up_to(("star", ("empty",)), 3), lang_up_to(("alt", ("empty",), ("eps",)), 3))
=> (set(), {""}, {""})
--- check case | Length 0 keeps only the empty string, if it is in the language
(lang_up_to(("sym", "a"), 0), lang_up_to(("star", ("sym", "a")), 0), lang_up_to(("cat", ("sym", "a"), ("sym", "b")), 1))
=> (set(), {""}, set())
--- check test | Eight expressions agree with Python's re, up to length 6
(lambda P, re: all(lang_up_to(A, 6) == {w for k in range(7) for w in ("".join(t) for t in P("ab", repeat=k)) if re.fullmatch(rx, w)} for A, rx in [(("cat", ("cat", ("cat", ("star", ("alt", ("sym", "a"), ("sym", "b"))), ("sym", "a")), ("sym", "b")), ("sym", "b")), "(a|b)*abb"), (("alt", ("sym", "a"), ("eps",)), "a|"), (("star", ("star", ("sym", "a"))), "(a*)*"), (("cat", ("sym", "a"), ("star", ("alt", ("sym", "b"), ("cat", ("sym", "a"), ("sym", "a"))))), "a(b|aa)*"), (("star", ("eps",)), "()*"), (("cat", ("star", ("sym", "a")), ("star", ("sym", "b"))), "a*b*"), (("star", ("alt", ("cat", ("sym", "a"), ("sym", "a")), ("cat", ("sym", "b"), ("sym", "b")))), "(aa|bb)*"), (("cat", ("star", ("cat", ("sym", "b"), ("star", ("sym", "a")))), ("sym", "b")), "(ba*)*b")]))(__import__("itertools").product, __import__("re"))

+++ practice | Parse an expression
--- task
Write `parse(s)` that turns a regular expression written as text into its syntax tree (as in the lesson). The text uses `|` for union, `*` for star, brackets, and any other character as a symbol; concatenation is writing things side by side. Follow the precedence rules: star binds tightest, then concatenation, then union. Build left to right, so `abc` is `("cat", ("cat", a, b), c)` and `a|b|c` is `("alt", ("alt", a, b), c)` (where `a` stands for `("sym", "a")`).

An empty part means ε: `parse("")` and `parse("()")` are `("eps",)`, and `parse("a|")` is `("alt", ("sym", "a"), ("eps",))`. A star can follow a star: `a**` is `("star", ("star", ("sym", "a")))`. You may assume the brackets are balanced.

Use **recursive descent**: one function per precedence level. `parse_alt` reads one or more concatenations separated by `|`. `parse_cat` reads starred items until it sees `|`, `)` or the end. `parse_star` reads one item and then any `*`s after it. `parse_item` reads a symbol, or a bracket, a whole `parse_alt`, and the closing bracket.
--- starter
def parse(s):
    return ("eps",)
--- solution
def parse(s):
    pos = 0

    def peek():
        return s[pos] if pos < len(s) else None

    def parse_alt():
        nonlocal pos
        node = parse_cat()
        while peek() == "|":
            pos += 1
            node = ("alt", node, parse_cat())
        return node

    def parse_cat():
        node = None
        while peek() is not None and peek() not in "|)":
            part = parse_star()
            node = part if node is None else ("cat", node, part)
        return ("eps",) if node is None else node

    def parse_star():
        nonlocal pos
        node = parse_item()
        while peek() == "*":
            pos += 1
            node = ("star", node)
        return node

    def parse_item():
        nonlocal pos
        c = peek()
        pos += 1
        if c == "(":
            node = parse_alt()
            pos += 1
            return node
        return ("sym", c)

    return parse_alt()
--- hint
Keep the current position in one variable shared by the inner functions (declare it `nonlocal` in the ones that move it), and a helper `peek()` that returns the character there, or `None` at the end.
--- hint
`parse_alt` calls `parse_cat` once, then, while the next character is `|`, skips it and joins another `parse_cat` with `"alt"`. `parse_cat` joins items with `"cat"` while the next character is not `|`, `)` or the end, and returns `("eps",)` if it read nothing.
--- hint
`parse_star` wraps its item in `"star"` once for each `*` that follows. `parse_item` either returns a symbol, or, at `(`, reads a whole `parse_alt` and then skips the `)`.
--- check case | Precedence: ab*|c
parse("ab*|c")
=> ("alt", ("cat", ("sym", "a"), ("star", ("sym", "b"))), ("sym", "c"))
--- check case | Left to right, for concatenation and union
(parse("abc"), parse("a|b|c"))
=> (("cat", ("cat", ("sym", "a"), ("sym", "b")), ("sym", "c")), ("alt", ("alt", ("sym", "a"), ("sym", "b")), ("sym", "c")))
--- check case | Brackets
parse("(a|b)*a")
=> ("cat", ("star", ("alt", ("sym", "a"), ("sym", "b"))), ("sym", "a"))
--- check case | Empty parts are ε
(parse(""), parse("()"), parse("a|"), parse("|b"))
=> (("eps",), ("eps",), ("alt", ("sym", "a"), ("eps",)), ("alt", ("eps",), ("sym", "b")))
--- check case | Stars on stars, and on brackets
(parse("a**"), parse("(ab)*"))
=> (("star", ("star", ("sym", "a"))), ("star", ("cat", ("sym", "a"), ("sym", "b"))))
--- check case | Other characters are symbols
parse("x0y")
=> ("cat", ("cat", ("sym", "x"), ("sym", "0")), ("sym", "y"))
--- check test | Nested brackets several levels deep
parse("((a))((b|c)*)") == ("cat", ("sym", "a"), ("star", ("alt", ("sym", "b"), ("sym", "c"))))

+++ practice | Empty, contains ε, or infinite?
--- task
Three questions about an expression can be answered from its syntax tree alone, without building any machine. Write three functions, each by structural recursion on the tree (as in the lesson):

- `is_empty(ast)`: `True` when the language is ∅.
- `nullable(ast)`: `True` when the language contains the empty string.
- `is_infinite(ast)`: `True` when the language has infinitely many strings.

The edge cases are where naive rules break: a concatenation with an empty part is empty, however big the other part is; `("star", R)` is never empty and always contains `""`, even when R is ∅; and a star is infinite only if its part has some **non-empty** string (so ε\* and ∅\* are finite). You may write a helper for "has a non-empty string".
--- starter
def is_empty(ast):
    return ast[0] == "empty"


def nullable(ast):
    return ast[0] in ("eps", "star")


def is_infinite(ast):
    return ast[0] == "star"
--- solution
def is_empty(ast):
    kind = ast[0]
    if kind == "empty":
        return True
    if kind in ("eps", "sym", "star"):
        return False
    if kind == "cat":
        return is_empty(ast[1]) or is_empty(ast[2])
    return is_empty(ast[1]) and is_empty(ast[2])


def nullable(ast):
    kind = ast[0]
    if kind in ("eps", "star"):
        return True
    if kind in ("empty", "sym"):
        return False
    if kind == "cat":
        return nullable(ast[1]) and nullable(ast[2])
    return nullable(ast[1]) or nullable(ast[2])


def has_nonempty(ast):
    kind = ast[0]
    if kind in ("empty", "eps"):
        return False
    if kind == "sym":
        return True
    if kind == "star":
        return has_nonempty(ast[1])
    if kind == "cat":
        return not is_empty(ast) and (has_nonempty(ast[1]) or has_nonempty(ast[2]))
    return has_nonempty(ast[1]) or has_nonempty(ast[2])


def is_infinite(ast):
    kind = ast[0]
    if kind in ("empty", "eps", "sym"):
        return False
    if kind == "star":
        return has_nonempty(ast[1])
    if kind == "cat":
        return not is_empty(ast) and (is_infinite(ast[1]) or is_infinite(ast[2]))
    return is_infinite(ast[1]) or is_infinite(ast[2])
--- hint
A concatenation is empty if either part is, and contains ε only if both parts do. A union is empty only if both parts are, and contains ε if either does.
--- hint
A concatenation of two non-empty languages is infinite when either part is infinite; if either part is empty, the whole thing is empty, so finite. A star is infinite exactly when its part contains a non-empty string, because then you can repeat that string as often as you like.
--- check case | The leaves
[(is_empty(A), nullable(A), is_infinite(A)) for A in [("empty",), ("eps",), ("sym", "a")]]
=> [(True, False, False), (False, True, False), (False, False, False)]
--- check case | ∅* and ε* are {ε}: not empty, nullable, finite
[(is_empty(A), nullable(A), is_infinite(A)) for A in [("star", ("empty",)), ("star", ("eps",))]]
=> [(False, True, False), (False, True, False)]
--- check case | An empty part makes a concatenation empty and finite, even next to a star
(lambda A: (is_empty(A), nullable(A), is_infinite(A)))(("cat", ("star", ("sym", "a")), ("empty",)))
=> (True, False, False)
--- check case | A union with ∅ keeps the other side
(lambda A: (is_empty(A), nullable(A), is_infinite(A)))(("alt", ("empty",), ("star", ("sym", "b"))))
=> (False, True, True)
--- check case | a* is infinite, (a|ε)(b|ε) is finite
[is_infinite(A) for A in [("star", ("sym", "a")), ("cat", ("alt", ("sym", "a"), ("eps",)), ("alt", ("sym", "b"), ("eps",)))]]
=> [True, False]
--- check case | A star of something that holds only ε after simplifying
(lambda A: (is_empty(A), nullable(A), is_infinite(A)))(("star", ("alt", ("eps",), ("cat", ("sym", "a"), ("empty",)))))
=> (False, True, False)
--- check test | Agrees with the listed language on 300 seeded random expressions
(lambda R: (lambda gen, lang, leaves: all((lambda A: (lambda L, k: is_empty(A) == (not L) and nullable(A) == ("" in L) and is_infinite(A) == any(len(w) > k for w in L))(lang(A, 2 * leaves(A) + 2), leaves(A)))(gen(gen, 4)) for _ in range(300)))(lambda g, d: R.choice([("sym", "a"), ("sym", "b"), ("eps",), ("empty",)]) if d == 0 or R.random() < 0.3 else R.choice([lambda: ("cat", g(g, d - 1), g(g, d - 1)), lambda: ("alt", g(g, d - 1), g(g, d - 1)), lambda: ("star", g(g, d - 1))])(), (lambda f: lambda A, n: f(f, A, n))(lambda f, A, n: set() if A[0] == "empty" else {""} if A[0] == "eps" else ({A[1]} if n else set()) if A[0] == "sym" else f(f, A[1], n) | f(f, A[2], n) if A[0] == "alt" else {x + y for x in f(f, A[1], n) for y in f(f, A[2], n) if len(x + y) <= n} if A[0] == "cat" else (lambda S: __import__("functools").reduce(lambda acc, _: acc | {x + y for x in acc for y in S if len(x + y) <= n}, range(n + 1), {""}))(f(f, A[1], n))), (lambda f: lambda A: f(f, A))(lambda f, A: 1 if A[0] == "sym" else 0 if A[0] in ("eps", "empty") else sum(f(f, p) for p in A[1:]))))(__import__("random").Random(6))

+++ practice | Debug: the star that must repeat
--- task
`thompson(ast)` builds the NFA of an expression with Thompson's construction, as in the lesson, and `matches(ast, w)` runs it. Everything works except the star: `matches(("star", ("sym", "a")), "")` gives `False`, and `(ab)*c` does not match `"c"`. A star must allow **zero** repetitions. Find the bug and fix it. It is one missing line.
--- starter
def thompson(ast):
    delta = {}
    made = [0]

    def new_state():
        made[0] += 1
        return made[0] - 1

    def arrow(p, c, q):
        delta.setdefault((p, c), set()).add(q)

    def build(node):
        kind = node[0]
        if kind in ("empty", "eps", "sym"):
            s, f = new_state(), new_state()
            if kind == "eps":
                arrow(s, "", f)
            elif kind == "sym":
                arrow(s, node[1], f)
            return s, f
        if kind == "cat":
            s1, f1 = build(node[1])
            s2, f2 = build(node[2])
            arrow(f1, "", s2)
            return s1, f2
        if kind == "alt":
            s1, f1 = build(node[1])
            s2, f2 = build(node[2])
            s, f = new_state(), new_state()
            arrow(s, "", s1)
            arrow(s, "", s2)
            arrow(f1, "", f)
            arrow(f2, "", f)
            return s, f
        s1, f1 = build(node[1])
        s, f = new_state(), new_state()
        arrow(s, "", s1)
        arrow(f1, "", s1)
        arrow(f1, "", f)
        return s, f

    start, accept = build(ast)
    return {"states": set(range(made[0])), "delta": delta, "start": start, "accept": {accept}}


def matches(ast, w):
    nfa = thompson(ast)

    def closure(states):
        found = set(states)
        stack = list(states)
        while stack:
            q = stack.pop()
            for r in nfa["delta"].get((q, ""), set()):
                if r not in found:
                    found.add(r)
                    stack.append(r)
        return found

    current = closure({nfa["start"]})
    for c in w:
        current = closure({r for q in current for r in nfa["delta"].get((q, c), set())})
    return bool(current & nfa["accept"])
--- solution
def thompson(ast):
    delta = {}
    made = [0]

    def new_state():
        made[0] += 1
        return made[0] - 1

    def arrow(p, c, q):
        delta.setdefault((p, c), set()).add(q)

    def build(node):
        kind = node[0]
        if kind in ("empty", "eps", "sym"):
            s, f = new_state(), new_state()
            if kind == "eps":
                arrow(s, "", f)
            elif kind == "sym":
                arrow(s, node[1], f)
            return s, f
        if kind == "cat":
            s1, f1 = build(node[1])
            s2, f2 = build(node[2])
            arrow(f1, "", s2)
            return s1, f2
        if kind == "alt":
            s1, f1 = build(node[1])
            s2, f2 = build(node[2])
            s, f = new_state(), new_state()
            arrow(s, "", s1)
            arrow(s, "", s2)
            arrow(f1, "", f)
            arrow(f2, "", f)
            return s, f
        s1, f1 = build(node[1])
        s, f = new_state(), new_state()
        arrow(s, "", s1)
        arrow(s, "", f)
        arrow(f1, "", s1)
        arrow(f1, "", f)
        return s, f

    start, accept = build(ast)
    return {"states": set(range(made[0])), "delta": delta, "start": start, "accept": {accept}}


def matches(ast, w):
    nfa = thompson(ast)

    def closure(states):
        found = set(states)
        stack = list(states)
        while stack:
            q = stack.pop()
            for r in nfa["delta"].get((q, ""), set()):
                if r not in found:
                    found.add(r)
                    stack.append(r)
        return found

    current = closure({nfa["start"]})
    for c in w:
        current = closure({r for q in current for r in nfa["delta"].get((q, c), set())})
    return bool(current & nfa["accept"])
--- hint
Count the ε-arrows in the star case and compare them with the four in the lesson's recipe.
--- hint
There is no path from the star's new start to its new accepting state that avoids the inner part. Add the "zero times" arrow from `s` to `f`.

=> True

=> [True, True, True, False, False]

=> [True, True, False]
