+++ question | What this grammar generates
--- ask
Which language does the grammar S → aSbb | ε generate?
--- choice
{aⁿbⁿ : n ≥ 0}
--- choice correct
{aⁿb²ⁿ : n ≥ 0}
--- choice
Every string of `a`s and `b`s with twice as many `b`s as `a`s
--- choice
{a²ⁿbⁿ : n ≥ 0}
--- why
By the same invariant as in the lesson, every sentential form is aⁿSb²ⁿ or aⁿb²ⁿ: each use of S → aSbb adds one `a` on the left and two `b`s on the right. So the language is {aⁿb²ⁿ}. It is not every string with twice as many `b`s: `bba` has that count but is never produced, because all `a`s come first.

+++ question | The step that is usually skipped
--- ask
To prove that S → aSb | ε generates **only** strings of the form aⁿbⁿ, what do you prove by induction on the number of derivation steps?
--- choice
That aⁿbⁿ can be derived for every n.
--- choice correct
That every sentential form derived from S is aⁿSbⁿ or aⁿbⁿ for some n.
--- choice
That every string of the form aⁿbⁿ has exactly one parse tree.
--- choice
That the grammar is unambiguous.
--- why
"Only" is the direction L(G) ⊆ {aⁿbⁿ}. The invariant about sentential forms covers every derivation, including ones you did not think of; since a finished string has no variables, it must be aⁿbⁿ. The first choice proves the other direction, and ambiguity is a different question.

+++ question | What makes a grammar ambiguous
--- ask
Which of these shows that a grammar is ambiguous?
--- choice
Some string has two different derivations.
--- choice correct
Some string has two different parse trees.
--- choice
Some variable has two different rules.
--- choice
The grammar generates an infinite language.
--- why
Almost every grammar gives some strings several derivations, just by replacing variables in a different order; those derivations build the same tree. Ambiguity means two different trees, which is the same as two different **leftmost** derivations. Having several rules per variable is normal: S → aSb | ε is unambiguous.

=== theory-08 | Parsing: Chomsky normal form and the CYK algorithm
--- teach
Last lesson you listed every short string a grammar generates. That answers "is w in L(G)?" only by listing everything up to the length of w, which takes time exponential in that length. A compiler needs the answer for a file of thousands of symbols. This lesson builds an algorithm that decides membership for **any** context-free grammar in time proportional to n³, where n is the length of the string. It is called **[[CYK|cyk-names]]**, and it is **[[dynamic programming|dp-again]]** over the pieces of the string. It needs the grammar in a tidy shape first, called **Chomsky normal form**, and you will implement both the conversion and the algorithm.

### Why a tidy shape helps

Picture a tree-shaped staff chart where every manager has exactly two direct reports, and every person at the bottom does exactly one job. Given a team, you can ask "who could manage this team?" by trying every way of cutting it into a left part and a right part, and asking the same question about each part. Grammar rules in general are messier: a rule can have a long body, a body with one variable (a **unit rule**, A → B), or an empty body (an **ε-rule**). A unit rule or an ε-rule lets a variable produce a piece without splitting it into smaller pieces, which breaks the "always smaller" pattern that dynamic programming needs.

### Chomsky normal form

A grammar is in **Chomsky normal form**, or **CNF**, when every rule has one of these shapes:

- A → BC, where B and C are variables and neither of them is the start variable;
- A → a, where a is a terminal;
- S → ε, where S is the start variable. This one is allowed so that the language can contain ε.

In everyday words: every rule either splits a piece of the string into exactly two smaller pieces, or produces exactly one symbol.

In a CNF parse tree every inner node has either two variable children or one terminal child. So for a string of length n ≥ 1, the tree has exactly n nodes of the form A → a, one per symbol. Those n nodes are the leaves of the tree formed by the A → BC nodes, in which every inner node has exactly two children, and such a tree with n leaves has exactly n − 1 inner nodes (each one joins two parts into one, and it takes n − 1 joins to turn n parts into one). **Every derivation of a string of length n ≥ 1 takes exactly 2n − 1 steps.**

### Converting a grammar in five steps

Every context-free grammar can be converted to CNF without changing its language. Here is the method on a small grammar, whose language is the strings aⁿbⁿ and aⁿcbⁿ:

```text
S → aSb | A
A → c | ε
```

**1. A new start variable.** Add S₀ → S. Now the start variable never appears on a right side, as CNF requires. The language is unchanged.

**2. Remove ε-rules.** A variable is **nullable** when it can derive ε. Find them all as a least fixed point, as in the last lesson: A is nullable if some body of A consists only of nullable variables (the empty body counts). Here A is nullable (A → ε), so S is too (S → A), so S₀ is too.

Now, for every rule, add every version with some of its nullable occurrences deleted, and then drop every empty body. S → aSb gains S → ab. S → A would become empty, which is dropped. Because S₀ is nullable, add back S₀ → ε, the one ε-rule CNF allows:

```text
S₀ → S | ε
S → aSb | ab | A
A → c
```

**Why the language is unchanged** (apart from ε, which S₀ → ε restores): take a parse tree for a non-empty string and cut off every subtree whose yield is ε. Each node that lost children now uses one of the shortened versions of its rule. Conversely, each shortened rule can be imitated by the original rule plus derivations of ε from the deleted symbols.

**3. Remove unit rules.** A **unit rule** has a single variable as its body, like S → A. For each variable A, find every variable B reachable from A through unit rules alone (a graph search, starting from A itself). Then give A every non-unit body of every such B, and delete the unit rules. Here S reaches A, so S gets S → c, and S₀ reaches S and A:

```text
S₀ → aSb | ab | c | ε
S → aSb | ab | c
A → c
```

A chain of unit nodes in a parse tree collapses into one node, which is why the language stays the same. The order of steps 2 and 3 matters: deleting ε can **create** unit rules (S → AB with B nullable gives S → A), while deleting unit rules never creates ε-rules.

**4. Terminals in long bodies.** In every body of length at least 2, replace each terminal a by a new variable T_a, with the rule T_a → a:

```text
S₀ → T_a S T_b | T_a T_b | c | ε
S → T_a S T_b | T_a T_b | c
T_a → a, T_b → b
```

**5. Split long bodies.** Replace A → X₁X₂…Xₖ, for k ≥ 3, by a chain of k − 1 rules through k − 2 new variables: A → X₁B₁, B₁ → X₂B₂, …, Bₖ₋₂ → Xₖ₋₁Xₖ. Here S₀ → T_a S T_b becomes S₀ → T_a B₁ with B₁ → S T_b, and the same for S with a new B₂. Now every rule has a CNF shape. (A → c no longer matters: no rule reaches A, and a variable nothing reaches does no harm.)

Every new variable needs a **fresh** name, one that the grammar does not already use. A grammar may already contain a variable called `T0` or `B1`, so check before you pick.

**What the conversion costs.** Steps 1, 3, 4 and 5 keep the grammar within a polynomial of its size. Step 2 can explode: a body with k nullable symbols has 2ᵏ shortened versions. The fix is to do step 5 before step 2, so that no body is longer than 2 when ε-rules are removed; then the CNF grammar has size at most proportional to the square of the original. For the small grammars in this course, the order above is fine.

### The CYK table

Take a CNF grammar and a string w = w₀w₁…wₙ₋₁. For every piece w[i:j] (the symbols at positions i up to but not including j, with i < j), let T[i, j] be the set of variables that derive that piece.

- **Pieces of length 1.** T[i, i + 1] = {A : A → wᵢ is a rule}.
- **Longer pieces.** A variable A derives w[i:j] exactly when there is a rule A → BC and a split point k with i < k < j such that B ∈ T[i, k] and C ∈ T[k, j].
- **The answer.** w ∈ L(G) exactly when S ∈ T[0, n]. The empty string has no cell: it is in L(G) exactly when S → ε is a rule.

Each cell uses only cells for shorter pieces, so fill the table **by increasing length**: all pieces of length 1, then 2, and so on up to n.

**Why it is correct**, by induction on the length of the piece. For length 1, a CNF tree for one symbol is a single node A → a. For length at least 2, the root of a CNF tree must use a rule A → BC. Neither B nor C can derive ε, because only the start variable has an ε-rule and it never appears on a right side. So B derives a non-empty first part w[i:k] and C the non-empty rest w[k:j]. Those are shorter, so by induction B ∈ T[i, k] and C ∈ T[k, j], and trying every k finds this split. Conversely, anything the rule puts into a cell really derives that piece.

### The table on an example

Take the CNF grammar

```text
S → AB | BC
A → BA | a
B → CC | b
C → AB | a
```

and the string `baaba`. Row L lists the cells for pieces of length L, from left to right:

| L | pieces and their cells |
|---|---|
| 1 | b: {B}, a: {A, C}, a: {A, C}, b: {B}, a: {A, C} |
| 2 | ba: {S, A}, aa: {B}, ab: {S, C}, ba: {S, A} |
| 3 | baa: ∅, aab: {B}, aba: {B} |
| 4 | baab: ∅, aaba: {S, A, C} |
| 5 | baaba: {S, A, C} |

For instance `aa` gets B because B → CC and each `a` has C in its cell. `baa` stays empty: the split b|aa gives the pairs (B, B), and ba|a gives (S, A), (S, C), (A, A) and (A, C), and no rule has any of those bodies. The top cell holds S, so `baaba` is in the language.

Here is the first row in Python, for a grammar in this course's dictionary format:

```python
G = {"variables": {"S", "A", "B", "C"}, "terminals": "ab",
     "rules": {"S": [("A", "B"), ("B", "C")], "A": [("B", "A"), ("a",)],
               "B": [("C", "C"), ("b",)], "C": [("A", "B"), ("a",)]},
     "start": "S"}
w = "baaba"
row = [{A for A, bodies in G["rules"].items() if (c,) in bodies} for c in w]
print([sorted(cell) for cell in row])
# [['B'], ['A', 'C'], ['A', 'C'], ['B'], ['A', 'C']]
```

### What it costs

There are about n²/2 cells, each tries fewer than n split points, and each split checks the grammar's binary rules. So CYK takes **O(n³ · |R|)** time and O(n²) space, where |R| is the number of rules. For a string of 100 symbols that is about 170,000 split points times the number of rules: well under a second in Python, where listing strings would never finish.

Keeping a **back pointer** in each cell (which rule and which split put A there) lets you rebuild a parse tree from the top cell down. Replacing the sets by counts gives the number of parse trees of every piece, and replacing them by probabilities gives the most likely tree. You will do the first two in the practice.

### Where it is used

- **Natural-language processing:** probabilistic grammars and the CKY algorithm (the same thing, spelled differently) were for years the standard way to parse sentences, picking the most likely of many trees with **[[probabilities|pcfg]]** on the rules.
- **Biology:** RNA structure prediction fills a table over the pieces of a strand, exactly like CYK, in O(n³) time.
- **Theory:** CYK shows that membership for every context-free language is decidable in polynomial time, which you will need in lessons 13 to 15. **[[Faster methods|valiant]]** exist, but none is known to run in time near n².
- **Compilers** use parsers that run in linear time, such as **[[LR parsers|lr-parsers]]**, on grammars restricted so that no guessing is needed. You will write one kind in the Compilers course. CYK is the general tool for when the grammar cannot be restricted.

**Watch out:**

- **The fill order.** A cell for a piece of length L reads cells for shorter pieces. Filling by start position instead of by length reads cells that have not been computed yet.
- **The split range.** With half-open pieces w[i:j], the split point k runs over i + 1, …, j − 1, so that both parts are non-empty.
- **The empty string.** The table has no cell for it; look for the start variable's ε-rule instead.
- **Conversion order.** Remove ε-rules before unit rules, keep the new start variable off every right side, and remember that the start variable may be nullable.

::: context cyk-names Who CYK is
The algorithm is named after John Cocke, Daniel Younger and Tadao Kasami, who found it independently in the 1960s: Kasami in a 1965 report, Younger in a 1967 paper that analysed its n³ running time, and Cocke in work on parsing that was not published separately. Books spell it CYK or CKY. Noam Chomsky described the normal form in 1959, which is why it carries his name.
:::

::: context dp-again Dynamic programming, again
In DSA II you solved the longest common subsequence and matrix-chain order with tables: answer every subproblem once, smallest first, and build bigger answers from smaller ones. CYK is interval dynamic programming, the same pattern as matrix-chain order: the subproblems are the pieces w[i:j] of the input, and a piece is solved by trying every split point. The recurrence is correct because of Chomsky normal form, which guarantees that every tree for a piece of length at least 2 splits it into two shorter non-empty pieces.
:::

::: context pcfg Grammars with probabilities
A probabilistic context-free grammar gives each rule a probability, so that the rules for each variable add up to 1. The probability of a tree is the product of the probabilities of its rules. CKY with the sets replaced by "the best probability for this variable on this piece" finds the most likely tree in O(n³) time. Such grammars were learned from large collections of hand-parsed sentences, such as the Penn Treebank. Neural parsers have since taken over most of this work, but many still output trees and use CKY-style search.
:::

::: context valiant Parsing as matrix multiplication
In 1975 Leslie Valiant showed that context-free recognition can be done as fast as multiplying Boolean matrices, which today's best methods do in about n²·³⁷ steps. Those methods are too complicated to be faster in practice. In the other direction, it was shown in 2002 that a much faster general parser would also give faster matrix multiplication, so a simple general algorithm running in near n² time is unlikely to exist.
:::

::: context lr-parsers Parsers that never guess
An LR parser reads the input once, left to right, keeping a stack of grammar symbols, and at each step a table tells it exactly what to do: push the next symbol, or replace the top few symbols by the variable they came from. There is never a choice, so it runs in linear time. It only works for grammars in which a fixed amount of lookahead always settles the choice, which is enough for most programming languages. Tools such as yacc and Bison generate these tables, and lesson 9 shows that such a parser is a deterministic pushdown automaton.
:::
--- task
Grammars are dictionaries as in the last lesson. Write two functions.

- `to_cnf(G)`: return a grammar in Chomsky normal form with the same language and terminals as `G`, using the five steps of the lesson. Every rule of the result is `A → BC` (two variables, neither of them the start variable), `A → a` (one terminal), or the start variable's `()`. Every variable of the result is a key of its `"rules"`, and its `"variables"` set holds exactly those keys. Choose fresh names for new variables: `G` may already use names such as `"S0"` or `"T1"`. Do not change `G`.
- `cyk(G, w)`: `G` is in Chomsky normal form. Return `True` when the string `w` is in L(G) and `False` otherwise, using the CYK table. It must handle a string of about 100 symbols quickly, and the empty string.
--- starter
def to_cnf(G):
    return G


def cyk(G, w):
    return False
--- solution
from itertools import product


def fresh_name(taken, base):
    k = 0
    while f"{base}{k}" in taken:
        k += 1
    taken.add(f"{base}{k}")
    return f"{base}{k}"


def to_cnf(G):
    taken = set(G["rules"])
    rules = {A: {tuple(b) for b in bodies} for A, bodies in G["rules"].items()}

    # 1. A new start variable.
    start = fresh_name(taken, "S")
    rules[start] = {(G["start"],)}

    # 2. Remove ε-rules.
    nullable = set()
    changed = True
    while changed:
        changed = False
        for A, bodies in rules.items():
            if A not in nullable and any(all(X in nullable for X in b) for b in bodies):
                nullable.add(A)
                changed = True
    for A in rules:
        shorter = set()
        for b in rules[A]:
            options = [((X,), ()) if X in nullable else ((X,),) for X in b]
            for pick in product(*options):
                shorter.add(sum(pick, ()))
        shorter.discard(())
        rules[A] = shorter
    if start in nullable:
        rules[start].add(())

    # 3. Remove unit rules.
    def is_unit(b):
        return len(b) == 1 and b[0] in rules

    no_units = {}
    for A in rules:
        reach = {A}
        stack = [A]
        while stack:
            for b in rules[stack.pop()]:
                if is_unit(b) and b[0] not in reach:
                    reach.add(b[0])
                    stack.append(b[0])
        no_units[A] = {b for B in reach for b in rules[B] if not is_unit(b)}

    # 4. Terminals in long bodies, and 5. split long bodies.
    final = {A: set() for A in no_units}
    for_terminal = {}
    for A, bodies in no_units.items():
        for b in bodies:
            if len(b) <= 1:
                final[A].add(b)
                continue
            symbols = []
            for X in b:
                if X not in no_units:
                    if X not in for_terminal:
                        for_terminal[X] = fresh_name(taken, "T")
                        final[for_terminal[X]] = {(X,)}
                    X = for_terminal[X]
                symbols.append(X)
            head = A
            while len(symbols) > 2:
                rest = fresh_name(taken, "B")
                final[rest] = set()
                final[head].add((symbols[0], rest))
                head = rest
                symbols = symbols[1:]
            final[head].add(tuple(symbols))

    rules = {A: list(bodies) for A, bodies in final.items()}
    return {"variables": set(rules), "terminals": G["terminals"], "rules": rules, "start": start}


def cyk(G, w):
    n = len(w)
    if n == 0:
        return () in G["rules"][G["start"]]
    pairs = [(A, b[0], b[1]) for A, bodies in G["rules"].items() for b in bodies if len(b) == 2]
    table = {}
    for i, c in enumerate(w):
        table[(i, i + 1)] = {A for A, bodies in G["rules"].items() if (c,) in bodies}
    for length in range(2, n + 1):
        for i in range(n - length + 1):
            j = i + length
            cell = set()
            for k in range(i + 1, j):
                left, right = table[(i, k)], table[(k, j)]
                if left and right:
                    for A, B, C in pairs:
                        if B in left and C in right:
                            cell.add(A)
            table[(i, j)] = cell
    return G["start"] in table[(0, n)]
--- hint
Start `cyk` with the empty string (look for `()` among the start variable's bodies), then the row of length-1 cells as in the lesson's example. Fill the longer cells in a loop over the length first, then the start position `i`, then the split point `k` from `i + 1` to `j - 1`.
--- hint
For `to_cnf`, copy the rules into a dictionary of sets of tuples, so that you never change `G` and duplicates vanish. Write a `fresh_name(taken, base)` helper that tries `base + "0"`, `base + "1"`, … until it finds a name not in `taken`. Step 2 finds the nullable set with rounds, then uses `itertools.product` over "keep or drop" choices for each nullable symbol of each body.
--- hint
Step 3: for each variable, search along unit rules to find every variable it reaches, and give it all their non-unit bodies; build the result in a new dictionary so you do not read half-updated rules. Steps 4 and 5 go body by body: bodies of length 0 or 1 stay; in longer ones, swap each terminal for its `T` variable, then peel off the first symbol into a new `B` variable while more than two symbols remain.

+++ practice | Is this grammar in Chomsky normal form?
--- task
Write `is_cnf(G)` that returns `True` when the grammar `G` (a dictionary as in the lesson) is in Chomsky normal form, and `False` otherwise. Every body must be one of:

- two symbols that are both variables (keys of `G["rules"]`), neither of them the start variable;
- one symbol that is a terminal (a character of `G["terminals"]`);
- the empty body `()`, but only in the start variable's rules.

So a unit rule `A → B`, a body with a terminal and a variable, a body of three variables, an ε-rule for a variable that is not the start, and a start variable that appears in a body all make it `False`. A grammar with no rules at all for some variable can still be in CNF.
--- starter
def is_cnf(G):
    return True
--- solution
def is_cnf(G):
    variables = G["rules"]
    for A, bodies in G["rules"].items():
        for body in bodies:
            if len(body) == 0:
                if A != G["start"]:
                    return False
            elif len(body) == 1:
                if body[0] not in G["terminals"] or body[0] in variables:
                    return False
            elif len(body) == 2:
                if not all(X in variables and X != G["start"] for X in body):
                    return False
            else:
                return False
    return True
--- hint
Go through every body of every variable, and sort it by its length: 0, 1, 2, or more.
--- hint
Length 0 is fine only for the start variable. Length 1 must be a terminal, not a variable. Length 2 must be two variables, and neither may be the start variable. Anything longer is never allowed.
--- check case | The lesson's CNF grammar is in CNF
is_cnf({"variables": {"S", "A", "B", "C"}, "terminals": "ab", "rules": {"S": [("A", "B"), ("B", "C")], "A": [("B", "A"), ("a",)], "B": [("C", "C"), ("b",)], "C": [("A", "B"), ("a",)]}, "start": "S"})
=> True
--- check case | A unit rule, a mixed body, and a body of three variables

=> [True, False, False]

+++ practice | Rebuild the parse tree
--- task
CYK says yes or no. Extend it to give the tree. Write `cyk_tree(G, w)`: `G` is a grammar in Chomsky normal form (as in the lesson). Return a parse tree of `w` with the start variable at the root, as a tuple like in the last lesson (the label, then the children), or `None` if `w` is not in L(G). For the empty string, the tree is `(start,)` when the start variable has the rule `()`.

In each cell, store for each variable one way it got there: the terminal, or the rule's two variables and the split point. Then build the tree from the top cell down. Any correct tree is accepted.
--- starter
def cyk_tree(G, w):
    return None
--- solution
def cyk_tree(G, w):
    n = len(w)
    start = G["start"]
    if n == 0:
        return (start,) if () in G["rules"][start] else None
    pairs = [(A, b[0], b[1]) for A, bodies in G["rules"].items() for b in bodies if len(b) == 2]
    back = {}
    for i, c in enumerate(w):
        back[(i, i + 1)] = {A: c for A, bodies in G["rules"].items() if (c,) in bodies}
    for length in range(2, n + 1):
        for i in range(n - length + 1):
            j = i + length
            cell = {}
            for k in range(i + 1, j):
                left, right = back[(i, k)], back[(k, j)]
                for A, B, C in pairs:
                    if A not in cell and B in left and C in right:
                        cell[A] = (k, B, C)
            back[(i, j)] = cell

    def build(A, i, j):
        how = back[(i, j)][A]
        if j == i + 1:
            return (A, how)
        k, B, C = how
        return (A, build(B, i, k), build(C, k, j))

    if start not in back[(0, n)]:
        return None
    return build(start, 0, n)
--- hint
Make each cell a dictionary instead of a set: for a piece of length 1, map A to the terminal; for a longer piece, map A to `(k, B, C)`, the first split and rule you found for it.
--- hint
A recursive `build(A, i, j)` looks up how A got into cell `(i, j)`. For length 1 it returns `(A, terminal)`; otherwise `(A, build(B, i, k), build(C, k, j))`.
--- check test | The lesson's grammar on baaba: a real parse tree with the right yield
(lambda G, TREEOK, Y: (lambda t: t is not None and TREEOK(G, t) and Y(t) == "baaba")(cyk_tree(G, "baaba")))({"variables": {"S", "A", "B", "C"}, "terminals": "ab", "rules": {"S": [("A", "B"), ("B", "C")], "A": [("B", "A"), ("a",)], "B": [("C", "C"), ("b",)], "C": [("A", "B"), ("a",)]}, "start": "S"}, (lambda G, tree: (lambda f: isinstance(tree, tuple) and len(tree) >= 1 and tree[0] == G["start"] and f(f, tree))(lambda f, t: t[0] in G["rules"] and tuple(c[0] if isinstance(c, tuple) else c for c in t[1:]) in [tuple(b) for b in G["rules"][t[0]]] and all(f(f, c) if isinstance(c, tuple) else (isinstance(c, str) and c in G["terminals"] and len(c) == 1) for c in t[1:]))), (lambda tree: (lambda f: f(f, tree))(lambda f, t: t if isinstance(t, str) else "".join(f(f, c) for c in t[1:]))))
--- check case | Strings not in the language give None
(lambda G: [cyk_tree(G, w) for w in ["baa", "aab", "", "bb"]])({"variables": {"S", "A", "B", "C"}, "terminals": "ab", "rules": {"S": [("A", "B"), ("B", "C")], "A": [("B", "A"), ("a",)], "B": [("C", "C"), ("b",)], "C": [("A", "B"), ("a",)]}, "start": "S"})
=> [None, None, None, None]
--- check case | The empty string, with and without the start variable's ε-rule
(cyk_tree({"variables": {"S", "A"}, "terminals": "a", "rules": {"S": [("A", "A"), ()], "A": [("a",)]}, "start": "S"}, ""), cyk_tree({"variables": {"S", "A"}, "terminals": "a", "rules": {"S": [("A", "A")], "A": [("a",)]}, "start": "S"}, ""))

+++ practice | Counting parse trees with CYK
--- task
Replace the sets in the CYK table by counts, and you count parse trees. Write `cyk_count(G, w)`: `G` is a grammar in Chomsky normal form. Return the number of different parse trees of `w` with the start variable at the root; 0 when `w` is not in L(G). For the empty string it is 1 when the start variable has the rule `()`, and 0 otherwise.

In each cell keep a dictionary from variable to the number of trees for that piece. A pair of trees for the two parts, under a rule A → BC at a split k, gives one tree for A; so add count(B, left part) × count(C, right part) over every rule and every split.

The checks use a CNF grammar for the ambiguous expressions `E → E+E | E*E | (E) | a`, where `a+a+…+a` with k plus signs has the k-th Catalan number of trees: more than six billion for k = 20, far too many to list.
--- starter
def cyk_count(G, w):
    return 0
--- solution
def cyk_count(G, w):
    n = len(w)
    if n == 0:
        return 1 if () in G["rules"][G["start"]] else 0
    pairs = [(A, b[0], b[1]) for A, bodies in G["rules"].items() for b in bodies if len(b) == 2]
    count = {}
    for i, c in enumerate(w):
        count[(i, i + 1)] = {A: 1 for A, bodies in G["rules"].items() if (c,) in bodies}
    for length in range(2, n + 1):
        for i in range(n - length + 1):
            j = i + length
            cell = {}
            for k in range(i + 1, j):
                left, right = count[(i, k)], count[(k, j)]
                for A, B, C in pairs:
                    if B in left and C in right:
                        cell[A] = cell.get(A, 0) + left[B] * right[C]
            count[(i, j)] = cell
    return count[(0, n)].get(G["start"], 0)
--- hint
A length-1 cell maps each variable with the rule `A → w[i]` to 1. A longer cell starts as an empty dictionary and, for each split and each rule `A → BC` whose B and C appear in the two part cells, adds the product of their counts to A's entry.
--- hint
Rules are not repeated in a grammar, so each (rule, split) pair gives different trees. Return the start variable's count in the top cell, or 0 if it is missing.
--- check case | a+a*a has 2 trees, a+a+a+a has 5, and a+ has none

=> (2, 5, 0)

=> 1

+++ practice | Removing ε-rules
--- task
Step 2 of the conversion on its own. Write two functions for grammars as in the lesson:

- `nullable(G)`: the set of variables that can derive ε.
- `remove_epsilon(G)`: a grammar for L(G) with ε removed (that is, L(G) − {ε}) that has **no** ε-rules at all, not even for the start variable. Keep the same variables, start and terminals. For each rule, add every version with some nullable occurrences deleted, and drop the empty bodies. A variable left with no bodies keeps an empty list. Do not change `G`.

Edge cases: a variable can be nullable through a chain of other variables; the same nullable variable can appear twice in one body, and each occurrence may be kept or dropped on its own (A → BB gives A → BB and A → B); and bodies that shrink to the same thing should not be repeated.
--- starter
def nullable(G):
    return {A for A, bodies in G["rules"].items() if () in bodies}


def remove_epsilon(G):
    return G
--- solution
from itertools import product


def nullable(G):
    found = set()
    changed = True
    while changed:
        changed = False
        for A, bodies in G["rules"].items():
            if A not in found and any(all(X in found for X in body) for body in bodies):
                found.add(A)
                changed = True
    return found


def remove_epsilon(G):
    null = nullable(G)
    rules = {}
    for A, bodies in G["rules"].items():
        shorter = []
        for body in bodies:
            options = [((X,), ()) if X in null else ((X,),) for X in body]
            for pick in product(*options):
                b = sum(pick, ())
                if b and b not in shorter:
                    shorter.append(b)
        rules[A] = shorter
    return {"variables": set(G["variables"]), "terminals": G["terminals"], "rules": rules, "start": G["start"]}
--- hint
`nullable` is a least fixed point: repeat rounds, adding A when one of its bodies has every symbol already nullable (the empty body qualifies at once, and a terminal never is nullable). Stop when a round adds nothing.
--- hint
For one body, build a list of choices per symbol: a nullable symbol offers "keep it" or "drop it", anything else only "keep it". `itertools.product(*choices)` then gives every version; join each into one tuple and skip the empty one and repeats.
--- check case | nullable through a chain: C → B, B → ε, and S → AC with A → a | ε
nullable({"variables": {"S", "A", "B", "C", "D"}, "terminals": "ad", "rules": {"S": [("A", "C")], "A": [("a",), ()], "B": [()], "C": [("B",), ("B", "C")], "D": [("d",), ("D", "A")]}, "start": "S"})
=> {"S", "A", "B", "C"}
--- check case | A body with the same nullable variable twice: A → BB gives BB and B
(lambda G: (lambda R: sorted(R["rules"]["A"]))(remove_epsilon(G)))({"variables": {"A", "B"}, "terminals": "b", "rules": {"A": [("B", "B")], "B": [("b",), ()]}, "start": "A"})
=> [("B",), ("B", "B")]
--- check test | No ε-rules at all, and no repeated bodies
(lambda R: all(len(b) > 0 for bs in R["rules"].values() for b in bs) and all(len(bs) == len(set(map(tuple, bs))) for bs in R["rules"].values()))(remove_epsilon({"variables": {"S", "A", "B"}, "terminals": "ab", "rules": {"S": [("A", "B", "A"), ("a",)], "A": [("B",), ()], "B": [("b",), ("A",), ()]}, "start": "S"}))
--- check test | The language loses only ε: brackets, the lesson's grammar and a chain
(lambda GEN: all(GEN(remove_epsilon(G), 8) == GEN(G, 8) - {""} for G in [{"variables": {"S"}, "terminals": "()", "rules": {"S": [("S", "S"), ("(", "S", ")"), ()]}, "start": "S"}, {"variables": {"S", "A"}, "terminals": "abc", "rules": {"S": [("a", "S", "b"), ("A",)], "A": [("c",), ()]}, "start": "S"}, {"variables": {"S", "A", "B", "C", "D"}, "terminals": "ad", "rules": {"S": [("A", "C", "D")], "A": [("a",), ()], "B": [()], "C": [("B",), ("B", "C")], "D": [("d",), ("D", "A")]}, "start": "S"}]))((lambda G, n, red=__import__("functools").reduce: (lambda step: (lambda f: f(f, {A: frozenset() for A in G["rules"]}))(lambda f, g: (lambda h: set(g[G["start"]]) if h == g else f(f, h))(step(g))))(lambda g: {A: frozenset(w for b in G["rules"][A] for w in red(lambda S, X: {u + v for u in S for v in (g[X] if X in G["rules"] else {X}) if len(u + v) <= n}, b, {""})) for A in G["rules"]})))
--- check test | A variable whose only rule was ε keeps an empty list; the input is unchanged
(lambda G: (lambda R: R["rules"]["B"] == [] and sorted(R["rules"]["S"]) == [("a",), ("a", "B")] and R["start"] == "S" and G["rules"] == {"S": [("a", "B")], "B": [()]})(remove_epsilon(G)))({"variables": {"S", "B"}, "terminals": "a", "rules": {"S": [("a", "B")], "B": [()]}, "start": "S"})
--- check test | 40 seeded random grammars: the language up to length 6 loses only ε

+++ practice | Debug: the table filled in the wrong order
--- task
This `cyk(G, w)` fills the table by start position `i`, and for each `i` by end position `j`. But the cell for `(i, j)` needs the cells `(k, j)` for every split `k > i`, and those belong to later start positions, which have not been filled yet. Because it reads missing cells as empty, it quietly gives wrong answers: for the grammar of aⁿbⁿ in CNF, it rejects `"aabb"`. Fix it so that every cell is computed after all the cells it reads.
--- starter
def cyk(G, w):
    n = len(w)
    if n == 0:
        return () in G["rules"][G["start"]]
    pairs = [(A, b[0], b[1]) for A, bodies in G["rules"].items() for b in bodies if len(b) == 2]
    table = {}
    for i, c in enumerate(w):
        table[(i, i + 1)] = {A for A, bodies in G["rules"].items() if (c,) in bodies}
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 2, n + 1):
            cell = set()
            for k in range(i + 1, j):
                left, right = table.get((i, k), set()), table.get((k, j), set())
                for A, B, C in pairs:
                    if B in left and C in right:
                        cell.add(A)
            table[(i, j)] = cell
    return G["start"] in table[(0, n)]
--- solution
def cyk(G, w):
    n = len(w)
    if n == 0:
        return () in G["rules"][G["start"]]
    pairs = [(A, b[0], b[1]) for A, bodies in G["rules"].items() for b in bodies if len(b) == 2]
    table = {}
    for i, c in enumerate(w):
        table[(i, i + 1)] = {A for A, bodies in G["rules"].items() if (c,) in bodies}
    for length in range(2, n + 1):
        for i in range(n - length + 1):
            j = i + length
            cell = set()
            for k in range(i + 1, j):
                left, right = table[(i, k)], table[(k, j)]
                for A, B, C in pairs:
                    if B in left and C in right:
                        cell.add(A)
            table[(i, j)] = cell
    return G["start"] in table[(0, n)]
--- hint
Which cells does `(i, j)` read? `(i, k)` and `(k, j)`: both are shorter pieces than `(i, j)`. So what should the outer loop go over?
--- hint
Loop over the length of the piece first (2 up to n), then over the start position, with `j = i + length`. (Filling the start positions from `n - 1` down to 0 would also work.)
--- check case | aⁿbⁿ in CNF: aabb and aaabbb are accepted, abab is not

=> [True, True, True, False, False]
--- check case | The lesson's grammar on baaba
cyk({"variables": {"S", "A", "B", "C"}, "terminals": "ab", "rules": {"S": [("A", "B"), ("B", "C")], "A": [("B", "A"), ("a",)], "B": [("C", "C"), ("b",)], "C": [("A", "B"), ("a",)]}, "start": "S"}, "baaba")
=> True
--- check test | 30 seeded random CNF grammars agree with the definition on every string up to length 7
(lambda R, GEN, P: all((lambda G: (lambda L: all(cyk(G, w) == (w in L) for k in range(8) for w in ("".join(t) for t in P("ab", repeat=k))))(GEN(G, 7)))({"variables": set("SABC"), "terminals": "ab", "rules": {V: [tuple(R.choice("ABC") for _ in range(2)) for _ in range(R.randint(1, 3))] + [(c,) for c in "ab" if R.random() < 0.5] + ([()] if V == "S" and R.random() < 0.3 else []) for V in "SABC"}, "start": "S"}) for _ in range(30)))(__import__("random").Random(19), (lambda G, n, red=__import__("functools").reduce: (lambda step: (lambda f: f(f, {A: frozenset() for A in G["rules"]}))(lambda f, g: (lambda h: set(g[G["start"]]) if h == g else f(f, h))(step(g))))(lambda g: {A: frozenset(w for b in G["rules"][A] for w in red(lambda S, X: {u + v for u in S for v in (g[X] if X in G["rules"] else {X}) if len(u + v) <= n}, b, {""})) for A in G["rules"]})), __import__("itertools").product)

+++ practice | Every complete frame in a telemetry stream
--- task
A telemetry stream is a string over `"[]x"`: `x` is a reading, and `[` … `]` wrap a frame, which holds readings and nested frames. A frame is described by a grammar in Chomsky normal form. Write `valid_segments(G, w)` that returns the sorted list of every pair `(i, j)` with `i < j` such that the piece `w[i:j]` is in L(G). Pieces of length 0 are never included.

The stream in the checks has 120 symbols. Running CYK separately on each of its 7,260 pieces takes far too long for the time limit. Notice that **one** CYK table on `w` already holds a cell for every piece, so build it once, in O(n³) time, and read the answer off it.
--- starter
def valid_segments(G, w):
    return []
--- solution
def valid_segments(G, w):
    n = len(w)
    pairs = [(A, b[0], b[1]) for A, bodies in G["rules"].items() for b in bodies if len(b) == 2]
    table = {}
    for i, c in enumerate(w):
        table[(i, i + 1)] = {A for A, bodies in G["rules"].items() if (c,) in bodies}
    for length in range(2, n + 1):
        for i in range(n - length + 1):
            j = i + length
            cell = set()
            for k in range(i + 1, j):
                left, right = table[(i, k)], table[(k, j)]
                if left and right:
                    for A, B, C in pairs:
                        if B in left and C in right:
                            cell.add(A)
            table[(i, j)] = cell
    return sorted(piece for piece, cell in table.items() if G["start"] in cell)
--- hint
The cell `(i, j)` of the CYK table holds the variables that derive `w[i:j]`, for every `i < j`, not only for the whole string.
--- hint
Fill the whole table once as in the lesson, then collect every `(i, j)` whose cell contains the start variable, and sort them.
--- check case | A short stream
valid_segments({"variables": {"F", "X", "L", "O", "C"}, "terminals": "[]x", "rules": {"F": [("O", "X")], "X": [("L", "C"), ("]",)], "L": [("x",), ("O", "X"), ("L", "L")], "O": [("[",)], "C": [("]",)]}, "start": "F"}, "x[x[]]]")
=> [(1, 6), (3, 5)]
--- check case | An empty frame, and a stream with no complete frame

=> ([(0, 2)], [], [])

(lambda R, G: (lambda w: valid_segments(G, w) == sorted((i, j) for i in range(len(w)) for j in range(i + 2, len(w) + 1) if w[i] == "[" and w[j - 1] == "]" and all(w[i:k].count("[") > w[i:k].count("]") for k in range(i + 1, j)) and w[i:j].count("[") == w[i:j].count("]")))((lambda f: "".join(f(f, 3) for _ in range(12))[:120])(lambda f, d: "[" + "x" * R.randint(0, 2) + "]" if d == 0 or R.random() < 0.3 else "[" + "".join(f(f, d - 1) if R.random() < 0.6 else "x" for _ in range(R.randint(1, 3))) + "]")))(__import__("random").Random(1), {"variables": {"F", "X", "L", "O", "C"}, "terminals": "[]x", "rules": {"F": [("O", "X")], "X": [("L", "C"), ("]",)], "L": [("x",), ("O", "X"), ("L", "L")], "O": [("[",)], "C": [("]",)]}, "start": "F"})
?? Build one table for the whole stream; every cell (i, j) answers the question for w[i:j].

+++ question | How many steps
--- ask
A grammar is in Chomsky normal form, and a string of length 10 is in its language. How many steps does every derivation of that string take?
--- answer
19
--- why
Each symbol comes from one rule A → a, which is 10 steps. The rest of the tree is binary with 10 leaves, so it has 10 − 1 = 9 nodes of the form A → BC. That is 10 + 9 = 19 steps, and in general 2n − 1 for a string of length n ≥ 1.

+++ question | Why ε-rules go before unit rules
--- ask
In the conversion to Chomsky normal form, why are ε-rules removed **before** unit rules?
--- choice correct
Removing ε-rules can create new unit rules, while removing unit rules never creates ε-rules.
--- choice
Unit rules can only be found once the grammar has no ε-rules.
--- choice
Removing unit rules first would change the language.
--- choice
The order does not matter; it is only a convention.
--- why
S → AB with B nullable gains the shortened rule S → A, a new unit rule. If unit rules were already gone, it would survive into the result, which would not be in CNF. Removing unit rules copies existing non-unit bodies, so it never makes an empty one. Either order keeps the language; only this one reaches the normal form.

+++ question | What CYK costs
--- ask
For a fixed grammar in Chomsky normal form, how does the running time of CYK grow with the length n of the string?
--- choice
O(n)
--- choice
O(n²)
--- choice correct
O(n³)
--- choice
O(2ⁿ)
--- why
There are about n²/2 cells, one for each piece w[i:j], and each cell tries up to n − 1 split points, each checking the grammar's binary rules, which is a constant when the grammar is fixed. That is O(n³). The space is O(n²) for the table.

=== theory-09 | Pushdown automata
--- teach
Grammars **generate** strings. For regular languages you had both sides: expressions that generate and DFAs that recognise. This lesson builds the recognising machine for context-free languages. Lesson 6 showed that a finite memory cannot check aⁿbⁿ. So give the finite automaton one extra thing: a **stack**, a memory of any size that it may only use at the top. The result is the **pushdown automaton**, or **PDA**. The lesson's main theorem is that PDAs recognise exactly the context-free languages. You will implement a PDA simulator and the half of the proof that turns any grammar into a PDA.

### A stack of plates

Picture the spring-loaded plate dispenser in a canteen. You can put a plate on top, or take the top plate off, and you can see only the top one. That is a **stack**: last in, first out, as in the stacks lesson of DSA I. Checking brackets needs nothing more: push a plate for every `(`, take one off for every `)`, and at the end the pile must be empty. The stack can grow as tall as the input needs, which is the unbounded counting a DFA lacks.

### The definition

A **pushdown automaton** is a 6-tuple P = (Q, Σ, Γ, δ, q₀, F):

- Q is a finite set of states, q₀ ∈ Q is the start state and F ⊆ Q the accepting states, as in a DFA;
- Σ is the input alphabet;
- Γ (the Greek capital gamma) is the **stack alphabet**, the symbols that can go on the stack;
- δ is the transition function. Each transition has the form **(q, a, X) → (r, γ)**: in state q, read the input symbol a, or read nothing when a = ε; pop the top stack symbol X, or leave the stack alone when X = ε; then move to state r and push the string γ (gamma), whose **first symbol ends up on top**.

Like an NFA, a PDA is **nondeterministic**: δ(q, a, X) is a set of choices, and several transitions may apply at once. (Some books let each transition push only one symbol. Pushing a string is shorthand: a chain of single pushes through extra states does the same.)

A **configuration** records everything about a moment of a run: the state, how much of the input has been read, and the stack, written top first. The run starts in the configuration (q₀, 0, empty stack). **P accepts w** when some sequence of transitions leads from the start configuration to a configuration in which **all of w has been read** and the state is in F. What is left on the stack does not matter.

### PDAs in Python

This course writes a PDA as a dictionary. `"delta"` maps a key `(q, a, X)` to a set of pairs `(r, push)`, where `a` or `X` may be `""` for ε and `push` is a tuple of stack symbols, top first. A stack is a tuple, top first as well. Here is a PDA for aⁿbⁿ. A PDA cannot ask "is the stack empty?", so it first pushes a **bottom marker**, here `⊥`, and later recognises the bottom by seeing it on top:

```python
anbn_pda = {
    "states": {"s", "a", "b", "f"},
    "alphabet": "ab",
    "stack": {"A", "⊥"},
    "delta": {
        ("s", "", ""): {("a", ("⊥",))},    # push the bottom marker
        ("a", "a", ""): {("a", ("A",))},   # each a: push an A
        ("a", "", ""): {("b", ())},        # guess that the a's are over
        ("b", "b", "A"): {("b", ())},      # each b: pop an A
        ("b", "", "⊥"): {("f", ())},       # the bottom is back on top: accept
    },
    "start": "s",
    "accept": {"f"},
}
```

A run on `aabb`, one configuration per line:

| state | read so far | stack (top first) | move |
|---|---|---|---|
| s | (none) | (empty) | push ⊥ |
| a | (none) | ⊥ | read a, push A |
| a | a | A ⊥ | read a, push A |
| a | aa | A A ⊥ | move to b, reading nothing |
| b | aa | A A ⊥ | read b, pop A |
| b | aab | A ⊥ | read b, pop A |
| b | aabb | ⊥ | pop ⊥ |
| f | aabb | (empty) | all read, f accepts |

On `aab` every branch fails: after the last `b` an `A` is still on top, so ⊥ cannot be popped. On `abb` a branch reaches f after reading only `ab`, but the `b` that is left can never be read, so that branch does not count.

Applying one transition to a stack is a slice and a join:

```python
stack = ("A", "A", "⊥")
X, push = "A", ()          # pop an A, push nothing
if stack[:1] == (X,):      # is X on top?
    stack = push + stack[1:]
print(stack)               # ('A', '⊥')
```

### Simulating a nondeterministic machine

As with NFAs, a program cannot guess; it follows every branch. Here the state of a branch is a whole configuration, so the simulation is a **[[breadth-first search|bfs-again]]** over configurations: start from the start configuration, and from each configuration generate every configuration one transition away. Accept as soon as you find one with all input read and an accepting state.

There is a catch. ε-transitions can push forever without reading anything, so a PDA can have infinitely many reachable configurations, and a plain search may never end. The simulator in this lesson therefore takes a limit, `max_stack`, and never builds a configuration whose stack is taller than that. Then there are finitely many configurations (states × input positions × stacks of bounded height), so the search always finishes, and it finds every accepting run that stays within the limit.

Is that a cheat? For the machines built from grammars below, a good limit is easy to state: for a grammar in Chomsky normal form, n + 2 is always enough for a string of length n, because every symbol on the stack below the top produces at least one input symbol that has not been read. And membership for PDAs can be decided exactly in O(n³) time by converting to a grammar and running CYK, which is how you would build a guaranteed parser. The bounded search is the simple simulator; it is exact whenever the limit is big enough.

### From a grammar to a PDA

Every context-free language has a PDA, and the construction is a parser. The PDA keeps the part of a sentential form that is still to be matched on its stack, and works on the top symbol:

1. Push the start variable on top of the bottom marker.
2. In the main state, repeat:
   - if the top is a variable A, pop it and push the body of **some** rule A → w (a nondeterministic choice, reading nothing);
   - if the top is a terminal c, read c from the input and pop it;
   - if the top is the bottom marker, pop it and move to the accepting state.

With S → aSb | ε, on the input `ab`, the stack goes: S⊥, then aSb⊥ (expand S), then Sb⊥ (match a), then b⊥ (expand S by ε), then ⊥ (match b), then accept.

**Why it is correct.** The invariant: whenever the PDA is in the main state, the input read so far followed by the stack above the marker is a sentential form of a **leftmost** derivation. Everything read is terminals, so the top of the stack is the leftmost symbol not yet matched: if it is a variable, it is the leftmost variable, and expanding it is one leftmost step. Matching a terminal moves a symbol from the stack to the "read" side, which keeps the form the same. So an accepting run spells out a leftmost derivation of the input, and every leftmost derivation can be followed by a run that makes the same choices. The PDA accepts exactly L(G).

The order of the pushed body matters: its **first** symbol must end up on top, because it is the leftmost one.

### From a PDA to a grammar

The other direction also holds, so the theorem is:

**A language is context-free if and only if some PDA recognises it.**

The proof idea: first tidy the PDA so that it has one accepting state, empties its stack before accepting, and every transition either pushes one symbol or pops one. Then make a variable A_pq for each pair of states, meaning "the strings that take the PDA from state p with an empty stack to state q with an empty stack". Follow a run from p to q. Either the first symbol pushed is popped only at the very end, which gives a rule A_pq → a A_rs b (a is read by the push, b by the pop), or the stack becomes empty somewhere in between, at a state r, which gives A_pq → A_pr A_rq. Finally A_pp → ε. Induction on the length of runs shows the grammar is right. You can build it in the practice.

### Deterministic PDAs are weaker

A PDA is **deterministic** when at most one transition applies in every configuration. The aⁿbⁿ machine above guesses when the `a`s are over, but aⁿbⁿ also has a deterministic PDA: move to the popping state when the first `b` is read, and accept the empty string in the start state itself, so that no ε-move competes with reading. But the even palindromes {wwᴿ} need a guess: the machine must push the first half and pop the second, and nothing in the input marks the middle. It is a theorem that no deterministic PDA recognises them. So, unlike finite automata, **nondeterminism adds power** to pushdown automata. The deterministic context-free languages are exactly the ones the linear-time parsers of compilers handle, which is why programming languages are designed to be deterministic (the [[LR parsers|knuth-lr]] from last lesson are deterministic PDAs).

### What it costs

- A deterministic PDA reads each symbol once, with a constant amount of work per move: **linear time** (after removing loops of ε-moves).
- The bounded search visits up to |Q| · (n + 1) · (number of stacks of height at most H) configurations, which can be **exponential** in the limit H.
- The guaranteed method for any PDA is PDA → grammar → Chomsky normal form → CYK: **O(n³)** for a fixed PDA.

### Where it is used

- **Parsers.** LR and LL parsers are deterministic PDAs; recursive-descent parsers use the call stack as their pushdown store, which is the [[top-down|top-down]] construction above.
- **Program analysis.** A program with recursion, but finitely many values in its variables, behaves like a PDA whose stack is the call stack. Tools that check such models (**[[pushdown systems|pushdown-analysis]]**) find bugs that cross function calls.
- **Streaming validators** for XML and JSON keep a stack of the elements that are open, and nothing else.
- **Flight software:** a command-script checker that allows nested `BEGIN … END` blocks needs a stack (or a fixed maximum depth, which makes it a DFA again).

**Watch out:**

- **Accepting too early.** A PDA accepts only when the whole input has been read and the state is accepting. Reaching an accepting state halfway through means nothing.
- **Forgetting the bottom marker.** Without it, the machine cannot tell when every push has been matched.
- **Pushing in the wrong order.** Pushing a body reversed puts its last symbol on top; then the grammar PDA matches symbols in the wrong order.
- **Searches that never end.** ε-moves that push can go on forever. Bound the stack, and search breadth-first.

::: context bfs-again Why breadth-first
A depth-first search follows one branch as far as it goes before trying another. If one branch pushes forever on ε-moves, a depth-first search without a limit falls down it and never returns, even when another branch accepts in three moves. A breadth-first search looks at every configuration one move away, then two moves away, and so on, so it finds the shortest accepting run first. With the stack limit, both searches end; breadth-first also gives the shortest run, which the practice uses.
:::

::: context knuth-lr LR parsing and deterministic PDAs
Donald Knuth introduced LR(k) parsing in 1965: read left to right, build a rightmost derivation in reverse, and look k symbols ahead to decide each move. He proved that the languages with LR(1) grammars are exactly the languages of deterministic PDAs. Tools like yacc (1970s) and Bison made LR parsers the standard for compilers, and a variant called LALR(1) keeps their tables small.
:::

::: context top-down Top-down parsing
The grammar-to-PDA construction is a **top-down** parser: it starts from the start variable and expands variables until the terminals match the input. A recursive-descent parser, which you will write in the Compilers course, does the same with one function per variable, and its call stack plays the role of the PDA's stack. To avoid guessing, it looks at the next input symbol to choose a rule; grammars where that always works are called LL(1). Left-recursive rules such as E → E+T make a naive top-down parser loop forever, just as they make the PDA push forever on ε-moves.
:::

::: context pushdown-analysis Pushdown systems in program analysis
Model a program by its control points and a finite abstraction of its data. A call pushes the return point, and a return pops it. The result is a pushdown system, and questions such as "can the program reach this error state?" become reachability questions for configurations. Remarkably, the set of reachable stack contents of a pushdown system is always regular, so these questions can be answered exactly. Model checkers and static analysers use this to reason about recursive code.
:::
--- task
PDAs are dictionaries as in the lesson: `"delta"` maps `(q, a, X)` to a set of pairs `(r, push)`, where `a` or `X` may be `""` for ε and `push` is a tuple of stack symbols, top first. The stack starts empty. Grammars are dictionaries as in lesson 7.

Write two functions.

- `pda_accepts(pda, w, max_stack)`: return `True` when some run of the PDA reads all of `w` and ends in an accepting state, never having a stack taller than `max_stack`, and `False` otherwise. Search the configurations `(state, symbols read, stack)` breadth-first, never building one whose stack is taller than `max_stack`, and never visiting a configuration twice. A transition with `X` other than `""` applies only when `X` is on top of the stack, and one with `a` other than `""` only when `a` is the next input symbol.
- `cfg_to_pda(G)`: return a PDA with exactly three states, `"start"`, `"loop"` and `"accept"`, that recognises L(G), built as in the lesson: push the start variable above a bottom marker, then expand variables, match terminals, and accept on the marker. Use a bottom marker that is not a variable or a terminal of `G`, such as `"⊥"`. The input alphabet is `G["terminals"]`.
--- starter
def pda_accepts(pda, w, max_stack):
    return False


def cfg_to_pda(G):
    return {"states": {"start"}, "alphabet": G["terminals"], "stack": set(), "delta": {}, "start": "start", "accept": set()}
--- solution
from collections import deque

BOTTOM = "⊥"


def moves(pda, config, w, max_stack):
    q, i, stack = config
    symbols = [""] + ([w[i]] if i < len(w) else [])
    tops = [""] + ([stack[0]] if stack else [])
    for a in symbols:
        for X in tops:
            for r, push in pda["delta"].get((q, a, X), ()):
                new_stack = tuple(push) + (stack[1:] if X else stack)
                if len(new_stack) <= max_stack:
                    yield (r, i + len(a), new_stack)


def pda_accepts(pda, w, max_stack):
    start = (pda["start"], 0, ())
    seen = {start}
    queue = deque([start])
    while queue:
        config = queue.popleft()
        q, i, stack = config
        if i == len(w) and q in pda["accept"]:
            return True
        for nxt in moves(pda, config, w, max_stack):
            if nxt not in seen:
                seen.add(nxt)
                queue.append(nxt)
    return False


def cfg_to_pda(G):
    delta = {("start", "", ""): {("loop", (G["start"], BOTTOM))}}
    for A, bodies in G["rules"].items():
        delta[("loop", "", A)] = {("loop", tuple(body)) for body in bodies}
    for c in G["terminals"]:
        delta[("loop", c, c)] = {("loop", ())}
    delta[("loop", "", BOTTOM)] = {("accept", ())}
    stack = set(G["rules"]) | set(G["terminals"]) | {BOTTOM}
    return {"states": {"start", "loop", "accept"}, "alphabet": G["terminals"], "stack": stack,
            "delta": delta, "start": "start", "accept": {"accept"}}
--- hint
From a configuration `(q, i, stack)`, the possible input parts are `""` and, if `i < len(w)`, `w[i]`; the possible pops are `""` and, if the stack is not empty, `stack[0]`. Look up `pda["delta"].get((q, a, X), ())` for each combination, and apply each move as in the lesson's stack example.
--- hint
Use a `collections.deque` as the queue and a `seen` set of configurations, both starting with `(pda["start"], 0, ())`. Skip any new configuration whose stack is longer than `max_stack`. Accept when you take out a configuration with `i == len(w)` and an accepting state.
--- hint
In `cfg_to_pda`, the key `("loop", "", A)` gets one move per body of A, pushing the body as a tuple (its first symbol on top). The key `("loop", c, c)` reads the terminal c and pops it, and `("loop", "", "⊥")` moves to `"accept"`.
--- check case | The lesson's PDA for aⁿbⁿ

=> [True, True, True, True, False, False, False, False]

=> (True, False)

+++ practice | A PDA for w c wᴿ
--- task
Write `wcwr_pda()` that returns a PDA (a dictionary as in the lesson) over the alphabet `"abc"` that accepts exactly the strings w`c`wᴿ, where w is any string of `a`s and `b`s and wᴿ is w reversed: for example `"c"`, `"aca"`, `"abcba"` and `"bbacabb"`, but not `"abcab"`, `"cc"` or `""`.

The middle is marked by the `c`, so no guessing is needed: push the first half, switch at the `c`, and pop the second half against it. Use a bottom marker so that you know when the stack is back to empty. A check runs your PDA on every string up to length 7 with a stack limit of 10.
--- starter
def wcwr_pda():
    return {"states": {0}, "alphabet": "abc", "stack": set(), "delta": {}, "start": 0, "accept": set()}
--- solution
def wcwr_pda():
    delta = {
        ("s", "", ""): {("push", ("⊥",))},
        ("push", "a", ""): {("push", ("a",))},
        ("push", "b", ""): {("push", ("b",))},
        ("push", "c", ""): {("pop", ())},
        ("pop", "a", "a"): {("pop", ())},
        ("pop", "b", "b"): {("pop", ())},
        ("pop", "", "⊥"): {("f", ())},
    }
    return {"states": {"s", "push", "pop", "f"}, "alphabet": "abc", "stack": {"a", "b", "⊥"},
            "delta": delta, "start": "s", "accept": {"f"}}
--- hint
Four states: one to push the bottom marker, one that pushes each `a` or `b` it reads, one that pops, and an accepting one. Reading `c` moves from the pushing state to the popping state.
--- hint
In the popping state, reading `a` pops an `a` and reading `b` pops a `b`. When the bottom marker is on top, pop it and move to the accepting state, reading nothing.

+++ practice | Show the accepting run
--- task
A yes is more useful with a reason. Write `accepting_run(pda, w, max_stack)` that returns an accepting run of the PDA on `w` as a list of configurations `(state, symbols read, stack)`, from the start configuration `(start, 0, ())` to one with all of `w` read and an accepting state, where each configuration follows from the one before by one transition and no stack is taller than `max_stack`. Return `None` when there is no such run.

Search breadth-first, as in the lesson, and remember for each configuration the one it came from, so you can walk back from the accepting one. Breadth-first search finds a run with the fewest transitions; any correct run is accepted.
--- starter
def accepting_run(pda, w, max_stack):
    return None
--- solution
from collections import deque


def accepting_run(pda, w, max_stack):
    start = (pda["start"], 0, ())
    came_from = {start: None}
    queue = deque([start])
    while queue:
        config = queue.popleft()
        q, i, stack = config
        if i == len(w) and q in pda["accept"]:
            run = []
            while config is not None:
                run.append(config)
                config = came_from[config]
            return run[::-1]
        for a in [""] + ([w[i]] if i < len(w) else []):
            for X in [""] + ([stack[0]] if stack else []):
                for r, push in pda["delta"].get((q, a, X), ()):
                    new_stack = tuple(push) + (stack[1:] if X else stack)
                    nxt = (r, i + len(a), new_stack)
                    if len(new_stack) <= max_stack and nxt not in came_from:
                        came_from[nxt] = config
                        queue.append(nxt)
    return None
--- hint
Replace the `seen` set of the lesson's search by a dictionary `came_from` that maps each configuration to the configuration it was first reached from (the start maps to `None`).
--- hint
When you take out an accepting configuration with all input read, follow `came_from` back to the start, collecting configurations, and reverse the list.

+++ practice | A PDA and a DFA, run together
--- task
The intersection of a context-free language and a regular language is always context-free, and the proof is the product construction of lesson 2 with a stack. Write `pda_and_dfa(pda, dfa)` that returns a PDA recognising L(pda) ∩ L(dfa). The DFA is total and has the same alphabet.

The states of the new PDA are the pairs `(q, s)` of a PDA state and a DFA state; it starts at `(pda start, dfa start)`, and accepts in the pairs where both parts accept. For each PDA transition `(q, a, X) → (r, push)` and each DFA state `s`: if `a` is `""`, the DFA part does not move, giving `((q, s), "", X) → ((r, s), push)`; otherwise the DFA part reads `a` too, giving `((q, s), a, X) → ((r, δ(s, a)), push)`. The stack alphabet stays the same.
--- starter
def pda_and_dfa(pda, dfa):
    return pda
--- solution
def pda_and_dfa(pda, dfa):
    delta = {}
    for (q, a, X), outs in pda["delta"].items():
        for s in dfa["states"]:
            t = s if a == "" else dfa["delta"][(s, a)]
            key = ((q, s), a, X)
            delta.setdefault(key, set())
            for r, push in outs:
                delta[key].add(((r, t), tuple(push)))
    states = {(q, s) for q in pda["states"] for s in dfa["states"]}
    accept = {(q, s) for q in pda["accept"] for s in dfa["accept"]}
    return {"states": states, "alphabet": pda["alphabet"], "stack": set(pda["stack"]),
            "delta": delta, "start": (pda["start"], dfa["start"]), "accept": accept}
--- hint
Loop over the PDA's transitions, and inside that over the DFA's states. The new key is `((q, s), a, X)`, and the DFA part of the target is `s` itself for an ε-move, or `dfa["delta"][(s, a)]` when a symbol is read.
--- hint
Collect the targets in a set per key (`setdefault` helps). The accepting pairs are all `(q, s)` with `q` accepting in the PDA and `s` accepting in the DFA.

+++ practice | Two kinds of brackets
--- task
Write `brackets_pda()` that returns a PDA over the alphabet `"()[]"` accepting exactly the strings in which round and square brackets are balanced and properly nested: `""`, `"()"`, `"[()]"`, `"()[]"` and `"[[]()]"` are in; `"(]"`, `"([)]"`, `"(()"`, `")("` and `"]"` are out.

Make it **deterministic**: in every configuration at most one transition applies, counting every transition whose input part is `""` or the next symbol and whose pop part is `""` or the top of the stack. That rules out the lesson's trick of guessing when the input is over: a transition that pops the bottom marker on ε while a transition that reads `(` could also apply is a choice.

Watch the edge cases: the empty string is accepted; a closing bracket with nothing open, or of the wrong kind, is rejected; brackets still open at the end are rejected. The checks use a stack limit of 16.
--- starter
def brackets_pda():
    return {"states": {0}, "alphabet": "()[]", "stack": set(), "delta": {}, "start": 0, "accept": {0}}
--- solution
def brackets_pda():
    delta = {
        ("s", "", ""): {("even", ("⊥",))},
        ("even", "(", ""): {("open", ("(",))},
        ("even", "[", ""): {("open", ("[",))},
        ("open", "(", ""): {("open", ("(",))},
        ("open", "[", ""): {("open", ("[",))},
        ("open", ")", "("): {("look", ())},
        ("open", "]", "["): {("look", ())},
        ("look", "", "("): {("open", ("(",))},
        ("look", "", "["): {("open", ("[",))},
        ("look", "", "⊥"): {("even", ("⊥",))},
    }
    return {"states": {"s", "even", "open", "look"}, "alphabet": "()[]", "stack": {"(", "[", "⊥"},
            "delta": delta, "start": "s", "accept": {"even"}}
--- hint
Let the **state** say whether everything read so far is balanced. An accepting state `"even"` means "only the bottom marker is on the stack"; a state `"open"` means "some bracket is still open". Reading an opening bracket in either state pushes it and goes to `"open"`.
--- hint
A closing bracket pops only the matching opening bracket, and then the machine does not know what is underneath. Go to a state `"look"` that has only ε-moves, one for each possible top: it pops the top and pushes it straight back, going to `"open"` for a bracket and to `"even"` for the marker. Those ε-moves never clash, because they need different tops.

+++ practice | Debug: accepting halfway through
--- task
This `pda_accepts(pda, w, max_stack)` should accept only when some run has read **all** of `w` and is in an accepting state. It returns `True` as soon as any configuration is in an accepting state, however much input is left. So the lesson's PDA for aⁿbⁿ wrongly accepts `"abb"`: it reaches its accepting state after reading `"ab"`. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
from collections import deque


def pda_accepts(pda, w, max_stack):
    start = (pda["start"], 0, ())
    seen = {start}
    queue = deque([start])
    while queue:
        q, i, stack = queue.popleft()
        if q in pda["accept"]:
            return True
        for a in [""] + ([w[i]] if i < len(w) else []):
            for X in [""] + ([stack[0]] if stack else []):
                for r, push in pda["delta"].get((q, a, X), ()):
                    new_stack = tuple(push) + (stack[1:] if X else stack)
                    nxt = (r, i + len(a), new_stack)
                    if len(new_stack) <= max_stack and nxt not in seen:
                        seen.add(nxt)
                        queue.append(nxt)
    return False
--- solution
from collections import deque


def pda_accepts(pda, w, max_stack):
    start = (pda["start"], 0, ())
    seen = {start}
    queue = deque([start])
    while queue:
        q, i, stack = queue.popleft()
        if i == len(w) and q in pda["accept"]:
            return True
        for a in [""] + ([w[i]] if i < len(w) else []):
            for X in [""] + ([stack[0]] if stack else []):
                for r, push in pda["delta"].get((q, a, X), ()):
                    new_stack = tuple(push) + (stack[1:] if X else stack)
                    nxt = (r, i + len(a), new_stack)
                    if len(new_stack) <= max_stack and nxt not in seen:
                        seen.add(nxt)
                        queue.append(nxt)
    return False
--- hint
What two things must be true of a configuration for it to show that `w` is accepted?
--- hint
The test must also check that the number of symbols read, `i`, equals `len(w)`.

=> [True, True, True, False, False, False]
--- check case | A start state that accepts: only the empty string when no transition reads
(lambda P: [pda_accepts(P, w, 3) for w in ["", "a", "aa"]])({"states": {0}, "alphabet": "a", "stack": set(), "delta": {}, "start": 0, "accept": {0}})
=> [True, False, False]
--- check test | Even palindromes, on every string up to length 8

+++ practice | From a PDA back to a grammar
--- task
Build the other half of the theorem. Write `pda_to_cfg(pda)` for a PDA in **normal form**:

- it has exactly one accepting state;
- its stack starts empty and is empty again whenever it accepts;
- every transition either pushes exactly one symbol without popping (a key `(p, a, "")` with targets `(r, (u,))`), or pops exactly one symbol without pushing (a key `(s, b, X)` with `X` not `""`, and targets `(q, ())`).

Make one variable `("A", p, q)` for every pair of states p and q; it generates the strings that take the PDA from p with an empty stack to q with an empty stack. The rules:

1. for every push `(p, a, "") → (r, (u,))` and every pop `(s, b, u) → (q, ())` of the **same** symbol u: `("A", p, q) → a ("A", r, s) b`, leaving out `a` or `b` when it is `""`;
2. for all states p, q and r: `("A", p, q) → ("A", p, r) ("A", r, q)`;
3. for every state p: `("A", p, p) → ε`.

The start variable is `("A", start, accepting state)`, and the terminals are the PDA's alphabet. A check lists the strings your grammar generates and compares them with what the PDA accepts.
--- starter
def pda_to_cfg(pda):
    return {"variables": set(), "terminals": pda["alphabet"], "rules": {}, "start": None}
--- solution
def pda_to_cfg(pda):
    states = pda["states"]
    rules = {("A", p, q): [] for p in states for q in states}
    pushes = [(p, a, r, push[0]) for (p, a, X), outs in pda["delta"].items() if X == "" for (r, push) in outs]
    pops = [(s, b, X, q) for (s, b, X), outs in pda["delta"].items() if X != "" for (q, push) in outs]
    for p, a, r, u in pushes:
        for s, b, X, q in pops:
            if X == u:
                body = tuple(x for x in (a, ("A", r, s), b) if x != "")
                rules[("A", p, q)].append(body)
    for p in states:
        for q in states:
            for r in states:
                rules[("A", p, q)].append((("A", p, r), ("A", r, q)))
        rules[("A", p, p)].append(())
    (accept,) = pda["accept"]
    return {"variables": set(rules), "terminals": pda["alphabet"], "rules": rules,
            "start": ("A", pda["start"], accept)}
--- hint
First list the pushes as `(p, a, r, u)` and the pops as `(s, b, u, q)`, reading them off the transition dictionary: a key with `X == ""` is a push, any other key is a pop.
--- hint
For rule 1, pair every push with every pop of the same symbol. Build the body as the tuple of `a`, `("A", r, s)` and `b`, dropping the parts that are `""`. Then add the rules of types 2 and 3 with three nested loops over the states and one loop.
