+++ question | Accepting means reading everything
--- ask
A PDA reads the first 5 symbols of a 7-symbol input and enters an accepting state. No transition from there reads input. Is the input accepted?
--- choice
Yes: it entered an accepting state.
--- choice correct
Not by this branch: a PDA accepts only when a run has read all of the input and is in an accepting state.
--- choice
Yes, if the stack is empty at that moment.
--- choice
It depends on whether the PDA is deterministic.
--- why
Acceptance is a property of a whole run: all of w read, and the state accepting at that point. A branch that stops early, or cannot read the rest, says nothing. Another branch might still accept, which is why a simulator must keep searching.

+++ question | Where nondeterminism matters
--- ask
Which statement about pushdown automata is true?
--- choice
Every PDA can be turned into a deterministic PDA for the same language, like the subset construction for NFAs.
--- choice correct
Some context-free languages, such as the even palindromes {wwᴿ}, are recognised by no deterministic PDA.
--- choice
A deterministic PDA can recognise a language that no nondeterministic PDA can.
--- choice
Nondeterminism only makes PDAs smaller, never more powerful.
--- why
The subset construction works for NFAs because a set of states is a finite object. For a PDA, each branch has its own stack, and a set of stacks cannot be kept on one stack. For {wwᴿ} the machine must guess where the middle is, and it is a theorem that no deterministic PDA can do without that guess. Deterministic PDAs are a special case of PDAs, so they never recognise more.

+++ question | The invariant of the grammar's PDA
--- ask
In the PDA built from a grammar, what is true whenever the machine is in its main state?
--- choice
The stack holds exactly the input read so far.
--- choice correct
The input read so far, followed by the stack contents above the bottom marker, is a sentential form of a leftmost derivation.
--- choice
The stack contains only variables.
--- choice
The stack height equals the number of derivation steps so far.
--- why
Terminals are matched from the top of the stack as they are read, so the read part is the prefix of terminals, and the stack holds the rest of the form, leftmost symbol on top. Expanding the top variable is therefore exactly one leftmost derivation step, which is why accepting runs and leftmost derivations match up.

=== theory-10 | Non-context-free languages and the pumping lemma
--- teach
The last three lessons built grammars, parsers and pushdown automata. A stack can match `a`s against `b`s. Can it also match them against `c`s? This lesson proves that it cannot: aⁿbⁿcⁿ is **not context-free**, and neither is the copy language {ww}. The tool is a second **pumping lemma**, like the one of lesson 6 but pumping in two places at once. You will turn its proof into a program that cuts a tall parse tree into the five pieces the lemma promises, and play the lemma's game against every possible opponent.

### A tall tree must repeat itself

Picture a family tree where every person has at most two children, and there are only k different family names. Follow any line of descent from the top that passes through more than k people: two of them must share a name, by the pigeonhole principle. Now apply that to parse trees.

Take a grammar in Chomsky normal form with |V| variables. In a parse tree, a path from the root down passes through variable nodes and ends at a terminal. If some path has more than |V| variable nodes, two of them carry the same variable, say R, one above the other. Call the upper one R₁ and the lower one R₂. Then:

- R₂'s subtree yields some piece x;
- R₁'s subtree yields a longer piece, v x y: the part v to the left of R₂'s subtree and the part y to its right;
- the whole tree yields u v x y z, where u and z are what lies outside R₁'s subtree.

Here is the trick. R₁ and R₂ carry the same variable, so either subtree may be put in place of the other. Replace R₂'s subtree by a copy of R₁'s: the yield becomes u v v x y y z. Do it again: u v³ x y³ z. Or replace R₁'s subtree by R₂'s: u x z. Every one of those trees is a valid parse tree, so **u vⁱ x yⁱ z ∈ L for every i ≥ 0**. The picture in the [[note|pump-picture]] shows the pieces.

### The pumping lemma for context-free languages

**The lemma.** If L is context-free, then there is a number p ≥ 1, the **pumping length**, such that every string s ∈ L with |s| ≥ p can be written as s = uvxyz where:

1. |vy| ≥ 1: at least one of v and y is not empty;
2. |vxy| ≤ p: the pumped part is short;
3. u vⁱ x yⁱ z ∈ L for **every** i ≥ 0.

**Proof.** Take a grammar in Chomsky normal form for L (lesson 8), let |V| be its number of variables, and let p = 2^|V|. Measure a tree's **height** as in lesson 7: the largest number of variable nodes on a path from the root downward. In CNF, every variable node has two children or one terminal, so a tree of height h yields at most 2^(h−1) symbols (by induction on h: height 1 is a single A → a; otherwise two subtrees of height at most h − 1 each yield at most 2^(h−2)).

Take s ∈ L with |s| ≥ p, and a parse tree for s. Since |s| ≥ 2^|V| > 2^(|V|−1), its height is more than |V|. Follow a **longest** path from the root. It has more than |V| variable nodes, so among its **lowest |V| + 1** variable nodes two carry the same variable. Walking up from the bottom, let R₁ be the first node whose variable already appeared below it on the path, and R₂ that lower node. Cut s into u, v, x, y, z as above. Then:

- **Condition 3** holds by the subtree swapping argument.
- **Condition 1:** R₁ is not the root of a single terminal, so in CNF it has two variable children. R₂ lies inside one of them, and the other one yields at least one symbol (a node below the root never yields ε in CNF), and that symbol is in v or in y.
- **Condition 2:** R₁ is among the lowest |V| + 1 variable nodes of a longest path, so its subtree has height at most |V| + 1 (a longer path below R₁ would make a longer path from the root). So vxy, its yield, has at most 2^|V| = p symbols.

### Using it: aⁿbⁿcⁿ is not context-free

The lemma is used exactly as in lesson 6: as a game against someone who claims L is context-free.

1. The opponent picks p.
2. You pick s ∈ L with |s| ≥ p.
3. The opponent picks any split s = uvxyz with |vy| ≥ 1 and |vxy| ≤ p. You must beat **every** such split.
4. You pick i ≥ 0, and win if u vⁱ x yⁱ z ∉ L.

For L = {aⁿbⁿcⁿ : n ≥ 0}, choose s = aᵖbᵖcᵖ. Take any legal split. Because |vxy| ≤ p and the block of `b`s has length p, the window vxy cannot touch both the `a`s and the `c`s. Choose i = 2.

- If v or y contains two different symbols, say v = `ab`, then v² = `abab` puts a `b` before an `a`, and the result is not of the form a∗b∗c∗.
- Otherwise v and y each contain one kind of symbol, and together at most two kinds (the window misses `a` or `c`). Pumping up adds at least one symbol (since |vy| ≥ 1) of at most two kinds, so the three counts are no longer equal.

Either way uv²xy²z ∉ L. Every split loses, so aⁿbⁿcⁿ is not context-free. (Here i = 0 works too.)

Pumping in code is two string multiplications:

```python
u, v, x, y, z = "0", "01", "1", "10", "0"
print([u + v * i + x + y * i + z for i in range(3)])
# ['010', '0011100', '00101110100']
```

### More languages that are not context-free

- **{aⁿ : n is a perfect square}**, over one symbol. With s = a^(p²), pumping up adds between 1 and p symbols, landing strictly between p² and (p + 1)² = p² + 2p + 1. The squares case of lesson 6 carries over unchanged.
- **{ww : w ∈ {a, b}∗}**, the **copy language**: a string followed by an exact copy of itself. It is not context-free, but the choice of s is delicate. For instance aᵖbaᵖb is in the language, yet it **can** be pumped: take v = the `a` just before the first `b`, x = that `b`, and y = the `a` just after it. Then uvⁱxyⁱz = a^(p−1+i) b a^(p−1+i) b, still a string followed by its copy. So that choice proves nothing. Finding an s that works is part of this lesson's task.
- **Declare before use.** Programs in which every variable used was declared earlier behave like {wcw}: matching a name here against the same name there. That is why a compiler checks such rules [[after parsing|declare-before-use]], not in the grammar.

### Closure properties: what the stack cannot do

Context-free languages are closed under union, concatenation and star (lesson 7). They are **not** closed under intersection:

- L₁ = {aⁿbⁿcᵐ : n, m ≥ 0} is context-free (aⁿbⁿ followed by c∗), and so is L₂ = {aᵐbⁿcⁿ : n, m ≥ 0};
- but L₁ ∩ L₂ = {aⁿbⁿcⁿ}, which is not.

Nor under complement: if they were, then L₁ ∩ L₂ would equal the complement of (complement of L₁ ∪ complement of L₂), which would be context-free by De Morgan's law and closure under union. It is not.

One intersection is safe: **a context-free language intersected with a regular language is context-free.** The proof is the product of a PDA with a DFA, from the practice of lesson 9. That gives the closure trick of lesson 6 a new use. Take E = the strings over {a, b, c} with equally many `a`s, `b`s and `c`s, in any order. If E were context-free, then E ∩ a∗b∗c∗ = aⁿbⁿcⁿ would be too. So E is not context-free. (A direct pumping proof with s = aᵖbᵖcᵖ also works for E, and there the bound |vxy| ≤ p is essential: the split v = aᵏbᵏ, x = the remaining `b`s, y = cᵏ keeps all three counts equal however often it is pumped, and only the bound forbids it.)

### What the lemma cannot do

As in lesson 6, the lemma only goes one way. Some languages that are not context-free still satisfy its conclusion, so pumping never proves that a language **is** context-free; for that, write a grammar or a PDA. A stronger tool, **[[Ogden's lemma|ogden]]**, lets you mark the positions that must be pumped, and handles languages the plain lemma cannot. Over a one-symbol alphabet the story is simpler: every context-free language over one symbol is in fact regular, a consequence of **[[Parikh's theorem|parikh]]**.

### What it costs

The pumping length p = 2^|V| is exponential in the size of the grammar, and that is a real lower bound on grammar size. A grammar in CNF for a finite language can have no repeated variable on any path (otherwise pumping would make the language infinite), so its trees have height at most |V| and its strings length at most 2^(|V|−1). So a CNF grammar for {aⁿbⁿcⁿ : n ≤ N}, whose longest string has length 3N, needs at least log₂(3N) + 1 variables.

### Where it is used

- **Language design and compilers:** type rules, "declare before use", and "a function is called with as many arguments as it takes" are not context-free, so compilers check them in a separate semantic pass over the parse tree. The Compilers course builds that pass.
- **Linguistics:** in 1985 it was shown that **[[Swiss German|swiss-german]]** has sentences with crossing dependencies that no context-free grammar can generate, which settled a long debate about whether human languages are context-free.
- **Knowing your tools:** a regex engine, a parser generator and a PDA-based validator each have a ceiling. Knowing where it is tells you when a problem needs a more powerful model: the Turing machine of the next lesson.

**Watch out:**

- **The window can straddle a boundary.** vxy may contain the end of one block and the start of the next. Handle every position of the window, which is why |vxy| ≤ p is so useful: it limits how many blocks it can touch.
- **v or y may be empty on its own.** Only |vy| ≥ 1 is promised. An argument that assumes both are non-empty misses cases.
- **v or y may contain mixed symbols.** Then pumping breaks the order of the symbols rather than their counts. Count-based arguments must deal with this case separately.
- **The same quantifier mistakes as before.** p is given, not chosen; the opponent picks the split, not you; and s must be in L and at least p long.

::: context pump-picture The five pieces in a picture
The outer triangle is the whole parse tree, with yield uvxyz. The middle triangle is the subtree of the upper R, with yield vxy. The inner triangle is the subtree of the lower R, with yield x. Because both are R, the middle triangle can be put in place of the inner one (pumping up) or the inner one in place of the middle one (pumping down).

```svg

<polygon points="180,66 90,180 270,180" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
<polygon points="180,118 140,180 220,180" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
<text x="180" y="12" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">S</text>
<text x="193" y="72" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">R</text>
<text x="193" y="124" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">R</text>
<line x1="90" y1="186" x2="90" y2="196" stroke="#6c7a93"/>
<line x1="140" y1="186" x2="140" y2="196" stroke="#6c7a93"/>

```
:::

::: context declare-before-use Checks that come after parsing
A grammar for a language like C cannot express "every variable is declared before it is used": that requires comparing a name in one place with the same name somewhere else, like the copy language. So compilers parse with a context-free grammar that allows undeclared names, then walk the tree with a symbol table and report the errors. Type checking, argument counts and "break only inside a loop" are handled the same way. This split between syntax and semantic analysis is one of the main ideas of the Compilers course.
:::

::: context ogden Ogden's lemma
William Ogden published this strengthening in 1968. You may mark at least p positions of s as "distinguished", and the lemma guarantees a split in which vy contains a distinguished position and vxy contains at most p of them. By marking only the part of the string you care about, you stop the opponent from hiding the pumping somewhere harmless. It proves, for example, that {aⁱbʲcᵏ : i = j or j = k} is inherently ambiguous, and that some languages which pump are still not context-free.
:::

::: context parikh Parikh's theorem
Rohit Parikh proved in 1961 that if you forget the order of the symbols and keep only how many of each there are, every context-free language looks exactly like some regular language. Over a single symbol the order carries no information, so every context-free language over one symbol is regular. That is why unary examples, such as the squares or the primes, are never context-free: they are not regular either, as lesson 6 showed.
:::

::: context swiss-german Crossing dependencies in Swiss German
Stuart Shieber showed in 1985 that Swiss German allows sentences in which a list of nouns is followed by a list of verbs, and the first noun goes with the first verb, the second with the second, and so on: the pattern of the copy language, not of nested brackets. Intersecting with a suitable regular language and applying the pumping lemma shows that no context-free grammar generates exactly the grammatical sentences. English puts nested dependencies in the same place, which is why context-free grammars work well enough for it.
:::
--- task
Grammars are dictionaries as in lesson 7, and parse trees are tuples. In this task the grammar has only rules A → BC and A → a (Chomsky normal form without ε), and its number of variables is `len(G["rules"])`.

Write three functions.

- `pump_split(G, tree)`: `tree` is a parse tree of `G`. Its height is the largest number of variable nodes on a path from the root downward (as `height` in lesson 7). If the height is at most the number of variables, return `None`. Otherwise follow the proof: take a **longest** path from the root, walk up from its bottom, stop at the first node whose variable already appeared below it on that path (that is R₁, and the node below with the same variable is R₂), and return the tuple `(u, v, x, y, z)` where x is the yield of R₂'s subtree, vxy the yield of R₁'s subtree, and uvxyz the yield of the whole tree. Then |vy| ≥ 1, |vxy| is at most 2 to the power of the number of variables, and every uvⁱxyⁱz is generated.
- `ww_string(p)` and `ww_i(u, v, x, y, z)`: your strategy in the pumping game for the copy language {ww : w ∈ {a, b}∗}. `ww_string(p)` returns a string in the language of length at least `p`, for any `p ≥ 1`. `ww_i` receives any legal split of that string (|vy| ≥ 1 and |vxy| ≤ p) and returns a whole number i ≥ 0 such that uvⁱxyⁱz is **not** in the language. The checks try **every** legal split, for p from 1 to 7.
--- starter
def pump_split(G, tree):
    return None


def ww_string(p):
    return "ab" * p


def ww_i(u, v, x, y, z):
    return 0
--- solution
def tree_yield(t):
    if isinstance(t, str):
        return t
    return "".join(tree_yield(c) for c in t[1:])


def deepest_path(node):
    best = [node]
    for child in node[1:]:
        if isinstance(child, tuple):
            below = deepest_path(child)
            if len(below) + 1 > len(best):
                best = [node] + below
    return best


def around(path, top, bottom):
    left = right = ""
    for t in range(top, bottom):
        kids = path[t][1:]
        pos = next(i for i, c in enumerate(kids) if c is path[t + 1])
        left += "".join(tree_yield(c) for c in kids[:pos])
        right = "".join(tree_yield(c) for c in kids[pos + 1:]) + right
    return left, right


def pump_split(G, tree):
    path = deepest_path(tree)
    if len(path) <= len(G["rules"]):
        return None
    lowest = {}
    for k in range(len(path) - 1, -1, -1):
        label = path[k][0]
        if label in lowest:
            upper, lower = k, lowest[label]
            break
        lowest[label] = k
    u, z = around(path, 0, upper)
    v, y = around(path, upper, lower)
    return u, v, tree_yield(path[lower]), y, z


def ww_string(p):
    return ("a" * p + "b" * p) * 2


def ww_i(u, v, x, y, z):
    return 0
--- hint
For `pump_split`, first get the longest path as a list of nodes from the root down (a recursive function that keeps the longest list among the children's). Its length is the height. Then walk it from the end, remembering in a dictionary the position where each variable was seen; the first variable you meet a second time gives R₁ (here) and R₂ (the remembered position).
--- hint
To find u and z, walk the path from the root to R₁: at each node, the children to the left of the next path node add to u, and those to the right add (in front) to z. Do the same from R₁ to R₂ for v and y. Compare nodes with `is` to find the next path node among the children.
--- hint
For the copy language, aᵖbaᵖb fails: the window "last `a`, `b`, first `a`" across the first `b` pumps to a^(p−1+i) b a^(p−1+i) b, which is still a copy. Choose s so that no window of length p can reach a spot like that: for example, four blocks of p symbols each. Then check whether pumping down (i = 0) already breaks every split.

=> ("", "", "a", "a", "a")

+++ practice | The game for i ≤ j ≤ k
--- task
The language {aⁱbʲcᵏ : i ≤ j ≤ k} is not context-free. Write your strategy for the pumping game: `ijk_string(p)` returns a string of the language with length at least `p` (for any `p ≥ 1`), and `ijk_i(u, v, x, y, z)` receives any legal split of it (|vy| ≥ 1 and |vxy| ≤ p) and returns a whole number i ≥ 0 such that uvⁱxyⁱz is not in the language. The checks try every legal split for p from 1 to 7.

This time one fixed i does not beat every split: sometimes you must pump up, and sometimes down. Remember that v or y may be empty.
--- starter
def ijk_string(p):
    return "a" * p + "b" * p + "c" * p


def ijk_i(u, v, x, y, z):
    return 2
--- solution
def ijk_string(p):
    return "a" * p + "b" * p + "c" * p


def ijk_i(u, v, x, y, z):
    if "a" in v + y:
        return 2
    return 0
--- hint
Use s = aᵖbᵖcᵖ. The window vxy is at most p long, so it cannot contain both `a`s and `c`s. Split the cases by whether vy contains an `a`.
--- hint
If vy contains an `a` (and so no `c`), pumping up makes the `a`s outnumber the `c`s, or breaks the order. If it contains no `a`, pumping down removes some `b`s or `c`s: then either the `b`s drop below the `a`s, or the `c`s drop below the `b`s.
--- check test | Your string is in the language and long enough, for p = 1 to 7
all((lambda s: __import__("re").fullmatch("a*b*c*", s) is not None and len(s) >= p and s.count("a") <= s.count("b") <= s.count("c"))(ijk_string(p)) for p in range(1, 8))
--- check test | Every legal split loses, for p = 1 to 7

?? A split inside the c's survives pumping up, and a split inside the a's survives pumping down. Choose i from what vy contains.
--- check test | A split with v empty and y inside the b's
(lambda i: isinstance(i, int) and (lambda w: not (w.count("a") <= w.count("b") <= w.count("c")))("aaa" + "" + "bb" + "b" * i + "ccc"))(ijk_i("aaa", "", "bb", "b", "ccc"))

+++ practice | All of the opponent's moves
--- task
Write `legal_splits(s, p)` that returns a list of every split the opponent may choose in the pumping game for context-free languages: every tuple `(u, v, x, y, z)` of strings with `u + v + x + y + z == s`, `len(v + y) >= 1` and `len(v + x + y) <= p`. Each split appears exactly once, in any order.

Watch the edge cases: `s` may be empty (then there is no legal split), `p` may be larger than `len(s)`, and x, u and z may be empty. Two splits with the same strings in the same places are the same split.
--- starter
def legal_splits(s, p):
    return [("", s, "", "", "")]
--- solution
def legal_splits(s, p):
    n = len(s)
    out = []
    for start in range(n + 1):
        for lv in range(p + 1):
            for lx in range(p + 1 - lv):
                for ly in range(p + 1 - lv - lx):
                    end = start + lv + lx + ly
                    if lv + ly == 0 or end > n:
                        continue
                    out.append((s[:start], s[start:start + lv], s[start + lv:start + lv + lx], s[start + lv + lx:end], s[end:]))
    return out
--- hint
A split is fixed by four numbers: where v starts, and the lengths of v, x and y. Loop over all of them, with the three lengths adding up to at most p.
--- hint
Skip the choices where v and y are both empty, and those that run past the end of `s`. Each remaining choice gives a different split, so there are no duplicates to remove.
--- check test | "ab" with p = 1: exactly the two splits that pump one symbol
sorted(legal_splits("ab", 1)) == sorted([("", "a", "", "", "b"), ("", "", "", "a", "b"), ("a", "b", "", "", ""), ("a", "", "", "b", "")])
--- check case | The number of splits for some small cases
[len(legal_splits(s, p)) for s, p in [("abc", 1), ("abc", 2), ("abcd", 2), ("abcd", 10), ("aaaaaa", 3)]]
=> [6, 16, 23, 55, 73]
--- check case | The empty string has no legal split, even with a large p
legal_splits("", 5)
=> []
--- check test | No duplicates, and every split is legal, on a longer string
(lambda L: len(L) == len(set(L)) == 415 and all("".join(t) == "aaaabbbbaaaabbbb" and len(t[1] + t[3]) >= 1 and len(t[1] + t[2] + t[3]) <= 4 for t in L))(legal_splits("aaaabbbbaaaabbbb", 4))

+++ practice | A grammar and a DFA, run together
--- task
A context-free language intersected with a regular language is context-free. This time build the grammar directly. Write `cnf_and_dfa(G, dfa)`: `G` is in Chomsky normal form (rules A → BC, A → a, and possibly the start variable's `()`), and `dfa` is a total DFA over the same alphabet. Return a grammar for L(G) ∩ L(dfa).

Use a variable `(p, A, q)` for every DFA state p, variable A of G, and DFA state q: it should generate the strings that A generates **and** that take the DFA from p to q.

- for each rule A → a and each state p: `(p, A, δ(p, a)) → a`;
- for each rule A → BC and all states p, r, q: `(p, A, q) → (p, B, r) (r, C, q)`;
- a new start variable `"START"` with `START → (start state, S, f)` for each accepting state f, and `START → ε` if S → ε is a rule and the DFA's start state is accepting.

Every `(p, A, q)` is a key of the result's rules, even if it gets no rules. The terminals are G's.
--- starter
def cnf_and_dfa(G, dfa):
    return G
--- solution
def cnf_and_dfa(G, dfa):
    states = dfa["states"]
    rules = {(p, A, q): [] for A in G["rules"] for p in states for q in states}
    for A, bodies in G["rules"].items():
        for body in bodies:
            if len(body) == 1:
                for p in states:
                    rules[(p, A, dfa["delta"][(p, body[0])])].append(tuple(body))
            elif len(body) == 2:
                B, C = body
                for p in states:
                    for r in states:
                        for q in states:
                            rules[(p, A, q)].append(((p, B, r), (r, C, q)))
    S, q0 = G["start"], dfa["start"]
    rules["START"] = [((q0, S, f),) for f in dfa["accept"]]
    if () in G["rules"][S] and q0 in dfa["accept"]:
        rules["START"].append(())
    return {"variables": set(rules), "terminals": G["terminals"], "rules": rules, "start": "START"}
--- hint
Create every `(p, A, q)` key first with an empty list. Then go through G's rules: a terminal rule A → a gives, for each p, one rule for `(p, A, δ(p, a))`; a binary rule gives one rule for each choice of three states.
--- hint
The middle state r in `(p, B, r) (r, C, q)` is where the DFA is after B's part of the string and before C's part. Finish with the `"START"` rules.
--- check test | aⁿbⁿ (n ≥ 0) with "even number of a's": aⁿbⁿ for even n, up to length 12
(lambda GEN, prod, G, D: GEN(cnf_and_dfa(G, D), 12) == {"", "aabb", "aaaabbbb", "aaaaaabbbbbb"})((lambda G, n, red=__import__("functools").reduce: (lambda step: (lambda f: f(f, {A: frozenset() for A in G["rules"]}))(lambda f, g: (lambda h: set(g[G["start"]]) if h == g else f(f, h))(step(g))))(lambda g: {A: frozenset(w for b in G["rules"][A] for w in red(lambda S, X: {u + v for u in S for v in (g[X] if X in G["rules"] else {X}) if len(u + v) <= n}, b, {""})) for A in G["rules"]})), __import__("itertools").product, {"variables": {"S0", "S", "X", "A", "B"}, "terminals": "ab", "rules": {"S0": [("A", "X"), ("A", "B"), ()], "S": [("A", "X"), ("A", "B")], "X": [("S", "B")], "A": [("a",)], "B": [("b",)]}, "start": "S0"}, {"states": {0, 1}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, "a"): 1, (1, "a"): 0, (0, "b"): 0, (1, "b"): 1}, "start": 0, "accept": {0}})

+++ practice | Squares, and the case p = 1
--- task
The language {aⁿ : n is a perfect square} (0, 1, 4, 9, … symbols) is not context-free. Write your strategy for the pumping game: `square_string(p)` returns a string of the language with at least `p` symbols, and `square_i(u, v, x, y, z)` receives any legal split of it (|vy| ≥ 1 and |vxy| ≤ p) and returns a whole number i ≥ 0 with uvⁱxyⁱz not in the language. The checks try every legal split for **every p from 1 to 8**.

Be careful with the smallest case. Pumping down looks natural, but for p = 1 it can land on 0 symbols, and 0 = 0² is a perfect square.
--- starter
def square_string(p):
    return "a" * (p * p)


def square_i(u, v, x, y, z):
    return 0
--- solution
def square_string(p):
    return "a" * (p * p)


def square_i(u, v, x, y, z):
    return 2
--- hint
Take s = a^(p²). Every legal split has 1 ≤ |vy| ≤ p. What lengths can uvⁱxyⁱz have for i = 2?
--- hint
Pumping up once gives a length between p² + 1 and p² + p, strictly between p² and the next square, (p + 1)² = p² + 2p + 1. That works for every p ≥ 1, including p = 1, where pumping down would give the square 0.

all((lambda s: all((lambda i: isinstance(i, int) and i >= 0 and int(len(sp[0] + sp[1] * i + sp[2] + sp[3] * i + sp[4]) ** 0.5 + 0.5) ** 2 != len(sp[0] + sp[1] * i + sp[2] + sp[3] * i + sp[4]))(square_i(*sp)) for sp in [(s[:a], s[a:a + lv], s[a + lv:a + lv + lx], s[a + lv + lx:a + lv + lx + ly], s[a + lv + lx + ly:]) for a in range(len(s) + 1) for lv in range(p + 1) for lx in range(p + 1 - lv) for ly in range(p + 1 - lv - lx) if lv + ly >= 1 and a + lv + lx + ly <= len(s)]))(square_string(p)) for p in range(1, 9))

+++ practice | Debug: the repeat found too high
--- task
This `pump_split(G, tree)` should follow the proof of the pumping lemma: on a longest path, find the **lowest** repeated variable, walking up from the bottom, so that |vxy| is at most 2 to the power of the number of variables. Instead it walks **down** from the root and stops at the first variable that appears again below, so R₁ can be high in the tree, and vxy can be far longer than the bound allows. The pieces still pump, but the proof's promise |vxy| ≤ p is broken, and that promise is what makes the lemma useful. Fix it.

The grammar has only rules A → BC and A → a; return `None` when the tree's height is at most the number of variables.
--- starter
def tree_yield(t):
    if isinstance(t, str):
        return t
    return "".join(tree_yield(c) for c in t[1:])


def deepest_path(node):
    best = [node]
    for child in node[1:]:
        if isinstance(child, tuple):
            below = deepest_path(child)
            if len(below) + 1 > len(best):
                best = [node] + below
    return best


def around(path, top, bottom):
    left = right = ""
    for t in range(top, bottom):
        kids = path[t][1:]
        pos = next(i for i, c in enumerate(kids) if c is path[t + 1])
        left += "".join(tree_yield(c) for c in kids[:pos])
        right = "".join(tree_yield(c) for c in kids[pos + 1:]) + right
    return left, right


def pump_split(G, tree):
    path = deepest_path(tree)
    if len(path) <= len(G["rules"]):
        return None
    for upper in range(len(path)):
        later = [k for k in range(upper + 1, len(path)) if path[k][0] == path[upper][0]]
        if later:
            lower = later[0]
            break
    u, z = around(path, 0, upper)
    v, y = around(path, upper, lower)
    return u, v, tree_yield(path[lower]), y, z
--- solution
def tree_yield(t):
    if isinstance(t, str):
        return t
    return "".join(tree_yield(c) for c in t[1:])


def deepest_path(node):
    best = [node]
    for child in node[1:]:
        if isinstance(child, tuple):
            below = deepest_path(child)
            if len(below) + 1 > len(best):
                best = [node] + below
    return best


def around(path, top, bottom):
    left = right = ""
    for t in range(top, bottom):
        kids = path[t][1:]
        pos = next(i for i, c in enumerate(kids) if c is path[t + 1])
        left += "".join(tree_yield(c) for c in kids[:pos])
        right = "".join(tree_yield(c) for c in kids[pos + 1:]) + right
    return left, right


def pump_split(G, tree):
    path = deepest_path(tree)
    if len(path) <= len(G["rules"]):
        return None
    lowest = {}
    for k in range(len(path) - 1, -1, -1):
        label = path[k][0]
        if label in lowest:
            upper, lower = k, lowest[label]
            break
        lowest[label] = k
    u, z = around(path, 0, upper)
    v, y = around(path, upper, lower)
    return u, v, tree_yield(path[lower]), y, z
--- hint
The proof uses the lowest |V| + 1 variable nodes of the path, not the highest. Which end of the path should the search start from?
--- hint
Walk the path from its last node upward, remembering where each variable was seen. The first variable seen a second time is R₁, and the remembered position is R₂.

?? Stopping at the first repeat seen from the root makes R₁ the root here, and vxy the whole string.

+++ practice | Catching a grammar that claims equal counts
--- task
Someone hands you a grammar with only rules A → BC and A → a over the terminals `"abc"`, and claims it generates exactly the **non-empty** strings with equally many `a`s, `b`s and `c`s, in any order. No grammar can, so it is wrong somewhere. Write `equal_counts_mistake(G)` that returns a string on which it is wrong: either a string in that language that `G` does not generate, or a string that `G` generates that is not in the language.

Turn the proof into the algorithm. Let p = 2 to the power of the number of variables, and s = aᵖbᵖcᵖ, which is in the language. If `G` does not generate s, return s. Otherwise build a parse tree of s (CYK with back pointers, from lesson 8), cut it with `pump_split` from this lesson, and return uv²xy²z. `G` generates it, and it is not in the language: vxy has at most p symbols, so it touches at most two of the three kinds, and pumping up adds at least one symbol of those kinds only.

The grammars in the checks have at most 4 variables, so s has at most 48 symbols.
--- starter
def equal_counts_mistake(G):
    return "abc"
--- solution
def tree_yield(t):
    if isinstance(t, str):
        return t
    return "".join(tree_yield(c) for c in t[1:])


def cyk_tree(G, w):
    n = len(w)
    pairs = [(A, b[0], b[1]) for A, bodies in G["rules"].items() for b in bodies if len(b) == 2]
    back = {}
    for i, c in enumerate(w):
        back[(i, i + 1)] = {A: c for A, bodies in G["rules"].items() if (c,) in bodies}
    for length in range(2, n + 1):
        for i in range(n - length + 1):
            j = i + length
            cell = {}
            for k in range(i + 1, j):
                left, right = back[(i, k)], back[(k, j)]
                for A, B, C in pairs:
                    if A not in cell and B in left and C in right:
                        cell[A] = (k, B, C)
            back[(i, j)] = cell

    def build(A, i, j):
        how = back[(i, j)][A]
        if j == i + 1:
            return (A, how)
        k, B, C = how
        return (A, build(B, i, k), build(C, k, j))

    if G["start"] not in back[(0, n)]:
        return None
    return build(G["start"], 0, n)


def deepest_path(node):
    best = [node]
    for child in node[1:]:
        if isinstance(child, tuple):
            below = deepest_path(child)
            if len(below) + 1 > len(best):
                best = [node] + below
    return best


def around(path, top, bottom):
    left = right = ""
    for t in range(top, bottom):
        kids = path[t][1:]
        pos = next(i for i, c in enumerate(kids) if c is path[t + 1])
        left += "".join(tree_yield(c) for c in kids[:pos])
        right = "".join(tree_yield(c) for c in kids[pos + 1:]) + right
    return left, right


def pump_split(G, tree):
    path = deepest_path(tree)
    if len(path) <= len(G["rules"]):
        return None
    lowest = {}
    for k in range(len(path) - 1, -1, -1):
        label = path[k][0]
        if label in lowest:
            upper, lower = k, lowest[label]
            break
        lowest[label] = k
    u, z = around(path, 0, upper)
    v, y = around(path, upper, lower)
    return u, v, tree_yield(path[lower]), y, z


def equal_counts_mistake(G):
    p = 2 ** len(G["rules"])
    s = "a" * p + "b" * p + "c" * p
    tree = cyk_tree(G, s)
    if tree is None:
        return s
    u, v, x, y, z = pump_split(G, tree)
    return u + v * 2 + x + y * 2 + z
--- hint
You need two earlier tools: CYK with back pointers that builds a tree (lesson 8's practice), and this lesson's `pump_split`. A tree of s has height more than the number of variables, because s is longer than 2 to the power of (that number minus 1), so `pump_split` never returns `None` here.
--- hint
Why p and not a small string like `abc`? With a short s, the window vxy could span all three kinds, and pumping might keep the counts equal (v = `abc` pumps to `abcabc`). The bound |vxy| ≤ p is what rules that out.
--- check test | "Every non-empty string" (one variable): the answer is generated but has unequal counts
(lambda CYK, G: (lambda w: CYK(G, w) and not (len(w) > 0 and w.count("a") == w.count("b") == w.count("c")))(equal_counts_mistake(G)))((lambda G, w, red=__import__("functools").reduce: (lambda n: (() in G["rules"][G["start"]]) if n == 0 else G["start"] in red(lambda T, L: {**T, **{(i, i + L): {A for A in G["rules"] for b in G["rules"][A] if len(b) == 2 and any(b[0] in T[(i, k)] and b[1] in T[(k, i + L)] for k in range(i + 1, i + L))} for i in range(n - L + 1)}}, range(2, n + 1), {(i, i + 1): {A for A in G["rules"] if (w[i],) in G["rules"][A]} for i in range(n)})[(0, n)])(len(w))), {"variables": {"S"}, "terminals": "abc", "rules": {"S": [("S", "S"), ("a",), ("b",), ("c",)]}, "start": "S"})

?? When G generates s, pump up once at the split from pump_split; the pumped string is generated and has unequal counts.

+++ question | Why |vxy| ≤ p matters
--- ask
E is the set of strings over {a, b, c} with equally many `a`s, `b`s and `c`s, in any order. In a pumping proof that E is not context-free, with s = aᵖbᵖcᵖ, why may the opponent not choose v = aᵏbᵏ (the last k `a`s and the first k `b`s), x = the remaining `b`s, and y = cᵏ?
--- choice
Because then v contains two different symbols, which is never allowed.
--- choice correct
Because vxy would then be p + 2k symbols long, and the lemma only allows splits with |vxy| ≤ p.
--- choice
Because pumping that split gives strings that are not in E.
--- choice
Because y must come right after v, with x empty.
--- why
That split pumps forever inside E: each round adds k of each symbol, and E ignores order. If it were allowed, the lemma would give nothing. The bound |vxy| ≤ p keeps the window inside at most two adjacent blocks of aᵖbᵖcᵖ, so pumping changes at most two of the three counts. Mixed symbols in v are allowed; they simply cannot help here.

+++ question | Closure under intersection
--- ask
L₁ = {aⁿbⁿcᵐ} and L₂ = {aᵐbⁿcⁿ} are both context-free. What does L₁ ∩ L₂ = {aⁿbⁿcⁿ} show?
--- choice correct
Context-free languages are not closed under intersection.
--- choice
aⁿbⁿcⁿ is context-free, since it is an intersection of two context-free languages.
--- choice
The pumping lemma is wrong.
--- choice
Context-free languages are not closed under union.
--- why
Closure under intersection would make aⁿbⁿcⁿ context-free, which the pumping lemma rules out. So intersecting two context-free languages can leave the class. Intersecting with a **regular** language cannot: the PDA and the DFA can run side by side, one stack between them.

+++ question | Empty pieces
--- ask
In the context-free pumping lemma, which of these splits can the opponent legally choose?
--- choice
Only splits where both v and y are non-empty.
--- choice correct
Splits where v is empty and y is not, or the other way round, as long as |vy| ≥ 1 and |vxy| ≤ p.
--- choice
Only splits where x is non-empty.
--- choice
Only splits where vxy lies inside a single block of equal symbols.
--- why
The lemma only promises |vy| ≥ 1, so one of v and y may be empty, and x may be empty too. The window may also straddle the boundary between blocks. A proof must beat all of these splits, which is why case analyses split on what vy contains rather than assuming a shape.

=== theory-11 | Turing machines
--- teach
Last lesson found languages beyond every pushdown automaton: aⁿbⁿcⁿ and the copy language {ww}. A stack forgets what it pops, and it can only be read at the top. This lesson removes both limits. A **Turing machine** has a finite control, like every machine so far, and a tape it can **read, write and move along in both directions**, as far as it likes. That small change gives the most powerful model of computation known: next lesson argues that it can do everything any computer can. You will build a Turing machine simulator and program machines for languages no PDA can recognise.

### A clerk with a strip of paper

In 1936 **[[Alan Turing|turing]]** asked what a person does when computing by hand with fixed rules. They look at a few symbols, write or erase one, move their attention a little, and change their state of mind, which can only be one of finitely many. Turing boiled this down to a machine: an endless strip of paper divided into cells, a head that sees one cell at a time, and a finite table of rules saying what to write, which way to move and what state to be in next. He used it to settle the **[[Entscheidungsproblem|entscheidung]]**, a famous open question of the time, as lesson 13 will show.

### The definition

A **Turing machine**, or **TM**, is a 7-tuple M = (Q, Σ, Γ, δ, q₀, q_accept, q_reject):

- Q is a finite set of states, with the start state q₀, and two special states q_accept and q_reject, which are different;
- Σ is the input alphabet, which does not contain the **blank** symbol, written ␣ (and `_` in code);
- Γ is the **tape alphabet**: it contains Σ, the blank, and any extra symbols the machine likes to write, such as marks;
- δ : Q × Γ → Q × Γ × {L, R} is the transition function: δ(q, a) = (r, b, L) means "in state q, reading a, write b, move the head one cell **left**, and go to state r". R means move right.

**A run.** The tape has a left end and goes on forever to the right. At the start, the input w sits in the first |w| cells, every other cell holds a blank, the head is on the first cell, and the state is q₀. Each step applies δ to the current state and symbol. Two conventions complete the definition:

- Moving left from the first cell leaves the head where it is.
- The machine **halts** as soon as it enters q_accept or q_reject. In this course, as in Sipser's book, a missing transition means "go to q_reject": the machine halts and rejects.

A **configuration** is a snapshot: the state, the tape contents and the head position. It is written **u q v**: the tape holds uv followed by blanks, the state is q, and the head is on the first symbol of v. For example `1 0 q₇ 0 1 1` means the tape is `10011`, the state is q₇, and the head is on the second `0`. If δ(q₇, 0) = (q₈, x, L), the next configuration is `1 q₈ 0 x 1 1`.

### Three ways a run can end

A run can **accept**, **reject**, or **never halt** (loop forever), and the third is new: every DFA, and every PDA simulation with a bound, always finished.

- The **language of M**, L(M), is the set of strings M accepts. A language is **Turing-recognisable** when some TM recognises it. On a string outside the language, such a machine may reject or may run forever.
- A TM is a **decider** when it halts on **every** input. A language is **decidable** when some decider recognises it.

For the machines in this lesson the difference seems small, because they all halt. In lesson 13 it becomes the central question of computability: some languages are recognisable but not decidable, and no one can tell in general whether a machine will halt.

### Turing machines in Python

This course writes a TM as a dictionary. `"delta"` maps `(state, symbol)` to `(new state, symbol to write, "L" or "R")`, and the tape alphabet is a string that includes the blank `"_"`. Here is a machine that adds 1 to a binary number written **least significant bit first**, so that `011` means 1 + 2 + 0 = 6 read from the left. The carry moves right, which avoids the left end:

```python
inc = {
    "states": {"carry", "done", "no"},
    "alphabet": "01",
    "tape": "01_",
    "delta": {
        ("carry", "1"): ("carry", "0", "R"),   # 1 plus carry: write 0, carry on
        ("carry", "0"): ("done", "1", "R"),    # 0 plus carry: write 1, finished
        ("carry", "_"): ("done", "1", "R"),    # past the end: a new top bit
    },
    "start": "carry",
    "accept": "done",
    "reject": "no",
}
```

Its run on `110` (the number 3), as configurations: `carry 110`, then `0 carry 10`, then `00 carry 0`, then `001 done`. The tape now holds `001`, which is 4. On `11` it runs past the input and writes a new cell: `carry 11`, `0 carry 1`, `00 carry ␣`, `001 done`.

A machine that leaves its answer on the tape **computes a function**: M computes f if, on every input w, it halts with f(w) on its tape. So TMs compute functions as well as decide languages; this one computes n + 1.

In Python, the tape can be a list that grows to the right when the head moves past its end. Writing a cell and moving the head look like this:

```python
tape = list("110") + ["_"]
head = 1
tape[head] = "x"
head = max(0, head - 1)      # moving left from cell 0 stays at cell 0
print("".join(tape), head)   # 1x0_ 0
```

### Programming by marking

Turing machines are programmed at **[[three levels of description|levels]]**: the formal table, a description of how the head moves, and the algorithm in words. The workhorse technique is **marking**: overwrite symbols with marks to remember what has been done, and sweep back and forth.

Here is a decider for the copy-like language {w#w : w ∈ {0, 1}∗}, described by how its head moves:

1. Zigzag across the `#`. Cross off the first unmarked symbol on the left (write `x`), remembering in the **state** whether it was a `0` or a `1`. Move right past the `#` to the first unmarked symbol there. If it is the same symbol, cross it off; otherwise reject.
2. Move back left to the rightmost `x` on the left side, then one step right: that is the next unmarked symbol. Repeat.
3. When the left side has no unmarked symbols, check that the right side has none either. Accept if so, otherwise reject.

The state carries a finite amount of information (here one bit, "I am matching a 0" or "a 1"), and the marks carry the unbounded part. Two details need care in every such machine:

- **Finding your way back.** A machine cannot see the left end of the tape: moving left there just stays put. So give it something to stop at, such as a mark in the first cell, or crossed-off symbols it can recognise.
- **Blanks.** The input ends at the first blank. Every state that sweeps right must say what to do on `_`, or the missing transition silently rejects.

### Simulating a Turing machine

Since a run may never end, a simulator takes a step limit and reports one of three results: accepted, rejected, or still running after the limit. It cannot do better: lesson 13 proves that no program can decide, for every machine and input, whether the run will halt. Each step is a dictionary lookup, a write and a move, so simulating t steps costs O(t) time, and the tape needs at most t + |w| + 1 cells.

### What it costs

The **running time** of a decider is the largest number of steps it takes on any input of length n. The marking machine for {w#w} crosses off one pair per zigzag, and each zigzag walks up to n cells each way, so it takes O(n²) steps. That is not a weakness of the algorithm: every one-tape TM for palindromes, or for {w#w}, needs time proportional to n² in the worst case, which can be proved with **[[crossing sequences|crossing]]**. A second tape (next lesson) brings it down to O(n). Lesson 15 builds the class P on this notion of running time.

### Where it is used

- **The theory of computing** rests on it: decidability (lessons 13 and 14) and complexity (lessons 15 to 17) are defined with Turing machines, because they are simple enough to reason about and powerful enough to capture every algorithm.
- **A model of a real computer.** The tape is memory, the head is the address being used, the states are the program. Real machines have random-access memory, which saves a polynomial factor of time, but not more.
- **Hard limits on what programs can know.** The **[[busy beaver|busy-beaver]]** problem asks how long a small TM can run before halting; its values grow faster than any computable function.

**Watch out:**

- **The left end.** Moving left from cell 0 stays at cell 0. A simulator in Python that just subtracts 1 reaches index −1, which is the **last** cell of the list: a real and common bug. And a machine that sweeps left "until the beginning" never finds it without a marker.
- **The empty input and the blank.** On `""` the head starts on a blank. Give every state that can meet a blank a transition for it, unless rejecting is what you want.
- **Destroying information you still need.** Mark symbols instead of erasing them when you will need them again, and restore them at the end if the output must be clean.
- **"Still running" is not "rejects".** A step limit reached means you do not know yet.

::: context turing Alan Turing
Alan Turing (1912–1954) was a British mathematician. His 1936 paper "On Computable Numbers, with an Application to the Entscheidungsproblem" introduced the machines now named after him, described a universal machine, and proved that some problems cannot be solved by any machine. During the Second World War he led work at Bletchley Park on breaking the German Enigma cipher. The highest award in computer science, the Turing Award, is named after him.
:::

::: context entscheidung The decision problem
In 1928 David Hilbert and Wilhelm Ackermann asked for a mechanical procedure that decides, for any statement of first-order logic, whether it is valid: the "Entscheidungsproblem", German for "decision problem". To prove that no such procedure exists, one first needs a precise definition of "mechanical procedure". Alonzo Church (with the lambda calculus) and Turing (with his machines) gave two definitions in 1936, and each proved the answer is no.
:::

::: context levels Three levels of description
Sipser's textbook describes a TM at three levels. The **formal description** is the full table of δ, like the Python dictionaries here. The **implementation description** says in words how the head moves and what it writes, like the zigzag for w#w. The **high-level description** gives the algorithm without mentioning the tape at all: "check that the two halves are equal". Once you trust that an implementation description can always be turned into a table, proofs work at the high level, and so will lessons 12 to 17.
:::

::: context crossing Crossing sequences
Draw a line between two neighbouring cells of the tape and list the states the machine is in each time the head crosses it: that list is the crossing sequence. For palindromes of the form w0ⁿwᴿ, where w has length n, two different choices of w cannot have the same crossing sequence at a line in the middle block, or the machine could be fooled into accepting a non-palindrome by cutting and pasting the two runs. Counting how many sequences are needed shows that the total length of the crossing sequences, and so the running time, must be proportional to n².
:::

::: context busy-beaver The busy beaver
Among all Turing machines with n states (and a two-symbol tape) that halt when started on a blank tape, which one runs the longest? For 4 states the answer is 107 steps. For 5 states it is 47,176,870 steps, proved only in 2024 by an online collaboration, the bbchallenge project, with a computer-checked proof. The function grows faster than any function a program can compute, so no algorithm can find its values in general. It is a vivid face of the halting problem of lesson 13.
:::
--- task
Turing machines are dictionaries as in the lesson: `"delta"` maps `(state, symbol)` to `(new state, symbol to write, "L" or "R")`, the blank is `"_"`, and `"accept"` and `"reject"` are single states.

Write two functions.

- `run_tm(tm, w, max_steps)`: run the machine on the input `w`, with the conventions of the lesson: the head starts on cell 0; moving left from cell 0 stays there; a missing transition sends the machine to its reject state without writing, moving or counting a step; the machine halts as soon as it is in its accept or reject state (even at the start). Return the tuple `(result, tape, steps)`: `result` is `"accept"`, `"reject"`, or `"running"` if the machine has taken `max_steps` steps without halting; `tape` is the tape contents from cell 0 up to the last cell that is not blank, as a string (blanks inside it stay as `"_"`); and `steps` is the number of transitions taken.
- `anbncn_tm()`: return a decider over the alphabet `"abc"` for {aⁿbⁿcⁿ : n ≥ 0}: it accepts `""`, `"abc"` and `"aabbcc"`, and rejects every other string, such as `"aabbc"`, `"abcabc"` and `"cba"`. It must halt on every input, within 10·(n + 1)² steps for an input of length n. Use marking, as in the lesson.
--- starter
def run_tm(tm, w, max_steps):
    return ("running", w, 0)


def anbncn_tm():
    return {"states": {"q0", "acc", "rej"}, "alphabet": "abc", "tape": "abc_", "delta": {}, "start": "q0", "accept": "acc", "reject": "rej"}
--- solution
def run_tm(tm, w, max_steps):
    tape = list(w) if w else ["_"]
    head = 0
    state = tm["start"]
    steps = 0
    while state != tm["accept"] and state != tm["reject"]:
        if steps == max_steps:
            return ("running", "".join(tape).rstrip("_"), steps)
        move = tm["delta"].get((state, tape[head]))
        if move is None:
            state = tm["reject"]
            break
        state, tape[head], direction = move
        if direction == "R":
            head += 1
            if head == len(tape):
                tape.append("_")
        else:
            head = max(0, head - 1)
        steps += 1
    result = "accept" if state == tm["accept"] else "reject"
    return (result, "".join(tape).rstrip("_"), steps)


def anbncn_tm():
    d = {}
    d[("q0", "_")] = ("acc", "_", "R")      # nothing left to match
    d[("q0", "a")] = ("q1", "X", "R")       # cross off an a
    d[("q0", "Y")] = ("q4", "Y", "R")       # no a left: check the rest
    for s in "aY":
        d[("q1", s)] = ("q1", s, "R")
    d[("q1", "b")] = ("q2", "Y", "R")       # cross off a b
    for s in "bZ":
        d[("q2", s)] = ("q2", s, "R")
    d[("q2", "c")] = ("q3", "Z", "L")       # cross off a c
    for s in "abYZ":
        d[("q3", s)] = ("q3", s, "L")
    d[("q3", "X")] = ("q0", "X", "R")       # back at the last crossed a
    d[("q4", "Y")] = ("q4", "Y", "R")
    d[("q4", "Z")] = ("q5", "Z", "R")
    d[("q5", "Z")] = ("q5", "Z", "R")
    d[("q5", "_")] = ("acc", "_", "R")
    return {"states": {"q0", "q1", "q2", "q3", "q4", "q5", "acc", "rej"}, "alphabet": "abc",
            "tape": "abcXYZ_", "delta": d, "start": "q0", "accept": "acc", "reject": "rej"}
--- hint
For `run_tm`, keep the tape as a list (with one blank if `w` is empty), the head position, the state and a step counter. Loop while the state is neither accept nor reject: first return `"running"` if the counter has reached `max_steps`, then look up the transition, write, move (`max(0, head - 1)` for L, and append a blank when the head passes the end), and count the step.
--- hint
For `anbncn_tm`, cross off one `a`, one `b` and one `c` per round with three different marks, say `X`, `Y` and `Z`. After crossing a `c`, sweep left to the rightmost `X` and step right: that cell holds the next `a`, or a `Y` when every `a` is crossed off.
--- hint
When the next cell holds `Y`, every `a` is used: sweep right over `Y`s, then `Z`s, and accept only on a blank. Anything else (a `b` or `c` left over, or a symbol out of order) has no transition, so the machine rejects. The empty input is a blank in the start state: accept it.

+++ practice | Palindromes on a tape
--- task
Write `palindrome_tm()` that returns a decider (a dictionary as in the lesson) over the alphabet `"ab"` for the palindromes: the strings equal to their own reversal, such as `""`, `"a"`, `"abba"` and `"babab"`. It must halt on every input.

Use the zigzag: erase the first symbol (write a blank there), remembering it in the state; run right to the end of the input; step back onto the last symbol and check that it matches, erasing it too; run back left to the first blank and step right; repeat. When nothing is left, or a single symbol, accept.
--- starter
def palindrome_tm():
    return {"states": {"q0", "acc", "rej"}, "alphabet": "ab", "tape": "ab_", "delta": {("q0", "_"): ("acc", "_", "R")}, "start": "q0", "accept": "acc", "reject": "rej"}
--- solution
def palindrome_tm():
    d = {("q0", "_"): ("acc", "_", "R"), ("q0", "a"): ("ra", "_", "R"), ("q0", "b"): ("rb", "_", "R")}
    for c in "ab":
        for s in "ab":
            d[("r" + c, s)] = ("r" + c, s, "R")
        d[("r" + c, "_")] = ("t" + c, "_", "L")
        d[("t" + c, c)] = ("back", "_", "L")
        d[("t" + c, "_")] = ("acc", "_", "R")
    for s in "ab":
        d[("back", s)] = ("back", s, "L")
    d[("back", "_")] = ("q0", "_", "R")
    return {"states": {"q0", "ra", "rb", "ta", "tb", "back", "acc", "rej"}, "alphabet": "ab",
            "tape": "ab_", "delta": d, "start": "q0", "accept": "acc", "reject": "rej"}
--- hint
Use a pair of states for each first symbol: `ra` runs right remembering an `a`, and `ta` tests the last symbol against `a`. Reading a blank in `ta` means the symbol erased was the only one left, which is fine.
--- hint
Erasing with blanks gives the machine its own left end: after a successful test, `back` runs left over `a`s and `b`s until it sees a blank, and steps right onto the next first symbol. A mismatch in `ta` or `tb` simply has no transition.
--- check test | Right on every string up to length 10

(lambda TM, M, R: (lambda w: TM(M, w + w[::-1], 10 ** 5)[0] == "accept" and TM(M, w + "b" + w[::-1], 10 ** 5)[0] == "accept" and TM(M, w + "ab" + w[::-1], 10 ** 5)[0] == "reject")("".join(R.choice("ab") for _ in range(40))))((lambda M, w, K: (lambda t, s, tw: [(lambda r: s.__setitem__(0, M["reject"]) if r is None else (t.__setitem__(s[1], r[1]), s.__setitem__(0, r[0]), s.__setitem__(1, max(0, s[1] + (1 if r[2] == "R" else -1))), s.__setitem__(2, s[2] + 1), t.append("_") if s[1] == len(t) else None))(M["delta"].get((s[0], t[s[1]]))) for _ in tw(lambda _: s[0] not in (M["accept"], M["reject"]), range(K))] and 0 or (("accept" if s[0] == M["accept"] else "reject" if s[0] == M["reject"] else "running"), "".join(t).rstrip("_"), s[2]))(list(w) + ["_"], [M["start"], 0, 0], __import__("itertools").takewhile)), palindrome_tm(), __import__("random").Random(5))
--- check test | The format: accept and reject are states, the blank is in the tape alphabet

+++ practice | A machine that copies its input
--- task
A Turing machine can compute functions: it halts with the answer on its tape. Write `copy_tm()` that returns a machine over the alphabet `"ab"` which, on input w, halts in its accept state with exactly `w#w` on the tape: for example `"ab"` becomes `"ab#ab"`, and the empty input becomes `"#"`. No marks may be left behind.

One way: cross off the first uncopied symbol (write `A` for `a`, `B` for `b`), carry it in the state to the right end, past the `#` (writing the `#` the first time), write it there, and come back left to the last capital letter. When the next symbol to copy is the `#`, turn the capitals back into small letters. The left end needs care: the first cell is crossed off first, so the way back always finds a capital letter; and when restoring, a small letter means you have turned back at cell 0.
--- starter
def copy_tm():
    return {"states": {"find", "acc", "rej"}, "alphabet": "ab", "tape": "ab#_", "delta": {("find", "_"): ("acc", "#", "R")}, "start": "find", "accept": "acc", "reject": "rej"}
--- solution
def copy_tm():
    d = {("find", "_"): ("acc", "#", "R")}
    for c, C in (("a", "A"), ("b", "B")):
        d[("find", c)] = ("carry" + c, C, "R")
        for s in "ab":
            d[("carry" + c, s)] = ("carry" + c, s, "R")
            d[("put" + c, s)] = ("put" + c, s, "R")
        d[("carry" + c, "#")] = ("put" + c, "#", "R")
        d[("carry" + c, "_")] = ("put" + c, "#", "R")
        d[("put" + c, "_")] = ("back", c, "L")
    for s in "ab#":
        d[("back", s)] = ("back", s, "L")
    for C in "AB":
        d[("back", C)] = ("find", C, "R")
    d[("find", "#")] = ("restore", "#", "L")
    d[("restore", "A")] = ("restore", "a", "L")
    d[("restore", "B")] = ("restore", "b", "L")
    d[("restore", "a")] = ("acc", "a", "R")
    d[("restore", "b")] = ("acc", "b", "R")
    states = {q for q, s in d} | {"acc", "rej"}
    return {"states": states, "alphabet": "ab", "tape": "abAB#_", "delta": d, "start": "find", "accept": "acc", "reject": "rej"}
--- hint
Use a state `carry_a` that runs right over `a` and `b`; at `#` it moves on to `put_a`, and at a blank (no `#` yet) it writes `#` and moves on to `put_a`. `put_a` runs right over `a` and `b` and writes `a` on the first blank. Then a state `back` runs left over `a`, `b` and `#` until it meets a capital letter, and steps right.
--- hint
`find` reads the next symbol to copy: a small letter starts a new round, `#` means everything is copied, and a blank means the input was empty (write `#` and accept). To restore, run left turning `A` into `a` and `B` into `b`; the first small letter you then meet is cell 0, which you have just restored, so accept there.
--- check test | Every input up to length 8: accepted with w#w on the tape

+++ practice | Every DFA is a Turing machine
--- task
Every regular language is decidable. Write `dfa_to_tm(dfa)`: given a total DFA (a dictionary as in lesson 2), return a Turing machine that decides the same language by moving right once per symbol, never writing anything new, and deciding when it reaches the first blank. On an input of length n it must halt after **exactly n + 1 steps**.

Name the machine's state for DFA state `q` with the tuple `("q", q)`, and use the strings `"ACCEPT"` and `"REJECT"` for its accept and reject states. The input alphabet is the DFA's, and the tape alphabet is the DFA's alphabet plus `"_"`.
--- starter
def dfa_to_tm(dfa):
    return {"states": {"ACCEPT", "REJECT"}, "alphabet": dfa["alphabet"], "tape": dfa["alphabet"] + "_", "delta": {}, "start": "ACCEPT", "accept": "ACCEPT", "reject": "REJECT"}
--- solution
def dfa_to_tm(dfa):
    delta = {}
    for (q, c), r in dfa["delta"].items():
        delta[(("q", q), c)] = (("q", r), c, "R")
    for q in dfa["states"]:
        verdict = "ACCEPT" if q in dfa["accept"] else "REJECT"
        delta[(("q", q), "_")] = (verdict, "_", "R")
    states = {("q", q) for q in dfa["states"]} | {"ACCEPT", "REJECT"}
    return {"states": states, "alphabet": dfa["alphabet"], "tape": dfa["alphabet"] + "_",
            "delta": delta, "start": ("q", dfa["start"]), "accept": "ACCEPT", "reject": "REJECT"}
--- hint
Each DFA move from `q` to `r` on `c` becomes a TM move from `("q", q)` to `("q", r)` that reads `c`, writes `c` back and moves right.
--- hint
Reading the blank means the input is over: from `("q", q)`, go to `"ACCEPT"` if q is an accepting DFA state, and to `"REJECT"` otherwise. That is the (n + 1)-th step.
--- check test | Binary numbers divisible by 3: right on every string up to length 9, in exactly n + 1 steps

+++ practice | Powers of two
--- task
Write `powers_tm()` that returns a decider over the alphabet `"0"` for the strings of `0`s whose length is a power of 2: 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, … Watch the edge cases: the empty string is **rejected** (0 is not a power of 2), `"0"` is **accepted** (1 = 2⁰), and every other odd length is rejected.

Use repeated halving: sweep left to right crossing off every other `0` (write `x`), which halves the number left. If a sweep finds exactly one `0`, accept. If it finds an odd number greater than 1, reject. Otherwise go back to the left end and sweep again. To find the left end, replace the very first `0` with a blank at the start, and treat that blank as the wall: it also counts as the `0` it replaced.
--- starter
def powers_tm():
    return {"states": {"q1", "acc", "rej"}, "alphabet": "0", "tape": "0x_", "delta": {("q1", "0"): ("acc", "0", "R")}, "start": "q1", "accept": "acc", "reject": "rej"}
--- solution
def powers_tm():
    d = {
        ("q1", "0"): ("q2", "_", "R"),
        ("q2", "x"): ("q2", "x", "R"),
        ("q2", "_"): ("acc", "_", "R"),
        ("q2", "0"): ("q3", "x", "R"),
        ("q3", "x"): ("q3", "x", "R"),
        ("q3", "0"): ("q4", "0", "R"),
        ("q3", "_"): ("q5", "_", "L"),
        ("q4", "x"): ("q4", "x", "R"),
        ("q4", "0"): ("q3", "x", "R"),
        ("q5", "0"): ("q5", "0", "L"),
        ("q5", "x"): ("q5", "x", "L"),
        ("q5", "_"): ("q2", "_", "R"),
    }
    return {"states": {"q1", "q2", "q3", "q4", "q5", "acc", "rej"}, "alphabet": "0", "tape": "0x_",
            "delta": d, "start": "q1", "accept": "acc", "reject": "rej"}
--- hint
Let the state track the parity of the `0`s seen in the current sweep. After the blank wall (which stands for one `0`), a state `q2` means "one so far": seeing a blank there means exactly one, so accept. Seeing a `0` crosses it off and moves to "even so far".
--- hint
In "even so far", a `0` is kept and moves to "odd so far"; in "odd so far", a `0` is crossed off and moves back to "even so far". Reaching the blank at the end in "odd so far" means an odd count above 1: that state simply has no blank transition. Reaching it in "even so far" means sweep again: run left to the wall and step right into `q2`. On the empty input, the start state reads a blank and has no transition, so it rejects.
--- check test | Accepts exactly the lengths 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32 and 64 among 0 to 80
(lambda TM, M: [n for n in range(81) if TM(M, "0" * n, 10 ** 5)[0] == "accept"] == [1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64])((lambda M, w, K: (lambda t, s, tw: [(lambda r: s.__setitem__(0, M["reject"]) if r is None else (t.__setitem__(s[1], r[1]), s.__setitem__(0, r[0]), s.__setitem__(1, max(0, s[1] + (1 if r[2] == "R" else -1))), s.__setitem__(2, s[2] + 1), t.append("_") if s[1] == len(t) else None))(M["delta"].get((s[0], t[s[1]]))) for _ in tw(lambda _: s[0] not in (M["accept"], M["reject"]), range(K))] and 0 or (("accept" if s[0] == M["accept"] else "reject" if s[0] == M["reject"] else "running"), "".join(t).rstrip("_"), s[2]))(list(w) + ["_"], [M["start"], 0, 0], __import__("itertools").takewhile)), powers_tm())

(lambda TM, M: [TM(M, "0" * n, 1000)[0] for n in [0, 1, 3, 6]])((lambda M, w, K: (lambda t, s, tw: [(lambda r: s.__setitem__(0, M["reject"]) if r is None else (t.__setitem__(s[1], r[1]), s.__setitem__(0, r[0]), s.__setitem__(1, max(0, s[1] + (1 if r[2] == "R" else -1))), s.__setitem__(2, s[2] + 1), t.append("_") if s[1] == len(t) else None))(M["delta"].get((s[0], t[s[1]]))) for _ in tw(lambda _: s[0] not in (M["accept"], M["reject"]), range(K))] and 0 or (("accept" if s[0] == M["accept"] else "reject" if s[0] == M["reject"] else "running"), "".join(t).rstrip("_"), s[2]))(list(w) + ["_"], [M["start"], 0, 0], __import__("itertools").takewhile)), powers_tm())

+++ practice | Debug: the head that wraps around
--- task
This `run_tm(tm, w, max_steps)` follows the lesson's conventions, except one: on a move to the left it subtracts 1 from the head position without stopping at cell 0. In Python, `tape[-1]` is the **last** cell of the list, so a machine that moves left from cell 0 suddenly reads and writes the far end of the tape. Find the bug and fix it, so that moving left from cell 0 leaves the head on cell 0. It returns `(result, tape, steps)` as in the lesson.
--- starter
def run_tm(tm, w, max_steps):
    tape = list(w) if w else ["_"]
    head = 0
    state = tm["start"]
    steps = 0
    while state != tm["accept"] and state != tm["reject"]:
        if steps == max_steps:
            return ("running", "".join(tape).rstrip("_"), steps)
        move = tm["delta"].get((state, tape[head]))
        if move is None:
            state = tm["reject"]
            break
        state, tape[head], direction = move
        if direction == "R":
            head += 1
            if head == len(tape):
                tape.append("_")
        else:
            head -= 1
        steps += 1
    result = "accept" if state == tm["accept"] else "reject"
    return (result, "".join(tape).rstrip("_"), steps)
--- solution
def run_tm(tm, w, max_steps):
    tape = list(w) if w else ["_"]
    head = 0
    state = tm["start"]
    steps = 0
    while state != tm["accept"] and state != tm["reject"]:
        if steps == max_steps:
            return ("running", "".join(tape).rstrip("_"), steps)
        move = tm["delta"].get((state, tape[head]))
        if move is None:
            state = tm["reject"]
            break
        state, tape[head], direction = move
        if direction == "R":
            head += 1
            if head == len(tape):
                tape.append("_")
        else:
            head = max(0, head - 1)
        steps += 1
    result = "accept" if state == tm["accept"] else "reject"
    return (result, "".join(tape).rstrip("_"), steps)
--- hint
Which head positions are allowed? What does the left move do when the head is already at 0?
--- hint
Clamp the new position: `head = max(0, head - 1)`.
--- check case | Moving left from cell 0 and writing there
run_tm({"states": {"a", "b", "c", "y", "n"}, "alphabet": "01", "tape": "01x_", "delta": {("a", "0"): ("b", "x", "L"), ("b", "x"): ("c", "1", "L"), ("c", "1"): ("y", "0", "R")}, "start": "a", "accept": "y", "reject": "n"}, "00", 100)
=> ("accept", "00", 3)
--- check case | Bouncing off the left end on a longer tape
run_tm({"states": {"s", "t", "y", "n"}, "alphabet": "ab", "tape": "ab_", "delta": {("s", "a"): ("t", "b", "L"), ("t", "b"): ("y", "a", "R")}, "start": "s", "accept": "y", "reject": "n"}, "abba", 10)
=> ("accept", "abba", 2)
--- check test | 200 seeded random machines that move left a lot agree with the definition
(lambda R, TM: all((lambda M, w: run_tm(M, w, 40) == TM(M, w, 40))((lambda Q: {"states": set(Q) | {"y", "n"}, "alphabet": "01", "tape": "01_", "delta": {(q, s): (R.choice(Q + ["y"] if R.random() < 0.15 else Q), R.choice("01_"), "L" if R.random() < 0.6 else "R") for q in Q for s in "01_"}, "start": "q0", "accept": "y", "reject": "n"})(["q0", "q1", "q2"]), "".join(R.choice("01") for _ in range(R.randint(0, 5)))) for _ in range(200)))(__import__("random").Random(22), (lambda M, w, K: (lambda t, s, tw: [(lambda r: s.__setitem__(0, M["reject"]) if r is None else (t.__setitem__(s[1], r[1]), s.__setitem__(0, r[0]), s.__setitem__(1, max(0, s[1] + (1 if r[2] == "R" else -1))), s.__setitem__(2, s[2] + 1), t.append("_") if s[1] == len(t) else None))(M["delta"].get((s[0], t[s[1]]))) for _ in tw(lambda _: s[0] not in (M["accept"], M["reject"]), range(K))] and 0 or (("accept" if s[0] == M["accept"] else "reject" if s[0] == M["reject"] else "running"), "".join(t).rstrip("_"), s[2]))(list(w) + ["_"], [M["start"], 0, 0], __import__("itertools").takewhile)))

+++ practice | Checking a multiplication
--- task
A flight computer receives a schedule written as aⁱbʲcᵏ: i ground stations, j passes per station, and k slots booked. It should accept only when every pass has a slot: k = i · j, with i, j and k all at least 1. Write `times_tm()` that returns a decider over the alphabet `"abc"` for {aⁱbʲcᵏ : i, j, k ≥ 1 and k = i·j}. It must reject strings out of order, such as `"abca"` or `"acb"`, and halt on every input.

A plan, as an implementation description:

1. Check the form a⁺b⁺c⁺ in one sweep to the right, marking the first `a` as `X` so that the left end can be found again; then return to it.
2. For each `a` (the `X` first, then crossing off each further `a` as `x`): for each `b`, cross off the `b` (write `y`) and one `c` (write `z`); if no `c` is left, reject. Then turn the `y`s back into `b`s.
3. When every `a` is crossed off, accept if no `c` is left over, and reject otherwise.
--- starter
def times_tm():
    return {"states": {"f0", "acc", "rej"}, "alphabet": "abc", "tape": "abc_", "delta": {}, "start": "f0", "accept": "acc", "reject": "rej"}
--- solution
def times_tm():
    d = {}
    # 1. Check the form a+b+c+, marking the first a, then go back to it.
    d[("f0", "a")] = ("f1", "X", "R")
    d[("f1", "a")] = ("f1", "a", "R")
    d[("f1", "b")] = ("f2", "b", "R")
    d[("f2", "b")] = ("f2", "b", "R")
    d[("f2", "c")] = ("f3", "c", "R")
    d[("f3", "c")] = ("f3", "c", "R")
    d[("f3", "_")] = ("back0", "_", "L")
    for s in "abc":
        d[("back0", s)] = ("back0", s, "L")
    d[("back0", "X")] = ("to_b", "X", "R")
    # 2. Pair every b with a c, for the current a.
    d[("to_b", "a")] = ("to_b", "a", "R")
    d[("to_b", "b")] = ("to_c", "y", "R")
    for s in "bz":
        d[("to_c", s)] = ("to_c", s, "R")
    d[("to_c", "c")] = ("back_b", "z", "L")
    for s in "zb":
        d[("back_b", s)] = ("back_b", s, "L")
    d[("back_b", "y")] = ("next_b", "y", "R")
    d[("next_b", "b")] = ("to_c", "y", "R")
    d[("next_b", "z")] = ("restore", "z", "L")
    # Turn y back into b, then move on to the next a.
    d[("restore", "y")] = ("restore", "b", "L")
    d[("restore", "a")] = ("back_a", "a", "L")
    d[("restore", "x")] = ("next_a", "x", "R")
    d[("restore", "X")] = ("next_a", "X", "R")
    d[("back_a", "a")] = ("back_a", "a", "L")
    d[("back_a", "x")] = ("next_a", "x", "R")
    d[("back_a", "X")] = ("next_a", "X", "R")
    d[("next_a", "a")] = ("to_b", "x", "R")
    # 3. Every a is used: no c may be left.
    d[("next_a", "b")] = ("check", "b", "R")
    for s in "bz":
        d[("check", s)] = ("check", s, "R")
    d[("check", "_")] = ("acc", "_", "R")
    states = {q for q, s in d} | {"acc", "rej"}
    return {"states": states, "alphabet": "abc", "tape": "abcXxyz_", "delta": d,
            "start": "f0", "accept": "acc", "reject": "rej"}
--- hint
Write the machine in three groups of states, one per step of the plan, and give each state a name that says what it is doing (`to_b`, `to_c`, `back_b`, …). Missing transitions reject, so only write the moves that belong to a correct schedule.
--- hint
Inside step 2: after crossing off a `c`, run left over `z`s and `b`s to the last `y`, and step right. If that cell holds a `b`, pair it; if it holds a `z`, every `b` is paired for this `a`, so run left turning `y` back into `b` until you reach the `a` region.
--- hint
To find the next `a`, run left over uncrossed `a`s to the last crossed one (`x` or `X`) and step right. If that cell is a `b`, every `a` is done: run right over `b`s and `z`s, and accept only on a blank, since a `c` there would be unpaired.

?? i, j or k equal to 0 must be rejected, and so must a c left over after the last a.
