+++ question | Recogniser or decider
--- ask
A Turing machine accepts every string in L. On strings not in L, it sometimes rejects and sometimes runs forever. What can you say?
--- choice
L is decidable, and this machine is a decider for it.
--- choice correct
L is Turing-recognisable; this machine is not a decider, and it does not tell you whether L is decidable.
--- choice
L is not decidable.
--- choice
L is regular, because the machine accepts every string of L.
--- why
The machine recognises L: its accepted strings are exactly L. A decider must halt on every input, and this one does not. But some other machine might decide L, so you cannot conclude that L is undecidable from one machine. Lesson 13 shows languages that really are recognisable but not decidable.

+++ question | The next configuration
--- ask
A machine is in the configuration `a b q₃ b a`: the tape holds `abba`, the state is q₃, and the head is on the second `b`. Its transition is δ(q₃, b) = (q₄, x, L). What is the next configuration?
--- choice
`a b x q₄ a`
--- choice correct
`a q₄ b x a`
--- choice
`a b q₄ x a`
--- choice
`q₄ a b x a`
--- why
The head writes `x` over the `b` it is reading, giving the tape `abxa`, then moves one cell left, onto the first `b`. In u q v notation the state is written just before the symbol under the head: `a q₄ b x a`.

+++ question | A missing transition
--- ask
In this course's convention, what happens when a Turing machine is in state q, reading symbol s, and δ(q, s) is not defined?
--- choice
The machine stays in q and waits.
--- choice correct
The machine halts and rejects.
--- choice
The machine halts and accepts if the tape is empty.
--- choice
The machine moves right and tries again.
--- why
Following Sipser's book, an undefined transition goes to the reject state. It lets a table list only the moves of a correct run: anything unexpected rejects. Some books instead say the machine halts without accepting, which comes to the same thing for deciding a language.

=== theory-12 | The Church–Turing thesis and the universal machine
--- teach
Last lesson's Turing machine has one tape, one head and tiny steps. It looks too clumsy to matter. This lesson shows why it matters anyway: machines with more tapes, with guessing, with stacks, and real programming languages can all be simulated by it, and it can simulate them. So "computable by a Turing machine" is a solid notion, and the **Church–Turing thesis** says it is exactly what "computable by an algorithm" means. Then comes the idea that made modern computers possible: a single **universal machine** that runs any machine it is given as data. You will implement the encoding of machines as strings and the universal machine itself.

### Equal power by simulation

Picture two kitchens with different equipment. If the cooks of each can imitate any step of the other with their own tools, even slowly, then they can cook exactly the same dishes. Models of computation are compared the same way: model A is **at least as powerful** as model B when every B-machine can be simulated by an A-machine that accepts, rejects and loops on the same inputs. Two models are **equivalent** when each simulates the other. The simulations below also say how much slower they are, which will matter in lesson 15.

### Staying put

Let a machine's moves be L, R or **S** (stay). A standard TM can imitate a stay: move right into an extra state, which moves back left whatever it reads. The order matters: right-then-left always returns to the same cell, but left-then-right from cell 0 would end up on cell 1, because a left move at cell 0 does nothing. Each stay costs two steps, so the running time at most doubles.

### Many tapes

A **k-tape Turing machine** has k tapes, each with its own head. The input starts on tape 1, the others are blank, and one transition reads all k symbols under the heads, writes k symbols and moves each head separately:

δ : Q × Γᵏ → Q × Γᵏ × {L, R, S}ᵏ.

Extra tapes make programming easier and machines faster. A 2-tape machine checks palindromes in about 3n steps: copy the input onto tape 2, move tape 2's head back to the start, then walk tape 1 backwards and tape 2 forwards, comparing. Every one-tape machine needs time proportional to n² for palindromes (last lesson's crossing sequences).

**Theorem.** Every multitape TM has an equivalent single-tape TM.

**Proof idea.** Write the k tapes one after another on the single tape, separated by `#`, and mark the cell under each head by using a **dotted** copy of its symbol (so the tape alphabet gains a dotted version of every symbol). Put a marker in the first cell so the machine can find the left end. To simulate one step, sweep from the left end to the last `#` to read the k dotted symbols (remembering them in the state), then sweep again to write the new symbols and move the dots. If a head moves onto a `#`, that tape needs a new cell: shift everything to its right one cell over, and write a blank.

**Cost.** In t steps no tape uses more than t + n cells, so the single tape has length O(t + n) and each simulated step takes O(t + n) moves. A run of t steps becomes O(t²) steps (for t ≥ n): a **polynomial** slowdown.

### Guessing

A **nondeterministic TM** has a set of possible moves for each state and symbol, δ : Q × Γ → P(Q × Γ × {L, R}). Its runs form a tree of configurations, and it accepts w if **some** branch reaches the accept state.

**Theorem.** Every nondeterministic TM has an equivalent deterministic TM.

**Proof idea.** Search the tree of configurations **breadth first**: all configurations after 1 step, then after 2 steps, and so on, accepting as soon as an accepting one appears. Depth-first search would be wrong: it can follow one infinite branch forever while another branch accepts in three steps. If every branch halts without accepting, the tree is finite (each configuration has finitely many children, and a tree like that with no infinite branch has finitely many nodes), so the search runs out of configurations and can reject; if some branch runs forever and none accepts, the search runs forever too, which is what the nondeterministic machine does.

**Cost.** If each configuration has at most b choices, the tree has up to bᵗ configurations at depth t. The simulation takes time **exponential** in t. Nobody knows how to do fundamentally better in general. Whether guessing can be simulated with only polynomial slowdown is the P versus NP question of lesson 15.

### Other models, the same power

- **A two-way infinite tape** is no stronger: fold it in half, and keep the left half on the odd cells.
- **Two stacks** are as strong as a tape: keep the part of the tape left of the head on one stack and the rest on the other, and a move pops from one and pushes onto the other. So a PDA with **two** stacks is as powerful as a Turing machine; the limit of lessons 9 and 10 was having only one.
- **Enumerators**, machines that print a list of strings, list exactly the Turing-recognisable languages. Turning a recogniser into an enumerator needs care, because the recogniser may loop on some strings; the trick, **dovetailing**, is in the practice.
- **Random-access machines** (with memory addressed by numbers, like a real CPU), the **lambda calculus**, and every general-purpose programming language: each can simulate a TM and be simulated by one. Your Python TM simulator is half of that proof for Python.

### The Church–Turing thesis

All these models, invented independently for different reasons, turned out to compute exactly the same functions. That is the evidence for the **[[Church–Turing thesis|church]]**:

**Every function that can be computed by an algorithm (a finite, mechanical, step-by-step procedure) can be computed by a Turing machine.**

It is a **thesis**, not a theorem, because "algorithm" is an informal idea. It cannot be proved; it could only be refuted, by a procedure everyone agrees is mechanical that no TM can imitate, and no one has ever found one. What it gives you is permission: to show a problem is decidable, describe an algorithm in words, and trust that it becomes a TM. And to show a problem is **not** decidable, which is what lessons 13 and 14 do, you prove that no TM can decide it, and the thesis says no algorithm of any kind can. Questions like **[[Hilbert's tenth problem|hilbert10]]** could only be settled once that precise meaning existed.

### Machines as data

A Turing machine is a finite object, so it can be written as a string, just as a graph or a grammar can. Write **⟨M⟩** for the encoding of M, and **⟨M, w⟩** for the encoding of M together with an input w. This course uses a binary encoding:

- Number the states 1, 2, 3, … with the **start state 1, the accept state 2 and the reject state 3**; the other states get 4, 5, … in any order.
- Number the tape symbols with the **blank as 1**, and the other symbols of the tape alphabet 2, 3, … in the order they appear in the machine's `"tape"` string.
- Number the directions L = 1 and R = 2.
- Write a whole number m in unary as m zeros. A transition δ(qᵢ, sⱼ) = (q_k, s_l, D) becomes five unary numbers separated by single `1`s: 0ⁱ 1 0ʲ 1 0ᵏ 1 0ˡ 1 0ᴰ.
- ⟨M⟩ is all the transitions, in any order, separated by `11`. ⟨M, w⟩ is ⟨M⟩, then `111`, then the numbers of w's symbols in unary separated by single `1`s (nothing at all when w is empty).

For example, the machine whose only transition is δ(q₁, ␣) = (q₂, ␣, R), "accept if the input is empty", has ⟨M⟩ = `0 1 0 1 00 1 0 1 00`, that is `01010010100`, and ⟨M, ε⟩ = `01010010100111`. Inside ⟨M⟩, `1`s come at most two in a row, so the first `111` always marks where the input begins.

Reading unary fields back is a split and a length:

```python
print("000100010".split("1"))                     # ['000', '000', '0']
print([len(f) for f in "000100010".split("1")])   # [3, 3, 1]
print("000110".split("1"))                        # ['000', '', '0']
```

The last line shows why every field must be at least one zero: `11` splits into an empty part, so an empty field could not be told apart from a separator.

### The universal Turing machine

**Theorem (Turing, 1936).** There is a Turing machine U that, on input ⟨M, w⟩, simulates M on w: U accepts if M accepts w, rejects if M rejects w, and runs forever if M runs forever on w.

**How it works.** U keeps three things: the code ⟨M⟩, M's tape (each cell holding a symbol number), and M's current state number and head position. To simulate one step, U scans ⟨M⟩ for the transition whose first two fields match the current state and symbol, then updates the tape, head and state as that transition says. If there is no such transition, M rejects, and so does U. Built with three tapes and converted to one tape by the theorem above, U is a single ordinary Turing machine, with a fixed, finite table, that can run **every** Turing machine.

**Cost.** Each simulated step scans the code once, so U is slower than M by a factor that depends on the size of ⟨M⟩ and, on one tape, on the length of the run; the overhead is polynomial. (With two tapes, a slowdown of only O(t log t) is possible, a result of Hennie and Stearns from 1966.)

This is the idea of the **[[stored-program computer|stored-program]]**: one fixed machine, with the program held in memory as data. Every interpreter and virtual machine is a universal machine. And because machines can now read the codes of machines, a machine can be asked questions about **itself**, which is how lesson 13 proves that the halting problem cannot be solved.

### Turing complete

A system is **Turing complete** when it can simulate any Turing machine, given as much memory as it needs. Every general-purpose programming language is. So, surprisingly, are **[[Conway's Game of Life|life]]**, C++ templates, SQL with recursive queries, and the card game Magic: The Gathering. Strictly, a real computer has finite memory, so it is an enormous finite automaton; the Turing machine is still the right model, because the memory is so large and can always be extended.

### Where it is used

- **Interpreters and virtual machines:** the CPython interpreter, the Java virtual machine and WebAssembly engines each read a program as data and run it. The Python you write in ORBIT runs in **[[Pyodide|pyodide]]**: CPython compiled to WebAssembly, run by your browser's WebAssembly engine. That is a universal machine running inside a universal machine.
- **Emulators** run the code of one processor on another, like the RISC-V emulator of the Computer Organization course.
- **Proofs of impossibility:** once "algorithm" means "Turing machine", results like the halting problem apply to every programming language at once.

**Watch out:**

- **The thesis is not a theorem**, and it is about **what** can be computed, not how fast. The simulations above cost polynomial time, except nondeterminism, which costs exponential time as far as anyone knows.
- **Depth first is wrong for guessing machines.** A single infinite branch traps a depth-first search.
- **The empty input.** A simulator must still give the machine a blank to read in cell 0.
- **Stay moves at the left end.** Simulate a stay as right-then-left, never left-then-right.

::: context church Church and Turing
In 1936 Alonzo Church, at Princeton, proposed that the functions computable by an effective method are exactly those definable in his **lambda calculus**, a notation for functions that is the ancestor of functional programming. Months later Turing, in Cambridge, independently proposed his machines, and proved they compute exactly the lambda-definable functions. Turing then went to Princeton to do his doctorate with Church. The two names are joined because the two definitions coincide, which is itself strong evidence that they capture something real.
:::

::: context hilbert10 Hilbert's tenth problem
In 1900 David Hilbert asked for a method that decides whether a polynomial equation with integer coefficients, such as x² + y² = z², has a solution in whole numbers. Seventy years later Yuri Matiyasevich, completing work by Martin Davis, Hilary Putnam and Julia Robinson, proved that no such method exists. The proof needed a precise meaning for "method", and the Church–Turing thesis supplied it: they showed that no Turing machine can solve the problem, and so no algorithm can.
:::

::: context stored-program The stored-program computer
Early computers such as ENIAC were programmed by setting switches and plugging cables, so changing the program meant rewiring the machine. In 1945 John von Neumann's report on the EDVAC described a computer that keeps its program in the same memory as its data, and runs whatever program it finds there. That is Turing's universal machine made of electronics, and almost every computer since, including the one you are using, follows that design.
:::

::: context life The Game of Life
John Conway's Game of Life (1970) is a grid of cells, each alive or dead, where every cell's next state depends only on its eight neighbours: a live cell survives with two or three live neighbours, and a dead cell comes alive with exactly three. From these rules, patterns emerge that move, collide and copy themselves, and they can be arranged into logic gates and memory. Conway and others showed in 1982 that it can simulate any Turing machine, and in 2000 Paul Rendell built a working Turing machine pattern.
:::

::: context pyodide Pyodide
Pyodide is the CPython interpreter, written in C, compiled to WebAssembly, a compact instruction format that browsers can run safely and quickly. When you press Run in ORBIT, your browser's WebAssembly engine runs the compiled interpreter, which reads your Python code as data and runs it. Each layer is a universal machine for the one above it, which is why the same programs run in the browser as on a laptop, only more slowly.
:::
--- task
Turing machines are dictionaries as in lesson 11. Implement the encoding and the universal machine of the lesson.

- `encode_tm(tm, w)`: return the string ⟨M, w⟩ for the machine `tm` and the input string `w`, exactly as the lesson describes: start state 1, accept state 2, reject state 3, other states 4, 5, … in any order; the blank `"_"` is symbol 1 and the other symbols of `tm["tape"]` are 2, 3, … in the order they appear there; L is 1 and R is 2; each transition is five unary fields joined by `"1"`; transitions are joined by `"11"` in any order; then `"111"`, then w's symbol numbers in unary joined by `"1"`.
- `universal(code, max_steps)`: `code` is any string ⟨M, w⟩ in this format. Decode it and run the machine on its input with the conventions of lesson 11 (head on cell 0 with a blank there if the input is empty, left moves stop at cell 0, a missing transition rejects without counting a step, halting on state 2 or 3). Return `(result, tape, steps)`: `result` is `"accept"`, `"reject"` or `"running"`, `tape` is a **tuple of symbol numbers** from cell 0 up to the last cell that is not blank (so blanks at the end, number 1, are dropped), and `steps` is the number of transitions taken, at most `max_steps`. It must not look at state or symbol names: everything it needs is in the code.
--- starter
def encode_tm(tm, w):
    return "111"


def universal(code, max_steps):
    return ("reject", (), 0)
--- solution
def encode_tm(tm, w):
    number = {tm["start"]: 1, tm["accept"]: 2, tm["reject"]: 3}
    for q in tm["states"]:
        if q not in number:
            number[q] = len(number) + 1
    symbol = {"_": 1}
    for s in tm["tape"]:
        if s not in symbol:
            symbol[s] = len(symbol) + 1
    parts = []
    for (q, s), (r, t, d) in tm["delta"].items():
        fields = [number[q], symbol[s], number[r], symbol[t], 1 if d == "L" else 2]
        parts.append("1".join("0" * f for f in fields))
    return "11".join(parts) + "111" + "1".join("0" * symbol[c] for c in w)


def decode(code):
    machine, _, word = code.partition("111")
    delta = {}
    if machine:
        for part in machine.split("11"):
            q, s, r, t, d = (len(f) for f in part.split("1"))
            delta[(q, s)] = (r, t, d)
    tape = [len(f) for f in word.split("1")] if word else []
    return delta, tape


def universal(code, max_steps):
    delta, tape = decode(code)
    if not tape:
        tape = [1]
    state, head, steps = 1, 0, 0
    while state != 2 and state != 3:
        if steps == max_steps:
            break
        move = delta.get((state, tape[head]))
        if move is None:
            state = 3
            break
        state, tape[head], d = move
        if d == 2:
            head += 1
            if head == len(tape):
                tape.append(1)
        else:
            head = max(0, head - 1)
        steps += 1
    while tape and tape[-1] == 1:
        tape.pop()
    result = {2: "accept", 3: "reject"}.get(state, "running")
    return (result, tuple(tape), steps)
--- hint
For `encode_tm`, build two dictionaries first: state → number (start, accept and reject fixed, the rest counted on from 4) and symbol → number (`"_"` first as 1, then the tape string's other symbols). Then turn each transition into its five numbers and each number into that many `"0"`s.
--- hint
For `universal`, cut the code at the first `"111"` with `code.partition("111")`. Split the machine part on `"11"` into transitions and each transition on `"1"` into five fields; the length of a field is its number. The input part, if not empty, splits on `"1"` the same way.
--- hint
Then run exactly like `run_tm` of lesson 11, but with numbers: start in state 1, stop in state 2 or 3, look up `(state, symbol)`, and treat direction 1 as L and 2 as R. The blank is 1, so a new cell is `1`, and the returned tuple drops trailing `1`s.

+++ practice | Removing stay moves
--- task
A machine may also have the move `"S"` (stay). Write `remove_stay(tm)` that returns an ordinary machine (moves only `"L"` and `"R"`) with the same results and the same final tape on every input. Replace each transition `(q, s) → (r, t, "S")` by `(q, s) → (("stay", r), t, "R")`, and give the new state `("stay", r)` a transition for **every** symbol `x` of the tape alphabet: `(("stay", r), x) → (r, x, "L")`. Keep every other transition as it is, and add the new states to `"states"`. Do not change `tm`.

Moving right then left always comes back to the same cell, even at cell 0. (Left then right would not: from cell 0 the left move does nothing, and the right move then lands on cell 1.)
--- starter
def remove_stay(tm):
    return tm
--- solution
def remove_stay(tm):
    delta = {}
    states = set(tm["states"])
    for (q, s), (r, t, d) in tm["delta"].items():
        if d == "S":
            back = ("stay", r)
            states.add(back)
            delta[(q, s)] = (back, t, "R")
            for x in tm["tape"]:
                delta[(back, x)] = (r, x, "L")
        else:
            delta[(q, s)] = (r, t, d)
    return {**tm, "states": states, "delta": delta}
--- hint
Build a new `delta` dictionary. A transition with `"L"` or `"R"` is copied; one with `"S"` becomes a right move into `("stay", r)`.
--- hint
The state `("stay", r)` must move left without changing anything, whatever it reads: one transition per symbol of `tm["tape"]`, writing back the same symbol.

+++ practice | Simulating a guessing machine
--- task
A nondeterministic TM is a dictionary like a TM, except that `"delta"` maps `(state, symbol)` to a **set** of moves `(new state, symbol, "L" or "R")`; a missing key means no move, and that branch rejects. Write `ntm_run(ntm, w, max_steps)` that searches the tree of configurations breadth first, one level of depth at a time:

- return `"accept"` if some configuration within `max_steps` steps of the start is in the accept state;
- return `"reject"` if every branch has halted without accepting within `max_steps` steps (the tree has died out);
- otherwise return `"unknown"`.

A configuration is `(state, head, tape)` with the tape as a tuple; start with `(start, 0, tuple(w))`, or a single blank for the empty input. Use the conventions of lesson 11 for moves. Depth-first search would fail here: one branch of a machine in the checks runs forever.
--- starter
def ntm_run(ntm, w, max_steps):
    return "unknown"
--- solution
def ntm_run(ntm, w, max_steps):
    frontier = {(ntm["start"], 0, tuple(w) if w else ("_",))}
    for depth in range(max_steps + 1):
        if any(q == ntm["accept"] for q, _, _ in frontier):
            return "accept"
        frontier = {c for c in frontier if c[0] != ntm["reject"]}
        if not frontier:
            return "reject"
        if depth == max_steps:
            return "unknown"
        following = set()
        for q, head, tape in frontier:
            for r, t, d in ntm["delta"].get((q, tape[head]), set()):
                cells = list(tape)
                cells[head] = t
                h = head + 1 if d == "R" else max(0, head - 1)
                if h == len(cells):
                    cells.append("_")
                following.add((r, h, tuple(cells)))
        frontier = following
    return "unknown"
--- hint
Keep a set `frontier` of the configurations at the current depth. At each depth: accept if one is in the accept state; drop those in the reject state; reject if nothing is left; stop with `"unknown"` at depth `max_steps`; otherwise build the next level from every move of every configuration.
--- hint
A configuration with no moves simply has no children, so its branch disappears, which is how a missing transition rejects. Using sets merges branches that reach the same configuration.
--- check test | "Some symbol equals the one two places later": right on every string up to length 8, never unknown

+++ practice | A machine with several tapes
--- task
Write `run_multitape(tm, w, max_steps)` for k-tape machines. The dictionary has `"tapes"`: k. Its `"delta"` maps `(state, (s1, …, sk))`, the symbols under the k heads, to `(new state, (t1, …, tk), (d1, …, dk))`: the symbols to write and each head's move, `"L"`, `"R"` or `"S"` (stay). The input starts on tape 1 and the other tapes are blank. Otherwise the conventions of lesson 11 hold for every tape: heads start on cell 0, left moves stop at cell 0, a missing transition rejects without counting a step.

Return `(result, tapes, steps)`: `result` as in lesson 11, and `tapes` a tuple of k strings, each tape from cell 0 to its last cell that is not blank. The checks include a 2-tape machine that decides palindromes in at most 3n + 4 steps, where any one-tape machine needs time proportional to n².
--- starter
def run_multitape(tm, w, max_steps):
    return ("reject", (w,), 0)
--- solution
def run_multitape(tm, w, max_steps):
    k = tm["tapes"]
    tapes = [list(w) if w else ["_"]] + [["_"] for _ in range(k - 1)]
    heads = [0] * k
    state, steps = tm["start"], 0
    while state != tm["accept"] and state != tm["reject"]:
        if steps == max_steps:
            break
        under = tuple(tapes[i][heads[i]] for i in range(k))
        move = tm["delta"].get((state, under))
        if move is None:
            state = tm["reject"]
            break
        state, writes, moves = move
        for i in range(k):
            tapes[i][heads[i]] = writes[i]
            if moves[i] == "R":
                heads[i] += 1
                if heads[i] == len(tapes[i]):
                    tapes[i].append("_")
            elif moves[i] == "L":
                heads[i] = max(0, heads[i] - 1)
        steps += 1
    result = {tm["accept"]: "accept", tm["reject"]: "reject"}.get(state, "running")
    return (result, tuple("".join(t).rstrip("_") for t in tapes), steps)
--- hint
Keep a list of tapes (each a list of symbols) and a list of head positions. Each step reads the tuple of symbols under the heads, looks it up with the state, and then writes and moves each tape in turn.
--- hint
A stay leaves the head where it is. At the end, join each tape into a string, strip the trailing blanks, and return them as a tuple.

+++ practice | Is this a valid encoding?
--- task
Not every binary string is a machine's code. Write `valid_code(s)` that returns `True` exactly when `s` is a well-formed ⟨M, w⟩ in the lesson's format:

- `s` is a machine part, then `"111"`, then an input part;
- the machine part is empty (a machine with no transitions) or one or more transitions separated by `"11"`;
- each transition is five fields separated by single `"1"`s, each field one or more `"0"`s, and the last field (the direction) is `"0"` or `"00"`;
- no two transitions have the same first two fields (a Turing machine has one move per state and symbol);
- the input part is empty, or one or more fields of `"0"`s separated by single `"1"`s.

Watch the edge cases: `"111"` alone is valid (no transitions, empty input); `""` is not; neither is a string with four `1`s in a row, a leading or trailing `1` in the wrong place, or a direction of three zeros.
--- starter
def valid_code(s):
    return "111" in s
--- solution
import re

TRANSITION = r"(0+1){4}0{1,2}"
CODE = re.compile(rf"({TRANSITION}(11{TRANSITION})*)?111(0+(10+)*)?")


def valid_code(s):
    if not CODE.fullmatch(s):
        return False
    machine = s.partition("111")[0]
    if not machine:
        return True
    keys = [tuple(part.split("1")[:2]) for part in machine.split("11")]
    return len(keys) == len(set(keys))
--- hint
The shape is regular, so a regular expression can check it: a transition is `(0+1){4}0{1,2}`, the machine part is an optional transition followed by any number of `11` plus a transition, then `111`, then an optional `0+(10+)*`. Use `re.fullmatch` so the whole string must match.
--- hint
The "same first two fields" rule is not about shape. After the pattern matches, cut at the first `"111"`, split the machine part into transitions, and compare their first two fields for repeats.
--- check case | The lesson's example, with and without an input
(valid_code("01010010100111"), valid_code("01010010100111" + "0010"), valid_code("01010010100111" + "001"))
=> (True, True, False)
--- check case | "111" alone is valid; the empty string and a lone transition without "111" are not
(valid_code("111"), valid_code(""), valid_code("01010010100"), valid_code("1110"), valid_code("11101"))
=> (True, False, False, True, False)
--- check case | A direction of three zeros, an empty field, and four 1s in a row
(valid_code("0101001010" + "000" + "111"), valid_code("01010010110" + "0111"), valid_code("01010010100" + "1111" + "0"))
=> (False, False, False)
--- check case | Two transitions for the same state and symbol are invalid; different ones are fine
(valid_code("01010010100" + "11" + "0101000100" + "111"), valid_code("01010010100" + "11" + "0100100010100" + "111"))
=> (False, True)
--- check test | Every code your lesson's encoder would make for 50 seeded random machines is valid, and damaged ones are not
(lambda R, ENC: all((lambda code: valid_code(code) and not valid_code(code + "1") and not valid_code("1" + code) and not valid_code(code.replace("111", "1111", 1)))(ENC((lambda Q: {"states": set(Q) | {"y", "n"}, "alphabet": "01", "tape": "01x_", "delta": {(q, s): (R.choice(Q + ["y", "n"]), R.choice("01x_"), R.choice("LR")) for q in Q for s in "01x_" if R.random() < 0.8}, "start": "q0", "accept": "y", "reject": "n"})(["q0", "q1", "q2"]), "".join(R.choice("01") for _ in range(R.randint(1, 5))))) for _ in range(50)))(__import__("random").Random(50), (lambda M, w: (lambda num, sym: "11".join("1".join("0" * f for f in (num[q], sym[s], num[r], sym[t], 1 if d == "L" else 2)) for (q, s), (r, t, d) in M["delta"].items()) + "111" + "1".join("0" * sym[c] for c in w))({**{q: i for i, q in enumerate(sorted((q for q in M["states"] if q not in (M["start"], M["accept"], M["reject"])), key=repr), start=4)}, M["start"]: 1, M["accept"]: 2, M["reject"]: 3}, {s: i for i, s in enumerate(["_"] + [c for c in M["tape"] if c != "_"], start=1)})))

+++ practice | Debug: the universal machine and the empty input
--- task
This `universal(code, max_steps)` runs a machine from its code ⟨M, w⟩ and returns `(result, tape, steps)` as in the lesson. It works for every non-empty input, but when w is empty, `"".split("1")` gives `[""]`, so it puts a symbol numbered **0** on the tape, a symbol that does not exist, instead of one blank (number 1). A machine that should accept the empty input by reading a blank finds no transition for symbol 0 and rejects. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
def universal(code, max_steps):
    machine, _, word = code.partition("111")
    delta = {}
    if machine:
        for part in machine.split("11"):
            q, s, r, t, d = (len(f) for f in part.split("1"))
            delta[(q, s)] = (r, t, d)
    tape = [len(f) for f in word.split("1")]
    state, head, steps = 1, 0, 0
    while state != 2 and state != 3:
        if steps == max_steps:
            break
        move = delta.get((state, tape[head]))
        if move is None:
            state = 3
            break
        state, tape[head], d = move
        if d == 2:
            head += 1
            if head == len(tape):
                tape.append(1)
        else:
            head = max(0, head - 1)
        steps += 1
    while tape and tape[-1] == 1:
        tape.pop()
    result = {2: "accept", 3: "reject"}.get(state, "running")
    return (result, tuple(tape), steps)
--- solution
def universal(code, max_steps):
    machine, _, word = code.partition("111")
    delta = {}
    if machine:
        for part in machine.split("11"):
            q, s, r, t, d = (len(f) for f in part.split("1"))
            delta[(q, s)] = (r, t, d)
    tape = [len(f) for f in word.split("1")] if word else [1]
    state, head, steps = 1, 0, 0
    while state != 2 and state != 3:
        if steps == max_steps:
            break
        move = delta.get((state, tape[head]))
        if move is None:
            state = 3
            break
        state, tape[head], d = move
        if d == 2:
            head += 1
            if head == len(tape):
                tape.append(1)
        else:
            head = max(0, head - 1)
        steps += 1
    while tape and tape[-1] == 1:
        tape.pop()
    result = {2: "accept", 3: "reject"}.get(state, "running")
    return (result, tuple(tape), steps)
--- hint
Try `"".split("1")` in Python. What list does it give, and what does the line that builds the tape turn it into?
--- hint
When the input part is empty, the tape must start as one blank cell, `[1]`.
--- check case | Accept if the input is empty
(universal("01010010100111", 10), universal("01010010100111" + "00", 10))
=> (("accept", (), 1), ("reject", (2,), 0))
--- check case | A machine that writes symbol 2 on the blank tape, then accepts
universal("0101001001" + "00" + "111", 10)
=> ("accept", (2,), 1)
--- check test | 150 seeded random machines on inputs that are often empty
(lambda R, TM, ENC: all((lambda M, w: (lambda ref, sym: universal(ENC(M, w), 40) == (ref[0], tuple(sym[c] for c in ref[1]), ref[2]))(TM(M, w, 40), {s: i for i, s in enumerate(["_"] + [c for c in M["tape"] if c != "_"], start=1)}))((lambda Q: {"states": set(Q) | {"y", "n"}, "alphabet": "01", "tape": "01_", "delta": {(q, s): (R.choice(Q + ["y", "n"] if R.random() < 0.3 else Q), R.choice("01_"), R.choice("LR")) for q in Q for s in "01_"}, "start": "q0", "accept": "y", "reject": "n"})(["q0", "q1"]), "" if R.random() < 0.5 else "".join(R.choice("01") for _ in range(R.randint(1, 4)))) for _ in range(150)))(__import__("random").Random(60), (lambda M, w, K: (lambda t, s, tw: [(lambda r: s.__setitem__(0, M["reject"]) if r is None else (t.__setitem__(s[1], r[1]), s.__setitem__(0, r[0]), s.__setitem__(1, max(0, s[1] + (1 if r[2] == "R" else -1))), s.__setitem__(2, s[2] + 1), t.append("_") if s[1] == len(t) else None))(M["delta"].get((s[0], t[s[1]]))) for _ in tw(lambda _: s[0] not in (M["accept"], M["reject"]), range(K))] and 0 or (("accept" if s[0] == M["accept"] else "reject" if s[0] == M["reject"] else "running"), "".join(t).rstrip("_"), s[2]))(list(w) + ["_"], [M["start"], 0, 0], __import__("itertools").takewhile)), (lambda M, w: (lambda num, sym: "11".join("1".join("0" * f for f in (num[q], sym[s], num[r], sym[t], 1 if d == "L" else 2)) for (q, s), (r, t, d) in M["delta"].items()) + "111" + "1".join("0" * sym[c] for c in w))({**{q: i for i, q in enumerate(sorted((q for q in M["states"] if q not in (M["start"], M["accept"], M["reject"])), key=repr), start=4)}, M["start"]: 1, M["accept"]: 2, M["reject"]: 3}, {s: i for i, s in enumerate(["_"] + [c for c in M["tape"] if c != "_"], start=1)})))

+++ practice | Listing a language whose machine may loop
--- task
A ground station has a recogniser for valid command strings: `recognizer(w, t)` returns `True` if the check accepts `w` within `t` steps. On some strings the check runs forever, so `recognizer(w, t)` is `False` for every `t`. The team wants a **list** of valid commands: an enumerator. Trying the strings one at a time would get stuck on the first string whose check loops. Instead, **dovetail**: give every string a little more time in each round.

Write `dovetail(recognizer, sigma, k, max_rounds)`. In round t, for t = 1, 2, …, `max_rounds`: go through the **first t strings** over the alphabet `sigma` in shortlex order (shorter first, then in the alphabet's order, starting with `""`), and for each one not listed yet, if `recognizer(w, t)` is `True`, add it to the list. Return the list as soon as it holds `k` strings, or after round `max_rounds` otherwise. Every string of the language appears eventually, however slow its check, and a string that loops never blocks the others.
--- starter
def dovetail(recognizer, sigma, k, max_rounds):
    return [w for w in [""] if recognizer(w, max_rounds)][:k]
--- solution
from itertools import count, product


def shortlex(sigma):
    for n in count():
        for letters in product(sigma, repeat=n):
            yield "".join(letters)


def dovetail(recognizer, sigma, k, max_rounds):
    source = shortlex(sigma)
    strings = []
    found = []
    listed = set()
    for t in range(1, max_rounds + 1):
        strings.append(next(source))
        for w in strings:
            if w not in listed and recognizer(w, t):
                found.append(w)
                listed.add(w)
                if len(found) == k:
                    return found
    return found
--- hint
Write a generator that yields the strings of Σ∗ in shortlex order (lengths 0, 1, 2, …, and `itertools.product` for each length), and take one more string from it at the start of each round, so the list holds exactly the first t strings in round t.
--- hint
Keep the output list and a set of the strings already listed. In each round, try every string in the list with the budget t, in order, and stop the moment the output reaches k strings.
--- check case | A checker that needs 1 + 5·(number of b's) steps: "aa" is listed before "b"
dovetail(lambda w, t: w.count("a") % 2 == 0 and t >= 1 + 5 * w.count("b"), "ab", 7, 200)
=> ["", "aa", "b", "aab", "aba", "bb", "baa"]
--- check case | A Turing machine that loops on an odd number of a's
(lambda TM, M: dovetail(lambda w, t: TM(M, w, t)[0] == "accept", "ab", 8, 100))((lambda M, w, K: (lambda t, s, tw: [(lambda r: s.__setitem__(0, M["reject"]) if r is None else (t.__setitem__(s[1], r[1]), s.__setitem__(0, r[0]), s.__setitem__(1, max(0, s[1] + (1 if r[2] == "R" else -1))), s.__setitem__(2, s[2] + 1), t.append("_") if s[1] == len(t) else None))(M["delta"].get((s[0], t[s[1]]))) for _ in tw(lambda _: s[0] not in (M["accept"], M["reject"]), range(K))] and 0 or (("accept" if s[0] == M["accept"] else "reject" if s[0] == M["reject"] else "running"), "".join(t).rstrip("_"), s[2]))(list(w) + ["_"], [M["start"], 0, 0], __import__("itertools").takewhile)), {"states": {"e", "o", "y", "n"}, "alphabet": "ab", "tape": "ab_", "delta": {("e", "a"): ("o", "a", "R"), ("o", "a"): ("e", "a", "R"), ("e", "b"): ("e", "b", "R"), ("o", "b"): ("o", "b", "R"), ("e", "_"): ("y", "_", "R"), ("o", "_"): ("o", "_", "R")}, "start": "e", "accept": "y", "reject": "n"})
=> ["", "b", "aa", "bb", "aab", "aba", "baa", "bbb"]
?? A string whose check loops is tried again every round with more time, and simply never gets listed; the others still do.
--- check case | Few rounds, and an empty language: return what was found
(lambda TM, M: (dovetail(lambda w, t: TM(M, w, t)[0] == "accept", "ab", 5, 6), dovetail(lambda w, t: False, "xy", 3, 30)))((lambda M, w, K: (lambda t, s, tw: [(lambda r: s.__setitem__(0, M["reject"]) if r is None else (t.__setitem__(s[1], r[1]), s.__setitem__(0, r[0]), s.__setitem__(1, max(0, s[1] + (1 if r[2] == "R" else -1))), s.__setitem__(2, s[2] + 1), t.append("_") if s[1] == len(t) else None))(M["delta"].get((s[0], t[s[1]]))) for _ in tw(lambda _: s[0] not in (M["accept"], M["reject"]), range(K))] and 0 or (("accept" if s[0] == M["accept"] else "reject" if s[0] == M["reject"] else "running"), "".join(t).rstrip("_"), s[2]))(list(w) + ["_"], [M["start"], 0, 0], __import__("itertools").takewhile)), {"states": {"e", "o", "y", "n"}, "alphabet": "ab", "tape": "ab_", "delta": {("e", "a"): ("o", "a", "R"), ("o", "a"): ("e", "a", "R"), ("e", "b"): ("e", "b", "R"), ("o", "b"): ("o", "b", "R"), ("e", "_"): ("y", "_", "R"), ("o", "_"): ("o", "_", "R")}, "start": "e", "accept": "y", "reject": "n"})
=> (["", "b", "aa"], [])
--- check case | Never listed twice, even though the recogniser keeps saying yes, and a 3-symbol alphabet
dovetail(lambda w, t: len(w) == 3 and t >= 3, "xyz", 5, 1000)
=> ["xxx", "xxy", "xxz", "xyx", "xyy"]
--- check test | A very slow check (2 to the length steps) still lists every string of the language, in the right order

+++ question | What the thesis claims
--- ask
Which statement is the Church–Turing thesis?
--- choice
Every Turing machine halts on every input.
--- choice correct
Every function that can be computed by an algorithm can be computed by a Turing machine.
--- choice
Every problem can be solved by some Turing machine.
--- choice
Turing machines are as fast as any other model of computation.
--- why
The thesis links the informal idea of an algorithm to the precise idea of a Turing machine. It says nothing about speed (a one-tape TM is much slower than a real computer), and it does not claim every problem is solvable: lesson 1 already showed that some languages have no program at all, and lesson 13 names one.

+++ question | Guessing, simulated
--- ask
A nondeterministic TM accepts w, but one of its branches runs forever. Which simulation by a deterministic machine is guaranteed to accept w?
--- choice
A depth-first search that tries the first move of each configuration before the others.
--- choice correct
A breadth-first search over configurations, one level of depth at a time.
--- choice
Running one randomly chosen branch until it halts.
--- choice
Running each branch for the same fixed number of steps, chosen in advance.
--- why
Depth first can go down the infinite branch and never come back. A random branch may be the infinite one. A fixed budget may be too small for the accepting branch. Breadth first reaches every configuration at depth d after finitely many steps, so it finds the accepting one, at a cost that can be exponential in its depth.

+++ question | The price of extra tapes
--- ask
A 5-tape Turing machine decides a language in t(n) steps, with t(n) ≥ n. How long does the single-tape simulation from the lesson take?
--- choice
O(t(n)), the same.
--- choice correct
O(t(n)²).
--- choice
O(2^t(n)).
--- choice
It depends on the language, and may be impossible.
--- why
All five tapes fit on one tape of length O(t(n)), since no head can go further than t(n) + n cells in t(n) steps. Each simulated step sweeps across that tape a constant number of times, so it costs O(t(n)), and there are t(n) steps. A quadratic slowdown is polynomial, which is why "polynomial time" in lesson 15 does not depend on the number of tapes.

=== theory-13 | Decidability and the halting problem
--- teach
Lesson 11 split Turing machines into **deciders**, which halt on every input, and **recognisers**, which may run forever on strings outside their language. Lesson 12 built the universal machine, which runs any machine from its code. This lesson asks the question those two ideas lead to: **which problems have a decider at all?** Many questions about automata and grammars do, and you have already written their deciders. Then comes the most famous result in computer science: no program can decide, for every program and input, whether that program halts. You will turn the proof into code that defeats any halting checker anyone hands you.

### Questions about machines are languages

Once machines are written as strings (lesson 12), a question about machines is a language. Write ⟨B, w⟩ for the encoding of a DFA B together with a string w. Then "does B accept w?" becomes

A_DFA = {⟨B, w⟩ : B is a DFA that accepts w}.

A decider for such a language first rejects every string that is not a proper encoding, then answers the question. You have built deciders for most of the classic ones already:

- **A_DFA**, acceptance: run B on w (lesson 2). It halts after |w| steps.
- **A_NFA** and **A_REX**: convert the NFA or the regular expression to a DFA (lessons 3 and 4), then run that.
- **E_DFA** = {⟨A⟩ : L(A) = ∅}, emptiness: search from the start state and accept exactly when no accepting state is reachable.
- **EQ_DFA** = {⟨A, B⟩ : L(A) = L(B)}: build the product DFA that accepts the strings accepted by **exactly one** of A and B (the **symmetric difference**), and test it for emptiness.
- **A_CFG**: convert the grammar to Chomsky normal form and run CYK (lesson 8).
- **E_CFG**, emptiness for grammars: mark, round by round, the variables that can derive some string of terminals; L(G) is empty exactly when the start variable is never marked. That one is the first practice problem.

Each of these halts on every input, because each is a loop over a finite object: states, pairs of states, cells of a table, variables. So all of these languages are **decidable**. Not every natural question is: "do two grammars generate the same language?" has no decider, as the next lesson explains.

### Programs as data, in Python

The proofs in this lesson and the next are about programs that run other programs. Written as Turing machine tables they would be unreadable, so the code uses Python, and the Church–Turing thesis says nothing is lost. In this course a **program** is a Python **generator function** of one input. Each `yield` is one **step** of work, and the value it returns is its answer:

```python
def even_bs(x):
    total = 0
    for c in x:
        yield                  # one step per symbol
        if c == "b":
            total += 1
    return total % 2 == 0      # True means accept
```

Calling `even_bs("ab")` runs nothing yet. It makes a **generator**: a run that is paused before its first line. Each call of `next` on it runs the program up to its next `yield`. When the program returns, `next` raises the exception `StopIteration`, and the returned value is in the exception's `.value`:

```python
def even_bs(x):
    total = 0
    for c in x:
        yield
        if c == "b":
            total += 1
    return total % 2 == 0

g = even_bs("ab")
next(g)                        # step 1
next(g)                        # step 2
try:
    next(g)
except StopIteration as stop:
    print(stop.value)          # False: one b is an odd number
```

The conventions: a program **halts** when its generator finishes; it **accepts** when it returns exactly `True`; any other return value (`False`, `None`, `1`, `"yes"`) means it **rejects**; and a program that keeps yielding **runs forever**:

```python
def spin(x):
    while True:
        yield                  # a step that leads nowhere, forever
```

A function is a generator function only if a `yield` appears somewhere in its body, even one that is never reached. So a program that accepts at once, in zero steps, is written with an unreachable `yield`:

```python
def accept_all(x):
    return True
    yield                      # never reached: it only makes this a generator function

print(type(accept_all("z")).__name__)   # generator
```

Programs are ordinary Python values, so a program can be given another program as its input, or even itself. That is everything "a program that reads programs" needs.

### Running with a budget

The universal machine runs a program to the end, if there is an end. A simulator that must always answer takes a **budget** of steps instead, and reports one of three things: accepted, rejected, or **still running**. Still running means "don't know yet": the program might halt at the very next step, or never.

The universal machine already gives a recogniser for

A_TM = {⟨M, w⟩ : M is a Turing machine that accepts w}:

run M on w, with no budget, and accept if M accepts. If M rejects, reject; if M runs forever, so does the recogniser. So **A_TM is Turing-recognisable**. The question is whether something cleverer could always finish.

### The diagonal argument, aimed at machines

In Discrete Mathematics you proved that no list of infinite 0-1 sequences contains them all: go down the **[[diagonal|cantor]]** and flip every entry. Lesson 1 used that to show some languages have no program. Now it hits a specific, useful language.

**Theorem (Turing, 1936).** A_TM is undecidable.

**Proof.** Suppose some Turing machine H decides A_TM: on every input ⟨M, w⟩, H halts; it accepts if M accepts w, and rejects otherwise. Use H to build a machine D:

D = "On input ⟨M⟩, where M is a Turing machine:
1. Run H on ⟨M, ⟨M⟩⟩.
2. If H accepts, reject. If H rejects, accept."

D halts on every input, because H does. Now run D on its own code ⟨D⟩. By step 2, D accepts ⟨D⟩ exactly when H rejects ⟨D, ⟨D⟩⟩. Since H is correct, H rejects ⟨D, ⟨D⟩⟩ exactly when D does **not** accept ⟨D⟩. Put together: D accepts ⟨D⟩ if and only if D does not accept ⟨D⟩. That is a contradiction, so no such H exists. ∎

Why "diagonal"? Picture a table with one row for each machine M₁, M₂, M₃, … and one column for each code ⟨M₁⟩, ⟨M₂⟩, …, where row i, column j says whether Mᵢ accepts ⟨Mⱼ⟩. A decider H could fill in every cell. D's row is the diagonal with every entry flipped, so it differs from row i in column i, for every i. D is on no row of the table, yet D is a machine, so it has a row. The only way out is that H does not exist.

In Python, suppose someone claims a function `accepts(p, x)` that always returns `True` or `False` and says whether program `p` accepts input `x`. The machine D becomes:

```python fragment
def D(x):
    prediction = accepts(x, x)     # what the checker says x does with itself as input
    return not prediction          # do the opposite
    yield
```

Ask whether D accepts D, and the checker's answer about `accepts(D, D)` is wrong whichever it is.

### The halting problem

HALT_TM = {⟨M, w⟩ : M halts on input w} is undecidable too, by the same kind of argument. Suppose a checker claims to predict halting. Build a program that asks the checker about itself and then does the opposite of the prediction: if the checker says "halts", run forever; if it says "runs forever", halt at once. The task asks you to build exactly that program, and the checks hand it one checker after another and watch each of them be wrong.

There is a second proof, which is a preview of the next lesson. If HALT_TM had a decider R, then A_TM would have one too: on ⟨M, w⟩, ask R whether M halts on w. If not, reject. If so, it is now **safe** to run M on w to the end, and answer as M does. That would decide A_TM, which is impossible, so R cannot exist. The third practice problem builds this.

### Decidable, recognisable, and neither

**Theorem.** A language L is decidable if and only if both L and its complement L̄ are Turing-recognisable.

**Proof.** If L has a decider, the decider recognises L, and the same decider with accept and reject swapped recognises L̄. For the other direction, let M₁ recognise L and M₂ recognise L̄. Every string w is in exactly one of L and L̄, so exactly one of the two machines accepts w, after finitely many steps. Run them **in parallel**: one step of M₁, one step of M₂, and so on, and stop as soon as either accepts. Accept if it was M₁ and reject if it was M₂. This always halts, and its answer is right. ∎

Running them one after the other would be wrong: M₁ might run forever on a string of L̄, and M₂ would never get its turn.

A language is **co-recognisable** when its complement is recognisable. The theorem says: decidable = recognisable and co-recognisable. It has a sharp consequence:

**Corollary.** The complement of A_TM is **not** Turing-recognisable. A_TM is recognisable, so if its complement were recognisable too, A_TM would be decidable.

So every language sits in one of four regions: decidable (A_DFA, A_CFG); recognisable but not decidable (A_TM, HALT_TM); co-recognisable but not decidable (their complements); and neither (the next lesson shows that "do two machines recognise the same language?" is one). By lesson 1's counting argument, most languages are in the last region.

### What it costs

The deciders above all run in polynomial time: A_DFA in O(|w|) steps, E_DFA in O(|Q|·|Σ|) for the search, EQ_DFA in O(|Q₁|·|Q₂|·|Σ|) for the product and its search, and A_CFG in O(n³·|G|) with CYK. For an undecidable problem, "how long?" has no answer: no amount of time is enough in general. It is even worse than that. No computable function f bounds how long a halting program of size n can run: if one did, you could decide halting by simulating for f(n) steps and saying "runs forever" if the program had not halted by then. That is why the **[[busy beaver|busy-beaver]]** numbers of lesson 11 grow faster than any computable function.

### Where it is used

- **Static analysis.** No compiler or linter can decide exactly whether a line is dead code, whether a pointer can be null, or whether a loop terminates, because each of those questions contains the halting problem. Real tools answer safely instead: they may warn about code that is actually fine, and they say which kind of mistake they allow.
- **[[Termination checkers|termination]].** Proof assistants must know that every function halts. They accept only recursion that visibly shrinks its argument, and reject some programs that do halt as the price.
- **Watchdogs in flight software.** No one can prove that every task finishes, so a watchdog timer gives each task a budget and resets it, or fails over, when the budget runs out. It decides "finished within t steps", which is decidable, rather than "will finish", which is not.
- **[[Malware detection|malware]].** No scanner can flag exactly the programs that are viruses; every real scanner has false alarms, misses, or both.

**Watch out:**

- **Undecidable is about all inputs at once.** For one fixed program and input, "does it halt?" has a definite answer, and you can often find it. What does not exist is **one** algorithm that is right for **every** program and input.
- **Running and waiting is a recogniser, not a decider.** If you wait and nothing has happened, you have learned nothing.
- **The contradiction needs the checker to halt on every input.** D is built from H. If H could run forever, D could too, and "D accepts ⟨D⟩ if and only if it does not" would no longer follow.
- **D must ask about itself.** The contradiction comes from running D on D's own code. Asking the checker about any other pair proves nothing.
- **Finite versions are decidable.** For the programs up to a fixed size there is a halting decider: a finite table of answers, even if nobody knows how to fill it in.

::: context cantor Cantor's diagonal argument
In 1891 Georg Cantor proved that the real numbers cannot be listed: from any list, build a number whose i-th digit differs from the i-th digit of the i-th number on the list; it differs from every listed number somewhere. Turing's proof is the same move with machines as the list and "accepts its own code" as the digit. The same pattern proves Gödel's incompleteness theorem, Russell's paradox in set theory, and the time hierarchy theorem of lesson 17. Whenever a system can talk about itself, a diagonal lurks.
:::

::: context busy-beaver Why no bound can be computed
Let BB(n) be the longest number of steps any n-state machine takes before halting, when started on a blank tape. If some program could compute a function f with f(n) ≥ BB(n) for all n, it could decide whether any n-state machine halts on a blank tape: simulate it for f(n) steps; if it has not halted by then, it never will. That decider cannot exist, so no such f can be computed. BB(5) = 47,176,870 was settled only in 2024, and BB(6) is known to be astronomically large.
:::

::: context termination How proof assistants insist on halting
In Coq, Lean and Agda a function is also a proof, and a function that never returns could "prove" anything. So these systems accept a recursive function only when each recursive call is on a structurally smaller argument, such as the tail of a list, or when the programmer supplies a measure that provably decreases. That rule accepts a large, safe class of programs, but some programs that do halt, such as a naive Collatz iteration, cannot be written without extra proof work.
:::

::: context malware Cohen's virus theorem
In 1987 Fred Cohen, who coined the term "computer virus", proved that no program can decide whether an arbitrary program is a virus. The proof is the diagonal again: given a claimed detector, write a program that runs the detector on itself and spreads only if the detector says it is harmless. Antivirus products therefore rely on signatures, heuristics and sandboxes, and accept some false alarms and some misses.
:::
--- task
Programs are generator functions as in the lesson: each `yield` is one step, the program halts when it returns, and it accepts exactly when it returns `True`. Write two functions.

- `run(p, x, budget)`: run program `p` on input `x` for at most `budget` steps (`budget ≥ 0`). If it halts after k steps, with k ≤ `budget`, return `("accept", k)` when it returned `True` and `("reject", k)` when it returned anything else. If it has taken `budget` steps without halting, return `("running", budget)`. A program that returns without ever yielding halts after 0 steps.
- `diagonal(halts)`: `halts(p, x)` is any function that returns `True` or `False` and claims to say whether program `p` halts on input `x`. Return a program `D` (a generator function of one input) such that `halts` is wrong about D run on D. On any input `p`, `D` must do the opposite of what `halts` predicts about `p` run on itself: run forever if `halts(p, p)` is `True`, and halt at once if it is `False`.
--- starter
def run(p, x, budget):
    return ("running", budget)


def diagonal(halts):
    def contrary(p):
        return True
        yield
    return contrary
--- solution
def run(p, x, budget):
    g = p(x)
    for steps in range(budget + 1):
        try:
            next(g)
        except StopIteration as stop:
            return ("accept" if stop.value is True else "reject", steps)
    return ("running", budget)


def diagonal(halts):
    def contrary(p):
        if halts(p, p):
            while True:
                yield
        return "halted"
    return contrary
--- hint
For `run`, make the generator with `p(x)` and call `next` on it in a loop. If the program has yielded k times, the (k + 1)-th call raises `StopIteration`. So up to `budget + 1` calls are needed to see a program halt after `budget` steps; catch the exception, and read the answer from its `.value`.
--- hint
For `diagonal`, define a generator function inside it that uses `halts`. It asks `halts(p, p)` about its own input `p`, then either loops forever with `yield` inside `while True`, or returns straight away. Return that inner function itself, not a call of it.
--- hint
Why it works: the checks run `D` on `D`. Then `D` asks `halts(D, D)`. If the answer is `True`, D runs forever, so the answer was wrong; if it is `False`, D halts, so the answer was wrong again.
--- check case | A program that takes 3 steps and accepts, and one that takes 2 steps and rejects
(run(lambda x: (yield from range(3)) or True, "", 10), run(lambda x: (yield from range(2)) or False, "", 10))
=> (("accept", 3), ("reject", 2))
--- check case | Only True accepts: returning None, 1 or "yes" rejects
(run(lambda x: (yield from range(1)), "", 5), run(lambda x: (yield from range(1)) or 1, "", 5), run(lambda x: (yield from range(1)) or "yes", "", 5))
=> (("reject", 1), ("reject", 1), ("reject", 1))
?? Compare the returned value with `is True`: 1 == True in Python, but 1 is not True.
--- check case | A program that runs forever is still running after exactly the budget
run(lambda x: (yield from __import__("itertools").repeat(None)), "abc", 40)
=> ("running", 40)
--- check case | Halting on the last allowed step counts; one step short is still running; zero steps

+++ practice | Emptiness for grammars
--- task
Grammars are dictionaries as in lesson 7: `"variables"` (a set), `"terminals"` (a string), `"rules"` (a dictionary from a variable to a list of bodies, each a tuple of symbols, with `()` for ε) and `"start"`. A variable may have no entry in `"rules"` at all, which means it has no rules. Every symbol in a body that is not a variable is a terminal.

The language E_CFG = {⟨G⟩ : L(G) = ∅} is decidable. Write its decider.

- `generating(G)`: return the set of **generating** variables: those that derive at least one string of terminals (possibly the empty string). Compute it in rounds: a variable becomes generating when one of its bodies consists only of terminals and variables already known to be generating. Stop when a round adds nothing.
- `cfg_empty(G)`: return `True` exactly when L(G) is empty, that is, when the start variable is not generating.

For example, with the rules S → AB | a S, A → a, B → B b, only A is generating: B can never get rid of its B, and S needs B or itself. So `cfg_empty` is `True`. Do not change `G`.
--- starter
def generating(G):
    return set(G["variables"])


def cfg_empty(G):
    return False
--- solution
def generating(G):
    marked = set()
    changed = True
    while changed:
        changed = False
        for A in G["variables"]:
            if A in marked:
                continue
            for body in G["rules"].get(A, []):
                if all(s in marked or s not in G["variables"] for s in body):
                    marked.add(A)
                    changed = True
                    break
    return marked


def cfg_empty(G):
    return G["start"] not in generating(G)
--- hint
Keep a set of marked variables, starting empty. In each round, go through the variables that are not marked yet and mark one when some body has every symbol either a terminal or already marked. An empty body `()` passes that test at once, since there is nothing to check.
--- hint
Repeat rounds until one marks nothing new. Each round either marks a variable or ends the loop, so there are at most as many rounds as variables, and the decider always halts.
--- check case | The lesson's example: only A is generating, and the language is empty
(lambda G: (generating(G), cfg_empty(G)))({"variables": {"S", "A", "B"}, "terminals": "ab", "rules": {"S": [("A", "B"), ("a", "S")], "A": [("a",)], "B": [("B", "b")]}, "start": "S"})
=> ({"A"}, True)
--- check case | S → aSb | ε is not empty; S → aS alone is empty; a start variable with no rules is empty
(cfg_empty({"variables": {"S"}, "terminals": "ab", "rules": {"S": [("a", "S", "b"), ()]}, "start": "S"}), cfg_empty({"variables": {"S"}, "terminals": "a", "rules": {"S": [("a", "S")]}, "start": "S"}), cfg_empty({"variables": {"S", "T"}, "terminals": "a", "rules": {"T": [("a",)]}, "start": "S"}))
=> (False, True, True)
--- check case | A cycle of rules with a way out, and a cycle with none
(cfg_empty({"variables": {"S", "A"}, "terminals": "b", "rules": {"S": [("A",)], "A": [("S",), ("b",)]}, "start": "S"}), cfg_empty({"variables": {"S", "A"}, "terminals": "b", "rules": {"S": [("A",)], "A": [("S",), ("b", "S")]}, "start": "S"}))
=> (False, True)
--- check test | A chain of 300 variables where only the last has a terminal rule: every variable is generating
(lambda G: generating(G) == G["variables"] and not cfg_empty(G))({"variables": set(range(300)), "terminals": "x", "rules": {**{i: [(i + 1, "x")] for i in range(299)}, 299: [("x",)]}, "start": 0})
?? One round is not enough: a variable can become generating only after the one it depends on does. Repeat until nothing changes.
--- check test | 300 seeded random grammars: the same generating set as the definition, and the grammar is unchanged
(lambda R, GEN, cp: all((lambda G: (lambda before: generating(G) == GEN(G) and cfg_empty(G) == (G["start"] not in GEN(G)) and G == before)(cp.deepcopy(G)))({"variables": set("SABCD"), "terminals": "ab", "rules": {A: [tuple(R.choice("SABCDab") for _ in range(R.randint(0, 3))) for _ in range(R.randint(0, 3))] for A in "SABCD" if R.random() < 0.9}, "start": "S"}) for _ in range(300)))(__import__("random").Random(3), (lambda ns: exec('def gen(G):\n    S = set()\n    while True:\n        T = {A for A in G["variables"] if any(all(s in S or s not in G["variables"] for s in b) for b in G["rules"].get(A, []))}\n        if T == S:\n            return S\n        S = T\n', ns) or ns['gen'])({}), __import__("copy"))

+++ practice | Deciding with two recognisers
--- task
The lesson proved that L is decidable when both L and its complement are recognisable. Write the decider from that proof.

`decide(p_in, p_out, w)` gets two programs (generator functions as in the lesson): `p_in` recognises a language L and `p_out` recognises its complement. So on every input exactly one of them accepts, and the other may reject or run forever. Run both on `w` **in parallel**, in rounds. Round r (for r = 1, 2, 3, …) calls `next` once on each program that is still running, `p_in` first. If a call makes a program return `True`, stop at once and return `(True, r)` if it was `p_in` or `(False, r)` if it was `p_out`. A program that returns anything else is out: never advance it again. If both programs have halted without accepting, the promise was broken: return `None`.

A program that halts after k yields finishes on its (k + 1)-th call, so in round k + 1. For example, if `p_in` accepts after 4 steps and `p_out` runs forever, the answer is `(True, 5)`.
--- starter
def decide(p_in, p_out, w):
    return (True, 0)
--- solution
def decide(p_in, p_out, w):
    running = [(True, p_in(w)), (False, p_out(w))]
    rounds = 0
    while running:
        rounds += 1
        still = []
        for answer, g in running:
            try:
                next(g)
                still.append((answer, g))
            except StopIteration as stop:
                if stop.value is True:
                    return (answer, rounds)
        running = still
    return None
--- hint
Make both generators at the start and keep a list of the ones still running, each paired with the answer it stands for (`True` for `p_in`, `False` for `p_out`). Each round calls `next` once on each of them, in order.
--- hint
Catch `StopIteration` around each `next`. If its `.value` is `True`, you are done. Otherwise just leave that generator out of next round's list. When the list is empty, return `None`.
--- check case | p_in accepts after 4 steps while p_out runs forever

=> (True, 5)
?? A program that yields 4 times finishes on its 5th call of next, in round 5. The one that runs forever must not stop you.
--- check case | p_in runs forever and p_out accepts after 6 steps; p_in rejecting early does not end the search
(decide(lambda w: (yield from __import__("itertools").repeat(None)), lambda w: (yield from range(6)) or True, "x"), decide(lambda w: (yield from range(1)) or False, lambda w: (yield from range(9)) or True, "x"))
=> ((False, 7), (False, 10))
--- check case | Both accept in the same round: p_in goes first; zero steps means round 1; both rejecting breaks the promise
(decide(lambda w: (yield from range(3)) or True, lambda w: (yield from range(3)) or True, ""), decide(lambda w: (yield from range(0)) or True, lambda w: (yield from range(0)) or True, ""), decide(lambda w: (yield from range(2)), lambda w: (yield from range(5)) or 0, ""))
=> ((True, 4), (True, 1), None)
--- check test | Recognisers for "the length is a multiple of 3" that run forever on the other strings: right on every string up to length 8

+++ practice | A halting checker would decide acceptance
--- task
The lesson's second proof that the halting problem is undecidable: a halting checker would give an acceptance checker, and that is impossible. Write the acceptance checker.

`accepts(halts, p, x)` gets a function `halts(p, x)` that correctly says whether program `p` halts on input `x` (pretend it exists; the checks supply one that is right for the programs they use), a program `p` (a generator function as in the lesson) and an input `x`. Return `True` if `p` accepts `x` and `False` otherwise. It must always return: never run a program that `halts` says runs forever, and never give up on a program that `halts` says will halt, however many steps it takes. Accepting means returning exactly `True`.
--- starter
def accepts(halts, p, x):
    g = p(x)
    for _ in range(1000):
        try:
            next(g)
        except StopIteration as stop:
            return stop.value is True
    return False
--- solution
def accepts(halts, p, x):
    if not halts(p, x):
        return False
    g = p(x)
    while True:
        try:
            next(g)
        except StopIteration as stop:
            return stop.value is True
--- hint
Ask `halts(p, x)` first. A program that runs forever never accepts, so that case is already decided.
--- hint
If `halts` says `True`, the run is guaranteed to end, so a loop of `next` calls with no budget is safe. Read the answer from the `StopIteration`.

+++ practice | Does the head ever move left?
--- task
Not every question about Turing machines is undecidable. Write a decider for

LEFT = {⟨M, w⟩ : M, run on w, takes at least one transition that moves left}.

Turing machines are dictionaries as in lesson 11, and `tm["states"]` lists every state the machine uses. The run follows lesson 11's conventions: the head starts on cell 0 (a blank if w is empty); a missing transition, or reaching the accept or reject state, halts the machine. A left move made on cell 0 still counts as a left move.

Write `ever_moves_left(tm, w)` that returns `True` or `False`, and always returns, even for a machine that runs forever. Here is why that is possible. Suppose the run takes only right moves. After |w| steps the head is past the input, on cells it has never visited, which hold blanks. From then on, what the machine does depends only on its state. Among the states at the next |Q| + 1 moments, two are equal, and from the first of them the machine repeats the same moves forever, all to the right. So if the first |w| + |Q| + 1 steps contain no left move and the machine has not halted, it never moves left.
--- starter
def ever_moves_left(tm, w):
    return any(d == "L" for (_, _, d) in tm["delta"].values())
--- solution
def ever_moves_left(tm, w):
    tape = list(w) if w else ["_"]
    head, state = 0, tm["start"]
    for _ in range(len(w) + len(tm["states"]) + 1):
        if state == tm["accept"] or state == tm["reject"]:
            return False
        move = tm["delta"].get((state, tape[head]))
        if move is None:
            return False
        state, tape[head], direction = move
        if direction == "L":
            return True
        head += 1
        if head == len(tape):
            tape.append("_")
    return False
--- hint
Simulate the run exactly as in lesson 11, but stop the moment a transition moves left (answer `True`), or the machine halts (answer `False`).
--- hint
Put a limit on the loop: `len(w) + len(tm["states"]) + 1` steps. If it runs out with no left move, the argument in the task says there never will be one, so answer `False`. The machine only moves right in this loop, so the head only ever grows by one.

(ever_moves_left({"states": {"s", "y", "n"}, "alphabet": "ab", "tape": "ab_", "delta": {("s", "a"): ("s", "a", "R"), ("s", "b"): ("s", "b", "R"), ("s", "_"): ("s", "_", "R")}, "start": "s", "accept": "y", "reject": "n"}, "abab"), ever_moves_left({"states": {"s", "t", "y", "n"}, "alphabet": "ab", "tape": "ab_", "delta": {("s", "a"): ("s", "a", "R"), ("s", "b"): ("s", "b", "R"), ("s", "_"): ("t", "_", "R"), ("t", "_"): ("s", "_", "L")}, "start": "s", "accept": "y", "reject": "n"}, "ab"))
=> (False, True)
?? The first machine runs forever. Your function must still return, after a bounded number of steps.
--- check case | A left move on cell 0 counts; halting first does not; a start state that accepts; the empty input
(ever_moves_left({"states": {"s", "y", "n"}, "alphabet": "a", "tape": "a_", "delta": {("s", "a"): ("y", "a", "L")}, "start": "s", "accept": "y", "reject": "n"}, "a"), ever_moves_left({"states": {"s", "y", "n"}, "alphabet": "a", "tape": "a_", "delta": {("s", "a"): ("s", "a", "R")}, "start": "s", "accept": "y", "reject": "n"}, "aaa"), ever_moves_left({"states": {"y", "n"}, "alphabet": "a", "tape": "a_", "delta": {("y", "a"): ("y", "a", "L")}, "start": "y", "accept": "y", "reject": "n"}, "a"), ever_moves_left({"states": {"s", "t", "y", "n"}, "alphabet": "a", "tape": "a_", "delta": {("s", "_"): ("t", "a", "R"), ("t", "_"): ("t", "a", "L")}, "start": "s", "accept": "y", "reject": "n"}, ""))
=> (True, False, False, True)

+++ practice | Debug: the infinity test that counts dead loops
--- task
INFINITE_DFA = {⟨D⟩ : L(D) is infinite} is decidable: L(D) is infinite exactly when some cycle of arrows runs entirely through **useful** states, those that can be reached from the start state **and** can reach an accepting state. (Going round such a cycle any number of times, on the way from the start to an accepting state, gives infinitely many accepted strings. Without one, every accepted string has length less than the number of states.)

`is_infinite(dfa)` takes a total DFA (a dictionary as in lesson 2) and should return `True` exactly when its language is infinite. It looks for a cycle with Kahn's algorithm from DSA I: repeatedly remove a state with no incoming arrows from the others; a cycle exists exactly when some states can never be removed. But it reports almost every total DFA as infinite, even the one for the single string `ab`, because the dead state loops to itself. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
def reachable(dfa):
    seen = {dfa["start"]}
    stack = [dfa["start"]]
    while stack:
        q = stack.pop()
        for c in dfa["alphabet"]:
            r = dfa["delta"][(q, c)]
            if r not in seen:
                seen.add(r)
                stack.append(r)
    return seen


def can_reach_accept(dfa):
    good = set(dfa["accept"])
    changed = True
    while changed:
        changed = False
        for (q, c), r in dfa["delta"].items():
            if r in good and q not in good:
                good.add(q)
                changed = True
    return good


def is_infinite(dfa):
    useful = reachable(dfa)
    indegree = {q: 0 for q in useful}
    for q in useful:
        for c in dfa["alphabet"]:
            r = dfa["delta"][(q, c)]
            if r in useful:
                indegree[r] += 1
    queue = [q for q in useful if indegree[q] == 0]
    removed = 0
    while queue:
        q = queue.pop()
        removed += 1
        for c in dfa["alphabet"]:
            r = dfa["delta"][(q, c)]
            if r in useful:
                indegree[r] -= 1
                if indegree[r] == 0:
                    queue.append(r)
    return removed < len(useful)
--- solution
def reachable(dfa):
    seen = {dfa["start"]}
    stack = [dfa["start"]]
    while stack:
        q = stack.pop()
        for c in dfa["alphabet"]:
            r = dfa["delta"][(q, c)]
            if r not in seen:
                seen.add(r)
                stack.append(r)
    return seen


def can_reach_accept(dfa):
    good = set(dfa["accept"])
    changed = True
    while changed:
        changed = False
        for (q, c), r in dfa["delta"].items():
            if r in good and q not in good:
                good.add(q)
                changed = True
    return good


def is_infinite(dfa):
    useful = reachable(dfa) & can_reach_accept(dfa)
    indegree = {q: 0 for q in useful}
    for q in useful:
        for c in dfa["alphabet"]:
            r = dfa["delta"][(q, c)]
            if r in useful:
                indegree[r] += 1
    queue = [q for q in useful if indegree[q] == 0]
    removed = 0
    while queue:
        q = queue.pop()
        removed += 1
        for c in dfa["alphabet"]:
            r = dfa["delta"][(q, c)]
            if r in useful:
                indegree[r] -= 1
                if indegree[r] == 0:
                    queue.append(r)
    return removed < len(useful)
--- hint
Read the definition of a useful state in the task again, and compare it with the set the program calls `useful`. Which helper function is never used?
--- hint
A cycle through a state that cannot reach an accepting state, like the dead state's loop, adds no accepted strings. Restrict the search to states that are reachable **and** can reach an accepting state: the intersection of the two sets.
--- check case | The single string ab, with a dead state: finite
is_infinite({"states": {0, 1, 2, 3}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, "a"): 1, (0, "b"): 3, (1, "a"): 3, (1, "b"): 2, (2, "a"): 3, (2, "b"): 3, (3, "a"): 3, (3, "b"): 3}, "start": 0, "accept": {2}})

--- check test | 300 seeded random DFAs: infinite exactly when some accepted length lies between n and 2n − 1, for n states
(lambda R, DINF: all((lambda D: is_infinite(D) == DINF(D))((lambda n: {"states": set(range(n)), "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(q, c): R.randrange(n) for q in range(n) for c in "ab"}, "start": 0, "accept": {q for q in range(n) if R.random() < 0.25}})(R.randint(1, 7))) for _ in range(300)))(__import__("random").Random(31), (lambda ns: exec('def dinf(D):\n    n = len(D["states"])\n    cur = {D["start"]: 1}\n    for L in range(2 * n):\n        if L >= n and any(q in D["accept"] for q in cur):\n            return True\n        nxt = {}\n        for q in cur:\n            for c in D["alphabet"]:\n                r = D["delta"][(q, c)]\n                nxt[r] = 1\n        cur = nxt\n    return False\n', ns) or ns['dinf'])({}))

+++ practice | Halting is decidable when memory is finite
--- task
A flight computer has a fixed amount of memory, so, unlike a Turing machine, it has only finitely many configurations. That makes halting decidable for it, at a price.

Write `bounded_run(tm, w)` for a Turing machine (a dictionary as in lesson 11) whose tape is **exactly** `len(w) + 1` cells long: the input followed by one blank (so a single blank cell for the empty input). The head can never leave: moving left from cell 0 stays on cell 0, as usual, and moving right from the last cell stays on the last cell. A missing transition rejects without taking a step, and the machine halts when it is in its accept or reject state, as in lesson 11.

A configuration is the state, the head position and the whole tape. Return:

- `("accept", k)` or `("reject", k)` if the machine halts after k steps;
- `("loops", k)` if, after k steps, the machine is in a configuration it was already in after fewer steps. From then on it repeats the same cycle forever, so it never halts.

One of these always happens, because there are at most |Q| · (n + 1) · |Γ|ⁿ⁺¹ configurations for an input of length n: finite, but exponential in n.
--- starter
def bounded_run(tm, w):
    return ("loops", 0)
--- solution
def bounded_run(tm, w):
    tape = list(w) + ["_"]
    head, state, steps = 0, tm["start"], 0
    seen = set()
    while True:
        if state == tm["accept"]:
            return ("accept", steps)
        if state == tm["reject"]:
            return ("reject", steps)
        config = (state, head, tuple(tape))
        if config in seen:
            return ("loops", steps)
        seen.add(config)
        move = tm["delta"].get((state, tape[head]))
        if move is None:
            return ("reject", steps)
        state, tape[head], direction = move
        if direction == "R":
            head = min(head + 1, len(tape) - 1)
        else:
            head = max(head - 1, 0)
        steps += 1
--- hint
Keep a set of the configurations seen so far, each as a tuple `(state, head, tuple(tape))`: a list cannot go in a set, but a tuple can. Before each step, check for a halting state, then check whether the current configuration is already in the set.
--- hint
The tape never grows here: start with `list(w) + ["_"]`, and clamp the head with `min(head + 1, len(tape) - 1)` for a right move and `max(head - 1, 0)` for a left one.
--- check case | Right then left, back to the start: a loop after 2 steps
