=> ("loops", 2)
--- check case | Pushing against the right end is a loop; a machine that accepts at the blank; a missing transition
(bounded_run({"states": {"s", "y", "n"}, "alphabet": "a", "tape": "a_", "delta": {("s", "a"): ("s", "a", "R"), ("s", "_"): ("s", "_", "R")}, "start": "s", "accept": "y", "reject": "n"}, "aa"), bounded_run({"states": {"s", "y", "n"}, "alphabet": "a", "tape": "a_", "delta": {("s", "a"): ("s", "a", "R"), ("s", "_"): ("y", "_", "R")}, "start": "s", "accept": "y", "reject": "n"}, "aa"), bounded_run({"states": {"s", "y", "n"}, "alphabet": "ab", "tape": "ab_", "delta": {("s", "a"): ("s", "a", "R")}, "start": "s", "accept": "y", "reject": "n"}, "ab"))
=> (("loops", 3), ("accept", 3), ("reject", 1))
?? Moving right from the last cell stays there, so the configuration after that move equals the one before it.
--- check test | A binary counter on 10 cells counts through all 1024 values before it overflows: thousands of steps, but a verdict
(lambda M, BRUN: bounded_run(M, "#" + "0" * 10) == BRUN(M, "#" + "0" * 10) and BRUN(M, "#" + "0" * 10)[0] == "accept" and BRUN(M, "#" + "0" * 10)[1] > 2000)({"states": {"inc", "back", "y", "n"}, "alphabet": "#01", "tape": "#01_", "delta": {("inc", "#"): ("inc", "#", "R"), ("inc", "1"): ("inc", "0", "R"), ("inc", "0"): ("back", "1", "L"), ("inc", "_"): ("y", "_", "L"), ("back", "0"): ("back", "0", "L"), ("back", "1"): ("back", "1", "L"), ("back", "#"): ("inc", "#", "R")}, "start": "inc", "accept": "y", "reject": "n"}, (lambda ns: exec('def brun(M, w):\n    t = list(w) + ["_"]\n    h, q, s, seen = 0, M["start"], 0, set()\n    while True:\n        if q == M["accept"]:\n            return ("accept", s)\n        if q == M["reject"]:\n            return ("reject", s)\n        c = (q, h, tuple(t))\n        if c in seen:\n            return ("loops", s)\n        seen.add(c)\n        r = M["delta"].get((q, t[h]))\n        if r is None:\n            return ("reject", s)\n        q, t[h], d = r\n        h = min(h + 1, len(t) - 1) if d == "R" else max(h - 1, 0)\n        s += 1\n', ns) or ns['brun'])({}))
--- check test | 400 seeded random machines: the same verdict and step count as the definition
(lambda R, RTM, BRUN: all((lambda M, w: bounded_run(M, w) == BRUN(M, w))(RTM(R, R.randint(1, 4), "01_", 0.15, 0.9), "".join(R.choice("01") for _ in range(R.randint(0, 5)))) for _ in range(400)))(__import__("random").Random(41), (lambda ns: exec('def rtm(R, n, syms, ph=0.2, pd=0.9, moves="LR"):\n    Q = ["q%d" % i for i in range(n)]\n    d = {}\n    for q in Q:\n        for s in syms:\n            if R.random() < pd:\n                d[(q, s)] = (R.choice(Q + ["y", "n"]) if R.random() < ph else R.choice(Q), R.choice(syms), R.choice(moves))\n    return {"states": set(Q) | {"y", "n"}, "alphabet": syms.replace("_", "")[:2], "tape": syms, "delta": d, "start": "q0", "accept": "y", "reject": "n"}\n', ns) or ns['rtm'])({}), (lambda ns: exec('def brun(M, w):\n    t = list(w) + ["_"]\n    h, q, s, seen = 0, M["start"], 0, set()\n    while True:\n        if q == M["accept"]:\n            return ("accept", s)\n        if q == M["reject"]:\n            return ("reject", s)\n        c = (q, h, tuple(t))\n        if c in seen:\n            return ("loops", s)\n        seen.add(c)\n        r = M["delta"].get((q, t[h]))\n        if r is None:\n            return ("reject", s)\n        q, t[h], d = r\n        h = min(h + 1, len(t) - 1) if d == "R" else max(h - 1, 0)\n        s += 1\n', ns) or ns['brun'])({}))

+++ question | Where the proof needs the checker to halt
--- ask
The proof that A_TM is undecidable assumes a decider H and builds D = "on ⟨M⟩, run H on ⟨M, ⟨M⟩⟩ and do the opposite". Which step uses the assumption that H halts on every input?
--- choice
Writing down the code ⟨D⟩, because a machine that may loop has no code.
--- choice correct
Concluding that D accepts ⟨D⟩ exactly when D does not: if H could run forever on ⟨D, ⟨D⟩⟩, D would run forever too, and would neither accept nor reject.
--- choice
Running D on ⟨D⟩ at all, because a machine cannot be given its own code as input.
--- choice
None: the proof works just as well if H is only a recogniser.
--- why
Every machine has a code, and giving a machine its own code is perfectly legal. The contradiction needs D to either accept or reject ⟨D⟩, and D inherits halting from H. With a mere recogniser in place of H, D may simply run forever on ⟨D⟩, and there is no contradiction. That is exactly why A_TM is recognisable but not decidable.

+++ question | The complement of acceptance
--- ask
Is the complement of A_TM (the codes ⟨M, w⟩ where M does not accept w, plus the strings that are not proper codes) Turing-recognisable?
--- choice
Yes: run M on w and accept if M rejects.
--- choice
Yes: it is the complement of a recognisable language, and recognisable languages are closed under complement.
--- choice correct
No: A_TM is recognisable, so if its complement were recognisable too, A_TM would be decidable.
--- choice
It depends on the encoding of machines.
--- why
Running M on w catches the w that M rejects, but never the w on which M runs forever, which are also in the complement. Recognisable languages are not closed under complement; decidable ones are. The theorem "decidable exactly when recognisable and co-recognisable" turns the undecidability of A_TM into a stronger fact about its complement.

+++ question | Programs shorter than a thousand characters
--- ask
Is there a program that correctly decides, for every Python program shorter than 1,000 characters, whether it halts when run on the empty input?
--- choice correct
Yes. There are finitely many such programs, so a program with a table of the right answers exists, even though nobody knows how to write the table.
--- choice
No, because the halting problem is undecidable.
--- choice
No, because the checker would have to be longer than 1,000 characters, which makes the diagonal argument apply.
--- choice
Only if P = NP.
--- why
Every finite language is decidable: a lookup table decides it. Undecidability says no single program is right for **all** programs; it says nothing against a program that is right for a fixed finite set. The table exists in the mathematical sense, but filling it in would require solving each case, some of which may be as hard as open problems in mathematics.

=== theory-14 | Reductions and Rice's theorem
--- teach
Last lesson proved one problem undecidable from scratch, with a diagonal argument. Doing that again for every new problem would be exhausting, and it is not how anyone works. This lesson gives the tool that spreads undecidability from one problem to the next: the **reduction**. With it you will prove that emptiness, regularity and equivalence of Turing machines are all undecidable, and then prove **Rice's theorem**, which settles a whole family of questions at once: every non-trivial question about what a program accepts is undecidable. You will write reductions as code that builds programs.

### "If I could solve that, I could solve this"

Suppose you had a machine that answers "does this program accept anything at all?". Could you use it to answer "does program p halt on input x"? Yes: build a new program q that ignores its own input, runs p on x, and accepts when p halts. Then q accepts something exactly when p halts on x. Ask the machine about q.

That is a reduction. It turns every question of one kind into a question of another kind with the **same answer**. Since the halting question cannot be answered, the emptiness machine cannot exist either.

### Mapping reductions

A function f : Σ∗ → Σ∗ is **computable** when some Turing machine, on every input w, halts with f(w) on its tape (lesson 11).

**Definition.** A language A is **mapping reducible** to a language B, written **A ≤m B**, when there is a computable function f such that for every string w:

w ∈ A if and only if f(w) ∈ B.

f is called a **reduction** from A to B. It maps yes-instances to yes-instances and no-instances to no-instances. It does not have to be onto, or one-to-one; it only has to be computable and to keep the answer.

**Theorem.** If A ≤m B and B is decidable, then A is decidable.

**Proof.** Let M decide B and let f be the reduction. On input w, compute f(w), run M on it, and answer as M does. Computing f always halts, and M always halts, so this halts; it accepts exactly when f(w) ∈ B, that is, when w ∈ A. ∎

The contrapositive is the one you use: **if A ≤m B and A is undecidable, then B is undecidable.** The same proof, with "recognises" in place of "decides", gives: if A ≤m B and B is recognisable, so is A. And since w ∈ A exactly when f(w) ∈ B, the same f shows Ā ≤m B̄. Reductions also chain: if A ≤m B and B ≤m C, then A ≤m C, by composing the two functions.

### Reductions as programs that build programs

In this course's Python model, the instances are programs (generator functions, as in lesson 13) and their inputs, so a reduction is a function that takes a program and **returns a new program**, without running anything. One new tool makes that easy: `yield from`. Inside a generator, `result = yield from p(y)` runs the program `p` on `y`, with each of p's steps as a step of the outer program, and gives p's return value when it finishes. If p runs forever, so does the outer program.

```python
def three_steps(y):
    yield
    yield
    yield
    return len(y) > 2

def wrapper(y):
    answer = yield from three_steps(y)     # 3 steps, taken as the wrapper's own
    yield                                  # plus one step of its own
    return not answer

g = wrapper("abc")
steps = 0
try:
    while True:
        next(g)
        steps += 1
except StopIteration as stop:
    print(steps, stop.value)               # 4 False
```

Here is a reduction from A_TM (acceptance) to HALT_TM (halting). Given p and x, build q: run p on its input; if p accepted, halt and accept; otherwise, run forever. Then q halts on x exactly when p accepts x.

```python
def accept_to_halt(p, x):
    def q(y):
        answer = yield from p(y)
        if answer is not True:
            while True:
                yield
        return True
    return q, x
```

Notice what `accept_to_halt` does **not** do: it never runs p. It only writes down a new program, which takes constant time however long p runs. That is what makes it a computable function, and the most common mistake in reductions is to forget it. The task asks for the reduction in the other direction, from halting to acceptance, and for Rice's construction.

### Emptiness is undecidable

E_TM = {⟨M⟩ : L(M) = ∅}.

**Theorem.** E_TM is undecidable, and it is not even Turing-recognisable.

**Proof.** Reduce HALT_TM to the complement of E_TM. Given ⟨M, w⟩, let f output ⟨Q⟩, where

Q = "On input y: run M on w; if it halts, accept."

If M halts on w, Q accepts every y, so L(Q) = Σ∗ ≠ ∅. If not, Q accepts nothing. So ⟨M, w⟩ ∈ HALT_TM exactly when ⟨Q⟩ is in the complement of E_TM. HALT_TM is undecidable, so the complement of E_TM is undecidable, and so is E_TM, since a decider for one gives a decider for the other by swapping answers.

For the second part: the complement of E_TM is recognisable. On ⟨M⟩, **dovetail** (lesson 12): in round t, run M for t steps on each of the first t strings, and accept if any run accepts. If L(M) is non-empty, some string is accepted in some number of steps, and a large enough round finds it. So if E_TM were recognisable as well, it would be decidable (lesson 13), which it is not. ∎

### Regularity is undecidable

REGULAR_TM = {⟨M⟩ : L(M) is regular}. Reduce HALT_TM to it. Given ⟨M, w⟩, build

Q = "On input y: if y has the form aⁿbⁿ, accept. Otherwise run M on w, and accept if it halts."

If M halts on w, Q accepts every string, and Σ∗ is regular. If not, Q accepts exactly the strings aⁿbⁿ, which lesson 6 proved not regular. So M halts on w exactly when L(Q) is regular. The trick is the same as before: the new program's language is one of two languages, and which one it is depends on whether M halts on w.

### Equivalence is neither

EQ_TM = {⟨M₁, M₂⟩ : L(M₁) = L(M₂)} is **neither recognisable nor co-recognisable**. E_TM reduces to it: pair each machine M with a machine that rejects everything, so the two languages are equal exactly when L(M) = ∅. E_TM is not recognisable, so EQ_TM is not recognisable either. A reduction from A_TM to EQ_TM, in the second practice problem, shows it is not co-recognisable either. So EQ_TM lies outside both regions of lesson 13, in the fourth one.

### Rice's theorem

The proofs above share a shape. **[[Henry Rice|rice]]** noticed in 1951 that they always work.

A **property** of machines is a set P of machine codes. It is a **property of the language** when machines with the same language are always both in P or both out: if L(M₁) = L(M₂), then ⟨M₁⟩ ∈ P exactly when ⟨M₂⟩ ∈ P. It is **non-trivial** when some machine is in P and some machine is not.

**Rice's theorem.** Every non-trivial property of the language recognised by a Turing machine is undecidable.

**Proof.** Let P be such a property. Let M_∅ be a machine that rejects everything, so L(M_∅) = ∅. Suppose first that ⟨M_∅⟩ ∉ P. Because P is non-trivial, some machine M_yes has ⟨M_yes⟩ ∈ P. Reduce HALT_TM to P. Given ⟨M, w⟩, output ⟨Q⟩ with

Q = "On input y: run M on w. If it halts, run M_yes on y and answer as it does."

If M halts on w, Q behaves like M_yes on every y, so L(Q) = L(M_yes) and ⟨Q⟩ ∈ P, because P depends only on the language. If M runs forever on w, Q accepts nothing, so L(Q) = ∅ = L(M_∅) and ⟨Q⟩ ∉ P. So HALT_TM ≤m P, and P is undecidable. If instead ⟨M_∅⟩ ∈ P, apply the same argument to the complement of P, which is also a non-trivial property of the language and does not contain ⟨M_∅⟩; a decider for P would decide its complement. ∎

So all of these are undecidable, with no new proof needed: "does M accept the empty string?", "is L(M) finite?", "is L(M) context-free?", "does M accept some palindrome?", "does M accept exactly one string?".

### What Rice's theorem does not cover

Read the conditions carefully, because questions that break them may well be decidable.

- **Properties of the machine, not its language.** "Does M have at most 10 states?" is decided by counting. "Does M, on input w, ever move its head left?" was decided in last lesson's practice. Two machines with the same language can differ on these, so Rice says nothing.
- **Bounded behaviour.** "Does M halt on w within 1,000 steps?" is decided by simulating 1,000 steps.
- **Trivial properties.** "Is L(M) recognisable?" is true of every machine, so it is decided by the program that always says yes.

Some machine properties are still undecidable, by a direct reduction instead: "does M ever enter state q₇?" is one.

### Beyond machines: computation histories and PCP

Undecidability reaches problems that mention no machines at all. The tool is the **computation history**: the list of configurations C₁, C₂, …, C_k of a run of M on w, each following from the one before by one transition, from the start configuration to an accepting one. M accepts w exactly when an accepting history exists. Checking that a given string is a valid history is easy and local: compare each configuration with the next, symbol by symbol. Encoding that check into another problem gives a reduction from A_TM.

- **[[Post's correspondence problem|pcp]].** Given dominoes, each with a string on top and one on the bottom, is there a sequence of them (repeats allowed) whose top strings and bottom strings spell the same word? For the dominoes b/ca, a/ab, ca/a, abc/c, the sequence a/ab, b/ca, ca/a, a/ab, abc/c spells `abcaaabc` on both rows. Deciding this for arbitrary dominoes is undecidable: the dominoes can be built so that the only matches spell accepting histories.
- **Grammars.** From PCP, it follows that "is this context-free grammar ambiguous?", "does this grammar generate every string?" (ALL_CFG) and "do these two grammars generate the same language?" (EQ_CFG) are all undecidable.

### What it costs

A reduction must be computable, and every reduction in this lesson runs in time polynomial in the size of its input, usually linear: it writes down a fixed wrapper around the code it was given. That will matter in lesson 15, which uses the same idea with a time limit on f. What a reduction never costs is a run of the given machine: the output program does the running, later, if anyone runs it.

### Where it is used

- **Compilers.** An optimiser would like to know "is this function's result always 0?" or "do these two functions compute the same thing?". Rice's theorem says no algorithm answers that for all programs, so optimisers use conservative rules: they transform code only when a simple, decidable test proves it safe.
- **[[Sound and complete analysis|sound]].** Every program analysis must give up something: it can never report exactly the programs with a bug. Type checkers reject some correct programs; bug finders miss some bugs.
- **Mathematics.** Hilbert's tenth problem, whether a polynomial equation has whole-number solutions, was proved undecidable in 1970 by reducing the halting problem to it.
- **Complexity theory.** The next three lessons use reductions with a time limit to compare problems by difficulty, which is what NP-completeness is.

**Watch out:**

- **The direction.** To show B is undecidable, reduce a known undecidable problem **to** B: A ≤m B. Reducing B to a known undecidable problem proves nothing about B.
- **The reduction must not run the machine.** A function that simulates M on w to decide what to output is not computable when M runs forever. Build a program that would do the running.
- **Both directions of the "if and only if".** Check that yes-instances go to yes-instances **and** that no-instances go to no-instances.
- **Rice is about languages.** If two machines with the same language can differ on the property, Rice's theorem does not apply, and the property may be decidable.

::: context rice Henry Gordon Rice
Henry Rice proved his theorem in his 1951 doctoral thesis at Syracuse University and published it in 1953, in a paper on classes of recursively enumerable sets. The theorem is short to state and to prove, but it explains decades of frustration in program verification: any tool that claims to decide a non-trivial property of what arbitrary programs do must be wrong on some programs. Modern verification tools accept that and aim to be right on the programs people actually write.
:::

::: context pcp Emil Post and his dominoes
Emil Post, a Polish-born American logician, reached ideas close to Turing's in the 1920s and published a machine model in 1936, the same year. In 1946 he introduced the correspondence problem as a simple combinatorial question with no mention of machines, and proved it undecidable. Because it is about strings only, it became the standard starting point for proving questions about grammars and formal languages undecidable. A version with two dominoes is decidable; with five or more it is known to be undecidable.
:::

::: context sound Sound and complete
An analysis that looks for a property, say "this program can divide by zero", is **sound** if it never misses a program that has the property, and **complete** if it never flags one that does not. Rice's theorem says that for any non-trivial semantic property, no analysis that always halts can be both. Type checkers choose soundness and reject some correct programs; many bug finders choose completeness and miss some bugs. Knowing which one a tool chose tells you how to read its output.
:::
--- task
Programs are generator functions as in lesson 13: each `yield` is a step, a program halts when it returns, and it accepts exactly when it returns `True`. A reduction takes an instance and builds a new one, **without running anything**. Write two reductions.

- `halt_to_accept(p, x)`: a reduction from HALT_TM to A_TM. Return a pair `(q, y)` of a program and an input such that `q` accepts `y` exactly when `p` halts on `x`, whatever `p` returns when it halts. If `p` runs forever on `x`, `q` must run forever on `y` too.
- `rice_program(p, x, yes)`: Rice's construction. Return a program `q` that, on any input `y`, first runs `p` on `x` (ignoring its answer) and then runs `yes` on `y` and returns whatever `yes` returns. So if `p` halts on `x`, `q` behaves exactly like `yes`, taking `p`'s steps plus `yes`'s steps; if `p` runs forever on `x`, `q` accepts nothing.

Neither function may start `p` running: they must return at once, even when `p` runs forever. Use `yield from` inside the program you build.
--- starter
def halt_to_accept(p, x):
    def q(y):
        return True
        yield
    return q, x


def rice_program(p, x, yes):
    return yes
--- solution
def halt_to_accept(p, x):
    def q(y):
        yield from p(y)
        return True
    return q, x


def rice_program(p, x, yes):
    def q(y):
        yield from p(x)
        return (yield from yes(y))
    return q
--- hint

+++ practice | Two small reductions
--- task
Two warm-ups on the definition of A ≤m B: a computable f with w ∈ A exactly when f(w) ∈ B.

- `make_reduction(in_a, yes_b, no_b)`: `in_a(w)` decides a language A (it returns `True` or `False` and always halts), `yes_b` is a string in B and `no_b` is a string not in B. Return a function `f` that is a reduction from A to B: `f(w)` is `yes_b` when w ∈ A and `no_b` otherwise. (This proves that every decidable language reduces to every language that is neither empty nor everything.)
- `eq_to_empty(d1, d2)`: a reduction from EQ_DFA to E_DFA. Given two total DFAs over the same alphabet (dictionaries as in lesson 2), return a total DFA whose language is **empty exactly when** L(d1) = L(d2): the product DFA that accepts the strings accepted by exactly one of them. Its states are the pairs `(q1, q2)` of all states (reachable or not), it starts at the pair of start states, and it moves on symbol c from `(q1, q2)` to the pair of the two DFAs' moves.
--- starter
def make_reduction(in_a, yes_b, no_b):
    return lambda w: yes_b


def eq_to_empty(d1, d2):
    return d1
--- solution
def make_reduction(in_a, yes_b, no_b):
    def f(w):
        return yes_b if in_a(w) else no_b
    return f


def eq_to_empty(d1, d2):
    states = {(p, q) for p in d1["states"] for q in d2["states"]}
    delta = {}
    for p, q in states:
        for c in d1["alphabet"]:
            delta[((p, q), c)] = (d1["delta"][(p, c)], d2["delta"][(q, c)])
    accept = {(p, q) for p, q in states if (p in d1["accept"]) != (q in d2["accept"])}
    return {"states": states, "alphabet": d1["alphabet"], "delta": delta,
            "start": (d1["start"], d2["start"]), "accept": accept}
--- hint
`make_reduction` returns a new function. It never needs to look at B at all: the two strings it was given already have the right answers.
--- hint
For `eq_to_empty`, build the product as in lesson 2. A pair accepts when exactly one of its two states accepts: `(p in d1["accept"]) != (q in d2["accept"])`.
--- check case | make_reduction from "even length" to "contains aa"

=> ["aa", "b", "aa", "b"]

+++ practice | Equivalence of machines is neither recognisable nor co-recognisable
--- task
Programs are generator functions as in lesson 13. Write two reductions from A_TM (does `p` accept `x`?) to questions about pairs of programs. Each returns a pair `(q1, q2)` of programs, and must not start `p` running.

- `to_equal(p, x)`: L(q1) = L(q2) exactly when `p` accepts `x`. Use a `q1` that accepts every input at once, and a `q2` that, on any input, runs `p` on `x` and accepts exactly when `p` accepted. This shows A_TM ≤m EQ_TM, so EQ_TM is not co-recognisable.
- `to_unequal(p, x)`: L(q1) ≠ L(q2) exactly when `p` accepts `x`. Use a `q1` that rejects every input at once, and the same kind of `q2`. This shows A_TM ≤m the complement of EQ_TM, so EQ_TM is not recognisable.

Accepting means returning exactly `True`. If `p` runs forever on `x`, `q2` must run forever too.
--- starter
def to_equal(p, x):
    def q(y):
        return True
        yield
    return q, q


def to_unequal(p, x):
    return to_equal(p, x)
--- solution
def accept_all(y):
    return True
    yield


def reject_all(y):
    return False
    yield


def runs_p(p, x):
    def q(y):
        answer = yield from p(x)
        return answer is True
    return q


def to_equal(p, x):
    return accept_all, runs_p(p, x)


def to_unequal(p, x):
    return reject_all, runs_p(p, x)
--- hint
Write the two fixed programs first, each with an unreachable `yield` after its `return`. Then a helper that builds `q2`: it runs `p(x)` with `yield from`, keeps the answer, and returns `answer is True`.
--- hint
Why it works: `q2` accepts every input if `p` accepts `x`, and nothing otherwise. So it has the same language as "accept everything" exactly when `p` accepts `x`, and a different language from "reject everything" exactly when `p` accepts `x`.

+++ practice | Checking a computation history
--- task
An accepting **computation history** of a Turing machine M on input w is a list of configurations C₁, …, C_k where C₁ is the start configuration, each Cᵢ₊₁ follows from Cᵢ by one transition, and C_k is in the accept state. Histories are the tool behind the undecidability of PCP and of questions about grammars, because checking one is easy and local.

Here a configuration is a tuple `(state, head, tape)`: the state, the head position, and the tape as a string. Cells past the end of the string are blanks, so two configurations are the same when their states and heads are equal and their tapes are equal after removing blanks (`"_"`) from the right end.

Write `is_accepting_history(tm, w, history)` for a Turing machine as in lesson 11. Return `True` exactly when:

- `history` is not empty, and its first configuration is `(start state, 0, w)`;
- each configuration after the first is the one that lesson 11's step gives from the one before (a missing transition means there is no next configuration);
- the last configuration is in the accept state, and no earlier one is in the accept or reject state (the machine halts as soon as it reaches one).
--- starter
def is_accepting_history(tm, w, history):
    return bool(history) and history[-1][0] == tm["accept"]
--- solution
def same(c, d):
    return c[0] == d[0] and c[1] == d[1] and c[2].rstrip("_") == d[2].rstrip("_")


def step(tm, config):
    state, head, tape = config
    cells = list(tape.rstrip("_"))
    while len(cells) <= head:
        cells.append("_")
    move = tm["delta"].get((state, cells[head]))
    if move is None:
        return None
    new_state, cells[head], direction = move
    head = head + 1 if direction == "R" else max(0, head - 1)
    return (new_state, head, "".join(cells))


def is_accepting_history(tm, w, history):
    if not history or not same(history[0], (tm["start"], 0, w)):
        return False
    for before, after in zip(history, history[1:]):
        if before[0] in (tm["accept"], tm["reject"]):
            return False
        following = step(tm, before)
        if following is None or not same(following, after):
            return False
    return history[-1][0] == tm["accept"]
--- hint
Write a helper that computes the next configuration from one configuration, as lesson 11's simulator does one step, but starting from a tuple: pad the tape with blanks up to the head, read, write, move. Return `None` when there is no transition.
--- hint
Compare configurations with a helper that strips trailing blanks from both tapes. Then walk through consecutive pairs with `zip(history, history[1:])`, rejecting the history as soon as a halting state appears before the end or a pair does not match.

1)\n        s += 1\n    return ("accept" if q == M["accept"] else "reject", "".join(t).rstrip("_"), s)\n', ns) or ns['tm'])({}))

+++ practice | Never halting reduces to halting everywhere
--- task
TOTAL = {p : program p halts on every input} is not even recognisable. The proof reduces the complement of HALT (programs that run forever on a given input) to TOTAL with a **bounded simulation**. Write it.

`never_halts_to_total(p, x)` returns a program `q` (a generator function) that, on input `y`, runs `p` on `x` for at most `len(y)` steps. If `p` halted within those steps (after k ≤ `len(y)` steps), `q` runs forever. Otherwise `q` accepts. Then:

- if `p` never halts on `x`, `q` accepts every input, so `q` is in TOTAL;
- if `p` halts on `x` after T steps, `q` runs forever on every `y` with `len(y) ≥ T`, so `q` is not in TOTAL.

Mind the edges: a `p` that halts after 0 steps makes `q` run forever even on `""`, and halting after exactly `len(y)` steps counts as halting within them. Building `q` must not start `p`. Include lesson 13's `run(p, x, budget)` in your answer if you use it.
--- starter
def never_halts_to_total(p, x):
    def q(y):
        return True
        yield
    return q
--- solution
def run(p, x, budget):
    g = p(x)
    for steps in range(budget + 1):
        try:
            next(g)
        except StopIteration as stop:
            return ("accept" if stop.value is True else "reject", steps)
    return ("running", budget)


def never_halts_to_total(p, x):
    def q(y):
        verdict, _ = run(p, x, len(y))
        if verdict != "running":
            while True:
                yield
        return True
    return q
--- hint
Inside `q`, simulate `p` on `x` with a budget of `len(y)` steps; lesson 13's `run` does exactly that and always returns. It does not matter whether the simulation's steps count as `q`'s steps: either way `q` halts when the simulation says "running".
--- hint
Then branch on the verdict: anything other than `"running"` means `p` halted within the budget, so loop forever with `yield`; otherwise return `True`.
--- check case | p halts after 3 steps: q accepts inputs shorter than 3 and runs forever on longer ones, the boundary included
(lambda RUN: [RUN(never_halts_to_total(lambda x: (yield from range(3)) or True, "w"), y, 5000)[0] for y in ["", "a", "ab", "abc", "abcd"]])((lambda ns: exec('def run(p, x, b):\n    g = p(x)\n    for k in range(b + 1):\n        try:\n            next(g)\n        except StopIteration as e:\n            return ("accept" if e.value is True else "reject", k)\n    return ("running", b)\n', ns) or ns['run'])({}))
=> ["accept", "accept", "accept", "running", "running"]
?? A budget of len(y) steps must see a program that halts after exactly len(y) steps.
--- check case | p halts at once: q runs forever even on the empty input; the answer p gives does not matter
(lambda RUN: [RUN(never_halts_to_total(lambda x: (yield from range(0)) or False, "w"), y, 5000)[0] for y in ["", "a"]])((lambda ns: exec('def run(p, x, b):\n    g = p(x)\n    for k in range(b + 1):\n        try:\n            next(g)\n        except StopIteration as e:\n            return ("accept" if e.value is True else "reject", k)\n    return ("running", b)\n', ns) or ns['run'])({}))

--- check test | p never halts on x: q accepts every input up to length 20, and halts in time

--- check test | p is simulated on x, not on q's input: p halts only on "go"
(lambda RUN, it: (lambda q1, q2: RUN(q1, "yyyyyy", 5000)[0] == "running" and RUN(q2, "yyyyyy", 5000)[0] == "accept")(never_halts_to_total(lambda x: (yield from range(2)) if x == "go" else (yield from it.repeat(None)), "go"), never_halts_to_total(lambda x: (yield from range(2)) if x == "go" else (yield from it.repeat(None)), "stop")))((lambda ns: exec('def run(p, x, b):\n    g = p(x)\n    for k in range(b + 1):\n        try:\n            next(g)\n        except StopIteration as e:\n            return ("accept" if e.value is True else "reject", k)\n    return ("running", b)\n', ns) or ns['run'])({}), __import__("itertools"))

+++ practice | Debug: the reduction that runs the program
--- task
`halt_to_nonempty(p, x)` should be a reduction from HALT to the complement of E_TM: it returns a program `q` that accepts some input (in fact every input) exactly when `p` halts on `x`, and accepts nothing otherwise.

This version decides the answer while it builds `q`: it simulates `p` for 10,000 steps and returns a program that accepts everything or nothing. That is not a reduction at all. It gives the wrong program for any `p` that halts after more than 10,000 steps, and no budget would fix that; a reduction must never run `p`. Fix it so that the returned program does the running itself, and the function returns at once whatever `p` is.
--- starter
def run(p, x, budget):
    g = p(x)
    for steps in range(budget + 1):
        try:
            next(g)
        except StopIteration as stop:
            return ("accept" if stop.value is True else "reject", steps)
    return ("running", budget)


def halt_to_nonempty(p, x):
    verdict, _ = run(p, x, 10000)
    if verdict == "running":
        def q(y):
            return False
            yield
    else:
        def q(y):
            return True
            yield
    return q
--- solution
def run(p, x, budget):
    g = p(x)
    for steps in range(budget + 1):
        try:
            next(g)
        except StopIteration as stop:
            return ("accept" if stop.value is True else "reject", steps)
    return ("running", budget)


def halt_to_nonempty(p, x):
    def q(y):
        yield from p(x)
        return True
    return q
--- hint
The returned program, not `halt_to_nonempty`, is the one allowed to run `p`. What should `q` do first on any input?
--- hint
`q` runs `p(x)` with `yield from` and then returns `True`. If `p` runs forever, so does `q`, and it accepts nothing, which is exactly right. `run` is no longer needed.
--- check test | p halts after 20,000 steps: q must accept
(lambda RUN: RUN(halt_to_nonempty(lambda x: (yield from range(20000)) or False, "w"), "any", 50000)[0] == "accept")((lambda ns: exec('def run(p, x, b):\n    g = p(x)\n    for k in range(b + 1):\n        try:\n            next(g)\n        except StopIteration as e:\n            return ("accept" if e.value is True else "reject", k)\n    return ("running", b)\n', ns) or ns['run'])({}))
?? No budget chosen while building q can be big enough. Let q run p itself.
--- check test | p runs forever or halts quickly: q accepts nothing, or everything
(lambda RUN, it: all(RUN(halt_to_nonempty(lambda x: (yield from it.repeat(None)), "w"), y, 3000)[0] == "running" and RUN(halt_to_nonempty(lambda x: (yield from range(5)) or False, "w"), y, 3000)[0] == "accept" for y in ["", "a", "abc"]))((lambda ns: exec('def run(p, x, b):\n    g = p(x)\n    for k in range(b + 1):\n        try:\n            next(g)\n        except StopIteration as e:\n            return ("accept" if e.value is True else "reject", k)\n    return ("running", b)\n', ns) or ns['run'])({}), __import__("itertools"))

+++ practice | Searching for a PCP match
--- task
An instance of **Post's correspondence problem** is a list of dominoes, each a pair `(top, bottom)` of non-empty strings. A **match** is a non-empty sequence of domino indices, repeats allowed, whose top strings, joined, equal its bottom strings, joined. PCP is undecidable, so no program can always say whether a match exists; a search can only look up to a limit.

Write `pcp_solve(dominoes, max_len)` that returns a match of **at most `max_len` dominoes** as a list of indices, or `None` if there is none that short. Return a **shortest** match, and among the shortest, the first in dictionary order of the index lists. For the lesson's dominoes `[("b", "ca"), ("a", "ab"), ("ca", "a"), ("abc", "c")]` the answer is `[1, 0, 2, 1, 3]`.

It must be fast: trying every sequence takes kᵐ steps for k dominoes and length m. Search breadth first over the **overhang** instead: after some dominoes, one row has spelled a prefix of the other, and only the leftover part of the longer row matters for the future (if neither row is a prefix of the other, that sequence is dead). Visit each overhang only the first time it appears. The checks include a search to length 60.
--- starter
from itertools import product


def pcp_solve(dominoes, max_len):
    for n in range(1, min(max_len, 6) + 1):
        for seq in product(range(len(dominoes)), repeat=n):
            if "".join(dominoes[i][0] for i in seq) == "".join(dominoes[i][1] for i in seq):
                return list(seq)
    return None
--- solution
def pcp_solve(dominoes, max_len):
    level = [((), "", "")]
    seen = set()
    for _ in range(max_len):
        following = []
        for seq, top, bottom in level:
            for i, (t, b) in enumerate(dominoes):
                a, c = top + t, bottom + b
                if a.startswith(c):
                    a, c = a[len(c):], ""
                elif c.startswith(a):
                    a, c = "", c[len(a):]
                else:
                    continue
                if a == "" and c == "":
                    return list(seq) + [i]
                if (a, c) not in seen:
                    seen.add((a, c))
                    following.append((seq + (i,), a, c))
        level = following
    return None
--- hint
Keep one level of the search at a time: a list of `(sequence, top leftover, bottom leftover)`, where at most one leftover is non-empty. Add each domino to each entry, cut off the common prefix (one of the two new strings must start with the other, or the entry dies), and collect the survivors for the next level.
--- hint
Stop the moment both leftovers are empty: that sequence is a match. Going through the level in order, and the dominoes in index order, finds the shortest match and the first in dictionary order among those. A set of leftovers already seen keeps the levels small: a later sequence with the same leftover can do nothing the earlier one could not.
--- check case | The lesson's dominoes, and an instance whose tops are always longer
(pcp_solve([("b", "ca"), ("a", "ab"), ("ca", "a"), ("abc", "c")], 10), pcp_solve([("ab", "a"), ("ba", "b")], 10))
=> ([1, 0, 2, 1, 3], None)
--- check case | A match exists but is longer than the limit; a single domino that matches itself
(pcp_solve([("1", "111"), ("10111", "10"), ("10", "0")], 3), pcp_solve([("1", "111"), ("10111", "10"), ("10", "0")], 4), pcp_solve([("ab", "ab"), ("a", "a")], 5))
=> (None, [1, 0, 0, 2], [0])
--- check test | 300 seeded random instances: the same answer as trying every sequence up to length 5
(lambda R, PCP: all((lambda D: pcp_solve(D, 5) == PCP(D, 5))([("".join(R.choice("ab") for _ in range(R.randint(1, 3))), "".join(R.choice("ab") for _ in range(R.randint(1, 3)))) for _ in range(R.randint(1, 3))]) for _ in range(300)))(__import__("random").Random(23), (lambda ns: exec('def pcp(D, L):\n    import itertools\n    for n in range(1, L + 1):\n        for seq in itertools.product(range(len(D)), repeat=n):\n            if "".join(D[i][0] for i in seq) == "".join(D[i][1] for i in seq):\n                return list(seq)\n    return None\n', ns) or ns['pcp'])({}))
?? Among the shortest matches, return the first in dictionary order: try dominoes in index order, level by level.
--- check test | A long search: the shortest match for these dominoes has 36 dominoes
(lambda D, r: r is not None and len(r) == 36 and "".join(D[i][0] for i in r) == "".join(D[i][1] for i in r))([("a", "ab"), ("aba", "a"), ("b", "aa")], pcp_solve([("a", "ab"), ("aba", "a"), ("b", "aa")], 60))

+++ question | Which way to reduce
--- ask
You want to prove that a new problem B is undecidable, and you know that HALT is undecidable. What must you show?
--- choice
B ≤m HALT: a computable f turning each instance of B into an instance of HALT with the same answer.
--- choice correct
HALT ≤m B: a computable f turning each instance of HALT into an instance of B with the same answer.
--- choice
That B and HALT have the same number of yes-instances.
--- choice
That some instance of B cannot be decided.
--- why
If HALT ≤m B, a decider for B would give a decider for HALT (compute f, then ask B's decider), which cannot exist. B ≤m HALT only says B is no harder than HALT, which is true of many easy problems. And a single instance is always decidable: one of the two constant programs answers it.

+++ question | Does Rice's theorem apply?
--- ask
Which of these questions about a Turing machine M does Rice's theorem prove undecidable?
--- choice
Does M have more than 100 states?
--- choice
Does M halt on the empty input within 1,000 steps?
--- choice correct
Does M accept at least one palindrome?
--- choice
Does M, on input w, ever move its head left?
--- why
"Accepts some palindrome" depends only on L(M), and it is non-trivial: some machines do, some do not. The number of states is a property of the machine's description, which two machines with the same language can differ on. A bounded run is decided by simulating 1,000 steps. And the left-move question was decided in lesson 13's practice by a pigeonhole argument.

+++ question | The reduction that simulates
--- ask
A student reduces HALT to E_TM like this: "given ⟨M, w⟩, simulate M on w; if it halts, output a machine that accepts everything, otherwise output a machine that rejects everything." What is wrong?
--- choice
Nothing: the output machine's language is empty exactly when M does not halt on w.
--- choice correct
The function is not computable: when M runs forever on w, the simulation never ends, so no output is ever produced.
--- choice
The direction is wrong: it should reduce E_TM to HALT.
--- choice
A reduction may only output machines with finite languages.
--- why
A reduction must halt on every input. "Otherwise output ..." assumes you can tell when M does not halt, which is the halting problem itself. The fix is to output a machine Q that does the simulation when it is run: "on input y, run M on w, and accept if it halts". Building Q only writes down its description, which always finishes.

=== theory-15 | Time complexity: P, NP and polynomial-time reductions
--- teach
Lessons 13 and 14 sorted problems into solvable and unsolvable. But a problem that is decidable only by a program running for longer than the age of the universe is not solved in any useful sense. This lesson measures **how long** a decider takes, on the Turing machines of lessons 11 and 12, and builds the two most important classes of problems: **P**, what can be decided in polynomial time, and **NP**, what can be **checked** in polynomial time. DSA II's lesson 33 met these as working definitions; here they get precise definitions and proofs. You will build both directions of the theorem that links guessing machines to checkers.

### Counting the steps of a decider

Let M be a deterministic Turing machine that halts on every input. Its **running time** is the function t(n) = the largest number of steps M takes on any input of length n. So running time is always **worst case**, and always a function of the **input length**.

**TIME(t(n))** is the class of languages decided by some single-tape Turing machine with running time O(t(n)).

For example, {0ᵏ1ᵏ : k ≥ 0} is in TIME(n²): zigzag across the input, crossing off one 0 and one 1 per pass, as in lesson 11. A cleverer single-tape machine does it in O(n log n) by crossing off every other 0 and every other 1 and checking that the counts have the same parity each time. A two-tape machine does it in O(n): copy the 0s to the second tape and match them against the 1s.

So the exact class depends on the model. But lesson 12 showed that a k-tape machine running in time t(n) ≥ n can be simulated by a single-tape machine in time O(t(n)²), and a random-access machine, which is what real computers are, can be simulated with a polynomial slowdown too. Polynomial factors, then, are the price of changing models, and a class that ignores them does not depend on the model.

### The class P

**P** = ⋃ₖ TIME(nᵏ): the languages decided in polynomial time by a deterministic single-tape Turing machine.

P is taken as the precise meaning of "efficiently solvable", an idea called the **[[Cobham–Edmonds thesis|cobham]]**. Two reasons, both from the robustness above: P is the same for every reasonable deterministic model, and P is closed under composition, since a polynomial number of calls to a polynomial-time subroutine, on inputs of polynomial size, takes polynomial time.

**Reasonable encodings.** Input length means the length of a reasonable encoding. A graph can be given as an adjacency matrix or as edge lists: their lengths are polynomially related, so either gives the same class. A number must be written in **binary** (or any base above 1): the number N takes about log₂ N bits. Writing N in **unary**, as N ones, makes the input exponentially longer, and then a slow algorithm looks fast. Trial division of N up to √N is about 2^(b/2) steps for a b-bit number: exponential, not polynomial.

### Three problems in P, with proofs

**PATH** = {⟨G, s, t⟩ : the directed graph G has a path from s to t}. Mark s; then repeatedly mark every vertex that has an edge from a marked vertex, until a round marks nothing new; accept if t is marked. There are at most n rounds (each marks a vertex or stops), and each round looks at every edge, so the time is polynomial. Trying every possible path instead would take up to n! steps.

**RELPRIME** = {⟨a, b⟩ : gcd(a, b) = 1}. Euclid's algorithm replaces (a, b) by (b, a mod b) until b = 0. **Claim:** every two iterations at least halve the first number. After one iteration the pair is (b, a mod b), with b ≤ a. If b ≤ a/2, the next first number, a mod b, is less than b ≤ a/2. If b > a/2, then a mod b = a − b < a/2. So after two iterations the first number is below a/2. It can halve only about log₂ a times, so there are O(log a) iterations: linear in the number of bits. Each iteration is a division of numbers with O(log a) bits, so the total is polynomial in the input length.

**Every context-free language is in P.** For a fixed grammar in Chomsky normal form, CYK (lesson 8) fills an n × n table, each cell in O(n) time, so it decides membership in O(n³).

### Nondeterministic time and NP

A nondeterministic Turing machine (lesson 12) is a **decider** when every branch of its computation halts on every input. Its running time t(n) is the length of the **longest branch** on any input of length n. **NTIME(t(n))** is the class of languages decided by such a machine in time O(t(n)), and

**NP** = ⋃ₖ NTIME(nᵏ).

Lesson 12 simulated a nondeterministic machine deterministically by searching its tree of branches breadth first. With at most b choices per step and branches of length t(n), there are at most b^t(n) branches, each simulated in O(t(n)) steps, so NTIME(t(n)) ⊆ TIME(2^O(t(n))). Nobody knows how to do fundamentally better.

### Verifiers

The same class has a second description that needs no guessing machines.

A **verifier** for a language A is an algorithm V such that

A = {w : V accepts ⟨w, c⟩ for some string c}.

The string c is a **certificate**. V is a **polynomial-time verifier** when it runs in time polynomial in |w|, the length of w alone. It can then read only polynomially many symbols of c, so certificates can be taken to have length polynomial in |w|.

**Theorem.** A language is in NP if and only if it has a polynomial-time verifier.

**Proof.** Suppose a nondeterministic machine N decides A in time nᵏ. Number the possible moves in each state and symbol 0, 1, 2, … in a fixed order. A branch of N on w is then described by a sequence of choice numbers, one per step, of length at most nᵏ. Let V, on input ⟨w, c⟩, simulate N on w, taking at step i the move numbered cᵢ; V accepts if that branch accepts, and rejects if the branch rejects, if a number names a move that does not exist, or if the numbers run out. V takes polynomial time, and some c makes it accept exactly when some branch of N accepts w, that is, when w ∈ A.

Conversely, suppose V verifies A in time nᵏ. Build N: on input w, **guess** a string c of length at most nᵏ, one symbol per step (each symbol is a nondeterministic choice), then run V on ⟨w, c⟩ and answer as it does. Every branch halts in polynomial time, and some branch accepts exactly when some certificate works. ∎

The task builds both directions for real machines: the verifier from a nondeterministic machine, and the search that finds a certificate when one exists.

In code, the fixed order of moves is just `sorted`: the certificate's numbers index into the sorted list of moves.

```python
moves = {("q", "a"): {("q", "a", "R"), ("p", "x", "L")}}
options = sorted(moves[("q", "a")])
print(options)       # [('p', 'x', 'L'), ('q', 'a', 'R')]: choice 0 is the first
```

### Problems in NP

Each of these has a short certificate that is fast to check.

- **SAT**: a satisfying assignment. Check each clause.
- **CLIQUE** = {⟨G, k⟩ : G has k vertices that are all joined to each other}: the k vertices. Check that they are k distinct vertices and check every pair.
- **HAMPATH** = {⟨G, s, t⟩ : a directed path from s to t visits every vertex once}: the path, in order.
- **SUBSET-SUM**: the chosen numbers.
- **COMPOSITES** = {x : x = pq for whole numbers p, q > 1}: a factor p. Check that 1 < p < x and p divides x.
- **3-COLOUR**: a colour for each vertex.

A verifier must check **everything** the definition asks, and reject malformed certificates, because it must reject every c when w ∉ A. For 3-colouring:

```python
def valid_colouring(n, edges, colour):
    if len(colour) != n or any(c not in (0, 1, 2) for c in colour):
        return False
    return all(colour[u] != colour[v] for u, v in edges)

print(valid_colouring(3, [(0, 1), (1, 2)], [0, 1, 0]))   # True
print(valid_colouring(3, [(0, 1), (1, 2)], [0, 0, 1]))   # False: edge (0, 1)
```

### What is known

- **P ⊆ NP**: a verifier can ignore the certificate and decide the language itself.
- **NP ⊆ EXPTIME** = ⋃ₖ TIME(2^(nᵏ)): try every certificate of length up to nᵏ.
- **coNP** is the class of complements of NP languages: problems whose **no**-instances have short certificates. "This formula is unsatisfiable" has no known short certificate. P is closed under complement (swap the answers of a deterministic decider), so P ⊆ NP ∩ coNP. Swapping the answers of a nondeterministic machine does **not** complement its language, since "some branch accepts" becomes "some branch rejects"; nobody knows whether NP = coNP.
- **P = NP?** is open. It asks whether every problem whose solutions can be checked quickly can be solved quickly. Most researchers believe not. **[[Gödel's letter|godel]]** of 1956 already asked a version of it.

### Polynomial-time reductions

Lesson 14's reductions had to be computable. For complexity they must also be fast.

**Definition.** A **polynomial-time reduction** from A to B is a function f computable in polynomial time such that w ∈ A exactly when f(w) ∈ B. Then A is **polynomial-time reducible** to B: **A ≤p B**.

**Theorem.** If A ≤p B and B ∈ P, then A ∈ P.

**Proof.** Let f take time at most nᶜ and let B be decided in time mᵈ. A machine that takes nᶜ steps writes at most nᶜ symbols, so |f(w)| ≤ nᶜ (for n ≥ 1). On input w, compute f(w), then decide it: that takes nᶜ + (nᶜ)ᵈ = nᶜ + n^(cd) steps, a polynomial. ∎

The same argument shows that ≤p is transitive, and that if A ≤p B and B ∈ NP, then A ∈ NP.

**Example: CLIQUE ≤p INDEPENDENT-SET.** An independent set has no edge between any two of its vertices. Map ⟨G, k⟩ to ⟨Ḡ, k⟩, where the **complement graph** Ḡ has an edge exactly where G has none. A set S is a clique in G exactly when every pair in S is an edge of G, exactly when no pair in S is an edge of Ḡ, exactly when S is independent in Ḡ. Building Ḡ takes O(n²) time. So the two problems are equally hard, up to polynomial time.

### What it costs

The gap between polynomial and exponential is the point of all this. At a billion steps per second: n³ steps for n = 1,000 take one second; 2ⁿ steps for n = 100 take about 4 × 10¹³ years. Doubling the computer's speed lets an n³ algorithm handle inputs 26% larger in the same time, and a 2ⁿ algorithm inputs **one** element larger. A polynomial-time verifier costs almost nothing to run; the question is whether the search for a certificate can ever be made as cheap.

### Where it is used

- **Cryptography** depends on problems in NP that are believed hard: factoring (behind RSA) and discrete logarithms. Both are in NP ∩ coNP, so they are probably **not** NP-complete, yet no fast algorithm is known.
- **Certificates in practice.** A SAT solver that says "unsatisfiable" can emit a proof log that a small, trusted checker verifies; a solver that says "satisfiable" emits the assignment. **[[Proof of work|work]]** in cryptocurrencies is a puzzle whose answer is hard to find and quick to check.
- **Primality.** PRIMES is in NP (the fourth practice problem builds the certificate) and in coNP (a factor), and since **[[2002 it is in P|aks]]**.

**Watch out:**

- **Input size is the length of the encoding.** For numbers, that is the number of digits. An algorithm that loops N times on input N is exponential.
- **NP does not mean "not polynomial".** It means nondeterministic polynomial time, equivalently "checkable in polynomial time". Every problem in P is in NP.
- **The certificate must be short, and the verifier must check everything.** A verifier that trusts part of the certificate, such as the claim that k listed vertices are distinct, accepts wrong certificates, and then it verifies a different language.
- **The verifier's time is measured in |w|, the input, not in the certificate.**

::: context cobham Cobham and Edmonds
In 1965 Alan Cobham and Jack Edmonds, independently, proposed polynomial time as the formal meaning of "efficient". Edmonds, in a paper on matchings in graphs, wrote of a "good" algorithm as one whose running time grows like a power of the input size, and contrasted it with exhaustive search. The thesis has exceptions at both ends: an n¹⁰⁰ algorithm is useless, and an exponential algorithm can be fine on the inputs that arise. But natural problems in P almost always turn out to have algorithms with small exponents.
:::

::: context godel Gödel's lost letter
In March 1956 Kurt Gödel wrote to John von Neumann, who was dying of cancer, asking how many steps a machine needs to decide whether a mathematical statement has a proof of length n. If it were about n or n², he noted, mathematicians' creative work could be replaced by machines. That is essentially the P versus NP question, fifteen years before Stephen Cook's formulation. The letter was rediscovered in the late 1980s.
:::

::: context work Proof of work
To add a block to Bitcoin's ledger, a miner must find a number, the nonce, such that the SHA-256 hash of the block, including the nonce, starts with many zero bits. Finding one takes, on average, trillions of attempts across the whole network; checking a proposed nonce takes one hash. It is the verifier asymmetry of NP made into an economic mechanism, although hash puzzles are believed hard for different reasons than NP-complete problems.
:::

::: context aks Primality in polynomial time
In 2002 Manindra Agrawal and his students Neeraj Kayal and Nitin Saxena, at IIT Kanpur, published "PRIMES is in P": a deterministic algorithm that tests whether a b-bit number is prime in time polynomial in b. It settled a question open for decades. In practice, randomised tests such as Miller–Rabin are faster and are what cryptographic libraries use, with an error probability that can be made smaller than the chance of a hardware fault.
:::
--- task
Nondeterministic Turing machines are dictionaries as in lesson 12's practice: `"delta"` maps `(state, symbol)` to a **set** of moves `(new state, symbol to write, "L" or "R")`, and a missing key means no move. States and symbols are strings. The moves for a state and symbol are numbered in **sorted order**: `sorted(ntm["delta"].get((state, symbol), set()))`, so choice 0 is the first of that list. The run follows lesson 11's conventions (head on cell 0, a blank there for the empty input, left moves stop at cell 0).

Write both directions of the lesson's theorem.

- `verify_branch(ntm, w, choices)`: the verifier. `choices` is a list of whole numbers. Follow the branch they describe, taking move number `choices[i]` at step i. Return `True` exactly when the machine is in its accept state after using **all** the choices. Return `False` if the branch reaches the accept or reject state while choices remain, if a number is not a valid index into the sorted moves (including when there are no moves), or if the choices run out in any state other than accept.
- `find_certificate(ntm, w, max_steps)`: the search. Return the shortest list of choices, of length at most `max_steps`, for which `verify_branch` would return `True`, and among the shortest, the first in dictionary order. Return `None` if there is none. Search breadth first, one depth at a time, trying moves in sorted order, and skip a configuration (state, head, tape) that has already been reached.
--- starter
def verify_branch(ntm, w, choices):
    return False


def find_certificate(ntm, w, max_steps):
    return None
--- solution
def moves(ntm, state, symbol):
    return sorted(ntm["delta"].get((state, symbol), set()))


def verify_branch(ntm, w, choices):
    tape = list(w) if w else ["_"]
    head, state = 0, ntm["start"]
    for c in choices:
        if state == ntm["accept"] or state == ntm["reject"]:
            return False
        options = moves(ntm, state, tape[head])
        if not 0 <= c < len(options):
            return False
        state, tape[head], direction = options[c]
        head = head + 1 if direction == "R" else max(0, head - 1)
        if head == len(tape):
            tape.append("_")
    return state == ntm["accept"]


def find_certificate(ntm, w, max_steps):
    start = (ntm["start"], 0, tuple(w) if w else ("_",))
    level = [(start, [])]
    seen = {start}
    for depth in range(max_steps + 1):
        following = []
        for (state, head, tape), choices in level:
            if state == ntm["accept"]:
                return choices
            if state == ntm["reject"]:
                continue
            for i, (new_state, symbol, direction) in enumerate(moves(ntm, state, tape[head])):
                cells = list(tape)
                cells[head] = symbol
                h = head + 1 if direction == "R" else max(0, head - 1)
                if h == len(cells):
                    cells.append("_")
                config = (new_state, h, tuple(cells))
                if config not in seen:
                    seen.add(config)
                    following.append((config, choices + [i]))
        level = following
    return None
--- hint
For `verify_branch`, simulate the machine as in lesson 11, one choice per step. Before each step, a halting state means the choices are too many; after the loop, accept only if the state is the accept state.
--- hint
For `find_certificate`, keep one level of the search at a time: a list of (configuration, choices so far), with the tape as a tuple so a configuration can go in a set. Check each entry for the accept state before expanding it; expand in sorted-move order and keep only configurations not seen before.
--- hint
Why the first accepting entry is the right answer: the levels are in order of length, and inside a level the entries are in dictionary order of their choices, because parents are expanded in order and children in move order. A configuration skipped as already seen was first reached by a choice list that is shorter, or equally long and earlier in dictionary order, and anything it leads to, the earlier one leads to as well.

+++ practice | Euclid's algorithm is polynomial
--- task
RELPRIME = {⟨a, b⟩ : gcd(a, b) = 1} is in P because Euclid's algorithm takes a number of iterations that grows with the number of **digits**, not with the numbers themselves.

Write two functions, for whole numbers `a, b ≥ 0`:

- `gcd_steps(a, b)`: run Euclid's algorithm: while b is not 0, replace (a, b) by (b, a mod b). Return the pair `(gcd, iterations)`, where `iterations` counts the replacements. So `gcd_steps(12, 0)` is `(12, 0)`, `gcd_steps(0, 12)` is `(12, 1)`, and `gcd_steps(0, 0)` is `(0, 0)`.
- `relprime(a, b)`: `True` exactly when gcd(a, b) = 1. (gcd(0, 0) is 0, so `relprime(0, 0)` is `False`, while `relprime(1, 0)` is `True`.)

The checks use numbers with hundreds of digits, where counting up to a would never finish, and check the lesson's bound: at most 2·log₂(a) + 2 iterations when a ≥ b ≥ 1.
--- starter
def gcd_steps(a, b):
    return (max(a, b), 0)


def relprime(a, b):
    return gcd_steps(a, b)[0] == 1
--- solution
def gcd_steps(a, b):
    iterations = 0
    while b != 0:
        a, b = b, a % b
        iterations += 1
    return (a, iterations)


def relprime(a, b):
    return gcd_steps(a, b)[0] == 1
--- hint
The loop is three lines: while `b != 0`, set `a, b = b, a % b` and count one iteration. When the loop ends, `a` holds the gcd.
--- hint
The edge cases follow from the loop itself: with `b = 0` it runs zero times and returns `a`; with `a = 0` the first iteration swaps the numbers.
--- check case | Small cases and the zeros
[gcd_steps(12, 0), gcd_steps(0, 12), gcd_steps(0, 0), gcd_steps(12, 18), gcd_steps(17, 5)]
=> [(12, 0), (12, 1), (0, 0), (6, 3), (1, 3)]
--- check case | relprime, with the zero cases
[relprime(8, 15), relprime(8, 14), relprime(1, 0), relprime(0, 0), relprime(0, 1), relprime(5, 5)]
=> [True, False, True, False, True, False]
--- check test | Consecutive Fibonacci numbers are the worst case: F(k+1), F(k) takes k − 1 iterations
(lambda F: all(gcd_steps(F[k + 1], F[k]) == (1, k - 1) for k in range(2, 300)))((lambda f: [f.append(f[-1] + f[-2]) or f for _ in range(302)][-1])([0, 1]))
--- check test | 200 seeded pairs of numbers with up to 300 digits: the right gcd, within 2·log₂(a) + 2 iterations
(lambda R, m: all((lambda a, b: (lambda g, k: g == m.gcd(a, b) and k <= 2 * m.log2(max(a, b)) + 2)(*gcd_steps(max(a, b), min(a, b))))(R.randint(1, 10 ** R.randint(1, 300)) * R.choice([1, 7, 91]), R.randint(1, 10 ** R.randint(1, 300)) * R.choice([1, 7, 13])) for _ in range(200)))(__import__("random").Random(4), __import__("math"))

+++ practice | Closure under concatenation, in P and in NP
--- task
The **concatenation** of languages A and B is AB = {uv : u ∈ A, v ∈ B}. Both P and NP are closed under it. Write the two constructions.

- `concat_decide(in_a, in_b, w)`: `in_a` and `in_b` are deciders (functions from a string to `True` or `False`). Return `True` exactly when w = uv for some u with `in_a(u)` and v with `in_b(v)`. Try every split point, from 0 to `len(w)`: that is n + 1 splits and at most 2(n + 1) calls, so polynomially many calls to polynomial-time deciders.
- `concat_verify(ver_a, ver_b, w, cert)`: `ver_a(u, c)` and `ver_b(v, c)` are verifiers. A certificate for w is a tuple `(i, c1, c2)`: a split point `i`, a whole number with 0 ≤ i ≤ `len(w)`, and certificates for the two parts. Return `True` exactly when the certificate has that form and `ver_a(w[:i], c1)` and `ver_b(w[i:], c2)` both hold. Anything else, such as a split point outside the string (a negative one included: Python's slicing would quietly accept it) or a certificate of the wrong shape, gives `False`.
--- starter
def concat_decide(in_a, in_b, w):
    return in_a(w) or in_b(w)


def concat_verify(ver_a, ver_b, w, cert):
    i, c1, c2 = cert
    return ver_a(w[:i], c1) and ver_b(w[i:], c2)
--- solution
def concat_decide(in_a, in_b, w):
    return any(in_a(w[:i]) and in_b(w[i:]) for i in range(len(w) + 1))


def concat_verify(ver_a, ver_b, w, cert):
    if not isinstance(cert, tuple) or len(cert) != 3:
        return False
    i, c1, c2 = cert
    if type(i) is not int or not 0 <= i <= len(w):
        return False
    return bool(ver_a(w[:i], c1) and ver_b(w[i:], c2))
--- hint
For `concat_decide`, loop over `i` in `range(len(w) + 1)` and test `w[:i]` and `w[i:]`. Both `i = 0` (u empty) and `i = len(w)` (v empty) must be tried.
--- hint
For `concat_verify`, check the certificate's shape before using it: a tuple of length 3 whose first item is an `int` between 0 and `len(w)`. Only then call the two verifiers.
--- check case | "Even-length palindromes" followed by "only b's": splits at both ends are tried
(lambda pal, bs: [concat_decide(pal, bs, w) for w in ["", "abba", "abbabbb", "bb", "ab", "abab"]])(lambda u: u == u[::-1] and len(u) % 2 == 0, lambda v: set(v) <= {"b"})
=> [True, True, True, True, False, False]
--- check test | At most 2(n + 1) calls to the deciders, on a string of length 40
(lambda C: (concat_decide(lambda u: C.append(1) or False, lambda v: C.append(1) or True, "a" * 40), len(C) <= 82)[1])([])
--- check case | concat_verify: composite unary length with a factor, then a square number of b's with its root
(lambda va, vb: [concat_verify(va, vb, "a" * 6 + "b" * 9, c) for c in [(6, 2, 3), (6, 3, 3), (6, 1, 3), (6, 2, 2), (5, 2, 3), (-9, 2, 3), (6, 2), [6, 2, 3], (6.0, 2, 3), (16, 2, 3)]])(lambda u, c: set(u) <= {"a"} and type(c) is int and 1 < c < len(u) and len(u) % c == 0, lambda v, c: set(v) <= {"b"} and type(c) is int and c * c == len(v))
=> [True, True, False, False, False, False, False, False, False, False]
?? Python's slicing accepts a negative split point without complaint, so check 0 <= i <= len(w) yourself.

+++ practice | 2-SAT is in P
--- task
SAT is NP-complete, as the next lesson shows, but when every clause has at most **two** literals it is in P. A formula is a list of clauses, each a list of one or two nonzero integers (`3` means x₃ and `-3` means ¬x₃), over the variables 1 to `nvars`.

The method: a clause (a ∨ b) says "if not a, then b" and "if not b, then a". Build the **implication graph**, with a vertex for each of the 2·nvars literals and those two edges for each clause (a one-literal clause (a) is (a ∨ a): the edge ¬a → a). The formula is unsatisfiable exactly when some variable x has x and ¬x in the same strongly connected component (DSA I, Kosaraju). Otherwise, number the components in topological order (Kosaraju's second pass finds them in that order) and make x true exactly when the component of x comes **later** than the component of ¬x.

Write `two_sat(clauses, nvars)` that returns a satisfying assignment, as a dict from every variable 1 to `nvars` to `True` or `False`, or `None` if there is none. It must be fast: a check uses 20,000 variables, so use loops rather than recursion for the depth-first searches.
--- starter
def two_sat(clauses, nvars):
    return {v: True for v in range(1, nvars + 1)}
--- solution
def two_sat(clauses, nvars):
    def node(lit):
        return 2 * (abs(lit) - 1) + (0 if lit > 0 else 1)

    n = 2 * nvars
    graph = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    reverse = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    for clause in clauses:
        a, b = clause[0], clause[-1]
        for u, v in ((node(-a), node(b)), (node(-b), node(a))):
            graph[u].append(v)
            reverse[v].append(u)

    order = []
    visited = [False] * n
    for s in range(n):
        if visited[s]:
            continue
        visited[s] = True
        stack = [(s, 0)]
        while stack:
            v, i = stack.pop()
            if i < len(graph[v]):
                stack.append((v, i + 1))
                u = graph[v][i]
                if not visited[u]:
                    visited[u] = True
                    stack.append((u, 0))
            else:
                order.append(v)

    comp = [-1] * n
    count = 0
    for s in reversed(order):
        if comp[s] != -1:
            continue
        comp[s] = count
        stack = [s]
        while stack:
            v = stack.pop()
            for u in reverse[v]:
                if comp[u] == -1:
                    comp[u] = count
                    stack.append(u)
        count += 1

    assignment = {}
    for x in range(1, nvars + 1):
        if comp[node(x)] == comp[node(-x)]:
            return None
        assignment[x] = comp[node(x)] > comp[node(-x)]
    return assignment
--- hint
Give literal x the vertex 2(x − 1) and ¬x the vertex 2(x − 1) + 1. For each clause, with `a = clause[0]` and `b = clause[-1]` (the same literal for a one-literal clause), add the edges ¬a → b and ¬b → a, and the reversed edges for the second pass.
--- hint
Kosaraju: a first depth-first search on the graph records vertices in the order they finish; a second pass, on the reversed graph, takes vertices in reverse finishing order and labels everything it reaches with a new component number. Write both searches with an explicit stack. The component numbers come out in topological order of the implication graph.
--- hint
If some x and ¬x share a component, return `None`: x implies ¬x and ¬x implies x. Otherwise set x to `True` when `comp[x] > comp[¬x]`: then ¬x does not lead to x through the graph's order, so choosing the later component never forces a contradiction.
--- check case | (x₁) and (¬x₁) is unsatisfiable; (x₁ ∨ x₂)(¬x₁ ∨ x₂)(¬x₂ ∨ x₃) forces x₂ and x₃
(two_sat([[1], [-1]], 1), (lambda a: a is not None and a[2] and a[3])(two_sat([[1, 2], [-1, 2], [-2, 3]], 3)))
=> (None, True)
--- check case | The empty formula: every variable gets a value; no variables at all
(sorted(two_sat([], 3)), two_sat([], 0))
=> ([1, 2, 3], {})
--- check test | 400 seeded random formulas: None exactly when unsatisfiable, otherwise an assignment that satisfies every clause
(lambda R, SAT, OK: all((lambda n, C: (lambda a: (a is None) == (not SAT(C, n)) and (a is None or (set(a) == set(range(1, n + 1)) and OK(C, a))))(two_sat(C, n)))(*(lambda n: (n, [[R.choice([1, -1]) * R.randint(1, n) for _ in range(R.randint(1, 2))] for _ in range(R.randint(0, 3 * n))]))(R.randint(1, 7))) for _ in range(400)))(__import__("random").Random(22), (lambda ns: exec('def sat(C, n):\n    import itertools\n    for bits in itertools.product([False, True], repeat=n):\n        if all(any(bits[abs(l) - 1] == (l > 0) for l in c) for c in C):\n            return True\n    return False\n', ns) or ns['sat'])({}), (lambda ns: exec('def satisfies(C, a):\n    return isinstance(a, dict) and all(any(a.get(abs(l)) is (l > 0) for l in c) for c in C)\n', ns) or ns['satisfies'])({}))

+++ practice | A certificate that a number is prime
--- task
COMPOSITES is in NP: a factor is a short certificate. **PRIMES** is in NP too, which is less obvious. In 1975 Vaughan Pratt showed how to certify that n is prime, using a theorem of Lucas: n ≥ 3 is prime exactly when some g satisfies g^(n−1) ≡ 1 (mod n) and g^((n−1)/q) ≢ 1 (mod n) for every prime q that divides n − 1. The certificate lists the prime factors q of n − 1, and, since each q must itself be shown prime, a certificate for each q, recursively.

A **Pratt certificate** for n is `None` when n = 2, and otherwise a tuple `(g, factors)` where `factors` is a list of pairs `(q, certificate for q)`. Write `verify_pratt(n, cert)` that returns `True` exactly when all of these hold:

- n = 2 and `cert` is `None`; or n ≥ 3 and `cert` is a tuple `(g, factors)` with g a whole number, 1 ≤ g < n;
- the q's in `factors` are distinct whole numbers of at least 2, each dividing n − 1, and n − 1 is a product of powers of them only: dividing n − 1 by every q as often as it goes leaves 1;
- pow(g, n − 1, n) is 1, and pow(g, (n − 1) // q, n) is not 1 for every q;
- every q's certificate is valid, by the same rules.

Everything else, such as n < 2, or a certificate of the wrong shape, gives `False`. Use Python's three-argument `pow`, which computes powers modulo n by repeated squaring: the checks use a 22-digit prime.
--- starter
def verify_pratt(n, cert):
    if n == 2:
        return cert is None
    g, factors = cert
    return pow(g, n - 1, n) == 1
--- solution
def verify_pratt(n, cert):
    if type(n) is not int or n < 2:
        return False
    if n == 2:
        return cert is None
    if not isinstance(cert, tuple) or len(cert) != 2:
        return False
    g, factors = cert
    if type(g) is not int or not 1 <= g < n or not isinstance(factors, list):
        return False
    rest = n - 1
    seen = set()
    for item in factors:
        if not isinstance(item, tuple) or len(item) != 2:
            return False
        q, sub = item
        if type(q) is not int or q < 2 or q in seen or (n - 1) % q != 0:
            return False
        seen.add(q)
        while rest % q == 0:
            rest //= q
        if pow(g, (n - 1) // q, n) == 1 or not verify_pratt(q, sub):
            return False
    return rest == 1 and pow(g, n - 1, n) == 1
--- hint
Handle the base case and the shape first: n = 2 needs `None`; anything below 2 is `False`; otherwise the certificate must be a pair `(g, factors)` with 1 ≤ g < n.
--- hint
Walk through the factors, keeping `rest`, a copy of n − 1 that you divide by each q as often as it goes. Reject a repeated q, a q that does not divide n − 1, or a q with pow(g, (n − 1) // q, n) equal to 1, and check each q's certificate by calling `verify_pratt` recursively. At the end, `rest` must be 1 and pow(g, n − 1, n) must be 1.

=> [True, True, True, True]

=> [False, False, False, False]
?? 96 = 2⁵ · 3: dividing out only the 2s leaves 3, so the list of factors is incomplete.
--- check case | Numbers that are not prime, and certificates of the wrong shape

=> [False, False, False, False, False, False]

?? Use pow(g, e, n): computing g ** e first would build a number with more digits than there are atoms in the universe.
