+++ practice | Debug: the clique checker that counts a vertex twice
--- task
`verify_clique(n, edges, k, c)` is a verifier for CLIQUE: the graph has vertices `0` to `n - 1` and undirected `edges` (a list of pairs), and the certificate `c` is a list of vertices. It should return `True` exactly when `c` holds `k` **different** vertices of the graph and every two of them are joined by an edge.

It accepts `[0, 0, 0]` as a clique of size 3 in any graph that has a vertex 0, and `[1, 2, 1]` whenever 1 and 2 are joined, so it verifies the wrong language: with it, every graph with an edge would "have" cliques of every size. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
def verify_clique(n, edges, k, c):
    joined = {frozenset(e) for e in edges}
    if len(c) != k:
        return False
    if not all(0 <= v < n for v in c):
        return False
    for i in range(len(c)):
        for j in range(i + 1, len(c)):
            if c[i] != c[j] and frozenset((c[i], c[j])) not in joined:
                return False
    return True
--- solution
def verify_clique(n, edges, k, c):
    joined = {frozenset(e) for e in edges}
    if len(c) != k or len(set(c)) != k:
        return False
    if not all(0 <= v < n for v in c):
        return False
    for i in range(len(c)):
        for j in range(i + 1, len(c)):
            if frozenset((c[i], c[j])) not in joined:
                return False
    return True
--- hint
Which pairs of the certificate does the inner loop skip without checking any edge?
--- hint
Two equal vertices are not "two vertices of a clique". Reject a certificate with a repeated vertex: `len(set(c))` must be `k` too. Then no pair needs to be skipped.
--- check case | Repeated vertices are rejected
[verify_clique(3, [(0, 1)], 3, [0, 0, 0]), verify_clique(3, [(1, 2)], 3, [1, 2, 1]), verify_clique(3, [(1, 2)], 2, [1, 1])]
=> [False, False, False]
--- check case | Real cliques, a missing edge, a vertex outside the graph, the wrong size, and k = 0
[verify_clique(4, [(0, 1), (1, 2), (0, 2), (2, 3)], 3, [2, 0, 1]), verify_clique(4, [(0, 1), (1, 2), (2, 3)], 3, [0, 1, 2]), verify_clique(3, [(0, 1), (1, 2), (0, 2)], 3, [0, 1, 3]), verify_clique(3, [(0, 1), (1, 2), (0, 2)], 2, [0, 1, 2]), verify_clique(3, [], 0, []), verify_clique(3, [], 1, [2])]
=> [True, False, False, False, True, True]
--- check test | 300 seeded random graphs and certificates: True exactly when the certificate is a clique of size k
(lambda R, it: all((lambda n, E, c, k: verify_clique(n, E, k, c) == (len(c) == k and len(set(c)) == k and all(0 <= v < n for v in c) and all(frozenset(p) in {frozenset(e) for e in E} for p in it.combinations(c, 2))))(*(lambda n: (n, [(u, v) for u in range(n) for v in range(u + 1, n) if R.random() < 0.6], [R.randrange(n + 1) for _ in range(R.randint(0, 4))], R.randint(0, 4)))(R.randint(1, 5))) for _ in range(300)))(__import__("random").Random(24), __import__("itertools"))

+++ practice | A survey route: Hamiltonian path to Hamiltonian cycle
--- task
A survey drone must visit every station of a directed network once, starting at station s and ending at station t (HAMPATH). A library routine only answers the cycle question: is there a directed cycle through every vertex exactly once (HAMCYCLE)? Reduce one to the other, in polynomial time, and translate the answer back.

The graph has vertices `0` to `n - 1` and directed `edges`, a list of pairs `(u, v)`.

- `hampath_to_hamcycle(n, edges, s, t)`: return `(n + 1, new_edges)`: the same graph with one new vertex, number `n`, and two new edges, t → n and n → s. Any Hamiltonian cycle of the new graph must pass through the new vertex, entering from t and leaving to s, so removing it leaves a Hamiltonian path from s to t; and a path from s to t closes into a cycle through the new vertex. Keep the old edges as they are.
- `cycle_to_path(n, cycle)`: `cycle` is a Hamiltonian cycle of that new graph, given as a list of its `n + 1` vertices in cycle order, starting anywhere. Return the Hamiltonian path of the original graph, from s to t, as a list of `n` vertices: the vertices after the new vertex `n`, going round the cycle, up to the one before it.

For example, for n = 3, s = 0, t = 2 and edges 0 → 1 → 2, the new graph has the cycle `[1, 2, 3, 0]`, and `cycle_to_path(3, [1, 2, 3, 0])` is `[0, 1, 2]`. The edge case n = 1 (s = t = 0) has the path `[0]`.
--- starter
def hampath_to_hamcycle(n, edges, s, t):
    return (n, list(edges) + [(t, s)])


def cycle_to_path(n, cycle):
    return cycle[:n]
--- solution
def hampath_to_hamcycle(n, edges, s, t):
    return (n + 1, list(edges) + [(t, n), (n, s)])


def cycle_to_path(n, cycle):
    i = cycle.index(n)
    return cycle[i + 1:] + cycle[:i]
--- hint
Adding just the edge t → s is not enough: a Hamiltonian cycle of the new graph might not use that edge at all, and then it says nothing about a path from s to t. A new vertex whose only edges are t → new → s forces every Hamiltonian cycle through t, new, s in that order.
--- hint
To translate back, find where the new vertex sits in the cycle with `cycle.index(n)`, and rotate: the part after it, then the part before it.
--- check case | The example: the new graph, and a cycle turned back into the path
(hampath_to_hamcycle(3, [(0, 1), (1, 2)], 0, 2), cycle_to_path(3, [1, 2, 3, 0]), cycle_to_path(3, [3, 0, 1, 2]), cycle_to_path(3, [0, 1, 2, 3]))
=> ((4, [(0, 1), (1, 2), (2, 3), (3, 0)]), [0, 1, 2], [0, 1, 2], [0, 1, 2])
--- check test | 150 seeded random directed graphs: a Hamiltonian path from s to t exists exactly when the new graph has a Hamiltonian cycle
(lambda R, HAM: all((lambda n, E, s, t: (lambda N, E2: sorted(E2) == sorted(E + [(t, n), (n, s)]) and N == n + 1 and (len(HAM(n, E, s, t)) > 0) == (len(HAM(N, E2, cycle=True)) > 0))(*hampath_to_hamcycle(n, E, s, t)))(*(lambda n: (n, [(u, v) for u in range(n) for v in range(n) if u != v and R.random() < 0.45], R.randrange(n), R.randrange(n)))(R.randint(2, 5))) for _ in range(150)))(__import__("random").Random(25), (lambda ns: exec('def ham(n, E, s=None, t=None, cycle=False):\n    import itertools\n    S = set(map(tuple, E))\n    out = []\n    for p in itertools.permutations(range(n)):\n        if cycle:\n            if n >= 1 and p[0] == 0 and all((p[i], p[(i + 1) % n]) in S for i in range(n)) and (n > 1 or (0, 0) in S):\n                out.append(list(p))\n        elif p[0] == s and p[-1] == t and all((p[i], p[i + 1]) in S for i in range(n - 1)):\n            out.append(list(p))\n    return out\n', ns) or ns['ham'])({}))
?? A single edge t → s would let the new graph have a Hamiltonian cycle that ignores it. Add a new vertex between t and s.

+++ question | How big is the input?
--- ask
A program tests whether a number N is prime by trying every divisor from 2 up to √N. The input is N written in decimal, b digits long. What is its running time as a function of the input length b?
--- choice
Polynomial: about √N divisions, and √N is smaller than N.
--- choice correct
Exponential: √N is about 10^(b/2), so the number of divisions grows exponentially with the number of digits.
--- choice
Linear, since each digit is looked at once.
--- choice
It cannot be measured, because the input is a number, not a string.
--- why
Complexity is measured against the length of the encoding. N has b ≈ log₁₀ N digits, so √N ≈ 10^(b/2), which is exponential in b. Adding one digit multiplies the work by about 3. That is why primality in polynomial time (AKS, 2002) was a real theorem, and why trial division never factors a 600-digit RSA modulus.

+++ question | Why certificates must be short
--- ask
Why does the definition of NP require the verifier to run in time polynomial in |w|, rather than in the combined length of w and the certificate?
--- choice
Because certificates are always shorter than w.
--- choice correct
Otherwise every decidable problem would be in NP: take as certificate the whole, possibly exponentially long, run of a decider, which is quick to check relative to its own length.
--- choice
Because a Turing machine cannot read two inputs.
--- choice
It makes no difference; both definitions give the same class.
--- why
If time could grow with the certificate, a certificate could simply be a complete trace of an exponential-time computation, and checking it step by step takes time polynomial in the trace. The requirement "polynomial in |w|" forces certificates of polynomial length, which is what makes NP a class of problems whose solutions are short and quick to check.

+++ question | Complements of the classes
--- ask
Which statement is known to be true?
--- choice
NP is closed under complement: swap the accept and reject states of the nondeterministic machine.
--- choice correct
P is closed under complement, so P ⊆ NP ∩ coNP.
--- choice
coNP is the class of problems not in NP.
--- choice
NP = coNP, since both contain P.
--- why
Swapping the answers of a deterministic decider decides the complement, so P = coP. For a nondeterministic machine, "some branch accepts" becomes "some branch rejects", which is a different language, not the complement. coNP is the class of complements of NP languages, not the problems outside NP, and whether NP = coNP is open; if P = NP, they would be equal.

=== theory-16 | NP-completeness: Cook–Levin and the classic reductions
--- teach
Last lesson defined P, NP and polynomial-time reductions. This lesson finds the **hardest** problems in NP: problems so universal that every problem in NP reduces to them. There are thousands, and they all begin with one theorem, Cook–Levin, which says that satisfiability is one of them. You will see how that proof turns any polynomial-time computation into a formula, and then you will build the chain of reductions from SAT to 3-SAT, CLIQUE, VERTEX-COVER and SUBSET-SUM, each with its proof, and check them against brute force.

### The hardest problems in NP

**Definition.** A language B is **NP-hard** when A ≤p B for **every** A in NP. It is **NP-complete** when, in addition, B is in NP.

Two facts make the definition useful.

**Theorem.** If some NP-complete language B is in P, then P = NP.

**Proof.** Every A in NP has A ≤p B, and B ∈ P, so A ∈ P by last lesson's theorem. ∎

**Theorem.** If B is NP-complete, B ≤p C, and C is in NP, then C is NP-complete.

**Proof.** For every A in NP, A ≤p B ≤p C, and ≤p is transitive (compose the two functions; a polynomial of a polynomial is a polynomial). ∎

So one NP-complete problem is enough to start a chain. To prove a new problem C NP-complete, you show two things: C is in NP (a verifier), and some known NP-complete problem reduces **to** C.

### The Cook–Levin theorem

**Theorem ([[Cook 1971, Levin 1973|cook]]).** SAT, the satisfiable Boolean formulas, is NP-complete.

SAT is in NP: an assignment is a certificate. The hard part is showing that every A in NP reduces to SAT. Take a nondeterministic machine N that decides A in time nᵏ (for simplicity, nᵏ − 3 steps, so everything fits). Given an input w of length n, the reduction writes a formula φ that is satisfiable exactly when N accepts w.

**The tableau.** An accepting branch of N on w can be written as an nᵏ × nᵏ table: row i is the configuration after i steps, written as a string with the state symbol just left of the head's cell (lesson 11's u q v notation), padded with blanks, and with a `#` at each end. Row 1 is the start configuration q₀w₁w₂…wₙ␣…␣. Each row follows from the one above by one move of N; if the machine accepts early, the accepting row is simply repeated. Call the set of symbols that can appear in a cell C: the states, the tape symbols and `#`.

**The variables.** For every cell (i, j) and every symbol s in C, a variable x_{i,j,s} means "cell (i, j) holds s". That is (nᵏ)² · |C| variables, a polynomial number.

**The formula** is φ = φ_cell ∧ φ_start ∧ φ_accept ∧ φ_move:

- **φ_cell**: every cell holds **exactly one** symbol. For each cell, one clause says "at least one" (x_{i,j,s} for all s, ORed together), and for each pair of different symbols s, t, a clause (¬x_{i,j,s} ∨ ¬x_{i,j,t}) says "not both". The fifth practice problem builds this part.
- **φ_start**: the first row is the start configuration: an AND of one variable per cell of row 1.
- **φ_accept**: some cell holds q_accept: an OR over all cells.
- **φ_move**: every row follows legally from the one above. Here is the key idea: this can be checked through **2 × 3 windows**. A window is a block of 2 rows and 3 columns. It is **legal** if it could appear in some correct pair of consecutive configurations: away from the head, the three cells are copied unchanged; around the head, they change as some move of N allows. There are finitely many legal windows, a list fixed by N and independent of w. φ_move says, for every window position, "the window holds one of the legal windows": an OR of ANDs of six variables, of constant size.

**Why windows suffice.** If the top row is a configuration and every window is legal, then every cell of the bottom row that is not next to the head equals the cell above it (a legal window cannot change a cell far from any state symbol), and the cells around the head change exactly as one move of N says. So the bottom row is a configuration that follows legally. By induction on the rows, a satisfying assignment describes an accepting branch, and an accepting branch fills in a satisfying assignment.

**Size and time.** There are (nᵏ)² cells and window positions, and each contributes a constant-size piece, so |φ| = O(n^(2k)), and the reduction writes it out in polynomial time. ∎

After Cook–Levin, no one needs to talk about tableaux again: every later proof reduces from a problem already known to be NP-complete.

### SAT ≤p 3-SAT

A formula is in **conjunctive normal form (CNF)** when it is an AND of clauses, each an OR of literals, and in **3-CNF** when every clause has exactly three literals (repeats allowed). The Cook–Levin formula can be written in CNF with only a polynomial increase (each φ_move window condition is a constant-size formula, so converting it to CNF costs a constant factor), so **CNF-SAT** is NP-complete. Now reduce CNF-SAT to **3-SAT**, clause by clause, keeping satisfiability.

- A clause with 3 literals is kept.
- (a) becomes (a ∨ a ∨ a), and (a ∨ b) becomes (a ∨ b ∨ b): repeating a literal does not change a clause.
- The empty clause is unsatisfiable. Replace it with (z ∨ z ∨ z) ∧ (¬z ∨ ¬z ∨ ¬z) for a new variable z: also unsatisfiable.
- A clause with k ≥ 4 literals, (l₁ ∨ l₂ ∨ … ∨ l_k), becomes a **chain** of k − 2 clauses with k − 3 new variables z₁, …, z_{k−3}:

(l₁ ∨ l₂ ∨ z₁) ∧ (¬z₁ ∨ l₃ ∨ z₂) ∧ (¬z₂ ∨ l₄ ∨ z₃) ∧ … ∧ (¬z_{k−3} ∨ l_{k−1} ∨ l_k).

**Claim.** For any fixed values of l₁, …, l_k, the chain can be satisfied by some choice of the z's exactly when at least one lᵢ is true.

**Proof.** If every lᵢ is false, the first clause forces z₁ true, the next forces z₂ true, and so on, until the last clause needs ¬z_{k−3}, which is false: no choice works. If some l_j is true, set z₁, …, z_{j−2} true and the rest false. The clauses before l_j's clause are satisfied by their z, l_j's clause by l_j, and the clauses after it by their ¬z. ∎

So the new formula is satisfiable exactly when the old one is, with new variables for each long clause kept separate. The output has at most 2 clauses per input clause or k − 2 for a clause of length k: linear size. The task implements this reduction and the assignment that goes with the proof.

The claim can be checked by brute force for a small chain. With k = 4 there is one new variable z, and the clauses (l₁ ∨ l₂ ∨ z) and (¬z ∨ l₃ ∨ l₄):

```python
from itertools import product

for l1, l2, l3, l4 in product([False, True], repeat=4):
    some_z = any((l1 or l2 or z) and ((not z) or l3 or l4) for z in (False, True))
    assert some_z == (l1 or l2 or l3 or l4)
print("the chain is satisfiable exactly when some literal is true")
```

### 3-SAT ≤p CLIQUE

Given a 3-CNF formula with m clauses, build a graph with one vertex for each literal occurrence: 3m vertices in m groups of three. Join two vertices by an edge when they are in **different** clauses and their literals are **not** opposite (not x and ¬x). Ask for a clique of size k = m.

- **Satisfiable gives a clique.** Pick one true literal in each clause. The m chosen vertices lie in different clauses, and no two are opposite (x and ¬x cannot both be true), so every pair is joined.
- **A clique gives a satisfying assignment.** Vertices in the same clause are never joined, so a clique of size m has exactly one vertex in each clause. No two of its literals are opposite, so they can all be made true at once. Then every clause has a true literal.

The graph has 3m vertices and O(m²) edges. DSA II's lesson 33 did the same with independent sets, which is the complement graph of this one.

### 3-SAT ≤p VERTEX-COVER

A **vertex cover** is a set of vertices touching every edge. Given a 3-CNF formula with variables x₁, …, x_v and clauses c₁, …, c_m, build:

- a **variable gadget** for each xᵢ: two vertices, labelled xᵢ and ¬xᵢ, joined by an edge;
- a **clause gadget** for each clause: a triangle of three vertices, one per literal occurrence;
- a **connecting edge** from each triangle vertex to the variable-gadget vertex with the same literal.

Ask for a cover of size k = v + 2m.

- **Satisfiable gives a cover.** Take the vertex of the true literal in each variable gadget: v vertices, covering the gadget edges. In each triangle, one literal is true; its connecting edge is already covered by the variable gadget, so take the **other two** triangle vertices: 2m more, covering the triangle and the other two connecting edges.
- **A cover gives a satisfying assignment.** Any cover takes at least one vertex of each variable edge and at least two of each triangle, so a cover of size v + 2m takes exactly one and exactly two. Make true the literal whose gadget vertex is taken. In each triangle, the vertex not taken has its connecting edge covered from the variable side, so its literal is true: every clause is satisfied.

### SUBSET-SUM, and many more

**SUBSET-SUM** is NP-complete by a reduction from 3-SAT that writes the formula into the **digits** of numbers: one column per variable and one per clause, numbers that put a 1 in the columns of the clauses a literal satisfies, and a target that asks for exactly one literal per variable and a full score in every clause column. The fourth practice problem builds it. The numbers have v + m digits, so a knapsack DP over the values, which is polynomial in the values, is exponential in this input's length: there is no contradiction with DSA II.

Richard **[[Karp|karp]]** showed 21 problems NP-complete in 1972, among them HAMPATH, 3-COLOUR, INTEGER PROGRAMMING and SET COVER, each by a gadget reduction like these. Today several thousand are known.

### How to prove a problem NP-complete

1. Show it is **in NP**: describe the certificate and a polynomial-time verifier.
2. Choose a known NP-complete problem A. 3-SAT is the usual choice.
3. Give the reduction f from A **to** your problem, and show it runs in polynomial time.
4. Prove **both directions**: a yes-instance of A maps to a yes-instance, and a no-instance maps to a no-instance, usually argued as "a yes answer for f(w) gives a yes answer for w".

### Deciding and finding

NP-completeness is about yes-or-no questions, but people want the satisfying assignment. For SAT, the two are equally hard: with a decider, fix x₁ = true (add the clause (x₁)) and ask whether the formula is still satisfiable; if not, x₁ must be false. Then x₂, and so on: n + 1 questions find an assignment. This is called **self-reducibility**, and it is the second practice problem.

### What it costs

The reductions are all small: SAT to 3-SAT is linear, CLIQUE is O(m²) edges, VERTEX-COVER is O(v + m), SUBSET-SUM writes 2v + 2m numbers of v + m digits. Cook–Levin is O(n^(2k)): polynomial, but only a theorem, never run. For solving, no algorithm faster than exponential is known for any NP-complete problem, though the base can be improved: 3-SAT can be solved in about 1.33ⁿ steps, far better than 2ⁿ.

### Where it is used

- **[[SAT solvers|cdcl]]** are one of the great engineering successes of computer science. Hardware makers check chip designs with them, and they find bugs in software, plan, schedule and break weak cryptography, on formulas with millions of variables. Many hard problems are solved in practice by reducing them **to** SAT, the opposite direction to a hardness proof.
- **[[Package managers|packages]].** Choosing versions of packages that satisfy every dependency and conflict is NP-complete, and tools such as conda and some Linux package managers call a SAT solver.
- **Knowing when to stop looking.** An NP-completeness proof tells a team to use heuristics, approximation, special cases or a solver, instead of hunting for a fast exact algorithm.

**Watch out:**

- **The direction.** To show C is hard, reduce a known NP-complete problem **to** C. Reducing C to SAT shows only that C is in NP.
- **Both directions of the "exactly when".** Many broken reductions map yes to yes but also map some no-instances to yes.
- **The reduction must not solve the problem.** It may not try all assignments to decide what to output: that takes exponential time.
- **NP-hard is not NP-complete.** The halting problem is NP-hard (SAT reduces to it: build a program that halts exactly when it finds a satisfying assignment) but it is not in NP, since it is not even decidable.
- **Two literals per clause is easy.** 2-SAT is in P (last lesson's practice), so a reduction must produce three-literal clauses to prove hardness.

::: context cook Cook and Levin
Stephen Cook proved in 1971, in the paper "The Complexity of Theorem-Proving Procedures", that every problem decided by a polynomial-time nondeterministic machine reduces to deciding whether a formula is a tautology, the complement of satisfiability. In the Soviet Union, Leonid Levin independently found the same idea for search problems and published it in 1973. Cook received the Turing Award in 1982. Levin, who left the USSR in 1978, is now a professor at Boston University.
:::

::: context karp Karp's 21 problems
In 1972 Richard Karp published "Reducibility Among Combinatorial Problems", showing that 21 well-known problems from graph theory, scheduling and logic are all NP-complete, by chains of reductions starting from SAT. It turned Cook's theorem from a curiosity about logic into a tool for every algorithm designer. Garey and Johnson's 1979 book "Computers and Intractability" listed about 300 more, and working programmers still use it as a catalogue.
:::

::: context cdcl How SAT solvers win
Modern solvers use conflict-driven clause learning (CDCL). They assign variables and propagate the consequences (unit propagation, as in the last practice problem). When a clause becomes false, they analyse why, add a new clause that rules out that whole cause, and jump back several decisions at once. Add good heuristics for which variable to try next and occasional restarts, and industrial formulas with millions of clauses are often solved in seconds, even though the worst case is still exponential.
:::

::: context packages Dependency hell is NP-complete
Suppose package A needs B version 2 or 3, B version 3 conflicts with C, and C is needed by D. Choosing a version of each package so that every requirement is met is a satisfiability problem, and a reduction from 3-SAT shows it is NP-complete. The Debian and Eclipse ecosystems, and the conda package manager for Python, use SAT solvers to resolve dependencies. pip uses a backtracking resolver instead, which can take a very long time on unlucky inputs.
:::
--- task
Formulas are lists of clauses, each a list of nonzero integers: `3` means x₃ and `-3` means ¬x₃. Variables are numbered 1 to `nvars`. Assignments are dicts from variable numbers to `True` or `False`. Implement the lesson's SAT ≤p 3-SAT reduction and the assignment from its proof.

- `sat_to_3sat(clauses, nvars)`: return `(new_clauses, new_nvars)`. Go through the clauses in order, and output for each:
  - length 3: the clause itself;
  - length 1, `[a]`: `[a, a, a]`; length 2, `[a, b]`: `[a, b, b]`;
  - length 0: `[z, z, z]` and `[-z, -z, -z]` for a new variable z;
  - length k ≥ 4: the chain `[l1, l2, z1]`, `[-z1, l3, z2]`, …, `[-z(k−3), l(k−1), lk]` with k − 3 new variables.

  New variables are numbered `nvars + 1`, `nvars + 2`, … in the order they are first needed. `new_nvars` is the largest variable number used (`nvars` if none are new). Every output clause is a list of exactly three literals.
- `extend_assignment(clauses, nvars, assignment)`: `assignment` satisfies `clauses`. Return an assignment for all variables 1 to `new_nvars` that satisfies `sat_to_3sat(clauses, nvars)`: keep the old values, and for each long clause whose first true literal is its j-th (counting from 1), make z1, …, z(j−2) true and the other new variables of that clause false.
--- starter
def sat_to_3sat(clauses, nvars):
    return ([c for c in clauses if len(c) == 3], nvars)


def extend_assignment(clauses, nvars, assignment):
    return dict(assignment)
--- solution
def sat_to_3sat(clauses, nvars):
    out = []
    next_var = nvars
    for clause in clauses:
        k = len(clause)
        if k == 0:
            next_var += 1
            z = next_var
            out.append([z, z, z])
            out.append([-z, -z, -z])
        elif k == 1:
            out.append([clause[0]] * 3)
        elif k == 2:
            out.append([clause[0], clause[1], clause[1]])
        elif k == 3:
            out.append(list(clause))
        else:
            zs = list(range(next_var + 1, next_var + k - 2))
            next_var += k - 3
            out.append([clause[0], clause[1], zs[0]])
            for i in range(1, k - 3):
                out.append([-zs[i - 1], clause[i + 1], zs[i]])
            out.append([-zs[-1], clause[-2], clause[-1]])
    return out, next_var


def extend_assignment(clauses, nvars, assignment):
    full = {v: assignment[v] for v in range(1, nvars + 1)}
    next_var = nvars
    for clause in clauses:
        k = len(clause)
        if k == 0:
            next_var += 1
            full[next_var] = False
        elif k >= 4:
            j = next(i for i, lit in enumerate(clause, start=1) if assignment[abs(lit)] == (lit > 0))
            for i in range(1, k - 2):
                full[next_var + i] = i <= j - 2
            next_var += k - 3
    return full
--- hint
Keep a counter `next_var`, starting at `nvars`, and add one to it for each new variable. For a clause of length k ≥ 4, its new variables are the next k − 3 numbers; the first chain clause uses the first two literals, each middle clause one literal, and the last clause the last two.
--- hint
In `extend_assignment`, walk through the clauses in the same order with the same counter, so that you meet the same new variable numbers. For a long clause, find j, the position (from 1) of the first true literal; then new variable number i of that clause is true exactly when i ≤ j − 2.
--- hint
Why that choice works: z1 … z(j−2) true satisfies the chain clauses before l_j's clause (each ends in a true z), l_j satisfies its own clause, and the false z's after it make every later clause start with a true ¬z. A formula with an empty clause has no satisfying assignment, so `extend_assignment` never needs to satisfy one; give its z any value.
--- check case | A clause of length 5 becomes a chain of three with two new variables
sat_to_3sat([[1, 2, 3, 4, 5]], 5)
=> ([[1, 2, 6], [-6, 3, 7], [-7, 4, 5]], 7)
--- check case | Short clauses are padded, the empty clause becomes an unsatisfiable pair, and numbering continues across clauses

=> ([[2, 2, 2], [-1, 3, 3], [4, 4, 4], [-4, -4, -4], [1, -2, 3], [1, 2, 5], [-5, 3, -1]], 5)
--- check case | No clauses at all, and a formula of only 3-literal clauses, are unchanged
(sat_to_3sat([], 4), sat_to_3sat([[1, 2, 3], [-1, -2, -3]], 3))
=> (([], 4), ([[1, 2, 3], [-1, -2, -3]], 3))

+++ practice | 3-SAT to CLIQUE
--- task
Implement the lesson's reduction from 3-SAT to CLIQUE. A formula is a list of clauses, each a list of exactly three nonzero integers (`-2` means ¬x₂; a literal may repeat inside a clause).

Write `three_sat_to_clique(clauses)` that returns `(n, edges, k)`:

- `n` is 3 times the number of clauses: literal number j (0, 1 or 2) of clause number i (from 0) is vertex `3 * i + j`;
- `edges` is a list of pairs `(u, v)` with `u < v`, with no pair twice: an edge joins two vertices exactly when they are in **different** clauses and their literals are not opposite (one is not the negation of the other). Two occurrences of the same literal are joined;
- `k` is the number of clauses.

For example, `[[1, 2, 3], [-1, -2, 3]]` gives 6 vertices, k = 2, and 7 edges: every pair across the two clauses except (0, 3) (x₁ against ¬x₁) and (1, 4) (x₂ against ¬x₂).
--- starter
def three_sat_to_clique(clauses):
    n = 3 * len(clauses)
    edges = [(u, v) for u in range(n) for v in range(u + 1, n)]
    return (n, edges, len(clauses))
--- solution
def three_sat_to_clique(clauses):
    n = 3 * len(clauses)
    literal = [lit for clause in clauses for lit in clause]
    edges = []
    for u in range(n):
        for v in range(u + 1, n):
            if u // 3 != v // 3 and literal[u] != -literal[v]:
                edges.append((u, v))
    return (n, edges, len(clauses))
--- hint
Make a flat list of the literals, so that vertex `u` has the literal `literal[u]`, and vertex `u` belongs to clause `u // 3`.
--- hint
Loop over all pairs `u < v`. Keep the pair when `u // 3 != v // 3` (different clauses) and `literal[u] != -literal[v]` (not opposite).
--- check case | The example from the task
(lambda r: (r[0], sorted(r[1]), r[2]))(three_sat_to_clique([[1, 2, 3], [-1, -2, 3]]))
=> (6, [(0, 4), (0, 5), (1, 3), (1, 5), (2, 3), (2, 4), (2, 5)], 2)
--- check case | One clause has no edges; no clauses give the empty graph with k = 0; repeated literals are joined across clauses
(three_sat_to_clique([[1, -1, 2]]), three_sat_to_clique([]), sorted(three_sat_to_clique([[1, 1, 1], [1, 1, -1]])[1]))
=> ((3, [], 1), (0, [], 0), [(0, 3), (0, 4), (1, 3), (1, 4), (2, 3), (2, 4)])
?? Only vertices in different clauses may be joined, and only when their literals are not x and ¬x.

(lambda R, SAT, CL: all((lambda n, C: (lambda g: len(set(map(tuple, g[1]))) == len(g[1]) and all(u < v for u, v in g[1]) and CL(g[0], g[1], g[2]) == SAT(C, n))(three_sat_to_clique(C)))(*(lambda n: (n, [[R.choice([1, -1]) * R.randint(1, n) for _ in range(3)] for _ in range(R.randint(1, 4))]))(R.randint(1, 3))) for _ in range(200)))(__import__("random").Random(29), (lambda ns: exec('def sat(C, n):\n    import itertools\n    for bits in itertools.product([False, True], repeat=n):\n        if all(any(bits[abs(l) - 1] == (l > 0) for l in c) for c in C):\n            return True\n    return False\n', ns) or ns['sat'])({}), (lambda ns: exec('def clique(n, E, k):\n    import itertools\n    S = {frozenset(e) for e in E}\n    return any(all(frozenset((u, v)) in S for u, v in itertools.combinations(c, 2)) for c in itertools.combinations(range(n), k))\n', ns) or ns['clique'])({}))

+++ practice | From deciding to finding
--- task
Suppose someone hands you a SAT **decider**: `is_sat(clauses)` returns `True` exactly when the formula is satisfiable. Use it to **find** a satisfying assignment, with only polynomially many questions.

Write `find_assignment(clauses, nvars, is_sat)` that returns a dict giving every variable 1 to `nvars` the value `True` or `False` so that every clause is satisfied, or `None` if the formula is unsatisfiable. Call `is_sat` **at most `nvars + 1` times**:

1. If `is_sat(clauses)` is `False`, return `None`.
2. For v = 1, 2, …, `nvars`: add the one-literal clause `[v]` to the formula so far, and ask whether it is still satisfiable. If so, keep that clause and set v to `True`; if not, keep `[-v]` instead and set v to `False` (the formula so far was satisfiable, so it must stay satisfiable with v false). Do not ask again for `[-v]`.

Do not change the list you are given.
--- starter
def find_assignment(clauses, nvars, is_sat):
    if not is_sat(clauses):
        return None
    return {v: True for v in range(1, nvars + 1)}
--- solution
def find_assignment(clauses, nvars, is_sat):
    if not is_sat(clauses):
        return None
    fixed = list(clauses)
    assignment = {}
    for v in range(1, nvars + 1):
        if is_sat(fixed + [[v]]):
            fixed.append([v])
            assignment[v] = True
        else:
            fixed.append([-v])
            assignment[v] = False
    return assignment
--- hint
Keep a growing copy of the formula, `fixed`, that is always satisfiable. For each variable, one question decides its value: is `fixed + [[v]]` satisfiable?
--- hint
If the answer is no, then v is false in every satisfying assignment of `fixed`, so add `[-v]` without asking. That is one question per variable, plus the first one.
--- check test | 300 seeded random formulas, with a brute-force decider that counts its calls: at most nvars + 1 calls, and a correct answer

+++ practice | 3-SAT to VERTEX-COVER
--- task
Implement the lesson's reduction from 3-SAT to VERTEX-COVER, and the cover that the proof builds from a satisfying assignment. Formulas are lists of clauses of exactly three literals over the variables 1 to `nvars`.

- `three_sat_to_vc(clauses, nvars)`: return `(n, edges, k)` with this numbering. Variable i (from 1) has the vertices `2 * (i - 1)` for xᵢ and `2 * (i - 1) + 1` for ¬xᵢ, joined by an edge. Clause j (from 0) has the vertices `2 * nvars + 3 * j + p` for its literals p = 0, 1, 2, joined in a triangle, and each of them is joined to the variable vertex of its literal. `n` is `2 * nvars + 3 * len(clauses)`, `k` is `nvars + 2 * len(clauses)`, and `edges` is a list of pairs `(u, v)` with `u < v`, no pair twice.
- `assignment_to_cover(clauses, nvars, assignment)`: `assignment` (a dict for every variable 1 to `nvars`) satisfies the formula. Return the proof's cover, as a sorted list: the vertex of the true literal of each variable, and in each clause's triangle the two vertices other than the one of its **first** true literal.
--- starter
def three_sat_to_vc(clauses, nvars):
    n = 2 * nvars + 3 * len(clauses)
    return (n, [], nvars + 2 * len(clauses))


def assignment_to_cover(clauses, nvars, assignment):
    return []
--- solution
def literal_vertex(lit):
    return 2 * (abs(lit) - 1) + (0 if lit > 0 else 1)


def three_sat_to_vc(clauses, nvars):
    edges = [(2 * i, 2 * i + 1) for i in range(nvars)]
    for j, clause in enumerate(clauses):
        base = 2 * nvars + 3 * j
        edges += [(base, base + 1), (base, base + 2), (base + 1, base + 2)]
        for p, lit in enumerate(clause):
            edges.append((literal_vertex(lit), base + p))
    return (2 * nvars + 3 * len(clauses), edges, nvars + 2 * len(clauses))


def assignment_to_cover(clauses, nvars, assignment):
    cover = [literal_vertex(v if assignment[v] else -v) for v in range(1, nvars + 1)]
    for j, clause in enumerate(clauses):
        base = 2 * nvars + 3 * j
        first = next(p for p, lit in enumerate(clause) if assignment[abs(lit)] == (lit > 0))
        cover += [base + p for p in range(3) if p != first]
    return sorted(cover)
--- hint
A helper `literal_vertex(lit)` gives the variable-gadget vertex of a literal: `2 * (abs(lit) - 1)`, plus 1 for a negative literal. Every variable vertex is smaller than every clause vertex, so a connecting edge written as `(literal_vertex(lit), clause vertex)` already has `u < v`.
--- hint
For the cover, take `literal_vertex(v)` or `literal_vertex(-v)` according to the value of v; then, for each clause, find the position of its first true literal and add the other two triangle vertices.

=> (9, [(0, 1), (0, 6), (2, 3), (3, 7), (4, 5), (4, 8), (6, 7), (6, 8), (7, 8)], 5)
--- check case | The cover for x₁ false, x₂ false, x₃ true: first true literal is ¬x₂
assignment_to_cover([[1, -2, 3]], 3, {1: False, 2: False, 3: True})
=> [1, 3, 4, 6, 8]

+++ practice | 3-SAT to SUBSET-SUM, digit by digit
--- task
SUBSET-SUM asks whether some of a list of numbers add up exactly to a target. Here is the reduction that proves it NP-complete. For a formula with variables 1 to `nvars` (call that v) and clauses 0 to l − 1, each clause a list of three literals (repeats allowed), every number has v + l decimal digits. Reading left to right, the first v digits belong to the variables 1 to v and the last l digits to the clauses 0 to l − 1. So variable i's digit is worth 10^(v + l − i), and clause j's digit is worth 10^(l − 1 − j).

- For each variable i, two numbers: **yᵢ** (xᵢ true) has a 1 in variable i's digit and, in each clause's digit, the number of times xᵢ occurs in that clause; **zᵢ** (xᵢ false) the same with ¬xᵢ.
- For each clause j, two **slack** numbers gⱼ and hⱼ, each with a 1 in clause j's digit only.
- The **target** has a 1 in every variable digit and a 3 in every clause digit.

No column ever adds up to more than 5, so there are no carries. A subset hits the target exactly when it takes one of yᵢ, zᵢ for each variable (an assignment), and each clause column reaches 3, which the slack can top up only if the chosen literals already give at least 1.

Write two functions:

- `three_sat_to_subset_sum(clauses, nvars)`: return `(numbers, target)`, with `numbers` in the order y₁, z₁, y₂, z₂, …, y_v, z_v, g₀, h₀, g₁, h₁, ….
- `assignment_to_subset(clauses, nvars, assignment)`: for a satisfying assignment, return the sorted list of **indices** into `numbers` of a subset that adds up to the target: yᵢ or zᵢ by the value of xᵢ, and, for each clause with c occurrences of true literals, 3 − c of its slack numbers, g before h.

Edge cases: a variable in no clause still gets its two numbers; with no clauses the target is v ones; with no variables and no clauses the answer is `([], 0)`.
--- starter
def three_sat_to_subset_sum(clauses, nvars):
    return ([1] * (2 * nvars + 2 * len(clauses)), nvars + 3 * len(clauses))


def assignment_to_subset(clauses, nvars, assignment):
    return []
--- solution
def three_sat_to_subset_sum(clauses, nvars):
    l = len(clauses)

    def var_digit(i):
        return 10 ** (nvars + l - i)

    def clause_digit(j):
        return 10 ** (l - 1 - j)

    numbers = []
    for i in range(1, nvars + 1):
        for lit in (i, -i):
            value = var_digit(i)
            for j, clause in enumerate(clauses):
                value += clause.count(lit) * clause_digit(j)
            numbers.append(value)
    for j in range(l):
        numbers += [clause_digit(j), clause_digit(j)]
    target = sum(var_digit(i) for i in range(1, nvars + 1)) + sum(3 * clause_digit(j) for j in range(l))
    return (numbers, target)


def assignment_to_subset(clauses, nvars, assignment):
    chosen = [2 * (i - 1) + (0 if assignment[i] else 1) for i in range(1, nvars + 1)]
    for j, clause in enumerate(clauses):
        true_count = sum(1 for lit in clause if assignment[abs(lit)] == (lit > 0))
        slack = 2 * nvars + 2 * j
        chosen += [slack, slack + 1][:3 - true_count]
    return sorted(chosen)
--- hint
Write two small helpers for the place values: variable i is worth `10 ** (nvars + l - i)` and clause j is worth `10 ** (l - 1 - j)`. Then yᵢ is variable i's place value plus, for each clause, `clause.count(i)` times the clause's place value; zᵢ uses `clause.count(-i)`.
--- hint
For the subset, yᵢ has index `2 * (i - 1)` and zᵢ the next one; clause j's slack numbers have indices `2 * nvars + 2 * j` and one more. Count the true literal occurrences c in each clause and take the first 3 − c slack indices.

=> ([1001, 1000, 100, 101, 11, 10, 1, 1], 1113)
--- check case | A repeated literal counts twice; a variable in no clause; no clauses; nothing at all
(three_sat_to_subset_sum([[1, 1, -1]], 2), three_sat_to_subset_sum([], 2), three_sat_to_subset_sum([], 0))
=> (([102, 101, 10, 10, 1, 1], 113), ([10, 10, 1, 1], 11), ([], 0))
?? Put the number of times the literal occurs in each clause's digit, not just 1.
--- check test | 150 seeded random formulas: some subset adds up to the target exactly when the formula is satisfiable
(lambda R, SAT, it: all((lambda n, C: (lambda nums, t: (t in {sum(s) for r in range(len(nums) + 1) for s in it.combinations(nums, r)}) == SAT(C, n))(*three_sat_to_subset_sum(C, n)))(*(lambda n: (n, [[R.choice([1, -1]) * R.randint(1, n) for _ in range(3)] for _ in range(R.randint(1, 4))]))(R.randint(1, 3))) for _ in range(150)))(__import__("random").Random(34), (lambda ns: exec('def sat(C, n):\n    import itertools\n    for bits in itertools.product([False, True], repeat=n):\n        if all(any(bits[abs(l) - 1] == (l > 0) for l in c) for c in C):\n            return True\n    return False\n', ns) or ns['sat'])({}), __import__("itertools"))

+++ practice | Debug: exactly one symbol per cell
--- task
The part φ_cell of the Cook–Levin formula says that every cell of the tableau holds exactly one symbol. Its building block is `exactly_one(vs)`: for a list of variables, one clause saying at least one is true (all of them, ORed), and, for every pair of **different positions** i < j in the list, a clause `[-vs[i], -vs[j]]` saying not both. For an empty list that is the single empty clause `[[]]`, which nothing satisfies.

`cell_constraints(rows, cols, nsym)` uses it for a whole table: the variable for "cell (i, j) holds symbol s" is `1 + (i * cols + j) * nsym + s`, and the result is the clauses of `exactly_one` for every cell, cell by cell.

The program makes every formula unsatisfiable, even for a single cell with two symbols. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
def exactly_one(vs):
    clauses = [list(vs)]
    for i in range(len(vs)):
        for j in range(i, len(vs)):
            clauses.append([-vs[i], -vs[j]])
    return clauses


def cell_constraints(rows, cols, nsym):
    clauses = []
    for i in range(rows):
        for j in range(cols):
            base = 1 + (i * cols + j) * nsym
            clauses += exactly_one([base + s for s in range(nsym)])
    return clauses
--- solution
def exactly_one(vs):
    clauses = [list(vs)]
    for i in range(len(vs)):
        for j in range(i + 1, len(vs)):
            clauses.append([-vs[i], -vs[j]])
    return clauses


def cell_constraints(rows, cols, nsym):
    clauses = []
    for i in range(rows):
        for j in range(cols):
            base = 1 + (i * cols + j) * nsym
            clauses += exactly_one([base + s for s in range(nsym)])
    return clauses
--- hint
Write out `exactly_one([7])` by hand, as the program builds it. What does the clause `[-7, -7]` say?
--- hint
The inner loop must start one past `i`, so that a variable is never paired with itself. A clause (¬x ∨ ¬x) forces x false.

+++ practice | Antenna scheduling with a SAT solver
--- task
A ground station has k antennas, and some pairs of satellite passes overlap in time, so they need different antennas. That is graph colouring: passes are vertices `0` to `n - 1`, overlapping pairs are `edges`, and antennas are colours `0` to `k - 1`. k-colouring is NP-complete for k ≥ 3, and a practical way to solve it is to reduce it **to** SAT and run a solver.

Write `colour_with_sat(n, edges, k)` that returns a list of `n` colours with no edge joining two vertices of the same colour, or `None` if there is none.

1. **Encode.** The variable `v * k + c + 1` means "vertex v has colour c". For each vertex: a clause saying it has at least one colour, and, for each pair of colours, a clause saying not both. For each edge (u, v) and each colour c: `[-(u*k + c + 1), -(v*k + c + 1)]`.
2. **Solve** with **DPLL**, which you write: repeatedly apply **unit propagation** (drop clauses that are already true, remove false literals from the rest; an empty clause means this branch fails; a clause with one literal left forces that literal), then, if clauses remain, pick a variable from the first remaining clause, try it true, and if that fails, try it false.
3. **Decode** the colour of each vertex from the variables that are true.

Edge cases: an edge from a vertex to itself makes colouring impossible; `n = 0` gives `[]`; `k = 0` with vertices gives `None`. The checks accept any valid colouring, and include a 60-vertex graph where trying all 3⁶⁰ colourings is out of the question.
--- starter
from itertools import product


def colour_with_sat(n, edges, k):
    if n > 12:
        return None
    for colours in product(range(k), repeat=n):
        if all(colours[u] != colours[v] for u, v in edges):
            return list(colours)
    return None
--- solution
def dpll(clauses, assignment):
    while True:
        unit = None
        remaining = []
        for clause in clauses:
            if any(assignment.get(abs(lit)) == (lit > 0) for lit in clause):
                continue
            rest = [lit for lit in clause if abs(lit) not in assignment]
            if not rest:
                return None
            if len(rest) == 1:
                unit = rest[0]
            remaining.append(rest)
        clauses = remaining
        if unit is None:
            break
        assignment = {**assignment, abs(unit): unit > 0}
    if not clauses:
        return assignment
    v = abs(clauses[0][0])
    for value in (True, False):
        found = dpll(clauses, {**assignment, v: value})
        if found is not None:
            return found
    return None


def colour_with_sat(n, edges, k):
    def var(v, c):
        return v * k + c + 1

    clauses = []
    for v in range(n):
        clauses.append([var(v, c) for c in range(k)])
        for c in range(k):
            for d in range(c + 1, k):
                clauses.append([-var(v, c), -var(v, d)])
    for u, v in edges:
        for c in range(k):
            clauses.append([-var(u, c), -var(v, c)])
    model = dpll(clauses, {})
    if model is None:
        return None
    return [next(c for c in range(k) if model.get(var(v, c))) for v in range(n)]
--- hint
Write `dpll(clauses, assignment)` to return a satisfying assignment (a dict) or `None`. Its propagation loop goes through the clauses: skip a clause with a true literal, keep the unassigned literals of the others, fail on an empty result, and remember a one-literal result as the unit to set. Repeat until no unit is found.
--- hint
If clauses are left, branch: take `abs(clauses[0][0])`, call `dpll` with it set to `True` (a new dict, so the other branch is unaffected), and if that returns `None`, with it set to `False`.
--- hint
The encoding handles the edge cases by itself: a self-loop (v, v) gives clauses [¬x, ¬x] that forbid every colour of v, and with k = 0 the "at least one colour" clause of a vertex is empty. When decoding, each vertex has exactly one true colour variable, because of the "not both" clauses.
--- check test | 200 seeded random graphs with up to 8 vertices and k from 1 to 3: a valid colouring exactly when one exists
(lambda R, COL: all((lambda n, E, k: (lambda c: (c is None) == (not COL(n, E, k)) and (c is None or (len(c) == n and all(0 <= x < k for x in c) and all(c[u] != c[v] for u, v in E))))(colour_with_sat(n, E, k)))(*(lambda n: (n, [(u, v) for u in range(n) for v in range(u + 1, n) if R.random() < 0.4], R.randint(1, 3)))(R.randint(1, 8))) for _ in range(200)))(__import__("random").Random(36), (lambda ns: exec('def col(n, E, k):\n    adj = {v: set() for v in range(n)}\n    for u, v in E:\n        if u == v:\n            return False\n        adj[u].add(v)\n        adj[v].add(u)\n    c = [-1] * n\n    def go(v):\n        if v == n:\n            return True\n        for x in range(k):\n            if all(c[u] != x for u in adj[v]):\n                c[v] = x\n                if go(v + 1):\n                    return True\n        c[v] = -1\n        return False\n    return go(0)\n', ns) or ns['col'])({}))
--- check case | A triangle needs 3 antennas; four mutually overlapping passes cannot share 3; a self-loop; no passes; no antennas
(colour_with_sat(3, [(0, 1), (1, 2), (0, 2)], 2), colour_with_sat(4, [(0, 1), (0, 2), (0, 3), (1, 2), (1, 3), (2, 3)], 3), colour_with_sat(2, [(0, 0)], 3), colour_with_sat(0, [], 3), colour_with_sat(1, [], 0))

(lambda E: colour_with_sat(11, E, 3) is None and (lambda c: c is not None and all(c[u] != c[v] for u, v in E))(colour_with_sat(11, E, 4)))([(i, (i + 1) % 5) for i in range(5)] + [(5 + i, (i + 1) % 5) for i in range(5)] + [(5 + i, (i - 1) % 5) for i in range(5)] + [(10, 5 + i) for i in range(5)])

?? Unit propagation does most of the work: once a vertex's colour is fixed, its neighbours lose that colour at once.

+++ question | Proving a new problem hard
--- ask
To prove that a new problem C in NP is NP-complete, which reduction do you give?
--- choice
C ≤p 3-SAT.
--- choice correct
3-SAT ≤p C.
--- choice
Both C ≤p P and P ≤p C.
--- choice
A reduction from C to every problem in NP.
--- why
3-SAT ≤p C means a fast algorithm for C would give one for 3-SAT, and so for all of NP. C ≤p 3-SAT holds for every problem in NP (by Cook–Levin and 3-SAT's completeness), so it shows nothing about hardness. The reduction runs from the known hard problem to the new one.

+++ question | The windows of the tableau
--- ask
In the Cook–Levin proof, why is it enough to check that every 2 × 3 window of the tableau is legal?
--- choice
Because a Turing machine can only read three cells at once.
--- choice correct
Because one step changes only the cells next to the head; a window around the head sees the whole change, and every window away from it must copy its top row.
--- choice
Because the formula would be too large with bigger windows.
--- choice
Because a satisfying assignment must choose exactly one symbol per window.
--- why
In one step the head writes one cell, moves one cell, and the state changes. In the u q v notation that changes at most three neighbouring cells of the row, all within a window of width 3 around the state symbol. So a local check of every window, together with "the top row is a configuration", forces each row to follow from the one above. The window size is fixed by the machine, which keeps each window's condition of constant size.

+++ question | Is the halting problem NP-complete?
--- ask
The halting problem HALT is NP-hard: SAT ≤p HALT. Is it NP-complete?
--- choice
Yes: every NP-hard problem is NP-complete.
--- choice
Yes, because SAT reduces to it in polynomial time.
--- choice correct
No: NP-complete also requires being in NP, and HALT is not even decidable.
--- choice
No, because SAT cannot reduce to an undecidable problem.
--- why
The reduction maps a formula φ to a program that tries every assignment and halts when one satisfies φ, looping forever otherwise; writing that program takes polynomial time. So HALT is at least as hard as everything in NP. But every problem in NP is decidable in exponential time, and HALT is not decidable at all, so it is NP-hard without being NP-complete.

=== theory-17 | Beyond NP: space, games and what hard means
--- teach
The last two lessons measured time, and found a huge class of problems, the NP-complete ones, that nobody can solve fast. This lesson looks further up. First it measures **memory** instead of time, which gives the class PSPACE and a surprising theorem: guessing saves almost no memory. Then it meets the natural PSPACE-complete problems, which are **games**, and the harder class EXPTIME, which contains problems **provably** outside P. It ends with the question engineers actually face: what "hard" means for the inputs you have. You will write the polynomial-space algorithm for quantified formulas, the problem at the top of PSPACE.

### Measuring memory

Let M be a Turing machine that halts on every input. Its **space** on input w is the number of tape cells it scans during the run. Its **space complexity** f(n) is the largest space on any input of length n. **SPACE(f(n))** is the class of languages decided by such machines using O(f(n)) cells, **NSPACE(f(n))** the same for nondeterministic deciders (the largest space over all branches), and

**PSPACE** = ⋃ₖ SPACE(nᵏ).

The key difference from time: **space can be reused**, time cannot. SAT shows how much that matters. Trying every assignment takes 2ⁿ steps, but it needs only the formula, the current assignment (n bits, overwritten each time) and a little bookkeeping: linear space. The same idea works for every problem in NP: try the certificates one at a time in the same cells. So **NP ⊆ PSPACE**, and coNP ⊆ PSPACE as well.

Here is the idea in Python: a counter that runs through all 2ⁿ assignments while holding only one at a time.

```python
def assignments(n):
    bits = [False] * n                 # the only memory used: n cells, reused
    while True:
        yield bits
        i = 0
        while i < n and bits[i]:
            bits[i] = False
            i += 1
        if i == n:
            return
        bits[i] = True

print(sum(1 for _ in assignments(10)))   # 1024 assignments, 10 cells of memory
```

### Space bounds time

**Theorem.** SPACE(f(n)) ⊆ TIME(2^O(f(n))) for f(n) ≥ n. In particular **PSPACE ⊆ EXPTIME**.

**Proof.** A machine with states Q and tape alphabet Γ that uses at most f(n) cells has at most |Q| · f(n) · |Γ|^f(n) configurations: a state, a head position and a tape content. That is 2^O(f(n)). A decider never repeats a configuration: the machine is deterministic, so a repeat would make it cycle forever. So it halts within that many steps. ∎

This is the counting argument of lesson 13's last practice problem, where a finite memory made halting decidable. The first practice problem measures the space of runs and computes the bound.

### Savitch's theorem: guessing costs little memory

For time, simulating nondeterminism seems to cost an exponential. For space, **[[Savitch|savitch]]** showed in 1970 that it costs only a square.

**Theorem (Savitch).** NSPACE(f(n)) ⊆ SPACE(f(n)²) for f(n) ≥ log n.

**Proof idea.** A nondeterministic machine N using f(n) space accepts w exactly when its configuration graph has a path from the start configuration to an accepting one, of length at most t = 2^O(f(n)) (the number of configurations). Decide reachability with a recursive procedure:

CANYIELD(c₁, c₂, t) = "can configuration c₁ reach c₂ in at most t steps?"

- If t = 1: check whether c₁ = c₂ or c₂ follows from c₁ in one step.
- Otherwise, for every configuration c_m: if CANYIELD(c₁, c_m, ⌈t/2⌉) and CANYIELD(c_m, c₂, ⌊t/2⌋), answer yes. If no c_m works, answer no.

The two halves together cover ⌈t/2⌉ + ⌊t/2⌋ = t steps. Each call halves t, so the recursion is log₂ t = O(f(n)) levels deep. Each level stores c₁, c₂, c_m and t, which is O(f(n)) cells, and the second recursive call **reuses the space** of the first. Total: O(f(n)) · O(f(n)) = O(f(n)²). The time is terrible, about t^(log t), but that is not what is being measured. ∎

**Corollary.** PSPACE = NPSPACE, because the square of a polynomial is a polynomial. For space, the P versus NP question has been answered.

### TQBF: the problem at the top

A **quantified Boolean formula** puts a quantifier in front of each variable: ∀ ("for all") or ∃ ("there exists"). For example

∀x₁ ∃x₂ [(x₁ ∨ x₂) ∧ (¬x₁ ∨ ¬x₂)]

is **true**: whatever x₁ is, choose x₂ = ¬x₁. With the quantifiers swapped, ∃x₂ ∀x₁ […], it is false. **TQBF** is the language of true, fully quantified formulas. SAT is the special case where every quantifier is ∃.

**TQBF is in PSPACE.** Evaluate recursively: for ∃x φ, set x to true and evaluate φ; if that is false, set x to false and evaluate again; the answer is the OR. For ∀x φ, the same with AND. With no quantifiers left, evaluate the formula under the assignment. The recursion is n levels deep and each level remembers one value, so the space is O(n) plus the formula: **linear**. The time is up to 2ⁿ. The task is exactly this algorithm.

**TQBF is PSPACE-complete** (Stockmeyer and Meyer, 1973). The reduction writes Savitch's CANYIELD as a formula. The obvious formula for "c₁ reaches c₂ in t steps", ∃c_m [φ(c₁, c_m, t/2) ∧ φ(c_m, c₂, t/2)], doubles in size at each level, which is exponential in the end. The trick is to use the half-formula **once**, with a ∀ choosing which half it checks:

∃c_m ∀(a, b) ∈ {(c₁, c_m), (c_m, c₂)} φ(a, b, t/2).

Now each level adds only a polynomial amount, over O(f(n)) levels. So quantifier alternation is the source of PSPACE's power, just as the single ∃ is NP's.

### Games

A true quantified formula is a winning strategy in a game. ∃x₁ ∀x₂ ∃x₃ … φ is a game where the **E-player** picks x₁, the **A-player** picks x₂, and so on; the E-player wins if φ ends up true. The formula is true exactly when the E-player has a winning strategy. That is why the natural PSPACE-complete problems are games whose number of moves is bounded by a polynomial:

- **Generalised geography**: players take turns moving a token along the edges of a directed graph, never revisiting a vertex; whoever cannot move loses. Deciding who wins is PSPACE-complete (the second practice problem solves it by recursion).
- **Generalised Hex, Othello and Go** with suitable rules on n × n boards: PSPACE-complete or harder.

When a game can go on for exponentially many moves, because positions can repeat, it climbs higher: **[[chess and checkers on n × n boards|chess]]** are EXPTIME-complete. Such games are solved by **retrograde analysis**: label every position as won or lost, working backwards from the finished positions. That takes time polynomial in the number of positions, which is exponential in the board size. The fourth practice problem does it for a pursuit game.

### EXPTIME and the hierarchy theorems

**EXPTIME** = ⋃ₖ TIME(2^(nᵏ)). Unlike NP and PSPACE, it is **known** to be bigger than P.

**Time hierarchy theorem ([[Hartmanis and Stearns|hierarchy]], 1965).** For every reasonable ("time-constructible") function f, there is a language decidable in time O(f(n)) but not in time o(f(n) / log f(n)).

**Proof idea.** Diagonalise, as in lesson 13, but with a clock. Let D be: "on input ⟨M⟩ of length n, simulate M on ⟨M⟩ for about f(n) / log f(n) steps; if M accepts within that time, reject; otherwise accept." D runs in time O(f(n)); the log factor pays for simulating with a counter. If some machine M decided L(D) in time o(f(n) / log f(n)), then on its own (long enough) code the simulation would finish, and D would answer the opposite of M. ∎

So TIME(n^k) ⊊ TIME(n^(k+1)), and **P ⊊ EXPTIME**: some problems are decidable but provably need exponential time. The **space hierarchy theorem** says the same for space, without the log factor, so **PSPACE ⊊ EXPSPACE**.

### Small space: L and NL

To talk about less than linear space, give the machine a read-only input tape and a separate work tape, and count only the work tape. **L** = SPACE(log n) and **NL** = NSPACE(log n). O(log n) bits hold a constant number of pointers into the input. PATH, directed reachability, is in NL (guess the path one vertex at a time, keeping only the current vertex and a step counter) and is NL-complete. Savitch gives NL ⊆ SPACE(log² n). And a surprise from 1987: **NL = coNL** (Immerman and Szelepcsényi), so non-reachability also has a nondeterministic log-space algorithm.

### The map

L ⊆ NL ⊆ P ⊆ NP ⊆ PSPACE = NPSPACE ⊆ EXPTIME ⊆ EXPSPACE.

The hierarchy theorems prove NL ≠ PSPACE, P ≠ EXPTIME and PSPACE ≠ EXPSPACE. So at least one inclusion in P ⊆ NP ⊆ PSPACE ⊆ EXPTIME is strict, and nobody knows which. Earlier lessons fit on this map: EQ_DFA is in P (lesson 13), while **EQ_NFA and ALL_NFA are PSPACE-complete**, which is the real cost of the subset construction of lesson 3. The third practice problem decides inclusion between NFAs within polynomial space. Beyond EXPSPACE there are decidable problems harder than any tower of exponentials, and beyond those, lesson 13's undecidable ones.

### What "hard" means in practice

Every class above is about the **worst case**, as n grows. A problem being NP-hard or PSPACE-complete says no algorithm is fast on every input, assuming the classes differ. It does not say your inputs are hard. The practical ladder, from DSA II's lesson 33 and this course:

- **Small inputs:** exponential algorithms are fine for small n, and good ones push the limit far (3-SAT in about 1.33ⁿ).
- **Special structure:** 2-SAT, and many graph problems on trees, planar or bounded-treewidth graphs, are in P.
- **A small parameter:** fixed-parameter algorithms, exponential only in the parameter (vertex cover in O(2^k · m)).
- **Near enough:** approximation algorithms. The **[[PCP theorem|pcp-theorem]]** shows some problems cannot even be approximated beyond a certain ratio unless P = NP.
- **Solvers:** SAT, integer programming and constraint solvers are very good on structured industrial inputs.
- **Randomness:** randomised algorithms (the class BPP, believed equal to P) are often simpler and faster, as with primality testing.
- **Quantum computers:** Shor's algorithm factors in polynomial time on a quantum computer, but quantum computers are not believed to solve NP-complete problems in polynomial time.

Cryptography needs the opposite of all this: problems that are hard **on average**, on randomly chosen keys, not merely in the worst case. NP-hardness does not give that, which is why cryptography rests on specific problems like factoring rather than on NP-complete ones.

### What it costs

| Class | Time | Space |
|---|---|---|
| P | nᵏ | at most nᵏ |
| NP, coNP | 2^(nᵏ) as far as anyone knows | nᵏ |
| PSPACE | 2^(nᵏ) | nᵏ |
| EXPTIME | 2^(nᵏ), provably needed for some problems | up to 2^(nᵏ) |

The TQBF evaluator shows the trade in one algorithm: linear space, exponential time. Savitch's algorithm is the extreme case: it saves space at a huge cost in time. Real programs choose between the two, as memoisation does in dynamic programming.

### Where it is used

- **Game engines** search the formula game directly: minimax with alpha-beta pruning is the TQBF recursion with good move ordering, run to a depth limit with an evaluation function at the leaves.
- **Model checking** asks whether every run of a hardware design or a protocol satisfies a temporal-logic property. It is PSPACE-complete for LTL, and tools still check large systems every day.
- **Planning.** Deciding whether a sequence of actions reaches a goal, in the STRIPS model used by robots and schedulers, is PSPACE-complete; the last practice problem plans a rover's day by searching the states.
- **Regular-expression tools** that test whether two patterns are equivalent face a PSPACE-complete problem, and use the on-the-fly subset search of the third practice problem.

**Watch out:**

- **PSPACE-complete does not mean "needs exponential space".** It means polynomial space is enough, and probably exponential time is needed.
- **Space is reused, time is not.** An algorithm's memory is its largest amount at one moment, not the total ever written.
- **The hierarchy theorems separate classes far apart.** They prove P ≠ EXPTIME, but say nothing about P versus NP or NP versus PSPACE.
- **"Hard" is a worst-case, asymptotic statement.** It does not predict how long your instance takes; measure, and try the ladder above.

::: context savitch Walter Savitch
Walter Savitch proved his theorem in his 1970 PhD thesis at Berkeley, supervised by Stephen Cook, a year before Cook's famous NP-completeness paper. The midpoint recursion is now a standard trick: it appears in the proof that TQBF is PSPACE-complete, and in algorithms that trade time for memory. Savitch later wrote widely used textbooks on programming in C++ and Java.
:::

::: context chess Chess on bigger boards
Real chess is a finite game, so it has a finite answer, and complexity classes say nothing about it. To ask how hard it is, generalise it: n × n boards with a suitable number of pieces. In 1981 Aviezri Fraenkel and David Lichtenstein proved that deciding whether White can force a win from a given position is EXPTIME-complete, so it provably takes exponential time. Checkers and Go with Japanese rules are also EXPTIME-complete; Go with a rule forbidding all repeated positions is harder to classify.
:::

::: context hierarchy Hartmanis and Stearns
Juris Hartmanis and Richard Stearns founded the field in their 1965 paper "On the Computational Complexity of Algorithms", which defined time complexity classes on multitape Turing machines and proved the time hierarchy theorem by diagonalisation. They received the Turing Award in 1993. The hierarchy theorems remain almost the only way anyone knows to prove that two natural classes differ.
:::

::: context pcp-theorem The PCP theorem
The PCP theorem (1992) says every NP statement has a proof that can be checked by reading only a constant number of randomly chosen bits of it, while rejecting a false statement with high probability. Its main consequence is about approximation: for MAX-3-SAT, for instance, no polynomial algorithm can guarantee satisfying more than 7/8 of the clauses of every satisfiable formula unless P = NP, and a random assignment already achieves 7/8. So some approximation limits are as hard as P versus NP itself.
:::
--- task
A quantified Boolean formula is given as a `prefix` and a list of `clauses`. The prefix is a string of `"A"` (for all) and `"E"` (there exists) characters: its i-th character (from 0) is the quantifier of the variable x₍ᵢ₊₁₎, and the variables are quantified in that order, x₁ outermost. The clauses form a CNF formula over those variables, written as in lesson 16 (`2` is x₂, `-2` is ¬x₂). Every variable in a clause appears in the prefix.

Write two functions.

- `tqbf(prefix, clauses)`: return `True` exactly when the formula is true. Use the lesson's recursion: for an `"E"` variable try the value `True` first and stop as soon as one value works; for an `"A"` variable try `True` first and stop as soon as one value fails. When every variable has a value, the formula is true when every clause has a true literal. With an empty prefix, the formula is true exactly when every clause has a true literal under no assignment: true for no clauses, false if there is an (empty) clause.
- `winning_move(prefix, clauses)`: the prefix starts with `"E"`: the first move of the formula game belongs to the E-player. Return `True` if choosing x₁ = true leaves a true formula for the rest, otherwise `False` if x₁ = false does, and `None` if neither does. If the prefix is empty or starts with `"A"`, return `None`.

A check uses 30 variables where only the stop-early rule makes the answer quick.
--- starter
def tqbf(prefix, clauses):
    return all(len(c) > 0 for c in clauses)


def winning_move(prefix, clauses):
    return True
--- solution
def satisfied(clauses, values):
    return all(any(values[abs(lit) - 1] == (lit > 0) for lit in clause) for clause in clauses)


def evaluate(prefix, clauses, values):
    i = len(values)
    if i == len(prefix):
        return satisfied(clauses, values)
    if prefix[i] == "E":
        return any(evaluate(prefix, clauses, values + [v]) for v in (True, False))
    return all(evaluate(prefix, clauses, values + [v]) for v in (True, False))


def tqbf(prefix, clauses):
    return evaluate(prefix, clauses, [])


def winning_move(prefix, clauses):
    if not prefix or prefix[0] != "E":
        return None
    for v in (True, False):
        if evaluate(prefix, clauses, [v]):
            return v
    return None
--- hint
Write a recursive helper `evaluate(prefix, clauses, values)`, where `values` is the list of values chosen so far for x₁, x₂, …. Its length tells you which variable comes next. When all variables have values, check every clause.
--- hint
For an `"E"` variable, the answer is `any(...)` over the two recursive results, and for an `"A"` variable `all(...)`. Python's `any` and `all` over a generator stop as soon as the answer is known, which is the stop-early rule, and they try `True` first if the generator does.
--- hint
`winning_move` calls the same helper with x₁ already chosen: first `[True]`, then `[False]`. Only the recursion depth, n levels with one value each, is ever stored: that is the polynomial space of the lesson.

(tqbf("AE", [[1, 2], [-1, -2]]), tqbf("EA", [[2, 1], [-2, -1]]))
=> (True, False)
?? In the second formula x₁ is outermost and existential: whatever it is, the A-player picks x₂ equal to it, and one of the clauses fails.
--- check case | An empty prefix: no clauses is true, an empty clause is false; SAT and tautology as special cases

=> (True, False, False, True, False)

+++ practice | The space a run uses
--- task
Space is the number of tape cells a run scans. Write two functions for Turing machines as in lesson 11.

- `run_space(tm, w, max_steps)`: run the machine as lesson 11's `run_tm` does, and return `(result, steps, space)`: `result` is `"accept"`, `"reject"` or `"running"` (after `max_steps` steps), `steps` the number of transitions taken, and `space` the number of cells scanned: `max(len(w), 1, h + 1)` where h is the largest head position reached during the run. (The input's cells count even if the head never reaches them, and the empty input still has its one blank cell.)
- `config_bound(tm, s)`: the number of configurations of the machine when it uses at most `s` cells: `len(tm["states"]) * s * len(tm["tape"]) ** s`. A decider that uses `s` cells halts within that many steps, as the lesson proves, which is why PSPACE ⊆ EXPTIME.
--- starter
def run_space(tm, w, max_steps):
    return ("running", 0, len(w))


def config_bound(tm, s):
    return len(tm["states"]) * s
--- solution
def run_space(tm, w, max_steps):
    tape = list(w) if w else ["_"]
    head, state, steps, highest = 0, tm["start"], 0, 0
    while state != tm["accept"] and state != tm["reject"]:
        if steps == max_steps:
            break
        move = tm["delta"].get((state, tape[head]))
        if move is None:
            state = tm["reject"]
            break
        state, tape[head], direction = move
        if direction == "R":
            head += 1
            if head == len(tape):
                tape.append("_")
        else:
            head = max(0, head - 1)
        highest = max(highest, head)
        steps += 1
    if state == tm["accept"]:
        result = "accept"
    elif state == tm["reject"]:
        result = "reject"
    else:
        result = "running"
    return (result, steps, max(len(w), 1, highest + 1))


def config_bound(tm, s):
    return len(tm["states"]) * s * len(tm["tape"]) ** s
--- hint
Start from lesson 11's simulator and keep one more number: the largest head position so far, updated after every move.
--- hint
At the end the space is `max(len(w), 1, highest + 1)`. `config_bound` is one line; Python's integers are exact, so `len(tm["tape"]) ** s` can be as large as it likes.
--- check case | The binary increment machine of lesson 11 on 110 and on 11: both runs end with the head one cell past the input
(lambda M: (run_space(M, "110", 100), run_space(M, "11", 100)))({"states": {"carry", "done", "no"}, "alphabet": "01", "tape": "01_", "delta": {("carry", "1"): ("carry", "0", "R"), ("carry", "0"): ("done", "1", "R"), ("carry", "_"): ("done", "1", "R")}, "start": "carry", "accept": "done", "reject": "no"})
=> (("accept", 3, 4), ("accept", 3, 4))
--- check case | A machine that bounces between two cells uses 2 cells however long it runs; one that sweeps right uses one more cell per step; the empty input
(lambda B, S: (run_space(B, "a", 1000), run_space(S, "a", 50), run_space(S, "", 0)))({"states": {"s", "t", "y", "n"}, "alphabet": "a", "tape": "a_", "delta": {("s", "a"): ("t", "a", "R"), ("t", "_"): ("s", "_", "L")}, "start": "s", "accept": "y", "reject": "n"}, {"states": {"s", "y", "n"}, "alphabet": "a", "tape": "a_", "delta": {("s", "a"): ("s", "a", "R"), ("s", "_"): ("s", "_", "R")}, "start": "s", "accept": "y", "reject": "n"})
=> (("running", 1000, 2), ("running", 50, 51), ("running", 0, 1))
?? A machine that never leaves the first cells uses little space, even if it runs for a long time: space is reused.
--- check test | 300 seeded random machines: the same result, steps and space as the definition, and the configuration bound

+++ practice | Generalised geography
--- task
In **generalised geography**, a token sits on a start vertex of a directed graph, and that vertex counts as used. Two players take turns, the first player moving first: a move takes the token along an edge to a vertex that has **not been used yet**, and that vertex becomes used. A player who cannot move loses. Deciding who wins is PSPACE-complete.

Write `geography_wins(graph, start)` that returns `True` exactly when the first player can force a win. `graph` is a dict from a vertex to a list of the vertices it has edges to; a vertex with no entry has no edges out.

Use recursion: the player to move wins exactly when some move leads to a position where the **other** player, now to move, loses. Keep one set of used vertices, add a vertex before exploring a move, and **remove it again afterwards**, so that the next move is explored from the right position. The recursion is at most as deep as the number of vertices: polynomial space.
--- starter
def geography_wins(graph, start):
    return len(graph.get(start, [])) > 0
--- solution
def geography_wins(graph, start):
    used = {start}

    def to_move_wins(v):
        for u in graph.get(v, []):
            if u not in used:
                used.add(u)
                other_wins = to_move_wins(u)
                used.remove(u)
                if not other_wins:
                    return True
        return False

    return to_move_wins(start)
--- hint
Write an inner function `to_move_wins(v)`: the token is on `v` and it is someone's turn. It is a win if some unused successor `u` gives a position that is a loss for the opponent, which is `not to_move_wins(u)` with `u` marked used.
--- hint
Undo the marking after each recursive call with `used.remove(u)`, before trying the next successor. With no unused successor, the player to move loses: return `False`.
--- check case | No moves; one move; a path of three edges; a cycle back to the used start
(geography_wins({}, 0), geography_wins({0: [1]}, 0), geography_wins({0: [1], 1: [2], 2: [3]}, 0), geography_wins({0: [1], 1: [0]}, 0))
=> (False, True, True, True)
--- check case | A self-loop at the start does not count; a choice between a trap and a win
(geography_wins({0: [0]}, 0), geography_wins({0: [1, 2], 1: [3], 2: []}, 0), geography_wins({0: [1], 1: [2, 3], 2: [], 3: [4]}, 0))
=> (False, True, False)
?? The player to move wins when some move leaves the opponent in a losing position: here moving to 2 leaves the second player stuck.
--- check test | 300 seeded random directed graphs with up to 9 vertices: the same winner as the definition
(lambda R, GEO: all((lambda n: (lambda G: geography_wins(G, 0) == GEO(G, 0))({v: [u for u in range(n) if R.random() < 0.35] for v in range(n) if R.random() < 0.9}))(R.randint(1, 9)) for _ in range(300)))(__import__("random").Random(43), (lambda ns: exec('def geo(G, s):\n    def win(v, seen):\n        return any(not win(u, seen | {u}) for u in G.get(v, []) if u not in seen)\n    return win(s, frozenset([s]))\n', ns) or ns['geo'])({}))
