+++ practice | Is one NFA's language inside another's?
--- task
Deciding whether L(A) ⊆ L(B) for two NFAs is PSPACE-complete, and so is equivalence (inclusion both ways). The standard algorithm uses the subset construction of lesson 3 **on the fly**: it never builds the whole DFA, only the pairs of sets it reaches.

NFAs are dictionaries as in lesson 3 (`"delta"` maps `(state, symbol)` to a set of states, `""` is an ε-move, missing pairs mean no move). Both have the same alphabet, `A["alphabet"]`.

Write `first_not_included(A, B)` that returns the **first string in shortlex order** (shorter first, then in the alphabet's order) that A accepts and B rejects, or `None` if L(A) ⊆ L(B). Search breadth first over pairs `(SA, SB)` of ε-closed sets of states, starting from the ε-closures of the two start states, trying the symbols in alphabet order, and never visiting a pair twice. The answer is the string that first reaches a pair where SA contains an accepting state of A and SB contains none of B's. Unlike lesson 3's practice, you may **not** skip the empty set for B: B being stuck is exactly when it rejects.
--- starter
def first_not_included(A, B):
    return None
--- solution
from collections import deque


def closure(nfa, states):
    seen = set(states)
    todo = list(states)
    while todo:
        q = todo.pop()
        for r in nfa["delta"].get((q, ""), set()):
            if r not in seen:
                seen.add(r)
                todo.append(r)
    return frozenset(seen)


def step(nfa, states, c):
    return closure(nfa, {r for q in states for r in nfa["delta"].get((q, c), set())})


def first_not_included(A, B):
    start = (closure(A, {A["start"]}), closure(B, {B["start"]}))
    queue = deque([(start, "")])
    seen = {start}
    while queue:
        (sa, sb), w = queue.popleft()
        if sa & set(A["accept"]) and not sb & set(B["accept"]):
            return w
        if not sa:
            continue
        for c in A["alphabet"]:
            pair = (step(A, sa, c), step(B, sb, c))
            if pair not in seen:
                seen.add(pair)
                queue.append((pair, w + c))
    return None
--- hint
Write `closure(nfa, states)` (lesson 3's ε-closure, returning a `frozenset`) and `step(nfa, states, c)`: every c-move from the set, then the closure. A pair of frozensets can go in a set of seen pairs.
--- hint
Breadth-first search with a queue of `(pair, string)`. Test each pair when you take it out. A pair where A's set is empty can never lead to an accepted string, so it need not be expanded; B's empty set, though, is a pair you must keep.
--- hint
Why this is polynomial space but exponential time: one pair of sets is small, but there can be 2^|A| · 2^|B| pairs to visit.
--- check case | "Contains aa" is not inside "ends in a": the first counterexample is aab; the other way round, "a" itself
(lambda C, E: (first_not_included(C, E), first_not_included(E, C)))({"states": {0, 1, 2}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, "a"): {0, 1}, (0, "b"): {0}, (1, "a"): {2}, (2, "a"): {2}, (2, "b"): {2}}, "start": 0, "accept": {2}}, {"states": {0, 1}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, "a"): {0, 1}, (0, "b"): {0}}, "start": 0, "accept": {1}})
=> ("aab", "a")
--- check case | Inclusion holds: None; an A that accepts ε while B rejects it; ε-moves in both
(first_not_included({"states": {0}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, "a"): {0}}, "start": 0, "accept": {0}}, {"states": {0}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, "a"): {0}, (0, "b"): {0}}, "start": 0, "accept": {0}}), first_not_included({"states": {0}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {}, "start": 0, "accept": {0}}, {"states": {0, 1}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, "a"): {1}}, "start": 0, "accept": {1}}), first_not_included({"states": {0, 1}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, ""): {1}, (1, "b"): {1}}, "start": 0, "accept": {1}}, {"states": {0, 1, 2}, "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(0, ""): {1}, (1, "b"): {2}, (2, "b"): {1}}, "start": 0, "accept": {1}}))
=> (None, "", "b")
?? B rejects a string when its set of states is empty or has no accepting state, so do not skip the empty set on B's side.
--- check test | 300 seeded random pairs of NFAs with ε-moves: the same answer as trying every string up to length 7
(lambda R, NI: all((lambda A, B: (lambda got, want: got == want if want is not None else (got is None or (len(got) > 7 and NI({**A, "accept": A["accept"]}, B, 0) is None)))(first_not_included(A, B), NI(A, B, 7)))(*[(lambda n: {"states": set(range(n)), "alphabet": "ab", "delta": {(q, c): {r for r in range(n) if R.random() < (0.15 if c == "" else 0.35)} for q in range(n) for c in ("a", "b", "")}, "start": 0, "accept": {q for q in range(n) if R.random() < 0.4}})(R.randint(1, 4)) for _ in range(2)]) for _ in range(300)))(__import__("random").Random(44), (lambda ns: exec('def ninc(A, B, L):\n    import itertools\n    def acc(N, w):\n        def cl(S):\n            S, todo = set(S), list(S)\n            while todo:\n                q = todo.pop()\n                for r in N["delta"].get((q, ""), set()):\n                    if r not in S:\n                        S.add(r)\n                        todo.append(r)\n            return S\n        S = cl({N["start"]})\n        for c in w:\n            S = cl({r for q in S for r in N["delta"].get((q, c), set())})\n        return bool(S & set(N["accept"]))\n    for n in range(L + 1):\n        for t in itertools.product(A["alphabet"], repeat=n):\n            w = "".join(t)\n            if acc(A, w) and not acc(B, w):\n                return w\n    return None\n', ns) or ns['ninc'])({}))

+++ practice | Cops and robbers: winning by working backwards
--- task
A guard drone chases an intruder around a space station's corridors, an undirected graph. The guard starts on vertex `cop` and the intruder on vertex `robber`; they alternate moves, **the guard first**. A move is to stay put or to go to a neighbouring vertex. The guard wins as soon as both are on the same vertex, which includes the start. Both players see everything. The intruder wins by avoiding capture forever.

Games that can go on forever are solved by **retrograde analysis**: find all positions from which the guard wins, working backwards. A position is `(guard vertex, intruder vertex, whose turn)`. Start with the captured positions, where the two vertices are equal. Then repeat until nothing changes:

- a guard-to-move position is winning if **some** guard move (stay or neighbour) reaches the intruder or a winning intruder-to-move position;
- an intruder-to-move position is winning if **every** intruder move (stay or neighbour) walks into the guard or reaches a winning guard-to-move position.

Write `cop_wins(graph, cop, robber)`: `graph` is a dict from each vertex to a list of its neighbours (edges are listed both ways; a vertex may have none). Return `True` exactly when the guard can force a capture. The number of positions is polynomial here; in games like chess on n × n boards it is exponential in n, which is why they are EXPTIME-complete.
--- starter
def cop_wins(graph, cop, robber):
    return cop == robber or robber in graph.get(cop, [])
--- solution
def cop_wins(graph, cop, robber):
    vertices = list(graph)
    moves = {v: [v] + list(graph[v]) for v in vertices}
    win = {(a, b, t) for a in vertices for b in vertices for t in (0, 1) if a == b}
    changed = True
    while changed:
        changed = False
        for a in vertices:
            for b in vertices:
                if (a, b, 0) not in win and any(x == b or (x, b, 1) in win for x in moves[a]):
                    win.add((a, b, 0))
                    changed = True
                if (a, b, 1) not in win and all(y == a or (a, y, 0) in win for y in moves[b]):
                    win.add((a, b, 1))
                    changed = True
    return (cop, robber, 0) in win
--- hint
Keep a set `win` of positions `(guard, intruder, turn)` known to be wins for the guard, with turn 0 for the guard to move and 1 for the intruder. It starts with every position where the two vertices are equal.
--- hint
Sweep over all positions again and again, adding a guard-to-move position when some move in `[a] + graph[a]` lands on the intruder or on a winning turn-1 position, and an intruder-to-move position when every move in `[b] + graph[b]` lands on the guard or on a winning turn-0 position. Stop when a sweep adds nothing; the answer is whether `(cop, robber, 0)` is in the set.

=> (True, False, True)
--- check case | Different components: the intruder is safe; an isolated vertex each; a triangle
(cop_wins({0: [1], 1: [0], 2: [3], 3: [2]}, 0, 3), cop_wins({0: [], 1: []}, 0, 1), cop_wins({0: [1, 2], 1: [0, 2], 2: [0, 1]}, 0, 1))
=> (False, False, True)
?? In a 4-cycle, whenever the guard steps next to the intruder, the intruder steps away to the far side.
--- check test | 150 seeded random graphs with up to 7 vertices, from every pair of starting vertices: the same answer as the definition
(lambda R, COP: all((lambda G: all(cop_wins(G, c, r) == COP(G, c, r) for c in G for r in G))((lambda n, E: {v: [u for u in range(n) if (min(u, v), max(u, v)) in E and u != v] for v in range(n)})(*(lambda n: (n, {(u, v) for u in range(n) for v in range(u + 1, n) if R.random() < 0.4}))(R.randint(1, 7)))) for _ in range(150)))(__import__("random").Random(45), (lambda ns: exec('def cop(G, c, r):\n    V = sorted(set(G) | {u for vs in G.values() for u in vs})\n    N = {v: set(G.get(v, [])) | {v} for v in V}\n    for v in V:\n        for u in G.get(v, []):\n            N[u].add(v)\n    W = {(a, b, t) for a in V for b in V for t in (0, 1) if a == b}\n    while True:\n        new = set(W)\n        for a in V:\n            for b in V:\n                if (a, b, 0) not in W and any(x == b or (x, b, 1) in W for x in N[a]):\n                    new.add((a, b, 0))\n                if (a, b, 1) not in W and all(y == a or (a, y, 0) in W for y in N[b]):\n                    new.add((a, b, 1))\n        if new == W:\n            return (c, r, 0) in W\n        W = new\n', ns) or ns['cop'])({}))

+++ practice | Debug: Savitch's midpoint that loses a step
--- task
`savitch(adj, s, t)` decides whether there is a path from vertex `s` to vertex `t` in a directed graph with vertices `0` to `n - 1`, where `adj[v]` is the list of `v`'s successors, the way Savitch's theorem does: in little memory, by recursion on midpoints. `can_yield(u, v, steps)` answers "is there a path from u to v of at most `steps` edges?": with `steps` at most 1, check `u == v` or an edge; otherwise try every vertex `m` as a midpoint, splitting the steps into two halves that must add up to `steps`. The top call uses `steps = n`, since a shortest path has at most n − 1 edges. It returns `(answer, depth)`, where `depth` is the largest number of nested `can_yield` calls, at most about log₂ n + 1.

It misses long paths: on the path 0 → 1 → 2 → … → 6 it says 6 cannot be reached from 0. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
def savitch(adj, s, t):
    n = len(adj)
    deepest = [0]

    def can_yield(u, v, steps, depth):
        deepest[0] = max(deepest[0], depth)
        if steps <= 1:
            return u == v or v in adj[u]
        for m in range(n):
            if can_yield(u, m, steps // 2, depth + 1) and can_yield(m, v, steps // 2, depth + 1):
                return True
        return False

    return (can_yield(s, t, n, 1), deepest[0])
--- solution
def savitch(adj, s, t):
    n = len(adj)
    deepest = [0]

    def can_yield(u, v, steps, depth):
        deepest[0] = max(deepest[0], depth)
        if steps <= 1:
            return u == v or v in adj[u]
        for m in range(n):
            if can_yield(u, m, (steps + 1) // 2, depth + 1) and can_yield(m, v, steps // 2, depth + 1):
                return True
        return False

    return (can_yield(s, t, n, 1), deepest[0])
--- hint
Add up the two halves for an odd number of steps, say 7. How many steps can the two recursive calls cover together?
--- hint
`7 // 2 + 7 // 2` is 6, so each odd split loses a step, and the losses add up down the recursion. Give the first half `(steps + 1) // 2`, the ceiling, and the second `steps // 2`: they always add up to `steps`.
--- check case | The path 0 → 1 → … → 6: reachable, with depth 4
savitch([[1], [2], [3], [4], [5], [6], []], 0, 6)
=> (True, 4)
?? For steps = 7 the halves 3 + 3 cover only 6 edges.
--- check case | Not reachable against the direction of the edges; a vertex reaches itself; one vertex
(savitch([[1], [2], []], 2, 0)[0], savitch([[1], []], 1, 1)[0], savitch([[]], 0, 0))
=> (False, True, (True, 1))
--- check test | 200 seeded random graphs with up to 10 vertices: the same answer as a breadth-first search, and depth at most ⌈log₂ n⌉ + 1
(lambda R, REACH, m: all((lambda n: (lambda adj, s, t: (lambda r: r[0] == REACH({v: adj[v] for v in range(n)}, s, t) and r[1] <= m.ceil(m.log2(n)) + 1)(savitch(adj, s, t)))([[u for u in range(n) if u != v and R.random() < 0.2] for v in range(n)], R.randrange(n), R.randrange(n)))(R.randint(1, 10)) for _ in range(200)))(__import__("random").Random(46), (lambda ns: exec('def reach(G, s, t):\n    seen, todo = {s}, [s]\n    while todo:\n        v = todo.pop()\n        for u in G.get(v, []):\n            if u not in seen:\n                seen.add(u)\n                todo.append(u)\n    return t in seen\n', ns) or ns['reach'])({}), __import__("math"))

+++ practice | Planning a rover's day
--- task
Planning, in the STRIPS model, is PSPACE-complete: with f facts there are 2ᶠ possible states. For a small domain, breadth-first search over the states still works.

A **state** is a set of facts (strings). An **action** is a tuple `(name, pre, add, delete)` of a name and three sets of facts. It can be applied in state S when `pre` is a subset of S, and the new state is `(S - delete) | add`. Write `plan(initial, goal, actions)` that returns a **shortest** list of action names that leads from the set `initial` to a state containing every fact in `goal`, or `None` if no plan exists. Return `[]` if `initial` already contains the goal.

Search breadth first over states (as frozensets), trying the actions in their list order, and never visiting a state twice. The checks accept any shortest plan.
--- starter
def plan(initial, goal, actions):
    state = set(initial)
    names = []
    for name, pre, add, delete in actions:
        if pre <= state:
            state = (state - delete) | add
            names.append(name)
    return names if goal <= state else None
--- solution
from collections import deque


def plan(initial, goal, actions):
    start = frozenset(initial)
    if goal <= start:
        return []
    came_from = {start: None}
    queue = deque([start])
    while queue:
        state = queue.popleft()
        for name, pre, add, delete in actions:
            if pre <= state:
                following = frozenset((state - delete) | add)
                if following in came_from:
                    continue
                came_from[following] = (state, name)
                if goal <= following:
                    names = []
                    s = following
                    while came_from[s] is not None:
                        s, n = came_from[s]
                        names.append(n)
                    return names[::-1]
                queue.append(following)
    return None
--- hint
This is breadth-first search on an implicit graph: the vertices are frozensets of facts, and each applicable action is an edge. Store, for each state reached, the state it came from and the action's name.
--- hint
Test the goal when a new state is generated, and rebuild the plan by following the stored links back to the start, then reversing. If the queue empties first, no plan exists.
--- check test | The rover's day: drive, drill, stow the core, photograph the ridge, and send the photo, in a shortest plan

?? Applying actions greedily in list order is not a search. Explore every applicable action from every state, level by level.
--- check case | The goal already holds; a goal that no action can reach; a plan of one action

+++ question | Why PSPACE fits inside EXPTIME
--- ask
Why is every language in PSPACE also in EXPTIME?
--- choice
Because a machine that uses nᵏ cells can write at most nᵏ symbols, so it takes at most nᵏ steps.
--- choice correct
Because a decider using nᵏ cells has at most 2^O(nᵏ) configurations and can never repeat one, so it halts within that many steps.
--- choice
Because of Savitch's theorem.
--- choice
Because every PSPACE problem is a game, and games take exponential time.
--- why
Space can be reused, so a machine can run far longer than its space: a binary counter on nᵏ cells runs for 2^(nᵏ) steps. What limits it is the number of distinct configurations, state times head position times tape contents, and a deterministic decider that repeated one would loop forever. Savitch's theorem is about nondeterministic space, not time.

+++ question | What Savitch's recursion stores
--- ask
Savitch's CANYIELD(c₁, c₂, t) tries every midpoint c_m and makes two recursive calls with t/2. Where does the space bound O(f(n)²) come from?
--- choice
Each call stores the whole configuration graph, of size f(n)².
--- choice correct
The recursion is O(f(n)) levels deep, since t = 2^O(f(n)) halves at each level, and each level stores a few configurations of size O(f(n)); the second call reuses the first one's space.
--- choice
The two recursive calls run at the same time, doubling the space at each level.
--- choice
The number of midpoints is f(n)², and each must be remembered.
--- why
Only one path of the recursion is active at a time. Each active call holds c₁, c₂, the current c_m and t, which is O(f(n)) cells, and there are log₂ t = O(f(n)) active calls. The midpoints are generated one at a time and overwritten, and the second recursive call starts only after the first has returned its space.

+++ question | Which separation is proved
--- ask
Which of these statements has been proved?
--- choice
P ≠ NP.
--- choice
NP ≠ PSPACE.
--- choice correct
P ≠ EXPTIME.
--- choice
PSPACE ≠ EXPTIME.
--- why
The time hierarchy theorem, a clocked diagonal argument, shows that some problems decidable in exponential time need more than polynomial time, so P ⊊ EXPTIME. It follows that at least one of P ⊆ NP, NP ⊆ PSPACE and PSPACE ⊆ EXPTIME is strict, but nobody knows which one. The other three statements are all open.

+++ problem | The smallest DFA for the reversed language
--- task
DFAs are dictionaries as in lesson 2 (total: every state has a move on every symbol). Write `min_reverse_size(dfa)` that returns the number of states of the **smallest total DFA**, over the same alphabet, for the reversed language L(dfa)ᴿ = {wᴿ : w ∈ L(dfa)}. A dead state counts if the smallest DFA needs one; the empty language and Σ∗ both have a one-state smallest DFA.

For example, the language "the 3rd symbol is `a`" has a 5-state DFA, but its reversal, "the 3rd symbol from the end is `a`", needs 8 states.
--- starter
def min_reverse_size(dfa):
    return len(dfa["states"])
--- solution
def min_reverse_size(dfa):
    sigma = dfa["alphabet"]
    back = {}
    for (q, c), r in dfa["delta"].items():
        back.setdefault((r, c), set()).add(q)
    start = frozenset(dfa["accept"])
    states = {start}
    todo = [start]
    move = {}
    while todo:
        x = todo.pop()
        for c in sigma:
            y = frozenset(q for r in x for q in back.get((r, c), ()))
            move[(x, c)] = y
            if y not in states:
                states.add(y)
                todo.append(y)
    block = {x: dfa["start"] in x for x in states}
    count = len(set(block.values()))
    while True:
        signature = {x: (block[x],) + tuple(block[move[(x, c)]] for c in sigma) for x in states}
        ids = {}
        block = {x: ids.setdefault(signature[x], len(ids)) for x in states}
        if len(ids) == count:
            return count
        count = len(ids)

+++ problem | Counting the matches of a regular expression
--- task
Regular expressions are tuple trees as in lesson 4: `("empty",)`, `("eps",)`, `("sym", c)`, `("alt", R, S)`, `("cat", R, S)`, `("star", R)`. Write `count_matches(regex, alphabet, n)` that returns the **exact number** of strings of length exactly `n` over `alphabet` (a string of symbols) that the expression matches.

It must be fast: a check uses n = 150, where there are 2¹⁵⁰ strings to consider. Turn the expression into an NFA, and count by dynamic programming over lengths on the sets of NFA states (the subset construction of lesson 3, done on the fly): keep, for each set reached, how many strings of the current length lead to it.
--- starter
from itertools import product


def count_matches(regex, alphabet, n):
    return 0 if n > 12 else len(list(product(alphabet, repeat=n)))
--- solution
def count_matches(regex, alphabet, n):
    moves = {}
    counter = [0]

    def new_state():
        counter[0] += 1
        return counter[0]

    def add(p, c, q):
        moves.setdefault((p, c), set()).add(q)

    def build(r):
        s, f = new_state(), new_state()
        kind = r[0]
        if kind == "eps":
            add(s, "", f)
        elif kind == "sym":
            add(s, r[1], f)
        elif kind == "alt":
            for sub in r[1:]:
                a, b = build(sub)
                add(s, "", a)
                add(b, "", f)
        elif kind == "cat":
            a, b = build(r[1])
            c, d = build(r[2])
            add(s, "", a)
            add(b, "", c)
            add(d, "", f)
        elif kind == "star":
            a, b = build(r[1])
            add(s, "", a)
            add(b, "", a)
            add(s, "", f)
            add(b, "", f)
        return s, f

    start, final = build(regex)

    def closure(states):
        seen = set(states)
        todo = list(states)
        while todo:
            q = todo.pop()
            for r in moves.get((q, ""), ()):
                if r not in seen:
                    seen.add(r)
                    todo.append(r)
        return frozenset(seen)

    counts = {closure({start}): 1}
    for _ in range(n):
        following = {}
        for states, k in counts.items():
            for c in alphabet:
                nxt = closure({r for q in states for r in moves.get((q, c), ())})
                if nxt:
                    following[nxt] = following.get(nxt, 0) + k
        counts = following
    return sum(k for states, k in counts.items() if final in states)

+++ problem | Why the reversed language needs so many states
--- task
Let Lₖ be the strings over `"ab"` whose k-th symbol **from the end** is `a` (a string shorter than k is not in Lₖ). An NFA with k + 1 states recognises it, but every DFA needs 2ᵏ states. Prove the lower bound with the Myhill–Nerode idea of lesson 5, as code:

- `fooling_set(k)`: return a list of 2ᵏ different strings that are pairwise **distinguishable** by Lₖ.
- `separator(x, y, k)`: for any two different strings `x` and `y` of length exactly k, return a string z such that exactly one of `x + z` and `y + z` is in Lₖ.

Each pair of strings in the fooling set must lead any DFA for Lₖ to different states, so the DFA has at least 2ᵏ states.
--- starter
def fooling_set(k):
    return ["a" * i for i in range(2 ** k)]


def separator(x, y, k):
    return ""
--- solution
from itertools import product


def fooling_set(k):
    return ["".join(t) for t in product("ab", repeat=k)]


def separator(x, y, k):
    i = next(i for i in range(k) if x[i] != y[i])
    return "a" * i
--- check test | For k = 1 to 6: 2ᵏ different strings over "ab", every two of them told apart by some suffix

--- check test | separator works for every pair of different strings of length k, for k up to 7
(lambda L, P: all(L(x + separator(x, y, k), k) != L(y + separator(x, y, k), k) for k in range(1, 8) for x in map("".join, P("ab", repeat=k)) for y in map("".join, P("ab", repeat=k)) if x != y))(lambda w, k: len(w) >= k and w[-k] == "a", __import__("itertools").product)

+++ problem | A pushdown automaton for twice as many b's
--- task
PDAs are dictionaries as in lesson 9: `"delta"` maps `(q, a, X)` to a set of pairs `(r, push)`, where `a` (the input symbol) or `X` (the symbol popped from the top) may be `""` for ε, and `push` is a tuple of stack symbols, top first. The stack starts empty; the PDA accepts when it has read all the input and is in an accepting state.

Write `twice_pda()` that returns a PDA over the alphabet `"ab"` accepting exactly the strings with **twice as many `b`s as `a`s**, in any order: for example `""`, `"abb"`, `"bab"`, `"bba"` and `"bbaabb"`, but not `"ab"`, `"abbb"` or `"a"`. The language is not regular (intersect it with a∗b∗ and pump), so the stack must count. The checks run your PDA on every string up to length 9 with a stack limit of 20.

One way: keep on the stack the value 2·(number of a's) − (number of b's) so far, as that many `P` symbols when it is positive or `N` symbols when it is negative, above a bottom marker. A `b` subtracts 1 and an `a` adds 2, which can take two moves when the top is `N`.
--- starter
def twice_pda():
    return {"states": {"s"}, "alphabet": "ab", "stack": set(), "delta": {}, "start": "s", "accept": {"s"}}
--- solution
def twice_pda():
    delta = {
        ("s", "", ""): {("q", ("#",))},
        ("q", "b", "P"): {("q", ())},
        ("q", "b", "N"): {("q", ("N", "N"))},
        ("q", "b", "#"): {("q", ("N", "#"))},
        ("q", "a", "P"): {("q", ("P", "P", "P"))},
        ("q", "a", "#"): {("q", ("P", "P", "#"))},
        ("q", "a", "N"): {("half", ())},
        ("half", "", "N"): {("q", ())},
        ("half", "", "#"): {("q", ("P", "#"))},
        ("q", "", "#"): {("f", ("#",))},
    }
    return {"states": {"s", "q", "half", "f"}, "alphabet": "ab", "stack": {"#", "P", "N"},
            "delta": delta, "start": "s", "accept": {"f"}}
--- check test | Every string over "ab" up to length 9: accepted exactly when it has twice as many b's as a's

+++ problem | A command grammar against a safety filter
--- task
A command language is given by a grammar in **Chomsky normal form**, a dictionary as in lesson 7 whose rules are `A → (B, C)` or `A → (a,)`; the start variable may also have the rule `()` (ε), and then it appears in no body. A safety filter is a total DFA (as in lesson 2) over the grammar's terminals. Before launch, the team wants the **length of the shortest command the grammar allows but the filter rejects**, or `None` if there is none.

Write `shortest_forbidden(G, dfa)`. The context-free languages are closed under intersection with regular languages (lesson 10), and the proof gives the algorithm: for every DFA state p, variable A and DFA state q, find the length of the shortest string that A derives and that takes the DFA from p to q. Start from the rules A → a; then keep improving with the rules A → BC, splitting at a middle state r, until nothing changes. The answer is the smallest value for the start variable from the DFA's start state to a **rejecting** state (and 0 if the grammar has ε and the start state rejects).

A check uses a grammar whose shortest forbidden command has length 20, so listing strings will not do.
--- starter
from itertools import product


def shortest_forbidden(G, dfa):
    return None
--- solution
def shortest_forbidden(G, dfa):
    Q = list(dfa["states"])
    best = {}
    for A, bodies in G["rules"].items():
        for body in bodies:
            if len(body) == 1:
                for p in Q:
                    key = (p, A, dfa["delta"][(p, body[0])])
                    best[key] = 1
    changed = True
    while changed:
        changed = False
        for A, bodies in G["rules"].items():
            for body in bodies:
                if len(body) != 2:
                    continue
                B, C = body
                for (p, X, r), left in list(best.items()):
                    if X != B:
                        continue
                    for q in Q:
                        right = best.get((r, C, q))
                        if right is not None and (best.get((p, A, q)) is None or left + right < best[(p, A, q)]):
                            best[(p, A, q)] = left + right
                            changed = True
    start, S = dfa["start"], G["start"]
    lengths = [best[(start, S, q)] for q in Q if q not in dfa["accept"] and (start, S, q) in best]
    if () in G["rules"].get(S, []) and start not in dfa["accept"]:
        lengths.append(0)
    return min(lengths) if lengths else None

+++ problem | A Turing machine that adds one, most significant bit first
--- task
Turing machines are dictionaries as in lesson 11. Write `increment_tm()` that returns a machine over the alphabet `"01"` that adds 1 to a binary number written **most significant bit first**, with no leading zeros (`"0"`, `"1"`, `"10"`, `"11"`, …; the input is never empty). It must halt in its accept state with exactly the binary form of n + 1 on the tape, most significant bit first, with no leading zeros and no marks left behind: `"1011"` becomes `"1100"`, `"0"` becomes `"1"`, and `"111"` becomes `"1000"`.

The carry travels **left**, and a machine cannot tell when it is back at cell 0 (a left move there just stays), so mark the first cell at the start with a symbol of your own. It must halt within 4·(n + 2) steps on an input of length n.
--- starter
def increment_tm():
    return {"states": {"s", "acc", "rej"}, "alphabet": "01", "tape": "01_", "delta": {("s", "0"): ("acc", "1", "R")}, "start": "s", "accept": "acc", "reject": "rej"}
--- solution
def increment_tm():
    d = {
        ("start", "0"): ("right", "Z", "R"),
        ("start", "1"): ("right", "O", "R"),
        ("right", "0"): ("right", "0", "R"),
        ("right", "1"): ("right", "1", "R"),
        ("right", "_"): ("carry", "_", "L"),
        ("carry", "1"): ("carry", "0", "L"),
        ("carry", "0"): ("home", "1", "L"),
        ("carry", "Z"): ("acc", "1", "R"),
        ("carry", "O"): ("append", "1", "R"),
        ("home", "0"): ("home", "0", "L"),
        ("home", "1"): ("home", "1", "L"),
        ("home", "Z"): ("acc", "0", "R"),
        ("home", "O"): ("acc", "1", "R"),
        ("append", "0"): ("append", "0", "R"),
        ("append", "_"): ("acc", "0", "R"),
    }
    return {"states": {"start", "right", "carry", "home", "append", "acc", "rej"}, "alphabet": "01",
            "tape": "01ZO_", "delta": d, "start": "start", "accept": "acc", "reject": "rej"}

+++ problem | Regularity of a machine's language is not co-recognisable
--- task
Programs are generator functions as in lesson 13 (each `yield` is a step; accepting means returning exactly `True`). Show that the complement of HALT reduces to REGULAR = {q : L(q) is regular}, which proves REGULAR is not even co-recognisable.

Write `regular_reduction(p, x)` that returns a program `q` which **halts on every input** and, on input `y`, runs `p` on `x` for at most `len(y)` steps. If `p` halted within those steps (after k ≤ `len(y)` steps), `q` accepts exactly when `y` is aⁿbⁿ for some n ≥ 0; otherwise `q` accepts. So:

- if `p` never halts on `x`, `q` accepts every string: L(q) = Σ∗, which is regular;
- if `p` halts on `x` after T steps, L(q) is aⁿbⁿ plus some strings shorter than T, which is not regular (a finite change of a non-regular language is not regular).

Building `q` must not start `p`.
--- starter
def regular_reduction(p, x):
    def q(y):
        return True
        yield
    return q
--- solution
def run(p, x, budget):
    g = p(x)
    for steps in range(budget + 1):
        try:
            next(g)
        except StopIteration:
            return True
    return False


def regular_reduction(p, x):
    def q(y):
        halted = run(p, x, len(y))
        if halted:
            half = len(y) // 2
            return len(y) % 2 == 0 and y == "a" * half + "b" * half
        return True
        yield
    return q

+++ problem | Subset sum reduces to partition
--- task
PARTITION asks whether a list of numbers can be split into two parts with equal sums. SUBSET-SUM asks whether some of a list of numbers add up to a target t. Reduce SUBSET-SUM to PARTITION, and translate answers back. Numbers are positive whole numbers (the list may be empty), and t ≥ 0.

- `subset_sum_to_partition(nums, t)`: let S be the sum of `nums`. If t > S, return `[1, 2]`, a list that cannot be split evenly. Otherwise return `nums` followed by the two numbers S + t and 2S − t. (The total is 4S, so each part must add up to 2S; the two new numbers add up to 3S, so when S > 0 they are in different parts, and the part holding 2S − t needs exactly t more from `nums`.)
- `partition_to_subset(nums, t, side)`: `side` is a list of indices into the list your reduction returned (for t ≤ S), whose numbers add up to half of its total. Return the sorted list of indices into `nums` of a subset that adds up to t: the original indices in the same part as the number 2S − t (the last one).
--- starter
def subset_sum_to_partition(nums, t):
    return list(nums) + [t]


def partition_to_subset(nums, t, side):
    return sorted(side)
--- solution
def subset_sum_to_partition(nums, t):
    total = sum(nums)
    if t > total:
        return [1, 2]
    return list(nums) + [total + t, 2 * total - t]


def partition_to_subset(nums, t, side):
    n = len(nums)
    chosen = set(side)
    if n + 1 not in chosen:
        chosen = set(range(n + 2)) - chosen
    return sorted(i for i in chosen if i < n)

=> ([3, 5, 8, 27, 21], [1, 2], [3, 5, 8, 16], [0, 0])

+++ problem | A diagonal with a clock
--- task
The time hierarchy theorem is a diagonal argument with a clock. Programs are generator functions as in lesson 13, and `progs` is a list of programs. Write `beat_the_clock(progs)` that returns a program `D` which, on input `i` (a whole number with 0 ≤ i < `len(progs)`), runs `progs[i]` on the input `i` for **at most 50·(i + 1) steps**, yielding once for each step it simulates. If `progs[i]` accepted within that budget, `D` returns `False`; otherwise (it rejected, or had not halted) `D` returns `True`.

So `D` halts on every input, within 50·(i + 1) + 1 steps, and it differs from every program on the list that halts within its budget. Accepting means returning exactly `True`.
--- starter
def beat_the_clock(progs):
    def D(i):
        return True
        yield
    return D
--- solution
def beat_the_clock(progs):
    def D(i):
        g = progs[i](i)
        for _ in range(50 * (i + 1) + 1):
            try:
                next(g)
            except StopIteration as stop:
                return stop.value is not True
            yield
        return True
    return D

+++ problem | Hamiltonian path as a SAT formula
--- task
Hard problems are often solved by reducing them **to** SAT. Encode the directed Hamiltonian path problem: the graph has vertices `0` to `n - 1` (n ≥ 1) and directed `edges` (a list of pairs); is there a path from `s` to `t` that visits every vertex exactly once?

- `hampath_to_sat(n, edges, s, t)`: return `(clauses, nvars)`, a CNF formula (lists of nonzero integers, as in lesson 16) with `nvars = n * n`, where the variable `p * n + v + 1` means "position p of the path holds vertex v" (p and v from 0 to n − 1). The clauses say: each position holds exactly one vertex; each vertex is at exactly one position; position 0 holds s and position n − 1 holds t; and for every position p < n − 1 and every ordered pair (u, v) that is **not** an edge, not both "p holds u" and "p + 1 holds v".
- `sat_to_path(n, assignment)`: turn a satisfying assignment (a dict from variable numbers to `True` or `False`) into the path, a list of the n vertices in position order.

The formula is satisfiable exactly when the path exists, and its size is polynomial: O(n³) clauses.
--- starter
def hampath_to_sat(n, edges, s, t):
    return ([], n * n)


def sat_to_path(n, assignment):
    return list(range(n))
--- solution
def hampath_to_sat(n, edges, s, t):
    def x(p, v):
        return p * n + v + 1

    clauses = []
    for p in range(n):
        clauses.append([x(p, v) for v in range(n)])
        for u in range(n):
            for v in range(u + 1, n):
                clauses.append([-x(p, u), -x(p, v)])
    for v in range(n):
        clauses.append([x(p, v) for p in range(n)])
        for p in range(n):
            for q in range(p + 1, n):
                clauses.append([-x(p, v), -x(q, v)])
    clauses.append([x(0, s)])
    clauses.append([x(n - 1, t)])
    edge = set(map(tuple, edges))
    for p in range(n - 1):
        for u in range(n):
            for v in range(n):
                if (u, v) not in edge:
                    clauses.append([-x(p, u), -x(p + 1, v)])
    return clauses, n * n


def sat_to_path(n, assignment):
    return [next(v for v in range(n) if assignment.get(p * n + v + 1)) for p in range(n)]
--- check test | 150 seeded random directed graphs with up to 5 vertices: satisfiable exactly when a Hamiltonian path from s to t exists, and the decoded path is one
(lambda R, HAM, MOD: all((lambda n, E, s, t: (lambda C, N: (lambda m: N == n * n and all(0 < abs(l) <= N for c in C for l in c) and (m is not None) == (len(HAM(n, E, s, t)) > 0) and (m is None or sat_to_path(n, m) in HAM(n, E, s, t)))(MOD(C)))(*hampath_to_sat(n, E, s, t)))(*(lambda n: (n, [(u, v) for u in range(n) for v in range(n) if u != v and R.random() < 0.5], R.randrange(n), R.randrange(n)))(R.randint(1, 5))) for _ in range(150)))(__import__("random").Random(57), (lambda ns: exec('def ham(n, E, s=None, t=None, cycle=False):\n    import itertools\n    S = set(map(tuple, E))\n    out = []\n    for p in itertools.permutations(range(n)):\n        if cycle:\n            if n >= 1 and p[0] == 0 and all((p[i], p[(i + 1) % n]) in S for i in range(n)) and (n > 1 or (0, 0) in S):\n                out.append(list(p))\n        elif p[0] == s and p[-1] == t and all((p[i], p[i + 1]) in S for i in range(n - 1)):\n            out.append(list(p))\n    return out\n', ns) or ns['ham'])({}), (lambda ns: exec('def dmodel(C):\n    def go(C, a):\n        while True:\n            u, R = None, []\n            for c in C:\n                if any(a.get(abs(l)) == (l > 0) for l in c):\n                    continue\n                r = [l for l in c if abs(l) not in a]\n                if not r:\n                    return None\n                if len(r) == 1:\n                    u = r[0]\n                R.append(r)\n            C = R\n            if u is None:\n                break\n            a = {**a, abs(u): u > 0}\n        if not C:\n            return a\n        v = abs(C[0][0])\n        return go(C, {**a, v: True}) or go(C, {**a, v: False})\n    return go([list(c) for c in C], {})\n', ns) or ns['dmodel'])({}))
--- check test | One vertex: satisfiable, and the path is [0]; two vertices with the edge the wrong way: unsatisfiable

--- check test | sat_to_path reads positions in order from a hand-made assignment
sat_to_path(3, {i: i in (2, 6, 7) for i in range(1, 10)}) == [1, 2, 0]

+++ question | Counting short strings
--- ask
What does this print?

```python
from itertools import product

print(sum(1 for n in range(4) for _ in product("ab", repeat=n)))
```
--- answer
15
--- why
It counts the strings over {a, b} of length 0, 1, 2 and 3: 1 + 2 + 4 + 8 = 15. In general there are 2^(n+1) − 1 strings of length at most n over a two-letter alphabet, which is why brute-force checks over "every string up to length n" stay small only for small n.

+++ question | The price of determinism
--- ask
An NFA has n states. How many states can the DFA from the subset construction need, in the worst case, even after minimisation?
--- choice
n, since minimisation removes the extra states.
--- choice
n², one per pair of NFA states.
--- choice correct
2ⁿ: for "the k-th symbol from the end is a", an NFA with k + 1 states suffices but every DFA needs 2ᵏ states.
--- choice
n!, one per ordering of the states.
--- why
The subset construction makes one DFA state per reachable set of NFA states, at most 2ⁿ. For the k-th-from-the-end language, all 2ᵏ strings of length k are pairwise distinguishable (Myhill–Nerode), so no DFA, minimal or not, has fewer than 2ᵏ states.

+++ question | The mistake in a pumping proof
--- ask
A student writes: "Suppose L = {aⁿbⁿ} is regular. Let p = 4. Take w = aaaabbbb. Split it as x = a, y = aa, z = abbbb. Then xy²z = aaaaaabbbb is not in L, so L is not regular." Which step is wrong?
--- choice
Choosing w: it is too long.
--- choice correct
Both "let p = 4" and choosing the split: p is given by the lemma, and the argument must defeat every split with |xy| ≤ p and |y| ≥ 1, not one chosen split.
--- choice
Pumping up with i = 2; only i = 0 is allowed.
--- choice
Nothing: the proof is correct.
--- why
The pumping lemma says there is some p, unknown, and for each long enough w in L some split that pumps. To contradict it you must handle every p (write w in terms of p, like aᵖbᵖ) and every legal split (here |xy| ≤ p forces y to be all a's). Any i ≥ 0 may be used.

+++ question | Parse trees of a sum
--- ask
With the grammar E → E + E | a, how many different parse trees does the string `a+a+a` have? Type a number.
--- answer
2
2 trees
two
--- why
The string can be grouped as (a+a)+a or a+(a+a), and each grouping is a different parse tree, so the grammar is ambiguous. The grammar E → E + a | a generates the same strings with a single, left-leaning tree for each.

+++ question | The cost of CYK
--- ask
What is the running time of the CYK algorithm on a string of length n, for a grammar G in Chomsky normal form?
--- choice
O(n), one pass over the string.
--- choice
O(n²·|G|), one step per table cell.
--- choice correct
O(n³·|G|): n² table cells, each trying up to n split points against every rule.
--- choice
O(2ⁿ), since it considers every parse tree.
--- why
The table has one cell per substring, about n²/2 of them. For each cell, CYK tries every split point (up to n) and every rule A → BC. Dynamic programming shares subresults, so it never lists parse trees one by one, which could be exponentially many.

+++ question | Context-free and regular together
--- ask
Which statement about context-free languages (CFLs) is true?
--- choice
The intersection of two CFLs is always context-free.
--- choice correct
The intersection of a CFL with a regular language is always context-free.
--- choice
The complement of a CFL is always context-free.
--- choice
Every CFL is recognised by a deterministic pushdown automaton.
--- why
Running a PDA and a DFA side by side (the product construction with a stack) gives a PDA for the intersection. Two stacks cannot be combined into one: aⁿbⁿcᵐ and aᵐbⁿcⁿ are context-free, but their intersection aⁿbⁿcⁿ is not. Closure under complement would then give intersection by De Morgan, so it fails too. Palindromes need nondeterminism: no deterministic PDA recognises them.

+++ question | Why |vxy| ≤ p
--- ask
In the pumping lemma for context-free languages, the split w = uvxyz satisfies |vxy| ≤ p. Why does that condition make aⁿbⁿcⁿ impossible to pump?
--- choice
Because v and y must be equal.
--- choice correct
Because a window of length at most p inside aᵖbᵖcᵖ cannot touch all three blocks, so pumping changes at most two of the three counts, or breaks the order.
--- choice
Because |vxy| ≤ p forces v and y to be empty.
--- choice
Because p is always 3 for this language.
--- why
The b-block has length p, so any window of length at most p meets at most two neighbouring blocks. Pumping v and y then adds or removes symbols of at most two kinds, leaving the third count unchanged, or mixes the order if v or y straddles a boundary. Either way the result is outside the language.

+++ question | One tape or many
--- ask
Why does the class P not depend on whether Turing machines have one tape or several?
--- choice
Because multitape machines are no faster than single-tape ones.
--- choice correct
Because a single-tape machine simulates t steps of a multitape machine in O(t²) steps, and the square of a polynomial is a polynomial.
--- choice
Because every problem in P can be solved in linear time on any machine.
--- choice
By the Church–Turing thesis, which says all models take the same time.
--- why
Multiple tapes can save a lot: palindromes take about n² steps on one tape and about n on two. But the simulation of lesson 12 costs only a squaring, so polynomial time stays polynomial. The Church–Turing thesis is about what can be computed, not how fast.

+++ question | Which problem is decidable?
--- ask
Which of these problems is decidable?
--- choice
Given two context-free grammars, do they generate the same language?
--- choice correct
Given a context-free grammar, is its language empty?
--- choice
Given a Turing machine, is its language empty?
--- choice
Given a context-free grammar, does it generate every string?
--- why
Emptiness of a grammar is decided by marking the variables that generate terminal strings, a fixpoint that always stops. Equivalence and universality of grammars are undecidable (by reductions through computation histories and PCP), and emptiness of Turing machines is undecidable by Rice's theorem, not even recognisable.

+++ question | What a reduction transfers
--- ask
Suppose A ≤m B, and B is Turing-recognisable. What follows?
--- choice
A is decidable.
--- choice correct
A is Turing-recognisable.
--- choice
B is decidable.
--- choice
Nothing, unless the reduction runs in polynomial time.
--- why
To recognise A, compute f(w) (which always halts) and run B's recogniser on it: it accepts exactly when f(w) ∈ B, that is, when w ∈ A. The same argument transfers decidability, and, with a polynomial-time f, membership in P. That is how "E_TM is not recognisable" follows from a reduction from a non-recognisable language.

+++ question | If 3-SAT were easy
--- ask
Suppose someone proves that 3-SAT can be solved in polynomial time. Which follows?
--- choice
Every decidable problem can be solved in polynomial time.
--- choice correct
P = NP, and every problem in NP, such as CLIQUE and HAMPATH, is solvable in polynomial time.
--- choice
PSPACE = EXPTIME.
--- choice
Only 3-SAT and SAT become easy; other NP-complete problems might still be hard.
--- why
3-SAT is NP-complete, so every NP problem reduces to it in polynomial time, and composing the reduction with the fast algorithm gives a polynomial algorithm for each. It says nothing about problems beyond NP: EXPTIME contains problems provably outside P, so not every decidable problem could become easy.

+++ question | Equivalence of automata
--- ask
Testing whether two DFAs accept the same language is in P. What about two NFAs?
--- choice
Also in P: convert each to a DFA and compare.
--- choice correct
PSPACE-complete: the subset construction can be exponentially large, but the pairs of subsets can be searched one at a time in polynomial space.
--- choice
Undecidable, like equivalence of Turing machines.
--- choice
NP-complete, because a counterexample string is a certificate.
--- why
Converting to DFAs can blow up to 2ⁿ states, so that approach takes exponential time. A search over pairs of subsets needs only polynomial space, so the problem is in PSPACE, and it is PSPACE-hard. A shortest counterexample can have exponential length, so it is not a polynomial certificate, and the problem is not believed to be in NP.
